Lieutenant Sutch was the first of General Feversham’s guests to arrive at Broad Place. He came up from the station road a little after five, while June sunlight lay warm on the Surrey slope, and the old red-brick house glowed as if it had stored the day’s heat in its walls the way a baker stores heat in a brick oven—slow, steady, inexorable.

He crossed the hall with his familiar limp. The portraits of the Fevershams—row upon row of faces hard as old iron—rose to the ceiling, their varnish catching the light in cold glints. Even with the windows open the hall felt close, smelling faintly of wax polish and stale coal-dust, and under it all the dry, papery odour of ancient canvas. Sutch went out to the stone-flagged terrace at the back.

General Feversham sat there upright, boy-straight, looking south towards the Sussex Downs. He rose briskly when he heard the uneven step.

“How’s the leg?” he asked.

He was a small wiry man, white-haired, alert in body; but his face—bony, high-foreheaded—was as barren as a scraped plate. His steel-blue eyes looked at you without taking you in.

“It gave me trouble during the winter,” Sutch answered. “But that was to be expected.”

Feversham nodded, and for a space they let the silence stand. The ground fell away from the terrace into a wide plain—brown earth, emerald fields, dark clumps of trees. Voices rose thin and perfectly clear through the sunshine: a child’s shout, the metallic tap of a gate, a distant dog’s bark—those clean country sounds that settle the mind because they belong there. Far off towards Horsham, a train’s coil of white smoke unrolled and vanished among the trees; even from here you could imagine the faint rhythm of its wheels, a measured industrial pulse intruding and then passing on. On the horizon the Downs lifted, patched with chalk, like flour dusted over a board.

“I thought I should find you here,” Sutch said.

“It was my wife’s favourite corner,” Feversham replied, in the same even tone he might have used for weather. “She would sit here by the hour. She had a queer liking for wide and empty spaces.”

“Yes,” said Sutch. “She had imagination. Her thoughts could people them.”

Feversham glanced at him as though he had spoken in a dialect he didn’t care to learn. What he did not understand he habitually let slip, as if it had no claim to existence. He changed the subject at once.

“There will be a leaf out of our table to-night.”

“Yes. Collins, Barberton, and Vaughan went this winter. Well, we are all permanently shelved upon the world’s half-pay list as it is. The obituary column is just the last formality which gazettes us out of the service altogether.” Sutch eased his crippled leg, remembering—without any wish to—how fourteen years ago that day it had been crushed and twisted by the fall of a scaling-ladder.

“I am glad you came before the others,” Feversham continued. “I want your opinion. This day is more to me than the anniversary of our attack upon the Redan. At the very moment when we were standing under arms in the dark—”

“To the west of the quarries; I remember,” Sutch broke in, drawing a breath he had not known he held. “How should one forget?”

“At that very moment Harry was born in this house.” Feversham said it as he might have said a dispatch had arrived. “I thought, therefore, that if you did not object, he might join us to-night. He happens to be at home. He will, of course, enter the service, and he might learn something, perhaps, which afterward will be of use—one never knows.”

“By all means,” Sutch said, with an eagerness that surprised himself.

For these anniversary dinners were the only times he saw General Feversham, and he had never met Harry. For years Sutch had turned over, like a puzzling recipe whose ingredients refused to marry, the question of what in Feversham had drawn Muriel Graham—so quick of mind, so finely made, so unlike this man in every discernible way. He had never found an explanation. He had had to content himself with the fact that she had, for some reason beyond him, married this older soldier with his narrow certainties. Personal courage and indomitable self-confidence—those, indeed, seemed the only virtues in Feversham that ever rose to the surface.

Sutch, sitting with the June air on his face, went back in thought over twenty years to a London season when he had come home fresh from the China station, before the Crimea, before the Redan. He told himself—because he valued his own impartiality—that his curiosity about Harry was no more than the natural curiosity of a man disabled young who had made a hobby of observing people. He wanted to see whether the boy took after his mother or his father. That was all.

That night Harry Feversham came down to dinner.

The table shone under lamps and candles; silver threw back points of fire; glass held wine dark as blood. The air thickened steadily with tobacco smoke, which blued the candlelight and gave it an underwater blur. Yet under those heavy indoor smells—hot wax, cigars, warmed cloth—Sutch fancied he still caught the faint breath of the house itself: brick that had baked all day, and the ghost of pine from the dark woods outside.

Harry took his place among them and listened while older men talked.

The stories were all of that dark winter in the Crimea, and a fresh story began before its predecessor ended, like voices overlapping in a trench at night. There were deaths spoken of in clipped phrases; hazardous exploits reduced to a shrug; famine and snow recalled as a man recalls bad quarters. The telling was matter-of-fact, as though those experiences were packed away and labelled in some store-room of the mind, brought out only on anniversaries. The strongest comment was “That’s curious”; the most significant exclamation a laugh.

But the boy listened as though those incidents were happening now, inside the walls of the room. His eyes—his mother’s eyes, dark and too awake—moved from speaker to speaker and waited, wide open, until the last word was dropped. The changes of expression shot and quivered across his face so vividly that Sutch could have sworn the boy heard what was not being said: the drone of bullets, the sudden hammer of a charge, the scream of horses, the metallic rip of shell. When a major of artillery spoke of the long suspense between parading the troops and the first command to advance, Harry’s shoulders worked as though minute by minute he bore that delay upon his own back.

And then he did more than work his shoulders. He threw a single furtive, wavering glance backwards.

Sutch was startled; more than startled—he was pained. For this was Muriel Graham’s son. The look was too familiar to him. He had seen it on recruits in their first experience under fire too often to mistake it: the backward search for refuge, for permission, for someone else to decide. One picture sprang up with cruel distinctness: an advancing square at Inkermann; a tall big soldier rushing forward in eagerness, then stopping as if he understood he was alone and must meet alone the charge of a mounted Cossack. Sutch remembered the fatal wavering glance thrown back towards comrades, and the queer sickly smile with it. He remembered the consequence too: the man, though armed, receiving without effort a lance-thrust in the throat.

Sutch looked hurriedly about the table, afraid that General Feversham or any of the guests had noticed the same look on Harry’s face. But no one had eyes for the boy; each man waited too greedily for his own turn to talk. Sutch drew a breath of relief and turned back. Harry sat with elbows on the cloth, head propped between his hands, as if the bright room and its glitter had ceased to exist. He was building, from the clipped anecdotes, a whole world of cries and wounds, riderless chargers, men writhing in a fog of cannon-smoke. The least graphic phrase about the biting days and nights in the trenches made him shiver; even his face grew pinched, as though the iron frost were actually eating into his bones.

Sutch touched him lightly on the elbow.

“You renew those days for me,” he said. “Though the heat is dripping down the windows, I feel the chill of the Crimea.”

Harry roused as if waking.

“The stories renew them,” he said.

“No,” Sutch answered. “It is you listening to the stories.”

Before the boy could speak again, General Feversham’s voice cut sharply from the head of the table.

“Harry, look at the clock!”

All eyes turned at once upon him. The hands made the acutest of angles. It was close upon midnight. From eight o’clock—without a question, without a murmur—he had sat listening.

He rose, reluctant.

“Must I go, father?” he asked.

The guests intervened at once, a chorus—half indulgent, half approving. The talk, they said, was good for him: a first taste of powder which might stand him in good stead. Besides, it was his birthday. A youngster of fourteen didn’t sit those hours without a kick against the table-leg unless he was entertained. Let him stay.

For once General Feversham relaxed.

“Very well,” he said. “Harry shall have an hour’s furlough from his bed. A single hour won’t make much difference.”

Harry’s eyes went to his father’s face and stayed there with a curious steady gaze. To Sutch it seemed like a question spoken without words—an old, private dialect between mother and son that the father could not hear: Are you blind?

But General Feversham had already turned back to his neighbours, and Harry sat down quietly. Again he propped his chin upon his hands and listened with all his soul. Yet it was not entertainment that held him; it was compulsion, as though a spell had been laid upon him. His face grew unnaturally white, his eyes unnaturally large, while the candles burned lower and redder through the haze of smoke and the wine in the decanters sank.

Half the hour went so. Then General Feversham, jogged by the unlucky mention of a name, blurted out in his jerky fashion:

“Lord Wilmington. One of the best names in England, if you please. Did you ever see his house in Warwickshire? Every inch of ground you’d think would have a voice to bid him play the man, if only in remembrance of his fathers. It seemed incredible—mere camp rumour—but the rumour grew. If it was whispered at the Alma, it was spoken aloud at Inkermann, it was shouted at Balaclava. Before Sebastopol the hideous thing was proved.

“Wilmington was acting as galloper to his general. I believe upon my soul the general chose him for the duty so the fellow might set himself right. There were three hundred yards of bullet-swept flat ground and a message to be carried across. Had Wilmington toppled off his horse, why, there were the whispers silenced for ever. Had he ridden through alive, he earned distinction besides.

“But he didn’t dare. He refused! Imagine it. He sat shaking on his horse and declined. You should have seen the general. His face turned the colour of that Burgundy.” Feversham nodded at the bottle as though it were a proper measure of shame. “‘No doubt you have a previous engagement,’ he said—in the politest voice you ever heard. Just that, not a word of abuse. A previous engagement on the battlefield! For the life of me I could hardly help laughing.

“But it was a tragic business for Wilmington. He was broken, of course, and slunk back to London. Every house closed to him; he dropped out of his circle like a lead bullet you let slip into the sea. The very women in Piccadilly spat if he spoke to them; and he blew his brains out in a back bedroom off the Haymarket. Curious that, eh? He hadn’t the pluck to face bullets when his name was at stake, yet he could blow his own brains out afterward.”

Sutch glanced at the clock as the story ended. It was a quarter to one. Harry still had a quarter of an hour’s furlough, and that time was taken by a retired surgeon-general with a great wagging beard who sat nearly opposite the boy.

“I can tell you an incident still more curious,” the surgeon-general said. “The man in this case had never been under fire before, but he was of my own profession. Life and death were part of his business. Nor was he in any particular danger. It happened during a hill campaign in India. We were encamped in a valley, and a few Pathans used to lie out on the hillside at night and take long shots into the camp. A bullet ripped through the canvas of the hospital tent—that was all. The surgeon crept out to his own quarters, and his orderly discovered him half an hour afterward lying in his blood stone-dead.”

“Hit?” the major exclaimed.

“Not a bit of it,” said the surgeon-general. “He had quietly opened his instrument-case in the dark, taken out a lancet, and severed his femoral artery. Sheer panic, do you see, at the whistle of a bullet.”

Even on these men, hardened to horrors, the bald simplicity struck. There were half-uttered exclamations of disbelief. Men moved restlessly, as if their chairs had turned rough beneath them. Someone gulped his wine as though to wash the knowledge away. Another shook his shoulders, like a dog shaking water.

Only Harry sat perfectly still.

His hands were clenched on his knees; he leaned forward a little across the table towards the surgeon-general. His cheeks were white as paper. His eyes burned—burned with ferocity. He looked, suddenly, like an animal trapped, muscles gathered, body taut, as if he might spring.

Sutch felt a fear so sharp it was almost physical: that the boy meant to leap across the table and strike, not from anger alone but from some savage despair at what he had heard. He had already half lifted a restraining hand when General Feversham’s voice intervened, flat as ever, and Harry’s posture slackened as if a string had been cut.

“Queer incomprehensible things happen,” Feversham said. “Here are two of them. You can only say they are the truth and pray God you may forget ’em. But you can’t explain, for you can’t understand.”

Sutch, moved, laid his hand upon Harry’s shoulder.

“Can you?” he asked—then regretted it almost as soon as the words escaped. Yet they were spoken.

Harry’s eyes turned swiftly to Sutch and rested on his face—not with guilt, not with a boy’s flurry, but quietly, inscrutably. He did not answer. The question was answered, instead, by General Feversham’s scorn.

“Harry understand!” the general exclaimed, with a snort. “How should he? He’s a Feversham.”

That mutely asked question—Are you blind?—seemed to flare again between father and son, only now Sutch felt it himself. Never had he heard an untruth so demonstrably untrue. One look at the two proved it. Harry wore his father’s name, but he had his mother’s eyes, his mother’s broad forehead, his mother’s delicacy of profile, his mother’s imagination—that burdensome gift which makes sounds sharper, shadows deeper, and stories into living presences.

Perhaps it needed a stranger to see. Feversham had looked on his son’s face so long that it had ceased to signify.

“Look at the clock, Harry.”

The hour’s furlough had run out. Harry rose and drew a breath.

“Good night, sir,” he said, and walked to the door.

The servants had long since gone to bed. As Harry opened the door, the hall beyond gaped black, like the mouth of night. For a second he hesitated on the threshold, and almost shrank back into the lighted room as though peril waited in that void. And peril did—the peril of his own thoughts.

He stepped out and closed the door behind him.

Inside, the decanter went round again. Soda-water bottles popped. The talk returned to its accustomed groove. Harry was forgotten at once by all but Sutch.

Sutch, despite his professed detachment, was the kindliest of men. He had more kindliness than observation by a great deal. And there were special reasons—reaching back to Muriel Graham—that made the boy’s isolation tug at him like a hand on a sleeve. He sat for a while, profoundly disturbed, then rose on impulse, went to the door, opened it noiselessly, slipped out, and closed it without a click.

This is what he saw.

Harry Feversham stood in the centre of the hall holding a lighted candle high above his head. He was looking up towards the portraits of the Fevershams as they climbed the walls and disappeared into roof-darkness. The dining-room voices came muffled through the panels, a blurred chorus; but the hall itself was silent—so silent that the tiny sounds seemed magnified: the faint hiss of the candlewick, the soft tick of cooling timber, the minute creak of the boy’s sleeve as his arm strained upward. The flame flickered, and with it the light wandered across painted faces—glowing on a red coat, glinting on steel, brightening a button, then sliding away.

There was not one portrait that did not glisten with uniform colours, and there were many. Father and son, the Fevershams had been soldiers from the family’s beginning. Father and son, in lace collars and bucket boots, in Ramillies wigs and breastplates, in velvet coats with powder in their hair, in shakos and swallow-tails, in high stocks and frogged coats—they looked down upon the last of the line, summoning him to the same service.

They were men stamped from one mould. No change of fashion could disguise their kinship: lean faces, thin lips, firm chins, straight level mouths, narrow foreheads, steel-blue inexpressive eyes. Men of courage and resolution, no doubt, but without nerves, without subtleties, without imagination. Sturdy men—let it be said frankly—rather stupid. First-class fighting men, but not one of them a first-class soldier.

Harry saw none of that. To him they were terrible and portentous. He stood before them like a criminal before judges, reading condemnation in those cold eyes.

Sutch understood then why the candle flame flickered. There was no draught in the hall. The boy’s hand shook.

And finally, as if he heard a verdict in the silence and accepted it, Harry bowed to the portraits.

As he raised his head he saw Lieutenant Sutch in the doorway embrasure. He did not start. He uttered no word. He let his eyes rest on Sutch and waited.

It was Sutch, the grown man, who found himself embarrassed.

“Harry,” he said, and though awkward he had the tact to use the tone and language one would use not to a child but to a comrade, “we meet for the first time to-night. But I knew your mother a long time ago. I like to think I have the right to call her by that much misused word—‘friend.’ Have you anything to tell me?”

“Nothing,” said Harry.

“The mere telling sometimes lightens a trouble.”

“It is kind of you. There is nothing.”

Sutch was at a loss. The boy’s loneliness stood in the hall like a second darkness. He tried again, more practically, as a man offers what he has.

He took his card-case from his pocket. “You will find my address upon this card. Perhaps some day you will give me a few days of your company. I can offer you on my side a day or two’s hunting.”

A spasm of pain crossed Harry’s face—fugitive but unmistakable, like the flinch at an unexpected sharp noise. It passed as swiftly as it came.

“Thank you, sir,” he said monotonously. “You are very kind.”

“And if ever you want to talk over a difficult question with an older man, I am at your service.” Sutch made his voice formally steady, lest the boy’s sensitiveness should imagine laughter where there was none.

Harry took the card and repeated his thanks.

Then he went upstairs to bed.

Sutch waited uncomfortably in the hall until the candlelight diminished and disappeared along the stair, leaving only the portraits’ dim sheen and the muffled rise and fall of talk through the door. Something was amiss; of that he was very sure. There were words he should have spoken, but he had not found them—had not found the right tongue for what he meant, as if the thing itself lived in a dialect he did not possess.

He went back into the dining-room. With a feeling—foolish and yet urgent—that he might repair his omission, he filled his glass, called for silence, and stood.

“Gentlemen,” he said, “this is June 15th,” and there was applause and rapping on the table. “It is the anniversary of our attack upon the Redan. It is also Harry Feversham’s birthday. For us, our work is done. I ask you to drink the health of one of the youngsters who are ousting us. His work lies before him. The traditions of the Feversham family are very well known to us. May Harry Feversham carry them on! May he add distinction to a distinguished name!”

At once the company was on its feet.

“Harry Feversham!”

The name was shouted with such hearty goodwill that the glasses rang on the table. “Harry Feversham, Harry Feversham,” they cried again and again, while old General Feversham sat aflush with pride, his chest lifted as if he wore his own decorations anew.

And a boy, a minute afterward, in a room high up in the house, heard the muffled words of their chorus—heard the rhythmic thump of voices as one might hear a distant song carried through floorboards—and believed they were drinking his father’s health.

He turned over in bed and lay shivering.

In his mind he saw a broken officer slinking at night in the shadows of London streets. He pushed back the flap of a tent and stooped over a man lying stone-dead in his blood, an open lancet clenched in his right hand. And he saw that the face of the broken officer and the face of the dead surgeon were one face—one unescapable face.

His own.

Thirteen years later—again in June—Harry Feversham’s health was drunk, though with less ceremony than in boyhood, and with no clatter of a large company. The toast was pledged in a dining room set high in a great, blunt-shouldered block of London masonry that rose above Westminster like a dark cliff with window-ledges of light. A man crossing St. James’s Park at night, passing the suspension bridge, might look up at those stacked, shining panes and imagine a mountain hollowed out in the city, and busy, secret hands working within.

On the tenth floor Harry had taken a flat for his furlough from India; and in that dining room, with its dark, restful furniture and a fire burning because the weather had turned sharp and raw in defiance of the calendar, four men sat after dinner with cigars and glasses. The room held its own small weather: the resinous scent of coal, a faint sweetness from the wine, the warm breath of the hearth. Beyond the bay window—blinds left up—London spread in tiers of lamps and moving light, and the low, ceaseless carriage-rumble rose like surf.

Harry Feversham looked much as he had in youth, save for the fair moustache that threw his darker hair into stronger contrast and the settled, athletic length of a grown man. His face was still the same face that once had been watched and weighed with such intentness; only the years had tightened it into its adult lines.

Of his companions, two were brother-officers on leave, gathered that afternoon at his club and brought home as one brings home the last good talk of the day. Captain Trench was small, already thinning on top, with a sharp, resourceful face and black eyes that never seemed at rest. Lieutenant Willoughby, by contrast, wore the heavy look of stubborn vacancy: round forehead, thick snub nose, and protruding eyes that gave him an air of invincible stupidity. He spoke rarely, and never quite to the point at hand—rather to some earlier point he had been turning over in his head as one might revolve a button between finger and thumb. All the while he twisted the ends of his moustache upward toward his eyes, as if trying to make ferocity out of dullness. One dismissed him at first glance, then reconsidered him: there was harm in his kind of man, because he was born as obstinate as he was slow. Few ideas, and he clung to them; argument slid off him unheard, and behind those vacant eyes his crippled thoughts went on satisfying him.

The fourth was Durrance of the East Surrey—Harry’s friend—who had come in answer to a telegram.

It was June, 1882. In drawing rooms and clubs, civilians spoke of Egypt with anxious brows; soldiers spoke of it with the eagerness that comes of hearing distant drums. Arabi Pasha was strengthening Alexandria’s defences in spite of threats, and farther south another danger was gathering—swelling, dark and charged, like a thundercloud above the dry plains. A year earlier a young Dongolawi, Mohammed Ahmed, slight and tall, had moved through the villages of the White Nile preaching the coming of a Saviour with the fervour of a revivalist. The men and women ground down by Turkish tax-gatherers listened and found his promise everywhere: repeated, so the story went, in the whisper through withered grass; stamped, by miracle or imagination, upon the eggs they gathered. In 1882 he declared himself that Saviour and won his first battles.

“There will be trouble,” said Trench; and on that text three of the four talked, turning it this way and that as soldiers do, half forecasting and half tasting the thing they are trained for.

But in a pause—rare, and a little awkward—Harry spoke of something else.

“I’m very glad you were all able to dine with me to-night,” he said, with the faint embarrassment of a man bringing private news into public company. “I telegraphed to Castleton as well—an officer of ours,” he explained to Durrance, “but he was dining with a big man in the War Office, and leaves for Scotland afterwards, so he could not come. I have news of a sort.”

They leaned forward, still full of Egypt and the rumour of marching. Harry’s news, however, had nothing to do with war.

“I only reached London this morning from Dublin,” he said. “I have been some weeks in Dublin.”

Durrance lifted his eyes from the tablecloth and looked at him steadily.

“Yes?” he asked, as if there were only one possible ending to that sentence and he meant to hear it plainly.

“I have come back engaged to be married.”

Durrance raised his glass and drank.

“Well, here’s luck to you, Harry,” he said; and that was all.

The words were curt, but nothing was missing from them in Harry’s hearing. Between those two men affectionate phrases had never been currency. Their friendship was a tool, sound and trustworthy, put into their hands for hard use; they knew its value and did not talk of it. Both were grateful for it as for a gift they had not earned. Both, too, understood that it might demand sacrifice—and that if it did, the sacrifice would be made without announcement, without pose, and without a word to make it heavier.

“Thank you, Jack,” said Harry. “I’m glad of your good wishes. It was you who introduced me to Ethne; I can’t forget it.”

Durrance set his glass down carefully. For a moment he sat with his hands on the table edge, eyes lowered, as though he were choosing words by touch.

“Yes,” he said levelly. “I did you a good turn then.”

He seemed about to add more, and to be uncertain how to put it; but Trench’s quick, practical voice cut in with a question that saved him.

“Will this make any difference?” Trench asked.

Harry put his cigar back between his lips before replying, as if the small ritual gave him time.

“You mean, shall I leave the service?” he asked slowly. “I don’t know.”

At that, Durrance rose and crossed to the bay window, turning his back on the room and on the three men behind him. Harry, mistaking the abrupt movement for a reproach, aimed his explanation not at Trench but at Durrance’s broad shoulders.

“I don’t know,” he repeated. “It will need thought. There’s much to be said. On the one side, of course, there’s my father—my career, such as it is. On the other hand there is her father—Dermod Eustace.”

“He wishes you to chuck your commission?” Willoughby asked.

“He has no doubt the Irishman’s objection to constituted authority,” Trench said with a laugh. “But need you subscribe to it, Feversham?”

“It isn’t merely that.” Harry’s voice stayed directed, stubbornly and a little defensively, toward the window. “Dermod is old. His estates are going to ruin. And there are other things. You know, Jack?”

He had to repeat the appeal; and even then Durrance answered without turning, as if his mind were elsewhere.

“Yes, I know,” he said; and then added, like a man quoting some foolish catchword to fill a gap, “If you want any whisky, rap twice on the floor with your foot. The servants understand.”

“Precisely,” Harry said, taking it as assent, and went on weighing his words as though placing them carefully on a scale. “Besides, there’s Ethne herself. Dermod for once did an appropriate thing when he gave her that name. She’s of her country—and more, of her county. She has the love of it in her bones. I don’t think she could be quite happy in India, or indeed in any place not within reach of Donegal: the smell of the peat, its streams, the brown friendliness of its hills. One has to consider that.”

He paused for a response. None came. So he continued, and if Durrance did not truly listen, Trench listened with the close attention of a man who likes reasons set out in order.

Durrance, meanwhile, had no thought of reproach. He knew Harry was speaking; he even wished, fiercely and secretly, that he would go on speaking a little longer—anything, so long as it kept the room’s eyes off him. He stood staring out at the city. Across from the window the glare from Pall Mall struck up into the low sky. Chains of light banked one above another as the town rose northward; below, the traffic made a constant, battering murmur, a million wheels and hoofbeats blended into one heavy sound. Far down at his feet lay St. James’s Park—silent and black, a quiet pool of darkness within glitter and noise.

He wanted to escape into that darkness, into the secrecy of trees and open air where the ear could find its own measure again, away from the unending grind and roar that pressed on the mind. But he could not leave without remark. So he kept his back turned, leaned his forehead against the cold glass, and held himself still. He was face to face with one of those sacrifices that must not be mentioned, and no sign must betray.

Harry’s explanations, seriously considered and sober, were enough at last for Trench.

“Well,” Trench said, lifting his glass again, “I drink to you, Feversham, with all the proper sentiments.”

“I too, old man,” Willoughby added obediently, following his senior’s lead.

They drank; their glasses rattled softly on the table. As the sound died, there came a knock at the door.

Trench and Willoughby looked up. Durrance turned from the window. Harry said, “Come in,” and his servant brought him a telegram on a small tray.

Harry tore the envelope open without care—read the message with the same careless motion—and then sat very still with the pink slip in his hand. His face changed at once, growing grave, not in the stunned way of a man struck down, but in the quiet, deliberate way of a man thinking hard.

A complete silence fell. His three guests looked away as if by instinct. Durrance turned back to the window; Willoughby twisted his moustache and stared at the ceiling; Trench shifted his chair and fixed his eyes on the fire. Each posture carried suspense, like men listening for a second knock.

“There is no answer,” Harry said at last; and then was silent again.

Once he lifted his head and looked at Trench as though he meant to speak, but thought better of it and dropped his eyes back to the telegram.

Then the silence was cut—not by any of the men in the room, but by the world outside. From the parade ground of Wellington Barracks the drums and fifes sounded the tattoo through the open window with startling clearness—sharp as a summons. The music diminished as the band marched away across gravel, then swelled again, then faded.

Harry did not move, but his face took on the look of a man listening and listening thoughtfully, as he had read thoughtfully. Years afterward, each of the three guests would see that moment again with an odd, indelible vividness: the warm, homely fire; the open window; London’s layers of light; Harry sitting with the telegram in his hand; and the tattoo sounding first like a command, then dwindling until it was small and pretty, as though it beckoned where a moment ago it had ordered.

Just before the last notes died, Harry rose abruptly. He crumpled the telegram in his hands and tossed it into the fire. Then he leaned back against the chimney-piece, a little to one side, and said again, as though he were summing up the earlier argument and pushing the message away into the same uncertainty—

“I don’t know.”

The spell broke. The room breathed again. But the fire caught the telegram and shook it so it moved like a living thing in pain. It twisted; part of it unrolled. For a second a smooth strip lay open, lit by yellow flame and not yet touched, and two or three words sprang clear, starkly legible, out of the glare. Then the flame seized even that, and it shrivelled into black tatters.

Trench had been staring into the fire all the while.

“You return to Dublin, I suppose?” Durrance asked. He had come back into the room again, and like the others he felt an unexplained relief, as if danger had been named and therefore postponed.

“To Dublin? No,” Harry said. “I go to Donegal in three weeks’ time. There is to be a dance. It is hoped you will come.”

“I’m not sure that I can manage it,” Durrance replied. “There is just a chance, I believe—should trouble come in the East—that I may go out on the staff.”

So talk swung back to Egypt and the likelihood of peace or war, and stayed there until the boom of Westminster’s clock told them it was eleven.

Trench rose on the last stroke; Willoughby and Durrance followed.

“I shall see you to-morrow,” Durrance said to Harry.

“As usual,” Harry answered.

They went down together and walked across the Park, their steps softened by the night air. At the corner of Pall Mall they separated: Durrance up St. James’s Street, Trench and Willoughby across into St. James’s Square.

There Trench, to Willoughby’s surprise—for he was not a demonstrative man—slipped his arm through Willoughby’s.

“You know Castleton’s address?” Trench asked.

“Albemarle Street,” Willoughby said, and gave the number.

“He leaves Euston at twelve o’clock. It is now ten minutes past eleven,” Trench said, his brisk mind already measuring distances and minutes. “Are you curious, Willoughby? I confess to curiosity. I am an inquisitive, methodical person; and when a man gets a telegram bidding him tell Trench something and he tells Trench nothing, I am curious—as a philosopher—to know what that something is. Castleton is the only other officer of our regiment in London. It is likely, therefore, that the telegram came from Castleton. Castleton, too, was dining with a big man from the War Office. I think that if we take a hansom to Albemarle Street, we shall just catch Castleton upon his doorstep.”

Willoughby understood very little of Trench’s chain of thought; nevertheless he agreed cordially.

“I think it would be prudent,” he said; and he hailed a passing cab.

A moment later the two men were driving toward Albemarle Street, the wheels striking sparks of sound from the stones as London’s night-noise closed around them again.

Durrance walked back alone to his lodging, and the streets of London—stone, soot, gaslight—fell away beneath the older, wetter country that memory carried in its mouth.

Two years ago, on a day when the air along the Lennon River tasted of peat smoke and rain-washed heather, old Dermod Eustace had sent for him by a whim that pretended to be hospitality and was in truth command. Durrance had gone unwillingly to the house in Donegal, expecting the blunt, agrarian tyranny of the father—salted talk, hard hands, a man who measured the world by fields and fences—yet it was the daughter who undid him.

Ethne had played the violin that night.

Not facing them—never facing them—because she would not let any stray gesture, any pitying blink, any approving smile, interrupt the exact thread of sound she was pulling from the strings. Her back was to the room, her shoulders still as a cook’s when the knife finds its rhythm. The fire in the grate crackled like fat spitting in a pan; outside, the river worried its stones; and between those domestic noises—those necessary, human noises—her bow drew a music that tasted of places she had never seen.

There was one piece, an overture—Melusine—whose opening had the salt-sob of waves against rock, the same note the Atlantic makes when it is hungry. It told him of long travel and strange faces; of moonlight laid like silver scales across open sea; of whispering voices from the underside of desert wind. It grew darker, deeper, as though the instrument had learned a dialect older than speech. It spoke of joys too high to be reached and griefs too immense to be complained of—griefs that carried a nobility simply because they did not bargain for mercy. It spoke of longings that no dominant language could pin down, longings that broke when translated and lost their flavour as surely as a crushed herb goes bitter.

And yet—never a note of mere complaint.

That thought, hard-won and held with care as one holds a hot dish steady, Durrance kept turning in his mind now, as he went through London: true music cannot complain. Perhaps that was why, when he rode into the Row next morning, his blue eyes looked out from his bronzed face with not a jot less of their usual friendliness. If the world meant to take from him, it would not be by teaching him to whine.

At half-past nine he waited where the lilacs and laburnums bunched at the end of the sand, their scent thick and sugary, like bruised blossoms in a bowl, and the bees working them made a small, contented industry of sound. But Harry Feversham did not come.

He did not come the next morning either, nor any morning for three weeks. Since Oxford it had been their habit—when both were in town—to meet there at that hour; and Durrance, puzzled, felt the uneasy absence of a familiar footfall in the day’s music. It was as though some key note had been removed from the park’s soundscape: the soft thud of hooves beside his, the shared cadence of talk. The city, meanwhile, grew louder with rumour—war spoken of everywhere, a rising industrial roar that flattened subtlety, until at last rumour hardened into certainty.

Then one morning Feversham kept the tryst.

Durrance had news ready on his tongue, bright as a new coin. “I told you luck might look my way. Well, she has. I go out to Egypt on General Graham’s staff. There’s talk we may run down the Red Sea to Suakin afterward.”

The lift in his voice brought a clear envy into Feversham’s eyes—an envy so plain Durrance could not miss it, and so strange it startled him into a brief, guilty pleasure. He understood it in the simplest way: a soldier wanting what another soldier had been offered.

“It is rough on you,” he said, with genuine sympathy, “that your regiment has to stay behind.”

Feversham rode beside him in silence, the leather of his saddle creaking with each shift of weight. The park was awake with early summer: birds calling in the chestnuts, distant wheels on the road like a river’s tumble, and the great city beyond making its constant, tiring thunder. At length, as they neared the chairs beneath the trees, Feversham spoke.

“That was expected. The day you dined with me I sent in my papers.”

“That night?” Durrance turned in the saddle.

“After we had gone?”

“Yes,” Feversham said, and wondered—as he often wondered now—whether corrections were meant to sting.

Durrance rode forward silently.

Feversham, feeling again that silence as a rebuke, misread it as he had misread so much lately. For Durrance’s thoughts were elsewhere, and when he spoke it was with a hearty simplicity and even a laugh.

“I remember. You gave us your reasons that night. But for the life of me I can’t help wishing that we had been going out together. When do you leave for Ireland?”

“To-night.”

“So soon?”

They turned their horses and rode westward down the long alley of trees. The morning was fresh enough to taste. The limes and chestnuts kept their early green; the May—late that year—still hung in delicate white clusters like sifted flour on branches, and the rhododendrons burned darkly beneath, red as coals under ash. Sunlight made a trembling haze over the grass. From far off, the city’s roar came and went like water over stones: not a single sound but a mass of them, a brutal mingling that swallowed the fine edges of hearing.

“It is a long time since we bathed in Sandford Lasher,” Durrance said, letting the remembered cold bite his skin.

“Or froze in the Easter vacations in the big snow-gully on Great End,” Feversham returned.

Both felt it—without naming it—that a volume in their book had come to its last page. The earlier pages were pleasant to reread; the next were unopened and might not keep their promise. So they looked backward, leaf by leaf, before putting the book away.

“You must stay with us, Jack, when you come back,” Feversham said.

Durrance had trained himself not to flinch at that “us.” He did not. If his left hand tightened on the reins, Feversham could not see it.

“If I come back,” Durrance said.

“You know my creed. I could never pity a man who died on active service. I would very much like to come by that end myself.”

It was a plain creed, spoken plainly. Not a prophecy, not a melodrama; merely a man stating that to die decently could be worth a good many years of merely existing. Even so, he feared for an instant that Feversham might hear something else in it, so he looked quickly at his friend.

He saw again that puzzling envy.

“You see there are worse things which can happen,” Durrance went on, as though to blunt the edge of any misunderstanding. “Disablement, for instance. Clever men could make a shift, perhaps, to put up with it. But what in the world should I do if I had to sit in a chair all my days? It makes me shiver to think of it.” He shook his broad shoulders as though to throw the very idea off. “Well—this is the last ride. Let us gallop.”

He let out his horse. Feversham followed. Side by side they raced down the sand, hooves striking a bright, quick percussion that rose cleanly above the distant urban din, a brief return to a sound that belonged to open air and muscle, not to engines and crowds.

At the bottom of the Row they stopped, shook hands, and—by the curtest nods—parted. Feversham rode out of the park. Durrance turned back and walked his horse up toward the seats beneath the trees.

As a boy at Southpool in Devonshire, on the wooded creek of the Salcombe estuary, he had always carried a restlessness in him like yeast in dough, working, lifting, never letting him lie flat. He would stand smelling tar and salt and wet rope, listening to the gulls’ ragged cries and the hush of tide on mud, and long to sail down the creek, out past the familiar dark woods, over the level sea toward queer outlandish countries and peoples. The restlessness had grown with him. “Guessens,” even when he inherited it with its farms and lands, stayed in his mind as a place to come home to, not a place to be contained by.

He exaggerated that restlessness now on purpose, as though by naming it he could make it do its work and carry him away from what hurt. And against it he set words of Feversham’s—words he knew were true: Ethne Eustace would hardly be happy outside Donegal. Even had things fallen out differently—so he phrased it, carefully—there might have been a clash. Perhaps it was as well that Harry Feversham was to marry Ethne, and not another than Feversham.

Thus he argued with himself until, for a moment, the world shifted.

The riders before him blurred and vanished. The ladies in coloured frocks beneath the trees dissolved into heat and light. The familiar English trees dwindled into ragged mimosas; the brown sand spread outward in a widening circle and took on the bright colour of honey; black stones began to heap themselves like coal and flash like mirrors in a ruthless sun. He was deep in anticipations of the Soudan—its glare, its thirst, its stripped-down truth—when his name was called softly in a woman’s voice.

He looked up. He was close by the rails.

“How do you do, Mrs. Adair?” he said, and stopped his horse.

Mrs. Adair gave him her hand across the rails. She was his neighbour at Southpool, a year or two older—a tall woman with thick brown hair that held many shades as if light had been kneaded into it, and a peculiar pallor to her face. At this moment the pallor had warmed; there was colour in her cheeks, as though news itself could season blood.

“I have news for you,” Durrance said. “Two special items. One—Harry Feversham is to be married.”

“To whom?” she asked quickly, too quickly.

“You should know. It was in your house in Hill Street that Harry first met her—and I introduced him. He has been improving the acquaintance in Dublin.”

Mrs. Adair already understood. The welcome in her eyes was unmistakable.

“Ethne Eustace!” she cried. “They will be married soon?”

“There is nothing to prevent it.”

“I am glad,” she said, and sighed as though a weight had been lifted from some private shelf of the heart.

“What is your second item?”

“As good as the first. I go out on General Graham’s staff.”

Mrs. Adair fell silent. Anxiety came into her eyes; the colour retreated from her cheeks as quickly as it had arrived, leaving her face pale again, a candlewick starved of air.

“You are very glad, I suppose,” she said slowly.

His answer left no room for doubt. “I should think I was. I go soon, too—and the sooner the better. I will come and dine some night, if I may, before I go.”

“My husband will be pleased to see you,” she said, and there was a chill in it, a small frost on the words.

Durrance did not notice. He had reasons of his own for making the most of this chance. He urged his enthusiasm aloud, laying it bare more for himself than for her, as a man might speak a vow into wind to hear it return to him. Mrs. Adair, to him, had always been a somewhat vague figure: handsome in a queer foreign way not uncommon along the Devon and Cornwall coasts, always well dressed, always friendly. Perhaps her chief merit in his eyes was that she had made friends with Ethne Eustace. Beyond that, his knowledge ended.

He was to learn more.

He rode away with the old regret that the fortunes of himself and his friend were, that morning, finally severed. Yet in truth—though he could not hear it yet—he had set the strands of a new rope twisting into place, a rope that would draw them together again in a strange and terrible relationship.

Mrs. Adair left the park too, and walked home very thoughtfully, as though listening for something she could not name amid the city’s many sounds.

Durrance had one week to provide his equipment and set his Devonshire affairs in order. It passed in a continuous hurry: lists, fittings, visits, signatures—life reduced to necessities packed tight. Each day his newspaper lay unfolded in his rooms, unread, like bread gone stale for want of appetite.

The general was to travel overland to Brindisi. Toward the end of July, on an evening of wind and rain, Durrance stepped from the Dover pier into the mail-boat for Calais. The air smelled of wet rope and seaweed and coal smoke; the wind worried at the boat’s rigging so that it sang and clattered, a harsh mechanical chorus. In spite of the weather and the gloom, a small crowd had gathered to give the general a send-off. When the ropes were cast off a feeble cheer rose, thin as watered soup, swallowed almost at once by the racket of wind and paddle and waves.

Durrance leaned on the bulwarks, looking back at England. He wondered idly whether this would be his last sight of it, and wished—sharply, unexpectedly—that some one of his friends had come down to see him go.

Then, for a moment, it seemed the wish was answered.

Beneath a gas-lamp on the pier he caught sight of a man, and the man had the stature and the likeness of Harry Feversham. Rain streaked the quay; the lamp’s tongue of light flickered in its glass; the wind jerked the flame’s steadiness into a nervous dance. Durrance rubbed his eyes and looked again. He could be certain only that a man stood there, and he could only vaguely distinguish the whiteness of a face.

An illusion, he told himself. A coincidence. Feversham would be in Ireland, most likely, under a clean sky in some high Donegal garden, listening to Ethne’s violin, the familiar domestic noises softened by grass and distance.

But as he turned away a lull came in the wind. The lights steadied, burning bright. For an instant the face leaped out from shadow—distinct in feature and expression—and Durrance’s breath caught as though he had swallowed cold water.

He leaned out over the side of the boat.

“Harry!” he shouted, the wonder in his voice breaking clean through the storm.

The figure under the lamp did not stir.

The wind gusted again. The lights wavered. The paddles churned; black water boiled; the mail-boat slid beyond the pier and the man under the lamp became once more a blur, a possibility, a smear of pale among wet boards.

It was an illusion, Durrance repeated. A stranger who happened to look like Harry Feversham.

It could not be Feversham—because the face he had seen, for that heartbeat of clarity, was haggard and wistful, stamped with an extraordinary misery: the face of a man cast out from among his fellows.

Durrance had been busy all week. He had clean forgotten the arrival of that telegram and the long, suspenseful perusal of it. His newspaper had lain open and neglected, its print mute as a dead language when no one speaks it.

But Harry Feversham had come to see him off.

Feversham had taken the night mail for Dublin after that ride with Durrance in the Row, and, before the next day had finished turning, he had crossed Lough Swilly on a squat little cargo steamer that smelt of tar, wet rope, and salt fish. Once a week it nosed its way up the Lennon River as far as Ramelton, pushing through water that held the colour of peat and old stories. The engine beat a steady iron pulse, but it was the river’s own voice he noticed most—the soft scold of current against piling, the slap where eddies worried the stones—as if the place had its music ready before he arrived.

On the quay Ethne waited in a dog-cart. She gave him her hand, and with it a smile that belonged to comrades who had shared weather and worry.

“You are surprised to see me,” she said, catching the look he tried to hide.

“I always am,” he answered. “For always you exceed my thoughts of you.”

Her smile altered—no longer merely comradeship; it warmed, and it wounded.

“I shall drive slowly,” she said when his traps were stowed. “I brought no groom on purpose. There will be guests to-morrow. We have only to-day.”

They went along the wide causeway by the riverside, and turned up the steep, narrow street. Feversham sat quiet beside her, letting his eyes take the town in with a curious, possessive pride, because this drowsy little place—its grey bridge, the dark thicket of trees climbing the far bank, the water’s continual singing above the shallows—was part of her daily life. Ramelton lay as if listening to itself: cartwheels were few, voices softened by habit; even a door-latch, lifted and dropped, seemed to have its proper note.

Ethne at twenty-one was tall and strong, supple as a young ash, square in the shoulders without losing grace. She moved with a deer’s lightness, hair dark brown and coiled low at her neck, cheeks bright with health as if the wind had seasoned her. Her clear grey eyes met other eyes frankly, as though she despised the little tricks of looking away. Her nature answered her looks: honest, simple with the straightforward simplicity of strength that can afford gentleness and has no need of violence. People in Ramelton still told, with a shiver of admiration, how once a horse bolted down that very steep hill at the clatter of a pail. The reins had fallen; she sat for a breath helpless. Then, cool as if measuring flour, she climbed over the front of the dog-cart as it pitched and raced, balanced along the shafts, reached the reins at the horse’s neck, and stopped it ten yards from the sharp curve above the bridge. There were thorns in her courage, too, though Feversham had not yet pricked his fingers on them.

For the first part of the drive she was almost as silent as he, and when she spoke it was with an absent air, as though a larger matter paced her thoughts. Not until they were clear of the town and out upon the undulating road towards Letterkenny did she turn quickly, as if she could bear it no longer, and say:

“I saw this morning that your regiment was ordered from India to Egypt. You could have gone with it, had I not come in your way. There would have been chances of distinction. I have hindered you, and I am very sorry. Of course you could not know there was any possibility—still, I can understand it is hard to be left behind. I blame myself.”

He stared at the road ahead until the hedgerows blurred. Then, in a voice that rasped as though something had caught in his throat:

“You need not.”

“How can I help it?” she went on. “I blame myself the more because I do not see things quite like other women. If you had gone to Egypt, and the worst had happened, I should have been lonely, of course, all my days—but I should have known quite surely that when those days were over, you and I would see much of one another.”

She spoke as evenly as if stating the time of tide. The sentence, simple as bread, struck him like a blade. He caught his breath, and sat rigid, keeping his face smooth by force. Her eyes were on him, waiting, confident that his first word would set all right.

“You see,” she said, “I can put up with the absence of the people I care about a little better, perhaps, than most. I do not feel I have lost them at all.” She hunted for her phrase, as if her thought were something delicate that might break if handled roughly. “You know how things happen. One goes along in a dull sort of way, and then suddenly a face springs out from the crowd of one’s acquaintances, and you know it at once—certainly—for the face of a friend. Rather, you recognise it though you have never seen it before. It is almost as though you had come upon some one long looked for and now gladly recovered. Such friends—they are few, no doubt, but only the few really count—such friends one does not lose, whether they are absent, or even—dead.”

“Unless,” he said slowly, “one has made a mistake. Suppose the face in the crowd is a mask—what then? One may make mistakes.”

She shook her head, decisive as a knife coming down on a board.

“Of that kind, no. One may seem to have made mistakes, perhaps for a long while. But in the end one would be proved not to have made them.”

Her faith took hold of him and tortured him. He could not keep his silence.

“Ethne,” he cried, “you don’t know—”

But she reined in, laughed, and pointed with her whip.

At the top of a hill, a couple of miles from Ramelton, the road ran between stone walls. On the left were open fields; on the right a wood of oak and beech held a cool, leaf-filtered darkness. Built into the left-hand wall was a scarlet letter-box, its paint bright as a berry.

“I wanted to show you that,” she interrupted. “It was there I used to post my letters to you during the anxious times.”

And his chance of speech slipped away like a fish under shadow.

“The house is behind the trees to the right,” she added.

“The letter-box is very convenient,” he said.

“Yes,” she answered, and drove on. A little farther the park wall had crumbled, and she stopped again.

“That’s where I used to climb over to post the letters. There’s a tree on the other side as convenient as the letter-box. I used to run down the half-mile of avenue at night.”

“There might have been thieves,” he exclaimed.

“There were thorns,” she said, as if that were the more practical danger.

They turned through the gates and drove up to the porch of a long, irregular grey house that seemed to have grown, like a family, by additions and accidents. Ethne flicked the reins lightly.

“Well, we have still a day before the dance.”

“I suppose the whole countryside is coming,” he said.

“It daren’t do anything else,” she laughed. “My father would send the police to fetch them if they stayed away—just as he fetched your friend Mr. Durrance here. By the way, Mr. Durrance has sent me a present—a Guarnerius violin.”

The door opened and a thin, lank old man came out upon the steps. His face was fierce and peaked, like a bird of prey’s; yet at the sight of Feversham it softened into friendliness. A stranger might have thought him winking, but in truth his left eyelid drooped continually, giving him a look of perpetual irony.

“How do you do?” he said briskly. “Glad to see you. Must make yourself at home. If you want any whiskey, stamp twice on the floor with your foot. The servants understand.” And he went straight back into the house as if the matter were settled.

Dermod Eustace, master of Lennon House, was already becoming legend while still hardly cold in the future grave of which people spoke with an almost proprietary certainty. Facts, however, held firm beneath the barnacles of invention. He practised an ancient, tyrannous hospitality on the road to Letterkenny—forced bed and board on strangers and acquaintances alike, as Durrance had once discovered. He belonged to another century, and glowered at the new one with anger, and could only be reconciled to it after much alcohol. He was like an intoxicated Coriolanus, believing people should be shepherded with a stick, yet never forgetting his manners even to the lowliest woman. Ramelton townsfolk said with pride that even at his worst—galloping down the steep cobbled streets on a big white mare, his inseparable collie trotting beside him, swaying with drink yet by miracle keeping his saddle—he had never ridden down any one except a man. Two further truths were known: he was rather afraid of his daughter, who wisely kept him uncertain whether she disapproved of him or not; and he had conceived a great liking for Harry Feversham.

Harry saw little of him that day. Dermod retired into the room he called his office, and Feversham and Ethne spent the afternoon fishing for salmon in the Lennon. The afternoon lay with a Sabbath stillness; even the birds seemed to hush as if the air itself were listening. From the house the lawns fell steeply, shaded by trees and dappled with sun, into a valley where the river ran swift and black under overarching boughs. There was a fall where water slid over rocks with such unbroken smoothness it looked solid, save where one sharp spur lifted and the river broke back on itself in an amber wave lit through by the sun.

Opposite that spur they sat a long while, sometimes speaking, but most often listening—the roar of water, the softer shiver of leaves, the distant, domestic clink from the house as if some kitchen door had been shut, some utensil laid down, ordinary life keeping time to the river’s loud insistence. The soundscape wrapped them; it soothed, and it made his thoughts, for once, less clamorous. Sunset came; shadows lengthened like slow hands. They stood, looked at each other, smiled, and walked back.

It was an afternoon he would remember with a pain sharpened by its calm, for the next night was the night of the dance.

As the band struck the opening bars of the fourth waltz, Ethne left her post at the drawing-room door and, taking Feversham’s arm, led him out into the hall. The hall was empty. The front door stood open to the cool summer night, and the garden’s trees whispered to one another, their speech threaded with the far-off pulse of crickets. From the ballroom came the swaying lilt of music, the beat of feet on boards, laughter riding on the strings. It was all sound and breath and human bodies in motion—life made audible.

Ethne drew a breath of relief at her reprieve from duty, then dropped his arm and crossed to a side table.

“The post is in,” she said. “There are letters—one, two, three—for you, and a little box.”

She held it out: a small white jeweller’s cardboard box. The instant it touched her hand she frowned.

“It must be empty,” she said.

Yet it was carefully sealed and tied. Feversham broke the seals, unfastened the string. He looked at the address; it had been forwarded from his lodgings, and he did not know the hand.

“There is some mistake,” he said, and lifted the lid.

Three white feathers fluttered out, rocked and swayed for a moment in the lamplight, and then drifted down one after another to the dark polished boards. They lay like flakes of snow. But they were not whiter than Harry Feversham’s cheeks. He stood staring at them until he felt a light touch upon his arm—Ethne’s gloved hand on his sleeve.

“What does it mean?” she asked. There was perplexity in her voice, no more than perplexity; her smile and her loyal confidence seemed to promise that his first word would clear the air. “What does it mean?”

“That there are things which cannot be hid, I suppose,” he answered.

She did not speak at once. The music floated languorously into the hall; the garden’s trees whispered through the open door, their sound clean and living beside the band’s glossy strain. Then she shook his arm gently, gave a breathless little laugh, and spoke as if pleading with a child.

“I don’t think you understand, Harry. Here are three white feathers. They were sent to you in jest—oh, of course in jest. But it is a cruel kind of jest—”

“They were sent in deadly earnest.”

He looked straight into her eyes as he spoke. Ethne’s hand fell from his sleeve.

“Who sent them?” she asked.

He had not thought of that. The message was everything; the senders were nothing. But Ethne reached out, took the box, and turned it over. Three visiting cards lay at the bottom. She drew them out and read aloud:

“Captain Trench, Mr. Castleton, Mr. Willoughby. Do you know these men?”

“All three are officers of my old regiment.”

For a moment she seemed dazed, as if the words had changed the room’s air. She knelt and gathered the feathers into her hand—perhaps thinking that to touch them would make them comprehensible. They lay on the palm of her white glove. She blew gently, and they rose and hung fluttering, rocking like small, indecisive birds. As they drifted down she caught them again, and slowly, as if feeling her way by touch, she asked:

“Were they justly sent?”

“Yes,” said Harry Feversham.

He did not think of denial. The dreaded thing, the thing he had anticipated for years with a sick foreknowledge, had happened. He was known for a coward. That word—long blazing across the wall of his thoughts—was now written in public places. He stood as though again before the portraits of his fathers, mute under sentence. It was the girl who denied it. Still kneeling, she said:

“I do not believe that is true. You could not look me in the face so steadily were it true. Your eyes would seek the floor, not mine.”

“Yet it is true.”

“Three little white feathers,” she said slowly; then with a sob tightening her throat, “This afternoon we were under the elms down by the Lennon River—do you remember, Harry?—just you and I. And then come three little white feathers, and the world’s at an end.”

“Oh, don’t!” he cried, and his voice broke. Until then he had spoken steadily, as steadily as his eyes. But her words pulled up the image of the river and the elms, the roar and hush of water, the whole clean, listening afternoon—its simple, domestic peace—and set it against these small accusatory things. It caught him by the heart.

Ethne, however, seemed not to hear the appeal. She was listening with her face turned toward the ballroom. Chatter and laughter grew louder and nearer. She understood the music had ceased. She rose quickly, clenching the feathers in her hand, opened a door—her sitting-room door.

“Come,” she said.

He followed her. She closed the door, shutting out the noise as one shuts a lid on boiling.

“Now,” she said, “will you tell me, if you please, why the feathers have been sent?”

She stood quietly before him. Her face was pale, but her expression showed little beyond a desire—almost a determination—to reach the truth. Her voice held the same quiet.

He answered directly, without softening.

“A telegram came. It was sent by Castleton. It reached me when Captain Trench and Mr. Willoughby were dining with me. It told me my regiment would be ordered on active service in Egypt. Castleton was dining with a man likely to know; I did not question the message. He told me to tell Trench. I did not. I thought it over with the telegram in front of me. Castleton was leaving that night for Scotland, and would go straight from Scotland to rejoin the regiment. He would not see Trench for weeks, and by then the telegram would likely be forgotten or its date confused. I did not tell Trench. I threw the telegram into the fire, and that night sent in my papers. But Trench found out somehow. Durrance was at dinner too—good God, Durrance!” he broke out suddenly. “Most likely he knows like the rest.”

It struck him, sharp and strange, that Durrance—who had once looked up to him—would likely count him a thing of scorn. But Ethne’s voice cut through that thought. What did Durrance matter, what did anyone matter, now that she knew?

“And is this all?” she asked.

“Surely it is enough,” he said.

“I think not,” she answered. Her voice lowered a little. “We agreed, didn’t we, that no foolish misunderstandings should ever come between us? We were to be frank, and to take frankness each from the other without offence. So be frank with me. Please.” There was pleading now. “I could, I think, claim it as a right. At all events I ask for it as I shall never ask for anything else in all my life.”

There was an explanation, he remembered—such as it was—but it seemed futile beside the consequence. Ethne had unclenched her hand. The three feathers lay upon the table between them, small and immaculate, impossible to reason with. He wore cowardice like a label. Besides, he could never make her understand; there were parts of fear that had no honest vocabulary. Yet she asked, and she had the right.

So he gathered his wits and spoke warily, because there was something he must still hide—for her sake, he told himself, at all costs.

“All my life I have been afraid that some day I should play the coward, and from the very first I knew I was destined for the army. I kept my fear to myself. There was no one to tell it to. My mother was dead, and my father—” He stopped, drew in a deep breath, and saw with painful clarity his father sitting at that moment in his mother’s favourite seat upon the terrace, looking over moonlit fields towards the Sussex Downs, dreaming of honours his son might win in Egypt—honours worthy of the Fevershams. The thought of the blow he had dealt that stern old heart made him bend forward and groan aloud.

“My father,” he resumed, “would, nay, could, never have understood. I know him. When danger came his way, it found him ready; but he did not foresee. That was always my trouble—I foresaw. Any peril to be encountered, any risk to be run, I foresaw them. And I foresaw something else. My father used to talk in his matter-of-fact way of the hours of waiting before the commencement of a battle, after the troops had been paraded. The mere anticipation of the suspense and strain of those hours was torture to me. I foresaw the possibility of cowardice. Then one evening, when my father had his old friends about him on one of his Crimean nights, two dreadful stories were told—one of an officer, the other of a surgeon—who had both shirked. I was confronted with the fact of cowardice. I took those stories to bed with me. They never left my memory; they became a part of me. I saw myself behaving now as one, now as the other, perhaps in the crisis of a battle bringing ruin on my country, certainly dishonouring my father and all the dead men whose portraits hung ranged in the hall. I tried to get the best of my fears. I hunted—but with a map of the country-side in my mind. I foresaw every hedge, every pit, every treacherous bank.”

“Yet you rode straight,” Ethne interrupted quickly. “Mr. Durrance told me so.”

“Did I?” he said vaguely. “Perhaps I did, once the hounds were off. Durrance never knew what the moments of waiting before the coverts were drawn meant to me. So when this telegram came, I took the chance it seemed to offer and resigned.”

He ended. He had spoken, but not wholly; his carefulness left a shape unfilled, a silence like an unspoken dialect between them. At once she suspected it.

“Were you afraid, too, of disgracing me?” she asked. “Was I in any way the cause that you resigned?”

He looked her in the eyes and lied.

“No.”

“If you had not been engaged to me, you would still have sent in your papers?”

“Yes.”

Slowly Ethne stripped a glove from her hand. Feversham turned away, as though the small sound of leather over skin accused him.

“I think I am rather like your father,” she said. “I don’t understand.”

In the silence that followed he heard something whirr and rattle softly on the table. He looked and saw she had slipped her engagement ring from her finger. It lay there, the stones winking as if trying to speak a language he could not bear.

“And all this—all that you have told me,” she exclaimed suddenly, her face stern now, “you would have hidden from me? You would have married me and hidden it, had not these three feathers come?”

She had held the words back from the beginning, giving him every chance, still hoping—by some miracle—there might be an explanation that would restore him in her mind. Now she struck and laid bare the worst of his disloyalty. He flinched and did not answer; his silence consented.

Ethne, however, was just; she was curious too, wanting the very bottom of the matter before she thrust it away into the back of memory.

“But yesterday,” she said, “you were going to tell me something. I stopped you to point out the letter-box.” She laughed, queerly empty. “Was it about the feathers?”

“Yes,” he answered wearily. What did these questions matter, since her ring lay there and the feathers had come? “Yes. I think what you were saying rather compelled me.”

“I remember,” she said, cutting him off as if afraid of where the memory might lead, “about seeing much of one another—afterwards. We will not speak of such things again.”

He swayed slightly, as though the room had shifted under his feet.

“I remember, too, you said one could make mistakes. You were right; I was wrong. One can do more than seem to make them.” She drew a breath, held her voice steady. “Will you, if you please, take back your ring?”

He picked it up and held it in his palm, very still. He had never cared for her so much; never seen her value so thoroughly as now, at the moment of losing her. In the quiet room she seemed to gleam—bright flowers in her hair, the fall of her dress, the white slipper on her foot—as if she belonged to some older, truer order of things, one not easily translated into his own broken speech. It seemed incredible he had ever won her; more incredible that he had lost her by disloyalty.

Then her voice came again.

“These, too, are yours. Will you take them, please?”

She pointed with her fan to the feathers on the table. He reached out obediently—and drew his hand back, startled.

“There are four,” he said.

Ethne did not answer. He looked at the fan and understood. It was ivory and white feathers. She had broken off one of its feathers and added it—quietly, deliberately—on her own account to the three.

Habit carried them as a river carries driftwood. In Lennon House the last hours of the ball ran on of themselves—laughter flung out on cue, compliments laid like cards upon the table, the soft conspiracy of faces trained to look at ease. No one, watching, would have guessed that something had snapped between Ethne Eustace and Harry Feversham as cleanly as a thread scorched through.

Feversham watched her from under the chandelier light and could not reconcile what he knew with what he saw. Ethne’s voice rose and fell among the talk as though it had never trembled; her smile went to the right people at the right moments; when she turned, colour seemed to obey her will and gather dutifully at her cheeks. When her feet took the floor, the rhythm of them—light, precise, almost careless—nearly persuaded him that her heart had kept time.

And then she passed him, and the persuasion broke. He felt the four feathers hidden against his breast as if they were hot coals stitched into his coat. It amazed him that the whole company could not smell their shame on him, as pungent and undeniable as singed paper. He moved among partners with the absurd, constant notion that each woman would draw back and speak the word he had earned; that the contempt which lay on Ethne’s tongue would leap free and echo through the room. Yet there was no fear in him. The place where fear had lived was empty. If the word came, let it come. He had already lost what mattered.

He looked, too, at the other women as if he might find in them some ease—some comparison that could blunt the edge of his loss—but he looked in vain, as he must have known he would. There were faces of beauty, yes, and grace, and bright cleverness; but Ethne stood apart by a character of beauty which did not depend on any single charm. The breadth of her forehead, the clean curve of her brows, the great steady grey eyes that seemed to hold a clear weather of their own; the red lips which could dimple into sudden tenderness and then set level with resolution; the very carriage of her head and shoulders, royal without effort—these made a distinction that would not be confused in any room, in any company. He watched her with a despairing wonder that he had ever been permitted to imagine she was his.

They even danced together. The orchestra spilled waltz after waltz, the floor turned under them, and their hands met with the correctness of long practice. Ethne did not let the strain show. If her body ached with the effort of it, she carried that weight where no one could see—like a woman carrying a heavy dish from kitchen to table without letting it tilt, because spillage would shame her more than pain.

Only once, and for no more than a second, did her endurance slip.

They were in the turn of a dance when her eyes went past his shoulder toward the windows. Behind the blinds a pallor had begun to form: the first, cold, colourless hint of day. Ethne’s mouth parted.

“Look,” she said.

The word was small, but Feversham felt it like a sudden leaning. All at once her weight came into his arms. The colour deserted her face. Grey rose up under her skin as if morning itself had entered her. Her eyes shut tight, then opened again; she swayed, and he thought she would fall there on the polished boards before all the world.

“The morning at last!” she exclaimed, and the brightness of the words could not conceal the weariness underneath. Then, in a voice that seemed rubbed raw by the night, she said, “I wonder whether it is right that one should suffer so much pain.”

“Hush,” he whispered, leaning nearer so that his breath would not carry. “Courage. A few minutes more—only a very few.”

He stopped with her where they stood, turning his body as a shield, holding her steady until the strength came back into her limbs and the grey retreated a little.

“Thank you,” she said, and there was gratitude in it—simple, earnest, almost childlike with exhaustion.

Then the bright wheel of the dance caught them again and spun them back among the others.

It was strange—almost grotesque—that he should be the one urging courage, she the one thanking him for help; but neither of them had room in that moment for irony. Ethne was simply tried past endurance; Feversham, seeing the drawn lines at her mouth, the deep pain in her eyes, understood with a shock how far his act had cut into her. He stopped thinking, even, I have lost her. The thought of his own loss shrank until it was nothing. Her sentence—quiet, wondering, condemned by its very simplicity—took possession of him. I wonder whether it is right that one should suffer so much pain. He felt it would go with him wherever he went, ringing on in Ethne’s own accent, and that, at the end, when other voices clustered around him, he would hear that one sentence above them all and recognise it as his judgment. For it was not right. Not right at all.

The ball died shortly afterward, as fires do—first the flare, then the dwindling, then the last red thread in the ash. The final carriage rolled away. Those who were staying in the house broke by custom into their little streams: the men drifting toward the smoking-room, the women to their rooms, the heavy, satisfied silence of a household at last allowed to sleep.

Feversham lingered in the hall with Ethne.

She knew why he stayed. She stood with her back to him, steadying her hand to light a candle as if it were the most ordinary thing in the world, as if the flame did not tremble at the same places her voice had trembled.

“There is no need,” she said. “I have told my father. I told him everything.”

He bowed his head. Acquiescence was all he had left that resembled dignity.

“Still, I must wait and see him,” he answered.

She did not object. But she turned quickly then, her brows drawn with a puzzled frown. To wait—for him to put himself in her father’s way, now—argued a courage she could not quite comprehend. And she, who had not feared the crowd of guests, felt an apprehension suddenly as the door of the study opened and Dermod Eustace’s footsteps sounded on the floor.

Dermod came straight up to Harry Feversham. The candlelight showed him for once exactly as he was: very old. Not merely aged, but burdened with years; the skin loose at the jaw, the eyes slightly bewildered as though the world had changed language in the night and he could not find the right words in it.

He stood and stared into Feversham’s face.

Twice his mouth opened. Twice nothing came out. At last he turned away, went to the table, and lit his own candle and Feversham’s. The small domestic gesture—so careful, so accustomed—was like a habit of kindness performed without thinking.

Then Dermod turned back rather quickly, and Ethne took a step forward as if to place herself between them. But he did nothing violent; he only stared again. A long time passed. The hall, emptied of music and voices, had its own sound: the faint settling creaks of wood, the distant breath of the house, the thin wick-hiss of the candles.

“Well—” he said at last, and stopped.

He lifted the snuffers and trimmed the wick with deliberation, as though if the flame were neatened the right expression might appear. He looked at Feversham again.

“Well—” he began, and stopped once more.

The candle seemed not to help him. He stared into the flame now rather than into the young man’s face, and the bewilderment on him deepened. Something must be said; the old, established forms demanded it; but the words would not come. At last he offered, lamely, the first thing his habits could provide.

“If you want any whiskey,” he said, “stamp twice on the floor with your foot. The servants understand.”

And with that he walked heavily up the stairs.

Feversham stood very still as the footsteps went away. Of all punishments, the old man’s forbearance—his inability even to summon anger into a proper sentence—cut with a quiet edge. There was no drama to hide behind. Nothing to answer, nothing to resist. Only that exhausted kindness of custom: a candle lit, an offer of whiskey, and the slow retreat of age.

* * *
