It was broad daylight when Ethne was at last alone in her room.

She drew up the blinds and opened the windows wide. The morning air poured in—cool, clean, and sharp as spring-water lifted from a deep well—and she drank it as if it could rinse her from the night. Outside, the world had not yet found its colours. The tall trees stood black and vertical, like ink strokes against the paling sky; the winding paths were a dead, singular white; the lawns lay grey and flattened, the dew on them webbed like frost. Everything looked washed of joy, as though daylight itself had been strained through muslin.

Yet the world was not silent. It was noisy with living sound. Blackbirds called from branches and from the grass with that bright insistence that belongs to mornings; and beneath the overhanging trees the Lennon ran on in a continuous music, a steadier song than any orchestra could sustain. It was as if the house had been filled all night with hired sound—measured, repetitive, too loud in places, too smooth in others—and now the land itself answered with something older, freer, not for sale, not for applause. The birds and river did not perform; they simply existed, and their existing made the mind feel less broken.

Ethne drew back from the window. She had much to do before she could sleep.

With her natural thoroughness she meant to make an end at once of all her associations with Harry Feversham. She wanted, when next she woke, to wake into a room cleared of him, a day in which nothing—no ribbon, no trinket, no scrap of paper—could pull her back into memory against her will. She set about it with a stubborn persistence, like someone scrubbing a stain from linen until her hands ache because she cannot bear to see the mark remain.

But as she collected the gifts he had given her, her resolve shifted.

Each thing had its history. The touch of her fingers upon them called up, one by one, the days before this miserable night fell across her happiness. A small ornament did not remain merely an ornament; it became a remembered afternoon, a particular phrase, the smell of a garden after rain, the texture of his glove as he handed it to her. Memory would not obey the brutal command to vanish. It rose in her like a language spoken in childhood—words you do not use now, perhaps, yet cannot truly forget because they are braided through your sense of self.

She decided she would keep one thing that had belonged to Harry Feversham—a small thing, of no value.

At first her hand went to a penknife he had once lent her and which she had forgotten to return. She held it an instant, then dropped it almost at once, and with a hurried movement, as if the metal had turned suddenly cold. She was an Irish girl; she did not perhaps believe in superstitions, not in the daylight of her mind. But where superstitions were concerned, she preferred—always—to be on the safe side.

In the end she chose his photograph. She did not look at it long. She locked it away in a drawer as one locks away a dangerous remedy: not thrown out, not displayed, but kept where it could not work upon her unless she chose.

All the rest she gathered together and packed carefully in a box. She fastened it, addressed it, and carried it down to the hall so that the servants might despatch it in the morning. The household was still, its corridors smelling faintly of extinguished candles and flowers past their best; somewhere a door sighed on its hinges; the quiet had the weight of a house that has entertained too hard and too late. She set the parcel aside with hands that did not tremble, because she would not permit them to.

Then she went back to her room and took his letters. She made a neat pile of them on the hearth and set them alight.

They did not burn quickly. The flame crept, nibbling the edges, lifting the paper into curled black tongues. The ink—the words that had once seemed so sure—darkened and ran, like a stream of spilled ink pushed by heat, until the writing was no longer readable. She sat upright in her chair and watched without moving, forcing herself to witness the end. In the last stage the sheets broke down into flakes of ash—light, drifting shapes—some grey, some startlingly pale. They looked like feathers, and among them were white flakes like white feathers, fragile and weightless, as though courage and shame could both be reduced to the same small debris.

The last sparks were barely out when she heard a cautious step on the gravel beneath her window.

Though it was broad daylight her candle still burned on the table, a yellow point in the morning. Instinct moved her faster than thought. She reached out and pinched the flame into darkness.

Then she sat rigid and listened.

For a while there were only the blackbirds, calling and answering from the trees, and the steady, throbbing music of the river. Those sounds held the garden in a living net; they made the air seem inhabited, not empty. Afterward she heard the footsteps again, retreating cautiously, as if whoever had come had thought better of being seen.

And then—despite her will, despite the box in the hall and the locked drawer and the ash on the hearth—she was mastered not by pain or humiliation but by a sudden, overpowering loneliness. It came like a physical cold. It made her feel as though she were seated high above a world that had collapsed into ruins, and she alone remained to look down upon it.

She rose quickly, as if movement could ward the feeling off. Her eyes fell upon a violin-case. Relief—pure, simple—went through her like warmth.

She opened it.

A little while later, one or two of the guests sleeping in the house woke and, half-dreaming, heard music floating down the corridors: a violin played very lovingly and low. The sound was not the public brilliance of the ballroom, not the polished noise meant to fill a room. It was close, intimate, meant for one listener—perhaps for none but the player herself. It fitted itself into the house’s morning hush without wounding it, and it spoke to the spaces where words failed.

Ethne did not know that the violin she held was the Guarnerius Durrance had sent to her. She only knew that, for the moment, she was not alone. The instrument rested against her throat like a living thing; its voice answered the birds and the river; and in that answering, it became a companion willing to share her solitude.

It was the night of August the thirtieth.

In Donegal, a month had slid by since the Lennon House ball, and still the countryside chewed upon one flavour as stubbornly as a bit of gristle: Harry Feversham’s disappearance. The climbing street in the town repeated it from doorstep to doorstep; the gentry, behind polished glass and carved sideboards, passed it with the salted butter and the roast, each serving of gossip accompanied by a fresh certainty. Somebody had seen him at dawn, they swore—still in dress clothes, face bleached like curd, moving along the causeway by Lennon Bridge. Mrs. Brien O’Brien corrected the time with the zeal of a woman pinching out scones by the clock: not five minutes to six, but ten past. A drag-net was suggested with the practical relish of men who like an answer that uses rope and muscle. Mr. Dennis Rafferty, on the Rathmullen road, refused salmon on the grounds that he was not a cannibal, and the joke, as jokes do, began to travel farther than the truth.

No one, in their dinner-talk, touched the real cause, because there were only two who knew it; and those two kept the old routine of living—chores, visits, pride held a shade higher—as if catastrophe were merely a weather change. Ethne, perhaps, had softened in small ways, the way a hand grows gentler when it learns what bruises; Dermod, perhaps, had sharpened, quick to anger like a blade honed too often. But that was all the evidence the world got. So the world filled the silence with its own noise.

Harry Feversham, meanwhile, was not in Donegal at all. He was in London, and Lieutenant Sutch learned it in a crowd.

All day the city had been shaken by rumours from Egypt—Kassassin, east of Ismailia—tidings arriving like gusts that turn a street’s head first one way and then another. Men ran messages as if chased; boys shouted victories and disasters with the same hoarse throat. There had been a moonlit charge, Drury-Lowe’s cavalry rolling up Arabi’s flank and taking guns; there were whispers of an English general dead; of the York and Lancaster cut to pieces. By eleven at night Pall Mall was packed beneath the gas-lamps, faces upturned toward the War Office blinds as if light itself might speak. The crowd was unnaturally still; only when a shadow crossed those lit panels did a shiver go through them and the mass sway, edge to edge, like wheat under a passing wind.

Lieutenant Sutch, careful with his wounded leg, stood on the outskirts with his back to the parapet of the Junior Carlton Club. A touch came on his arm—light, urgent, the sort of touch that cannot wait to be asked its name. He turned and saw Harry Feversham beside him.

Feversham’s face worked as if some private fever kneaded it; it was extraordinarily white, and his eyes shone too brightly, like eyes that had stared at a ceiling through long hours. Sutch, for an instant, was not sure whether he knew him—or whether, in that moment’s London exhaustion, he cared who any man was.

“I might have been out there in Egypt to-night,” Feversham said quickly, in a voice that stumbled over itself. “Think of it! I might have been out there, sitting by a camp-fire in the desert, talking over the battle with Jack Durrance; or dead perhaps. What would it have mattered? I might have been in Egypt to-night!”

Sutch read in his unexpected presence—no less than in that wandering tongue—that something had gone wrong beyond ordinary repair. A dozen questions rose in him. He asked none. Instead he took Feversham’s arm and drew him away from the gathered faces.

“I saw you in the crowd,” Feversham went on, as if he had been holding the sentence in his teeth until he found a listener. “I thought I would speak to you because—do you remember? a long time ago you gave me your card. I have always kept it. I have always feared I should have reason to use it. You said that if one was in trouble, the telling might help.”

Sutch stopped him and pointed him toward a doorway. “We will go in here. We can find a quiet corner in the upper smoking-room.”

Harry looked up. The steps belonged to the Army and Navy Club.

“Good God, not there!” he cried in a sharp low voice; and he stepped quickly into the roadway, where the gaslight fell less directly on his face.

Sutch limped after him. “Then where?”

“Not to-night. It is late. To-morrow, if you will—in some quiet place, after nightfall. I do not go out in daylight.”

Again Sutch did not ask why. He simply accepted the rule, as a cook accepts a guest’s aversion and alters the dish without comment.

“I know a quiet restaurant,” he said. “If we dine there at nine, we shall meet no one we know. Meet me just before nine to-morrow night at the corner of Swallow Street.”

So on the following evening they ate together at a small table in a corner of the Criterion grill-room. Feversham came in with a quick, restless glance, measuring the room as though each pair of shoulders might belong to a judge.

“I dine here often when I am in town,” Sutch said, as if to season the place with familiarity. “Listen.”

Under the talk, under the clink of glasses and the soft scuff of waiters’ shoes, there was the deep throbbing of engines feeding the electric light—an industrial pulse, steady, insistent. One could almost feel it through the soles of one’s feet.

“It reminds me of a ship,” Sutch said with a brief smile. “I can almost fancy myself in the gun-room again. We will have dinner. Then you shall tell me your story.”

“You have heard nothing of it?” Feversham asked, suspicion thick in the question.

“Not a word.”

Feversham’s shoulders loosened by a fraction, as if he had been holding them up by force. He had been living as though every passer-by knew his shame, as if contempt were served in the air with the soot and fog. To find one man ignorant of it felt, absurdly, like finding a quiet field after a week of street noise.

The meal came and went—courses placed and cleared with practiced hands. Aromas rose—hot fat and seared meat, the sharpness of mustard, the browned edge of bread—yet Feversham ate as though taste were a rumour, his nerves too tight to let food have its rightful dominion. Sutch watched him in the full light now: thin, haggard, the hollows under his eyes dark as bruises. He moved with a feverish restlessness, drawing breath as if the air were not quite enough.

Once or twice between courses Feversham began, but Sutch held him off with a gesture. “Not yet. Not until the cloth is cleared.”

At last the table was stripped to coffee and cigars. Sutch held out his case.

“Now,” he said. “Take your time, Harry.”

Then Feversham spoke—slowly, carefully, as if he were forcing his words through a narrow opening so that none could be embellished on the way. He told everything without exaggeration, without omission. He began with the Crimean night at Broad Place and ended with the ball at Lennon House. He made his own disgrace plain, not softened by excuses, and in the very steadiness of his recital there was something almost unreal—like listening to a man describe another man’s ruin simply to fill an hour.

“I came back across Lough Swilly early that morning,” he said when he reached the end, “and travelled at once to London. Since then I have stayed in my rooms all day, listening to the bugles calling in the barrack-yard beneath my windows. At night I prowl about the streets or lie in bed waiting for the Westminster clock to sound each new quarter of an hour. On foggy nights I can hear steam-sirens on the river.” He gave a laugh that had no warmth in it. “Do you know when the ducks start quacking in St. James’s Park? At two o’clock to the minute.”

Sutch listened without interrupting. Yet halfway through—at the moment Feversham spoke of concealing the telegram—Sutch’s posture changed. Until then he had sat forward, arms on the table, eyes fixed on Harry. After that he raised a hand to his forehead and kept it there, screening his face while the story continued.

Feversham had no doubt why. He thought Sutch was hiding scorn—hiding the involuntary curl of a lip. But he did not falter. He kept to his truth like a man walking through fire because he has decided to reach the other side.

When he finished, Sutch did not at once move his hand. Silence settled between them, broken only by the far-off clatter of a plate and the unceasing subterranean vibration of the engines. At last Sutch spoke, and the words struck Feversham with surprise.

“I am much to blame,” Sutch said. “I should have spoken that night at Broad Place, and I held my tongue. I shall hardly forgive myself.”

There was no contempt in his voice. It shook, but with contrition.

“You see, I understood,” he went on. “Your father, I am afraid, never would.”

“He never will,” Harry said, bitterly quiet.

“No,” Sutch agreed. “Your mother—had she lived—would have seen clearly. Few women would, I think, except your mother. Brute courage! Women make a god of it. That girl, for instance—”

“You must not blame her,” Harry broke in. “I was defrauding her into marriage.”

Sutch’s hand dropped from his forehead; he looked at Harry directly. “Suppose you had never met her. Would you still have sent in your papers?”

Harry considered, forcing himself to fairness. “I think not. Disgracing my name—and those dead men in the hall—I think I would have risked. I could not risk disgracing her.”

Sutch thumped his fist softly on the table, despairing not of Harry but of all the missed chances that had shaped him. “If only I had spoken at Broad Place. Harry, why didn’t you let me speak? I might have saved you many unnecessary years of torture. Good heavens! what a childhood you must have spent with that fear all alone with you. It makes me shiver to think of it. I might even have saved you from this last catastrophe. For I understood. I understood.”

He spoke as a man who has walked those inner corridors and knows where the mind hides its injuries. Sutch saw—more clearly than Harry could—the long harm done by growing up with an uncomprehending father and the relentless company of dead soldiers staring from the walls; by having no one to whom a boy might say, in his own faltering private language, what he was afraid of becoming. The fear had sapped at Harry incessantly; it had sat beside him at meals, gone to bed with him, haunted his dreams. It had made intimacy with friends dangerous. Sutch did not wonder that at last it had driven him into an irretrievable mistake.

“Did you ever read ‘Hamlet’?” Sutch asked abruptly.

“Of course.”

“But did you consider it? The same disability is clear in that character. The thing he foresaw, thought over, imagined in act and consequence—that he shrank from, upbraiding himself even as you have done. Yet when the moment of action comes—sharp and immediate—does he fail? No. He excels; and just by reason of that foresight. I have seen men in the Crimea tortured by imagination before the fight—once the fight had begun you must search among Oriental fanatics for their match. ‘Am I a coward?’ Do you remember the lines? ‘Am I a coward? Who calls me villain? Breaks my pate across? Plucks off my beard, and blows it in my face?’ There’s the case in a nutshell. If only I had spoken on that night!”

A few people passed their table on the way out. Sutch glanced around; the grill-room was thinning. He looked at his watch—eleven. It was late enough that decisions could not be postponed by politeness.

“You cannot stay in London,” he said, his voice tightening, “hiding by day, slinking about by night. That’s too like—” He stopped.

“That’s too like Wilmington,” Feversham finished quietly, summoning the old story his father had told him long ago—one he had never let himself forget. “But Wilmington’s end will not be mine. Of that I can assure you. I shall not stay in London.”

He spoke with a decision that had been forged in solitude. He had planned already what Sutch feared to ask.

“Who knows of the feathers?” Sutch said. “How many people? Give me their names.”

“Trench, Castleton, Willoughby,” Feversham began.

“All three are in Egypt. Besides, for the credit of their regiment they are likely to hold their tongues when they return. Who else?”

“Dermod Eustace and—Ethne.”

“They will not speak.”

“You,” Harry said, “Durrance perhaps, and my father.”

Sutch leaned back, startled. “Your father! You wrote to him?”

“No. I went into Surrey and told him.”

Remorse seized Sutch again, sharp as the taste of burnt coffee. “Why didn’t I speak that night?” he said helplessly. “A coward—and you go quietly down to Surrey and confront your father with that story to tell him! You do not even write! You stand up and tell it to him face to face! Harry, I reckon myself as good as another when it comes to bravery, but for the life of me I could not have done that.”

“It was not—pleasant,” Harry said simply. That was all he ever gave to any man, but in that one word Sutch could hear the room in Broad Place, the hard silence, the old man’s judgement falling like a blade.

Harry went on. “My father continues my allowance. I shall need it—every penny of it—otherwise I should have taken nothing. But I am not to go home again. I did not mean to go home for a long while in any case, if at all.”

He drew his pocket-book from his breast and took out the four white feathers. In the grimy light of the restaurant they looked both delicate and indecent, like something stolen from a bird and turned into an accusation. He laid them on the table before him.

“You have kept them?” Sutch exclaimed.

“Indeed, I treasure them,” Harry said quietly. “That seems strange to you. To you they are the symbols of my disgrace. To me they are much more. They are my opportunities of retrieving it.”

He separated three of the feathers and pushed them forward a little on the stained tablecloth. Then he leaned toward Sutch, as if lowering his voice could make the plan less mad.

“What if I could compel Trench, Castleton, and Willoughby to take back from me, each in his turn, the feather he sent? I do not say it is likely. I do not say even it is possible. But there is a chance—and I must wait upon that chance. There will be few men leading active lives as these three do who will not at some moment stand in great peril and great need. To be in readiness for that moment is from now my career. All three are in Egypt. I leave for Egypt to-morrow.”

A look of unexpected happiness came over Sutch’s face—quick and bright, like a lamp lit in a long-dark passage. This issue had not occurred to him, and once it appeared it seemed the only issue. His knowledge of prudence—the obstacles, the improbability, the old risk of shrinking at the last—fell away from him as if it belonged to someone else. His eyes shone with pride; in his heart the feathers were already returned.

Harry himself was more cautious. “There are endless difficulties. Just to cite one: I am a civilian; these three are soldiers, surrounded by soldiers—so much the less opportunity therefore for a civilian.”

“But it is not necessary that the three men should be themselves in peril,” Sutch objected. “For you to convince them that the fault is retrieved.”

“Oh, no. There may be other ways,” Harry agreed. “The plan came suddenly into my mind—indeed at the moment when Ethne bade me take up the feathers and added the fourth. I was on the point of tearing them across when this way out of it sprang clearly up. But I have thought it over since, during these last weeks while I sat listening to the bugles in the barrack-yard. And I am sure there is no other way. It is well worth trying.”

He drew a deep breath. His voice dropped. He kept his eyes on the table as though the dirty cloth could absorb what he was about to say.

“You see—if the three take back their feathers,” he murmured, “why, then she perhaps might take hers back too.”

“Will she wait, do you think?” Sutch asked.

Harry’s head snapped up. “Oh, no,” he cried. “I had no thought of that. She has not even a suspicion of what I intend to do. Nor do I wish her to have one until the intention is fulfilled.”

His next words came slowly, with a new hesitation, as if he were moving into ground that could not be crossed without reverence.

“My thought was different. I find it difficult to tell you. Ethne said something to me the day before the feathers came—something rather sacred. I think I will tell you, because what she said is just what sends me out upon this errand. But for her words, I would very likely never have thought of it. I find in them my motive and a great hope. They may seem strange to you, Mr. Sutch; but I ask you to believe they are very real to me.”

He paused, then spoke as though quoting a line he had repeated in the dark until it became part of his breathing.

“She said—it was when she knew no more than that my regiment was ordered to Egypt—she was blaming herself because I had resigned my commission, for which there was no need, because—these were her words—because had I fallen, although she would have felt lonely all her life, she would none the less have surely known that she and I would see much of one another—afterwards.”

He still did not look at Sutch.

“Do you understand?” he asked softly. “I have a hope that if—this fault can be repaired—” and he pointed to the feathers, “—we might still, perhaps, see something of one another—afterwards.”

It was a strange thing to say across a public table, amid the stale smell of smoke and coffee, with engines throbbing underfoot like an uneasy heart. Yet neither man felt it fanciful. They were not dealing in polite conversation now, but in the simple severe matters by which a life is measured. The incongruity of the place could not touch them.

Sutch said nothing for a long moment. When Harry finally looked up—prepared for ridicule—he saw Sutch’s right hand held out to him.

“When I come back,” Harry said, rising.

He gathered the feathers and returned them to his pocket-book with care, as if they were both wounds and instruments. “I have told you everything. You see, I wait upon chance opportunities. The three may not come in Egypt. They may never come at all, and in that case I shall not come back at all. Or they may come only at the very end and after many years. Therefore I thought I would like just one person to know the truth thoroughly in case I do not come back. If you hear definitely that I never can come back, I would be glad if you would tell my father.”

“I understand,” Sutch said.

“But don’t tell him everything,” Harry insisted. “Not the last part—not what I have just said about Ethne and my chief motive, for I do not think he would understand. Otherwise you will keep silence altogether. Promise.”

Sutch promised, but his face was absent—already wrestling with the sorrow of that limit. Even if Harry died without fulfilling his mission, Sutch was to hide from General Feversham what was best in the son, because the son was right: the father could not understand. That, to Sutch, was the most bitter taste of all.

Harry pressed him again. “You will breathe no word of this to man or woman, however hard you may be pressed, except to my father under the circumstances which I have explained.”

Sutch promised a second time, immediately, without hesitation. There was no reason to refuse; disclosure would look like a boast, and Sutch, whatever his odd kindnesses, had no love of cheap display. Yet as the promise bound him, his mind went back to the beginning of this misery—the mother dead too early to interpret the boy in the language of love; the father left to rule with a vocabulary of iron; and his own silence on the Crimean night at Broad Place.

“If only I had spoken,” he said sadly.

He dropped the end of his cigar into his coffee-cup. The little hiss was lost in the room’s greater hum. He stood and reached for his hat.

“Many things are irrevocable, Harry,” he said. “But one never knows whether they are irrevocable or not until one has found out. It is always worth while finding out.”

The next evening Feversham crossed to Calais.

The night was as wild as the one on which Durrance had left England—rain driven hard, wind worrying at the harbour like an animal that will not be turned away. Like Durrance, Feversham did not go alone into that darkness; a friend came to see him off. The last thing his eyes held, as the packet swung out from the pier, was Lieutenant Sutch’s face under a gas-lamp—pale in the hard light, steady against the weather.

Sutch stayed where he was after the boat had slipped into the blackness, until even the throb of the paddles could no longer be heard and the sea took back its own sound. Then he limped through the rain to his hotel, aware—regretfully aware—that he was growing old.

It was long since he had regretted it. Ever since the Crimea he had been, as he once told General Feversham, on the world’s half-pay list; and between that and the memory of a certain magical season before the Crimea, he had looked on old age as an approaching friend. But to-night he wanted time—time enough to hear, one day, a footstep returning; time enough to look into Muriel Graham’s son’s face and see honour redeemed and the great fault atoned.

“No one,” Durrance said, and with the same careful economy he showed in all small duties, he strapped the field-glasses into their leather case at his side.

“No one, sir,” Captain Mather agreed. “We will move forward.”

The scouts slipped out ahead, noiseless as moths. Behind them the line re-formed, the two seven-pounder mountain-guns closing up with their patient, metallic clink; and Durrance’s detachment of the Camel Corps came down from the sombre ridge of Khor Gwob—thirty-five miles southwest of Suakin—into the plateau of Sinkat. It was the last reconnaissance in strength before the evacuation of the eastern Soudan, and there was a finality in the day’s stillness, as though the land itself listened for the sound of their leaving.

All morning the camels had laboured up a shale gully between red, precipitous rocks. The stones grated under the broad pads with a dry whisper, and now and again a buckle struck a rifle-sling; that thin, hard clack travelled farther than it had any right to in the naked air. When at last the rocks fell back, the world opened into red mountain-heaps crumbled into a desolation of stones. There was hardly a patch of grass, hardly the ragged arm of a mimosa to catch the eye; only heat, rock, and the faint bitter smell of dust warmed in the sun.

Then, after that arid grinding, the green bushes of Sinkat, lying in the valley below, comforted the sight with the aspect of a park made by human hands. The troopers sat up. Even the camels seemed to take a different step where the sand ran smoother and the shrubs cast a shade that promised coolness, if only by comparison.

They moved in a diagonal line across the plateau toward the mountains of Erkoweet, a silent company on a plain more silent still. It was eleven o’clock. The sun rose toward the centre of a colourless, cloudless sky; the shadows of the camels shortened upon the sand; and the sand itself glistened white as a beach, dazzling enough to make the eyes ache beneath the brim of a helmet. There was no draught of air to stir the rich foliage. The shadows of branches lay so motionless and distinct that they seemed not shadows at all, but branches strewn there by some old storm and forgotten.

Sound became a thing one noticed with the same attention one gave to water. The sharp clank of weapons; the soft, ceaseless padding of camel feet; at times the sudden whirr of pigeons, disturbed from their perches. Even the men’s breathing, when they halted, felt loud.

Yet life moved on the plateau, though it moved with a thief’s caution. Far ahead, where the sand curved trim and smooth between shrubs like carriage drives, a herd of gazelle would start up from the ground and race—silent flashes of dappled brown and white—toward the enclosing hills. It was as if the country had been made in the last hour, set down fresh and unhandled.

“Yet this way the caravans passed southwards to Erkoweet and the Khor Baraka,” Durrance said, answering the thought in his mind. “Here the Suakis built their summer-houses.”

“And there Tewfik fought, and died with his four hundred men,” Mather returned, pointing forward.

For three hours they marched. It was May, and the sun laid its hand upon them with intolerable weight. Alertness drained away. Men rocked drowsily in their saddles, mouths dry, praying not for glory or orders but for evening and the silver shine of stars. For three hours the camels went mincing on with their queer, smirking motions of the head, making of endurance something almost comic—until, quite suddenly, a hundred yards ahead, Durrance saw a broken wall with window-spaces that let the sky through.

“The fort,” he said.

Three years had passed since Osman Digna had taken and wrecked it, but those years had brought another siege as relentless as any army’s. Quick-growing trees had girt it so close to rear and right and left that the traveller came upon it unexpectedly, as Childe Roland upon the Dark Tower in a plain. In front the sand still stretched open to the wells, where three great Gemeiza trees stood spaced like sentinels, their foliage dark and spreading, their shade precious as a ration.

In the shadow to the right front of the fort—where bushes fringed the open sand with the level regularity of a river bank—the soldiers unsaddled and prepared their food. There were the homely, practised sounds of a camp that must feed itself: tin clattered against tin; a knife rasped; a canteen was uncorked; water gurgled, dearer than coin. Someone crumbled hard biscuit into a mess-tin and let a little water soften it, stirring until it would submit to teeth; another man tore at dried meat and held it briefly in the steam of hot tea to take the edge of its hardness. The air filled with the smell of black tea brewed too long—strong, tannic, and comforting—and the faint animal warmth of camels as they settled and sighed.

Durrance and Captain Mather walked round the fort. At the southern corner Durrance stopped short.

“Hallo!”

“Some Arab has camped here,” Mather said, stopping too.

Grey ashes of a wood fire lay in a little heap upon a blackened stone.

“And lately,” Durrance added.

Mather mounted a few rough steps to the crumbled archway and passed into roofless corridors and rooms. Durrance stayed, turned the ashes over with his boot. A stump of twig, charred and whitened, glowed red as an eye opening.

He ground it beneath his heel. A tiny thread of smoke spurted into the air.

“Very lately,” he said to himself, and followed Mather within.

In the corners of the mud walls, in any fissure, even from the packed floor, young trees were sprouting—green persistence where men had meant only clay and gunfire. Rearward a steep glacis and a deep fosse defended the works. Durrance climbed to the parapet above the glacis and sat there while pigeons wheeled and circled overhead, their wings making soft, repetitive beats—one of the few living sounds in that sun-pressed hush. From this very spot, he thought, Tewfik must have strained his eyes day after day toward the pass from Suakin, looking—like another general far to the south—for the sunlight flashing on the weapons of help that did not come.

Mather sat beside him, but his thoughts travelled a different road.

“Already the Guards are steaming out through the coral reefs toward Suez,” he said. “A week and our turn comes. What a God-forsaken country!”

“I come back to it,” Durrance replied.

“Why?”

“I like it. I like the people.”

Mather did not pretend to understand. Yet he knew—because the army itself had shown him—that whatever that taste was, it had carried Durrance forward. Sympathy had stood him in stead of much ability. It had given him patience, and the power to understand: to read, in the tribes of the eastern Soudan, the old hatred of Turkish rule; to distinguish fanaticism from the pretence of it under Osman Digna’s compulsion; to hear, in the very manner of a man’s speech, whether fear or pride held the reins.

“Yes, I shall come back,” Durrance went on. “In three months’ time. For one thing, we know—every Englishman in Egypt knows—that this can’t be the end. I want to be here when the work’s taken in hand again. I hate unfinished things.”

The afternoon deepened. Men slept in the shade with their mouths open, as though the body itself tried to drink the cooler air. The plateau was as noiseless as the morning. The two officers sat compelled to silence by the silence around them—until Durrance’s eyes lost their abstraction and fixed intently upon the shrubbery beyond the glacis.

Without turning his head he saw that Mather, too, stared at the same patch of green.

“What are you thinking about?” Durrance asked suddenly.

Mather laughed, and answered with the odd tenderness a man gives to a private appetite when the world has offered him nothing else to hope for.

“I was drawing up the menu of the first dinner I will have when I reach London. I will eat it alone, I think—quite alone—and at Epitaux. It will begin with a watermelon.” He paused as though tasting it already: the cold, clean sweetness; the faint, grassy perfume when the knife first parts the rind. “And you?”

“I was wondering,” Durrance said, “why—now that the pigeons have got used to our presence—they should still be wheeling in and out of one particular tree. Don’t point to it, please. I mean the tree beyond the ditch, and to the right of two small bushes.”

All about them pigeons perched quietly upon branches, spotting the foliage like purple fruit. Only above the one tree they circled, calling timorously, their notes quickened with the thin edge of fear.

“We will draw that covert,” Durrance said. “Take a dozen men and surround it quietly.”

He remained on the glacis watching the tree and the thick undergrowth. He saw six soldiers creep round from the left and six from the right, each step measured so that even the sand should not complain. But before they could meet and ring the tree, the branches shook violently and an Arab burst out—yellowish dammar wound about his waist, a flat-headed spear in his hand, a hide shield on his arm. He dashed from shelter into open plain, running as though the air itself hunted him.

He ran only a few yards. Mather gave a sharp order; and the Arab, as though he understood it, stopped before a rifle could be lifted. Then, with a dignity that was neither insolence nor servility, he walked back.

They brought him up on to the glacis. He stood before Durrance and spoke in Arabic: he was a man of the Kabbabish, named Abou Fatma, friendly to the English, travelling to Suakin.

“Why did you hide?” Durrance asked.

“It was safer,” the man answered. “I knew you for my friends. But, my gentleman, did you know me for yours?”

Durrance’s quick glance sharpened.

“You speak English,” he said, and shifted into it.

The answer came without hesitation, but without pride.

“I know a few words.”

“Where did you learn them?”

“In Khartum.”

They left Abou Fatma alone with Durrance on the glacis, and the two men talked together for the better part of an hour. Voices carried strangely in that place; even low speech had to be weighed, as though the air might translate it into something louder. When at last they parted, Abou Fatma descended the glacis, crossed the trench, and went away toward the hills.

Durrance ordered the march resumed.

The water-tanks were filled; men replenished their zamshyehs, knowing that of all thirsts in the world the afternoon thirst is the worst—worse than the honest thirst of morning, because it comes with weariness and heat like a hand closing on the throat. Saddles went on again; the camels rose with their groaning and snarling, offended as ever by the notion of duty. The detachment moved northwestward from Sinkat at an acute angle to its morning’s track, skirting the hills opposite the pass by which they had descended. The bushes grew sparse. They came into a black country of stones, scantily relieved by yellow, tasselled mimosas that offered no real shade, only a gesture of it.

Durrance rode up to Mather.

“That Arab had a strange story to tell me,” he said. “He was Gordon’s servant in Khartum. At the beginning of ’84—eighteen months ago, in fact—Gordon gave him a letter which he was to take to Berber, so the contents could be telegraphed to Cairo. But Berber had just fallen when the messenger arrived. He was seized and imprisoned the day after his arrival. During the one day he was free he hid the letter in the wall of a house—and so far as he knows it has not been discovered.”

“He would have been questioned if it had been,” Mather said.

“Precisely. And he was not questioned. He escaped from Berber at night, three weeks ago.” Durrance’s voice lowered, as if the story demanded a quieter air. “The letter still remains in the wall.”

“It is curious,” Mather said. “Perhaps the man was telling lies.”

“He had the chain mark on his ankles,” Durrance replied.

The cavalcade turned left into the hills on the northern side of the plateau and climbed again over shale. Durrance’s thoughts moved ahead of his body. A letter from Gordon: perhaps scribbled on the palace roof beside the great telescope, a sentence written in haste before the eye went back to the lens to search over palm-tops for the smoke of steamers—and then carried down the Nile only to be buried in a mud wall in Berber, like seed dropped in bad ground.

“A letter from Gordon,” Durrance said, musing. “Yes—it’s curious.” He turned his face westward.

Even as he looked, the sun dipped behind the hills. The sky above darkened rapidly to violet; in the west it flamed with rich, iridescent colour. The violence softened; it blended delicately into a single rose hue. The rose lingered, faded, and left a sky of pure emerald green transfused with light from beneath the rim of the world.

“If only they had let us go last year westward to the Nile,” Durrance said with a kind of passion. “Before Khartum had fallen—before Berber had surrendered. But they would not.”

The sunset’s magic might have been painted on a wall for all the place it held in his mind. The letter had struck a chord of reverence: the image of that honest, great, impracticable soldier—despised by officials, thwarted by intrigue—going unflaggingly about his work, knowing that the moment his back was turned the work would be undone.

Darkness came upon the troops. The camels quickened their pace. From every tuft of grass the cicadas shrilled, turning the air into a vibrating cloth of sound; after the day’s unnatural hush, it felt almost like a consolation, a living music that belonged to the land and did not ask permission. The detachment moved down toward the well of Disibil.

That night Durrance lay long awake on his camp bedstead beneath the stars. The day’s heat had gone out of the stones; the air smelled cleaner, with a faint, cold mineral tang, and the camels’ occasional grumble came from the darkness like speech half remembered. He forgot the letter in the mud wall. Southward the Southern Cross hung slanting; above him glittered the curve of the Great Bear. In a week he would sail for England. He counted up the years since the packet had cast off from Dover pier, and the tale of them seemed good: Kassassin, Tel-el-Kebir, the rush down the Red Sea, Tokar, Tamai, Tamanieb—crowded moments coming back with such clarity that his body answered them. He thrilled at the recollection of Hadendowas leaping and stabbing through the breach of McNeil’s zareba six miles from Suakin; he recalled the obdurate defence of the Berkshires, the steadiness of the Marines, the rallying of broken troops. The years had been years of plenty, and had carried him to the brevet rank of lieutenant-colonel.

“A week more—only a week,” Mather murmured drowsily from somewhere in the dark.

“I shall come back,” Durrance said with a low laugh.

“Have you no friends?”

There was a pause long enough for the cicadas to fill it and the night wind—so slight it was more a change of temperature than a breath—to make a leaf whisper.

“Yes,” Durrance said at last. “I have friends. I shall have three months wherein to see them.”

He had written no word to Harry Feversham during these years. Not writing letters was part of him; correspondence was a difficulty, like speaking in a dialect that had never been his own. Yet he thought now he would surprise his friends by a visit to Donegal, or find them perhaps in London. He would ride once again in the Row. But in the end he would come back. For his friend was married—to Ethne Eustace—and as for himself, his life’s work lay here in the Soudan. He would certainly come back.

So, turning on his side, he slept dreamlessly while the hosts of the stars trampled across the heavens overhead.

At that same moment Abou Fatma of the Kabbabish slept under a boulder on the Khor Gwob. He rose early and went on across the broad plains to the white city of Suakin. There he repeated the story he had told Durrance to Captain Willoughby, acting deputy-governor. After he came from the Palace he told it again—this time in the native bazaar, in Arabic, in the open weave of market sound: vendors calling, metal struck, the dry hiss of sand under feet, and the deeper murmur of men bargaining as though the day were like any other.

A Greek seated outside a café close at hand caught fragments of the tale. He took Abou Fatma aside and, promising him much merissa with which to intoxicate himself, induced him to tell it a fourth time, very slowly, syllable by syllable—so that nothing of it would be lost, as words are lost when they pass between tongues and one man’s meaning becomes another man’s profit.

“Could you find the house again?” the Greek asked.

Abou Fatma had no doubt. He drew diagrams in the dust, not knowing that during his imprisonment Berber had been steadily pulled down by the Mahdists and rebuilt to the north—memory replaced by new walls, as if the town itself had changed its language.

“It will be wise to speak of this to no one except me,” the Greek said, and he jingled some significant dollars. For a long while the two men talked secretly together.

The Greek happened to be Harry Feversham—whom Durrance was proposing to visit in Donegal. Captain Willoughby was deputy-governor of Suakin, and after three years of waiting one of Harry Feversham’s opportunities had come.

Durrance came into London on a pale June morning when the city already had its summer voice: iron-rimmed wheels over granite, a thousand footsteps stitched together on the pavements, and above it all the ceaseless, thin scraping of commerce—bells, whistles, a distant hammering that never quite stopped. By afternoon he had done what men newly landed from far stations always did, as if to prove the country still held them; he went to Hyde Park and sat beneath a great tree, letting the green canopy soften the hard metallic din.

The grass smelled bruised and sweet where it had been cut; under the leaves the air was cooler, laced with the faint perfume of gloves and powdered hair. Women passed in light dresses that seemed made of summer itself, their laughter scattering like birdsong over the drone of the town. Durrance watched them with a kind of hunger—an exile’s astonishment at small, careful graces—and all the while his sunburnt face, with its set look about the eyes, marked him as one who had lived where horizons were bare and noise came from wind and rifle-fire rather than from streets.

Among the strollers one woman’s glance did not slide away. She smiled faintly, as though at something remembered, and when Durrance rose from his chair Mrs. Adair was beside him.

She was in black.

The colour struck him first—more than her face, more than the quick, almost secret measuring look with which she took him from head to foot, comparing what stood before her with the image she had carried for three years. He saw only the mourning, and the question he did not speak she answered before he could shape it.

“My husband died eighteen months ago,” she said, her voice quiet, as if she were giving a date from an old parish register. “Thrown from his horse during a run with the Pytchley. Killed at once.”

“I had not heard,” Durrance said, awkwardly, because condolences were a language he never commanded. “I am very sorry.”

She took the chair beside his. She did not say she was glad of his pity, nor that she needed it. She had always been a woman of pauses that seemed to contain more than speech could bear; her pale, composed face offered no clue to what those silences held. For a moment the Park’s soft noises—the rustle of skirts, the low hum of talk—seemed to press in on the two of them, and Durrance, remembering Adair as good-humoured and demonstrably fond of his wife, discovered with discomfort that he could not summon up a full grief. Adair had been, in that household, almost a shadow; the sorrow belonged to Mrs. Adair alone, and she wore it with a correctness that made it hard to touch.

He abandoned the attempt and turned instead to the other names that had haunted him all the way from the sea.

“Are Harry Feversham and his wife in town?”

Mrs. Adair’s answer took time, as though she were searching for the proper word and finding none that would not cut.

“Not yet,” she said at last; then, as if the phrase betrayed her into a lie of habit, she corrected herself quickly. “I mean—the marriage never took place.”

Durrance’s surprise, when it came, did not burst out in exclamation. It was a slight stiffening, a stillness. “I don’t think I understand. Why did it never take place?”

She looked sharply at him, as though his deliberate tone hid some accusation. “I don’t know why. Ethne can keep a secret if she wishes.”

Durrance nodded; that, at least, matched what he knew. “The marriage was broken off on the night of a dance at Lennon House.”

He turned fully towards her. “Just before I left England three years ago?”

“Yes.”

“Then you knew?”

“No. Only—” he paused, and in that pause the sea-wind of Dover seemed to blow through the leaves. “You have explained something which happened on the very night I left. What has become of Harry?”

Mrs. Adair lifted her shoulders in a small, indifferent shrug that somehow felt like a sentence. “I do not know. I have met no one who does know. I do not think I have met any one who has even seen him since that time. He must have left England.”

Durrance, staring past her at a carriage glittering by, suddenly saw again the man on the pier at Dover—his face twisted with trouble, despair clinging to him like a fog. So it had been Harry after all. His friend. And he had gone away as completely as if the Channel had swallowed him.

“And Miss Eustace?” Durrance asked after a moment, with a timidity that surprised him. “She has married since?”

Again she delayed, and the delay was its own answer before the word arrived. “No.”

“Then she is still at Ramelton?”

Mrs. Adair shook her head. “There was a fire at Lennon House a year ago.” She paused. “Did you ever hear of a constable called Bastable?”

“Indeed, I did.” The name brought back the sharp smell of wet leather and turf smoke from an Irish night, and the indignation of being hauled up like a criminal. “He was the means of introducing me to Miss Eustace and her father. I was travelling from Londonderry to Letterkenny. I received a letter from Mr. Eustace—whom I did not know, but who knew from friends at Letterkenny that I was coming past his house. He asked me to stay the night with him. Naturally I declined, and Bastable arrested me on a magistrate’s warrant as soon as I landed from the ferry.”

“That is the man,” Mrs. Adair said, and began, in that steady, feelingless way she had, to tell him what had happened.

Bastable’s claim to Dermod Eustace’s friendship, it seemed, rested upon a talent not of law but of the saucepan. There was, in that household, a particular toddy—whiskey warmed and sweetened, the water tempered just so, and a spice added with the confidence of long practice. Bastable had a refinement for it, an old country cunning: to make it perfect he would simmer in the saucepan a single oyster, just one, until its briny sweetness and faint sea-metal melted into the drink and gave it a depth no one could quite name. The thing was absurd and, to those who had tasted it, irresistible. It was as though the ocean itself had been persuaded to lend a whisper to the fire.

On a June night at two in the morning, the spirit lamp beneath the saucepan had been upset. The two men—confederates in drink—had not their wits about them. The small flame, fed too easily, had found wood, curtains, whatever it could catch; the air would have filled first with the sharp reek of burning spirit, then with the bitter, choking smell of charred fabric. By the time help came, half the house was burnt and the rest was ruined by the water that saved it.

“There were consequences still more distressing than the destruction of the house,” Mrs. Adair went on, her eyes fixed straight ahead as if she were reading from a page only she could see. “The fire was a beacon warning Dermod’s creditors, for one thing. Dermod, already overpowered with debts, fell in a day upon complete ruin. He was drenched by the hoses besides, and took a chill which nearly killed him. From the effects he has never recovered. You will find him a broken man. The estates are let, and Ethne is now living with her father in a little mountain village in Donegal.”

She had spoken as though necessity, not sympathy, forced each sentence out of her. Even when she stopped she did not turn her face towards him.

“So she has lost everything?” Durrance said.

“She still has a home in Donegal,” Mrs. Adair returned.

“And that means a great deal to her,” Durrance said slowly, as if tasting the truth of it.

“Yes, I think you are right.”

“It means,” Mrs. Adair said, “that Ethne with all her ill-luck has reason to be envied by many other women.”

Durrance did not answer at once. The Park went on around them, London’s easy chatter and the bright, careless laughter. Above it, always, that undertone of the city’s machinery—wheels, hooves, the faint clatter of harness—like an instrument no one could ever quite silence. In it he heard the difference between comfort and exile: out there, under wide skies, the world could be quiet enough for a man to hear his own thoughts coming; here the noise pressed and pressed, as though it feared what a person might remember in stillness.

At last his slow mind worked toward words.

“Women suffer much more than men when the world goes wrong with them,” he said, and it sounded, even to himself, less like a statement than a question set down carefully. “I know very little, of course. I can only guess. But I think women gather up into themselves what they have been through much more than we do. To them what is past becomes a real part of them, as much a part of them as a limb; to us it’s always something external—at the best the rung of a ladder, at the worst a weight on the heel. Don’t you think so? I phrase it badly. But put it this way: women look backwards, we look ahead; so misfortune hits them harder, eh?”

Mrs. Adair did not agree or contradict him. Her reply came from another place altogether, quiet and exact, like a direction written on the back of an envelope.

“The mountain village at which Ethne is living,” she said in a low voice, “is called Glenalla. A track strikes up towards it from the road halfway between Rathmullen and Ramelton.”

She rose as she finished, and held out her hand. “Shall I see you?”

“You are still in Hill Street?” Durrance asked.

“I shall be for a time in London.”

He took her hand. “Then you will see me.”

Mrs. Adair’s eyebrows lifted very slightly, her face sharpening with the suspicion she always had of motives. She could not understand why he stayed. If his thoughts were already in Donegal, why did he not follow them? She heard, as the weeks went, that he was continually at his Service Club; that he haunted its inner courtyard beneath the great tree, speaking to this man and that, as if he listened for a particular note in the sound of names.

She was more puzzled still when he told her, without emphasis, that he had gone down into Surrey and spent a day with General Feversham.

It was an ineffectual day. The old general kept him steadily to the campaign from which Durrance had returned—troop movements, orders, distances, the tidy arithmetic of war. The general’s house was quiet in a way London never was; but it was a brittle quiet, a silence packed tight around certain rooms. Only once did Durrance edge towards the subject that mattered. He spoke Harry’s name.

The effect was immediate. The general’s face set, as if plaster had been drawn over it. The features became blank; the eyes, which had been attentive, seemed to look past Durrance and see nothing.

“We will talk of something else, if you please,” he said.

Durrance returned to London not an inch nearer to Donegal, though his mind travelled there each night.

So he sat under the courtyard tree at his club through June afternoons and evenings, letting the soft shade make a small country within the city’s clangour. The place had its own soundscape: the muted clink of glasses carried out from the smoking-room, the dry slap of newspapers folded, the low, well-bred rumble of voices that never rose into true laughter. He spoke to whomever passed—any friend of Feversham’s, any man who had ever dined with him, ridden with him, served near him. The words were always the same in the end, turned and turned again like a question asked in different dialects in the hope one would unlock the answer.

But each time he met only shoulders lifted, eyes averted, a “Hanged if I know!” And it was as though Harry Feversham’s very name had begun to die on their tongues, slipping out of common speech; a thing no longer spoken of soon became, in that world, a thing unreal.

Toward the end of June an old naval officer limped into the courtyard. Lieutenant Sutch. Durrance saw him pause when he noticed the man beneath the tree, saw him hesitate, and then, with remarkable speed for his infirmity, attempt to move away as though he had mistaken the door.

Durrance was on his feet in an instant.

“Mr. Sutch,” he said.

The lieutenant’s embarrassment showed itself too plainly. “Colonel Durrance, to be sure,” he replied, forcing a geniality that creaked. “It is some while since we met, but I remember you very well now. I think we met—let me see—where was it? An old man’s memory, Colonel Durrance, is like a leaky ship. It comes to harbour with its cargo of recollections swamped.”

“We met at Broad Place,” Durrance said. He stepped close enough that Sutch could not drift away into safer company. “I wish you to give me news of my friend Feversham. Why was his engagement with Miss Eustace broken off? Where is he now?”

For a moment the lieutenant’s eyes gleamed with a quick satisfaction. He had never been sure whether Durrance knew of the disgrace that had fallen on Harry. Durrance plainly did not.

“There is only one person in the world, I believe,” Sutch said slowly, “who can answer both your questions.”

Durrance did not flinch. “Yes. I have waited here a month for you.”

Lieutenant Sutch pushed his fingers through his beard and stared down at him, as if weighing whether he was being trapped by honour or by curiosity.

“Well, it is true,” he admitted. “I can answer your questions—but I will not.”

“Harry Feversham is my friend.”

“General Feversham is his father, yet he knows only half the truth. Miss Eustace was betrothed to him, and she knows no more. I pledged my word to Harry that I would keep silence.”

“It is not curiosity which makes me ask.”

“I am sure that, on the contrary, it is friendship,” Sutch said warmly, and meant it. “Nor that entirely,” Durrance answered, and the faintest colour rose under his sunburn. “There is another aspect. I will not ask you to answer my questions, but I will put a third one to you. It is harder for me to ask than for you to answer. Would a friend of Harry Feversham be at all disloyal to that friendship if—if he tried his luck with Miss Eustace?”

The question startled Lieutenant Sutch into a frank exclamation. “You?”

He stood considering Durrance, recalling his rapid promotion, the look of him—strong, set, a man who had done things and would do them again—and imagining, in his simple theory of women, what such brute courage might mean to a woman stripped of house and fortune. In that instant the lieutenant felt something like jealousy on Harry’s behalf, fierce as an old nurse’s over a favourite child. He had cherished a secret hope that Harry would come back in the end to all he had lost, like a king who returns from exile; and now he saw the hope threatened by the very man who asked him the question with such restraint.

Sutch was tempted—sorely tempted—to lie. He knew Durrance well enough to believe the lie would do its work. Let him think it disloyal; he would go back to the Soudan and leave Ethne alone with her poor father and her mountain village.

“Well?” Durrance asked, pitiless in its quiet.

Sutch looked up at the sky and down at the flags, as if the answer were written somewhere in the spaces between. He thought of Harry under a burning sun, lost to all maps, and set that image against the comfortable courtyard, the successful soldier waiting under a tree while servants moved silently with trays. He felt, with a sudden anger, like breaking his word and telling all—where Harry had gone, why he had vanished, what shame had driven him.

“Well?” Durrance repeated.

Sutch’s mouth tightened. Honour held him by the throat, and something else held him too—the sense that words, once spoken, could never be taken back; that a name, once shaped in the dominant language of their world, could kill a man’s chance to be remembered any other way.

“No,” he said at last, regretfully. “There would be no disloyalty.”

And on that evening, while London clattered on with its indifferent noise, Durrance went to his rooms, packed, and took the train for Holyhead.

The farm-house at Glenalla stood a mile above the village, where the bridle-path forgot itself and simply ended at the door, as though it had brought you as far as any reasonable creature could go. Heather pressed close upon the garden beds and leaned, purple and insistent, over the low stone edging; beyond that, the moor rolled away in rough folds, and the hills rose on three sides in a kind of bowl, sheltering the place as snugly as a hand cupped round a flame.

Those hills had no trees—only granite pushing through moss and ling, and long sweeps of colour that shifted with a quick, almost willful change from week to week: emerald where the rain had lately washed, bruised russet where the wind had dried, and the deep purple of heather in bloom. They looked, to Durrance’s eye, less like dead land than like attentive guardians who dressed themselves each morning for the sake of the one life they harboured.

The house faced down the long slope toward the inlet of the Lough. From the windows you could follow, in a single slow glance, the sparse thickets and a few tilled fields, the whitewashed cottages dotted like crumbs of bread flung across the brown, and then the tall woods near the bank; and beyond, the bright slice of water where gulls hung, dipped, and cried, their calls stitching the air. The whole place had its own soundscape: wind combing the heather with a dry hiss; water speaking in the distance; sheep bells thin and far off; and, when the gusts fell, a silence that was not emptiness at all but a living quiet—so complete you could hear your own coat sleeve rasp as you moved.

Durrance rode up the track one afternoon and knew the house before he could have sworn to it by sight. For as he came nearer, a violin’s music floated out from an open window—no drawing-room prettiness, but a steady, searching voice, like a familiar dialect spoken with exact care. The notes rode the air cleanly, undrowned by any machine-clatter, any town din; they met him in that moorland quiet with the force of a welcome.

His hand checked on the reins. A hope—one he had let himself toy with lately, pretending it was only fancy—rose sharply in him and held his breath. He tied his horse at the gate and went in.

Outside, the house had the blunt look of a barrack, square and unornamented against the weather. Within, it was comfort—brasses kept bright, oak that glowed with rubbing, a room full of light and prospect. Durrance could see it, too, in another season: blinds drawn on a winter night, peat fire red on the hearth, the wind skirling around the hills and rapping at the panes like knuckles. Here, at least, noise was honest—wind and rain and the small domestic crackle of flame—not the hard metallic roar that cities had begun to worship as progress.

Ethne greeted him without any show of surprise.

“I thought that you would come,” she said, and smiled as if she had been expecting him as naturally as she expected the tide to turn.

Durrance laughed as they shook hands, sudden and unguarded. Ethne’s eyes followed his gaze to the violin lying on the table beside her. It was pale in colour, and near the bridge a small patch showed where worm-eaten wood had once been replaced—a scar mended with care.

“It is yours,” she said. “You were in Egypt. I could not well send it back to you there.”

“I have hoped lately, since I knew,” Durrance replied, “that, nevertheless, you would accept it.”

“You see I have.” She looked straight into his eyes. “I accepted it some while ago. There was a time when I needed to be assured that I had sure friends. And a thing tangible helped. I was very glad to have it.”

He took up the instrument as delicately as though it were a chalice, his fingers respectful of its varnish and age.

“You have played upon it? The Musoline overture, perhaps,” he said. “Do you remember that?”

She laughed. “Yes, I have played upon it, but only recently. For a long time I put my violin away. It talked to me too intimately of many things which I wished to forget.”

She said it without hesitation and without lowering her eyes, as if the truth—however sharp—was the only language she could endure.

The next day Durrance fetched up his luggage from Rathmullen and remained at the farm for a week. Yet up to the last hour of that visit neither he nor Ethne spoke Harry Feversham’s name. The silence sat between them like a covered dish no one would uncover at table: not because appetite was lacking, but because the smell might be too strong.

They were thrown much together, for Dermod Eustace was even more broken than Mrs. Adair’s account had prepared Durrance to find. The man’s speech had dwindled to monosyllables; his frame was shrunken, and his clothes hung upon him as upon a peg. Even his anger—once so quick—seemed clouded, as though time had breathed on it until it dulled. He stayed by the fire, dozing through most of the day even in July, and his longest walk was to the little grey church that stood bare upon a mound within view of the windows. Even that short distance taxed him. There was something painful in his gestures, and in the rare tones of his voice—like the mimicry of a dead man by someone who had once known him well. His collie dog seemed to have aged in sympathy; to see them together was to see weariness shared.

So Durrance and Ethne walked the hills in wet weather and fine. She went ahead sometimes, the colour glowing in her face, eyes jealous and eager for him to see rightly, to admire without condescension. She showed him the streams that cut the peat-black ground, the places where the granite broke through like bone, the distant line of woods that guarded the Lough. In the evenings she took up the violin and, as of old, sat with her face turned partly away and bade the strings speak of height and depth—of gulls and wind and the long moorland hush. Durrance watched the sweep of her arm, the absorbed set of her mouth, and counted his chances.

He had not come to Glenalla buoyed by Lieutenant Sutch’s cheerful prophecy of success. The shadow of Harry Feversham might well lie between them. Besides, he knew poverty would fall more lightly upon her than upon most women. Though Lennon House was ruined and the lands were gone, Ethne was still among her own people. They looked for her visits with the same bright hunger as children watching for bread to come from the oven. She remained, in that countryside, a kind of princess—less by title than by the way she listened, by the way she knew names and stories that others had begun to forget.

And she, for her part, took a frank pleasure in his company. She drew him out about his three years’ service in the East. No detail was too small for her. She wanted the texture of it: how the mornings began, what water tasted like from different wells, how a tent smelled after a sandstorm, what a man heard at night when the world’s machinery fell silent. While he spoke her eyes continually sounded him, as if she tested the grain of his words for honesty. Her smile approved when his account carried the weight of lived sensation rather than mere soldierly boasting.

Durrance did not understand what she sought. He could not have understood, unless he had known what had passed between Ethne and Harry Feversham. He wore the likeness of a man; she meant to be sure a man’s spirit inhabited it. He was, to her, like a dark lantern: there might be a flame within—or only vacancy. She kept pushing back the slide to see.

On the last day of his visit she led him to speak of Egypt. They sat on a hillside by a stream that leaped from ledge to ledge down a miniature gorge of rock and slid over deep pools between the ledges with swift purpose—a torrent of clear black water. Its voice filled the hollow with a continuous roar that was not harsh but alive, the kind of sound that steadies the mind because it belongs wholly to its place.

“I travelled once for four days amongst the mirages,” he said. “Lagoons, still as a mirror and fringed with misty trees. You could almost walk your camel up to the knees in them, before the lagoon receded and the sand glared at you. And one cannot imagine that glare. Every stone within view dances and shakes like a heliograph; you can see—yes, actually see—the heat flow breast-high across the desert swift as this stream here, only pellucid. So till the sun sets ahead of you level with your eyes. Imagine the nights which follow—nights of infinite silence, with a cool friendly wind blowing from horizon to horizon—and your bed spread for you under the great dome of stars.

“Oh,” he cried, drawing a deep breath, “but that country grows on you. It’s like the Southern Cross—four overrated stars when first you see them, but in a week you begin to look for them, and you miss them when you travel north again.”

He raised himself on his elbow and turned towards her.

“Do you know—I can only speak for myself—but I never feel alone in those empty spaces. On the contrary, I always feel very close to the things I care about, and to the few people I care about too.”

Her eyes shone on him, bright as water catching sun. Her lips parted in a smile. He moved nearer on the grass and sat with his feet gathered under him, leaning on one arm.

“I used to imagine you out there,” he said. “You would have loved it—from the start before daybreak, in the dark, to the camp-fire at night. You would have been at home. I used to think so as I lay awake wondering how the world went with my friends.”

“And you go back there?” she asked.

He did not answer at once. The torrent’s roar beat about them. When he spoke again the enthusiasm had gone; his gaze dropped into the stream as if the black water carried his words away.

“To Wadi Halfa. For two years. I suppose so.”

Ethne knelt on the grass beside him.

“I shall miss you,” she said.

She knelt just behind him as he sat; and for a moment there was only the sound of water and the thin far cry of a gull, as though the land itself listened.

“Of what are you thinking?” she asked.

“That you need not miss me,” he said.

He felt rather than saw her draw back and sink on her heels. Her stillness alarmed him; with Ethne, a fixed quiet was as plain a sign of distress as a cry in another woman. He turned.

Her head had been bent, but she lifted it as he faced her. Her lips smiled—yet trouble lay openly in her eyes.

Durrance’s first thought was the blunt one of a man: if she were willing, what obstacle stood in the way?

“There is your father,” he said.

“Yes,” she answered. “There is my father too. I could not leave him.”

“Nor need you,” he returned quickly. “That difficulty can be surmounted. To tell the truth I was not thinking of your father at the moment.”

“Nor was I,” she said.

He turned away and stared down into a wrinkled pool beneath the rocks. After all, it was Feversham’s shadow.

“I know, of course,” he said at last, “that you would never feel trouble, as so many do, with half your heart. You would neither easily care nor lightly forget.”

“I remember enough,” she replied in a low voice, “to make your words rather a pain to me. Some day perhaps I may bring myself to tell everything which happened at that ball three years ago, and then you will be better able to understand why I am a little distressed. All that I can tell you now is this: I have a great fear that I was to some degree the cause of another man’s ruin. I do not mean that I was to blame for it. But if I had not been known to him, his career might perhaps never have come to so abrupt an end. I am not sure, but I am afraid. I asked whether it was so, and I was told ‘no,’ but I think very likely that generosity dictated that answer. And the fear stays. I am much distressed by it. I lie awake with it at night. And then you come whom I greatly value, and you say quietly, ‘Will you please spoil my career too?’”

She struck one hand sharply into the other. “But that I will not do.”

“There is no need that you should,” he answered. “Wadi Halfa is not the only place where a soldier can find work to his hand.”

Hope sprang up in his voice, for he had listened with the greedy attention of a man translating another’s speech into the dictionary of his desires. She had not said friendship bounded her thoughts of him; therefore he did not believe it did. “One whom I greatly value,” “I shall miss you”—he heard, behind those words, meanings she had not put there. He even caught at her earlier confession about needing “sure friends,” as if tangible friendship must surely mean a hunger for more than friendship.

If he had understood her better, that very reasoning would have warned him how deep he had sunk in error. Ethne habitually meant exactly what she said—no less, and no more. And now she had weighed her words with particular care.

“No doubt,” she said, “a soldier can. But can this soldier find work so suitable? Listen, please, till I have done. I was so very glad to hear all that you have told me about your work and your journeys. I was still more glad because of the satisfaction with which you told it. For it seemed to me, as I listened and as I watched, that you had found the one true straight channel along which your life could run swift and smoothly and unharassed. And so few do that—so very few!”

She wrung her hands. “And now you spoil it all.”

Durrance swung round on her, and argument fell away. Passion took its place.

“I am for you, Ethne! There’s the true straight channel, and upon my word I believe you are for me. I thought—at one time—I would spend my life out there in the East, and the thought contented me. But I had schooled myself into contentment, for I believed you married.”

She flinched, ever so slightly. He heard his own voice and knew he had spoken too loud, with something almost brutal in the force of it.

“Do I hurt you?” he asked more quietly. “I am sorry. But let me speak the whole truth out. I cannot afford reticence. I want you to know the first and last of it. I say now that I love you. Yes, but I could have said it with equal truth five years ago. It is five years since your father arrested me at the ferry down there on Lough Swilly, because I wished to press on to Letterkenny and not delay a night by stopping with a stranger. Five years since I first saw you, first heard the language of your violin. I remember how you sat with your back towards me. The light shone on your hair; I could just see your eyelashes and the colour of your cheeks. I remember the sweep of your arm… My dear, you are for me; I am for you.”

He held out his hands. She drew back from them.

“No,” she said, very gently, with a decision he could not mistake.

She saw into him more clearly than he saw into himself. The restlessness of the born traveller, the craving for large lonely spaces at the far ends of maps, the intermittent fever of movement—those were deep-rooted in him. Passion might muffle them for a time; it would not cure them. In the end they would call, and the call would torture. A home would begin to feel like a cell. Within him desires would clash until peace became impossible.

She looked down the slope across the brown country. On the right the woods about Ramelton waved; at her feet a strip of the Lough flashed; gulls turned and cried above it. This was her country, and she belonged to it as a seed belongs to its soil, as a song belongs to its own tongue.

“No,” she repeated, rising to her feet.

Durrance rose too. He felt less disheartened than aware of a blunder—he had let her think marriage would interrupt his career. He might have framed his case differently, made her see that the channel could widen and still run true.

“We will say good-bye here,” she said, “in the open. We shall be none the less good friends because three thousand miles hinder us from shaking hands.”

They shook hands.

“I shall be in England again in a year’s time,” Durrance said. “May I come back?”

Her eyes and smile consented.

“I should be sorry to lose you altogether,” she replied, “although even if I did not see you, I should know that I had not lost your friendship.” Then she added, “I should also be glad to hear news of you and what you are doing, if ever you have the time to spare.”

“I may write?” he exclaimed, eager.

“Yes,” she answered; but his eagerness made her linger a little on the word, doubtfully. “That is, if you think it fair. I mean, it might be best for you, perhaps, to get rid of me entirely from your thoughts.”

Durrance laughed without bitterness, and in a moment Ethne found herself laughing too, though at what she laughed she could not easily have said—perhaps at the odd helplessness of words, which so often mean one thing in the mouth and another in the ear.

“Very well, write to me then,” she said. And she added drily, “But it will be about—other things.”

Again he read into her speech what he wanted to read; again she meant precisely what she said, and not a word more.

She stood where he left her, a tall, strong-limbed figure against the slope, until he had gone out of sight. Then she went down to the house.

In her room she took one violin from its case. But it was the one Durrance had given her, and before the bow had touched the strings she recognised it—felt it in her hands as unmistakably as one recognises an old flavour on the tongue. She put it away at once and snapped the case shut.

For a few moments she sat motionless. Then she crossed the room quickly, took her keys, and unlocked a drawer. At the bottom lay hidden a photograph. She drew it out and looked a long while, very wistfully, as though she tried to hear, in the silent paper, a voice that had once been spoken to her in a language she had trusted.

Durrance walked down to the trap waiting at the gate and saw Dermod Eustace standing in the road with his hat on his head.

“I will walk a few yards with you, Colonel Durrance,” Dermod said. “I have a word for your ear.”

Durrance matched his stride to the old man’s faltering step, and they walked behind the dog-cart in silence. It was not merely personal disappointment that weighed on Durrance now. As he looked back at that quiet Donegal corner—with its small cottages, its moorland, its tiny church—he was seized by a sadness at the thought of Ethne’s whole life spent in such seclusion: her grave one day under the church wall, and her memory lingering only a little while among those scattered white houses, like a tune remembered by a few and then lost when the last singer dies.

Dermod’s hand pressed his elbow, and Durrance started from his thoughts. A gleam of inquiry lived in the old man’s faded eyes, but even speech seemed difficult for him.

“You have news for me?” Dermod asked at last, with hesitation. “News of Harry Feversham? I thought that I would ask you before you went away.”

“None,” said Durrance.

“I am sorry,” Dermod replied wistfully, “though I have no reason for sorrow. He struck us a cruel blow, Colonel Durrance. I should have nothing but curses for him in my mouth and my heart. A black-throated coward my reason calls him, and yet I would be very glad to hear how the world goes with him. You were his friend. But you do not know?”

It was Harry Feversham Dermod spoke of, and the wistfulness in his voice and face struck Durrance with a sudden remorse.
