He had allowed Ethne, by her silence, to thrust his friend too far from his thoughts—as though a man might be erased by not being named. He had, at times, tried to piece together the mystery of Feversham’s disappearance from a handful of hints, sitting in the evenings on his verandah above the Nile at Wadi Halfa while the river’s slow voice filled the night and the desert lay listening beyond.

“Black-throated coward,” Dermod had called him. Ethne had said enough to make plain that something graver than any mere quarrel—something that destroyed her faith—had ended the betrothal. But Durrance could not guess the particular cause; and from his perplexity there grew, month by month, a real anger against the man who had been his friend.

So the winter passed, and summer came to the Soudan, and the month of May.

In that May, far away at Wadi Halfa, Durrance began to lift his eyes from the frontier and, with a longing that sat like thirst on the tongue, to look homeward. Yet the true business of his fate was being kneaded and baked in a heat he could not feel—five hundred miles to the south, beyond the great Nubian desert and the Belly of Stones, where men counted their lives by mouths of water and the sounds the wind allowed them.

On the deserted track between Berber and Suakin the wells of Obak lay sunk deep among mounds of shifting sand, as though the earth had swallowed its own cup and left only the rim to be found. Eastward ran a belt of trees, thin as a fence built for a modest garden, dividing the dunes from a hard stony plain shouldered up with granite hills. Westward the desert stretched fifty-eight waterless miles to Mahobey and Berber on the Nile—a flatness so pitiless that a tuft of grass no higher than a knee, seen from a mile, promised the shade of a great tree, and a pile of stones no bigger than those by any road under repair took on, in the distance, the stature of a hill.

In that particular May there could be no spot more empty. The sun struck it from six in the morning with an intolerable heat; at night the wind cut cold and restless, worrying the sand with the diligence of a cook’s hands at dough—raising pyramids house-high, flattening them again, tunnelling little valleys, silting slopes, so that the very face of the country was forever re-shaped. Vultures held the sky and sand-grouse held the ground in a tenancy without rent. Scattered here and there lay the bleached bones of camels, as if the desert had once taken a caravan as payment and left the accounts open for all to read. The remnants of a hut—branches bent in hoops—showed where Arabs had once penned goats and lived by milk and patience. Now the sun rose and set upon a round of honey-coloured earth. Silence brooded there like night on water; the stillness was so absolute it became a kind of expectation, as though one listened for a language one had half forgotten and feared never to hear again.

Yet in this May one man sojourned at the wells, and he did it secretly. Every morning at sunrise he drove down from the belt of trees two camels—swift riding-mares of the pure Bisharin breed—and watered them. Then he sat by the well-mouth for three hours, motionless, his presence as spare as a shadow at noon. Afterwards he drove them back into the trees and fed them delicately with dhoura, laying the grain out upon a cloth as a careful cook spreads spices to keep the sand from them. The dry kernels made a soft, clean rattle; the camels’ jaws worked with a slow contentment. Then, for the rest of the day, the man vanished.

For five mornings he came thus from his hiding-place and sat looking toward the sand-dunes and Berber, and no one approached him. On the sixth morning, as he was on the point of returning to the trees, he saw a man and a donkey outlined on the sky-line upon a crest of sand. The watcher by the well looked first at the donkey, and, noticing its grey coat, half rose. But as he rose he saw the driver: the jellab drawn forward to shield the face from the sun, and beneath it bare legs of ebony black against the pale sand. A negro.

The Arab sat down again, letting his body speak indifference. He did not even turn when the man’s feet sank and whispered close behind him.

“Salam aleikum,” said the negro as he stopped.

He carried a long spear and a short one, and a shield of hide. He laid them on the ground and sat beside the Arab, as though they were two men resting after market, not two strangers measuring one another’s lives. The Arab bowed his head.

“Aleikum es salam,” he answered, and waited.

“It is Abou Fatma?” asked the negro.

The Arab nodded.

“Two days ago,” the other went on, “a man of the Bisharin, Moussa Fedil, stopped me in the market-place of Berber, and seeing that I was hungry, gave me food. And when I had eaten he charged me to drive this donkey to Abou Fatma at the wells of Obak.”

Abou Fatma turned his eyes to the animal as though only now he had noticed it. He let the pause lengthen, the way one lets a pot come to the boil without peering.

“Tayeeb,” he said, carelessly. “The donkey is mine.”

He sat as if the matter were finished and the negro might rise and go. But the negro did not move.

“I am to meet Moussa Fedil again on the third morning from now, in the market-place of Berber,” he said. “Give me a token which I may carry back, so that he may know I have fulfilled the charge and reward me.”

Abou Fatma drew the knife from the small of his back, picked up a stick, and notched it—three cuts at each end—precise, even, the sort of mark that could not be mistaken for accident.

“This shall be a sign to Moussa Fedil,” he said, and handed it over.

The negro tied the stick securely into a corner of his wrap, then unlashed his water-skin from the donkey, filled it at the well, and slung it across his shoulders. He took up his spears and shield again. Abou Fatma watched him labour up the loose sand, step by step, until he reached the crest and slid out of sight down the far incline. The only music was the hush of grains falling and the faint clink of iron on wood—sounds so small they seemed to belong to a remembered world rather than this place.

Then Abou Fatma rose—and now without leisure. He drove the donkey among the trees, fastened it to a stem, and examined its shoulders. In the left shoulder a tiny incision had been made and neatly stitched with fine thread. He cut the stitches, pressed apart the edges of the wound, and forced out a small package no bigger than a postage stamp. It was a goat’s bladder, dried and tough and cleanly tied, as familiar to him as any kitchen skin used to keep a precious taste from spoiling in the heat.

Within the bladder was a note written in Arabic and folded very small.

Abou Fatma had not been Gordon’s body-servant for nothing. In those years he had learned letters as other men learn a trade: slowly, by use, and with a reverence for what can be carried when all else is stripped away. He unfolded the paper and read:

“The houses which were once Berber are destroyed, and a new town of wide streets is building. There is no longer any sign by which I may know the ruins of Yusef’s house from the ruins of a hundred houses; nor does Yusef any longer sell rock-salt in the bazaar. Yet wait for me another week.”

The hand that had written it was the hand of Harry Feversham.

He wore the patched jubbeh of the Dervishes over his stained skin; his hair was frizzed on the crown and fell on the nape in locks matted and gummed into the likeness of seaweed. He moved through New Berber—wide streets, gaping pits—searching for a name that had slipped its moorings. South of it, a mile or so across the sand, lay the old town where Abou Fatma had slept one night and hidden the letters: a warren of ruined houses facing narrow alleys and winding streets. Front walls had been pulled down, roofs carried away; only bare inner walls remained, so that by night Feversham wandering among them felt he had entered long lanes of open courts, crumbling into decay. Each court differed from its neighbour only by a shade of ruin. Foxes had already begun to burrow beneath the fallen edges, scratching their small histories into the dust.

He had calculated that one night would be the term of his stay in Berber. He was to slip through the gate at dusk, and before the grey light quenched the stars his face would be set toward Obak. Now he must go steadily forward among crowds like a man with business of moment—dreading conversation lest his tongue betray him, listening always for the name of Yusef to strike his ears, as one listens for the first syllable of a forgotten dialect that might suddenly restore a whole childhood.

At times despair walked beside him; fear never left. Out of the sharp pangs of those emotions a kind of madness grew—a frenzy of obstinacy, a fanatical belief, as dark and absolute as the religion of the men among whom he moved, that he could not now fail and the world go on; that no injustice in the universe could be large enough to lay such a burden on the one man least fitted to carry it and then, when he struggled under it, crush him for the attempt.

Fear had him in its grip on the morning—three days after he had left Abou Fatma at the wells—when he came over a slope and first saw the sand stretched like a lagoon up to the dark brown walls of the town, and beyond, on the Nile bank, the heavy foliage of great date palms. The palms’ fronds shifted in the air with a dry, whispering applause; the river itself was hidden, but its presence seemed to cool the sound. Within those walls were the crowded Dervishes. It seemed the merest madness for a man to imagine he could escape detection there, even for an hour.

Was it right, he began to ask himself, that a man should even try?

The longer he stood, the more insistent that question grew. The low mud walls took on a sinister patience. The green of the palms—after so many arid days of sun and sand and stones—felt like an invitation offered with a smile that concealed a knife. His mouth was dry; his skin smarted under the sun as if salt had been rubbed into it. He began to wonder whether he had not already done enough for honour in venturing so near.

His strength ebbed as though his veins had been opened. If he were caught—surely he would be caught; oh, very surely—he saw in his mind the fanatical faces crowding about him. Mutilations? He could not drive the thought away. The place was lonely, so lonely that the silence itself seemed to watch him. His knees shook. He turned and ran—leaping in a panic, alone and unpursued, across the naked desert under the sun, and from his throat broke feeble cries without words.

He ran only a few yards. The violence of his flight stopped him, as a sudden bitterness can stop a man mid-bite. Was this all the four years of anticipation had been worth? He had schooled himself in the tongue, lived in bazaars, sweated in disguise—to no end? He was still the craven who had sent in his papers? The quiet confidence with which he had told Lieutenant Sutch his plan over the table in the Criterion grill-room—had it been nothing but the vanity of a man practised in deceiving himself?

And Ethne.

He dropped to the ground and drew his coat over his head, making himself a brown irregularity in the waste, indistinguishable from the sand. He shut the prospect from his eyes, and over thousands of miles of continent and sea he drew Ethne’s face toward him until it filled the darkness. For a little while he was back in Donegal. A summer night breathed through the open doorway in the hall; in a room near by people danced to music. He saw the three feathers flutter to the floor. He watched the trouble grow upon Ethne’s face, like a shadow spreading across a table where supper waits untouched.

If he could do this thing—and the harder thing which he now knew lay beyond—perhaps, some day, he might see that face cleared of its trouble. Words rang in his ears with the edge of prophecy: “I should have no doubt that you and I would see much of one another afterwards.”

Toward the setting of the sun he rose from the ground. He walked down toward Berber and, with his heart thudding as loudly in his own head as any drum, passed between the gates.

A month later Durrance came back to London, and at his club a letter lay waiting—Ethne’s hand upon the envelope, the paper edged with a thin black border like a strip of burnt pastry laid along a plate. The sight of that mourning line carried its own flavour: dry, mineral, unmistakable, as if grief had a scent and it was the scent of extinguished hearth-ash.

He went to Hill Street the next afternoon and was shown into a quiet room where Ethne sat alone. The house had the softened hush of good breeding and recent sorrow—carpets that swallowed footsteps, curtains that held the street’s clatter outside as a cook holds back the boil with a steady hand.

“I did not write to Wadi Halfa,” she said at once, as though she must clear away the ordinary explanations before she could touch the harder truth. “I thought you would be on your way home before my letter could arrive. My father died last month—towards the end of May.”

“I was afraid when I got your letter that you would have this to tell me,” Durrance answered. “I am very sorry. You will miss him.”

“More than I can say.” The words were plain, but they had weight, like bread cut thick. “He died one morning early. I think—I will tell you, if you would care to hear.”

He inclined his head. He had known Dermod’s body failing long before the end, and he had learned in Egypt that deaths were rarely sudden in the telling, even when they were sudden in fact; they came with a long approach of small particulars, each one a note in the surrounding soundscape.

“It was a chill that began it,” she said, “though it was not so much the cause as the occasion. He died… by degrees, as though something in him loosened and fell away. But just before the end—” She paused, then continued with a curious steadiness, as though she were reciting a recipe she could not bear to spoil by excess feeling. “Just before the end he took back something of his old mastery. We knew he was dying. He knew it too. At seven o’clock in the afternoon after—after he had spoken for a little while to me, he called his dog by name.”

Durrance watched her hands as she spoke. They were still, folded, the fingers pale from much holding in.

“The dog sprang on to the bed at once,” she went on, “though my father’s voice had not risen above a whisper. It crouched close, and pushed its muzzle—whining—under his hand. Then my father told me to leave him and the dog altogether alone. I was to shut the door upon him. The dog would tell me when to open it again. I obeyed him.”

Her voice did not tremble; only the room seemed to tighten around that closed door she described.

“I waited outside until one o’clock,” she said. “Then a loud sudden howl moaned through the house.”

For a moment she stopped. That brief silence was the only crack in her composure, the only place where grief showed itself—not in tears, but in the way the air in the room seemed to lose its warmth, as if a fire had been left to die with no one to rake it into life again.

“It was trying,” she said at last, simply. “To wait outside that door while the afternoon faded and the night came. He would have no lamp left in his room. One imagined him just the other side of that thin panel—lying very still and silent in the great four-poster bed with his face towards the hills—and the light falling away. The room slipping into darkness. The windows looming larger as everything else disappeared. And the dog by his side, and no one else, right to the end. He would have it that way. But it was rather hard for me.”

Durrance said nothing. There are moments when speech is an intrusion, like striking a match in a room where someone is listening for a distant footfall. He gave her what he could: the steady companionship of quiet. Yet in his mind he saw her in that passage for six long hours of twilight and night, every small domestic sound amplified—the settling of boards, the far clink of a glass, the hush of servants moving as carefully as if they carried full bowls. He thought, with a sudden sting, that there had been something cruel in Dermod’s chosen solitude.

Ethne broke the silence herself.

“I said my father spoke to me just before he told me to leave him,” she said. “Of whom do you think he spoke?”

She looked directly at Durrance. Her voice was level, her eyes unreadable; but hope—swift and involuntary—caught at him like breath taken too quickly.

“Tell me,” he said, leaning forward.

“Of Mr. Feversham.”

Durrance drew back, abruptly. He looked down at the carpet so she should not see what passed over his face.

“My father was always very fond of him,” she continued gently, “and I think I would like to know if you have any knowledge of what he is doing, or where he is.”

Durrance did not answer at once. The name brought with it the old perplexity—how Harry Feversham, having done what he had done, still held a strange power over the affections of people who should have cast him out. Even Dermod, wronged and made to suffer, had remembered him with kindness upon his death-bed. The thought had bitterness in it for Durrance now; he feared his bitterness would betray itself if he spoke too soon.

“You have never come across him, I suppose?” Ethne asked.

Durrance rose and walked to the window. In the street below, the world moved with its usual indifferent purpose: wheels, voices, the faint metallic cry of a distant vendor’s call. London’s sound was never clean; it had no clear edge the way a Donegal evening might have, when the only music was wind through grasses and the far, honest bark of a dog. Here the air was always bruised by traffic, and even sorrow had to fight for a quiet place to sit.

“No,” he said, looking out, hiding his expression as best he could. “I never have.”

And then his anger—deep, long-stored, and ashamed of itself—rose and spoke for him.

“And I never wish to,” he cried. “He was my friend, I know. But I cannot remember that friendship now. I can only think that if he had been the true man we took him for, you would not have waited alone in that dark passage during those six hours.”

He turned back to the centre of the room and spoke abruptly, as though to escape what he had just revealed.

“You are going back to Glenalla?”

“Yes.”

“You will live there alone?”

“Yes.”

Silence fell again between them, not unfriendly, but full—like a room after a strong broth has been ladled out, when the smell still hangs, telling you what was there.

Durrance walked behind her chair. “You once said that you would perhaps tell me why your engagement was broken off.”

“But you know,” she said. “What you said at the window showed that you knew.”

“No, I do not. One or two words your father let drop. He asked me for news of Feversham the last time I spoke with him. But I know nothing definite. I should like you to tell me.”

She shook her head and leaned forward, elbows on her knees, looking down as if the answer lay on the floorboards.

“Not now,” she said.

Again the room held them in its hush. Durrance, feeling the year in Egypt ahead of him like an empty table set for a meal he did not want to eat alone, spoke of practicalities, as men do when their hearts are unsteady.

“I have only one more year at Halfa,” he said. “It would be wise to leave Egypt then, I think. I do not expect much will be done in the Soudan for some little while. I do not think I will stay there—in any case. I mean even if you should decide to remain alone at Glenalla.”

Ethne did not pretend not to understand the drift of his words. She answered with the plainness of someone who has measured what she can afford and will not be coaxed into debt.

“We are neither of us children,” she said. “You have all your life to think of. We should be prudent.”

“Yes,” he said, and an exasperation—at prudence, at fate, at the way good intentions could be used like chains—broke through. “But the right kind of prudence. The prudence which knows that it’s worth while to dare a good deal.”

She did not move. Her back was to him, and for a long while she sat still, as though listening not to him but to the deeper currents in herself: the remembered cadence of a voice, the old Donegal words that could name shades of weather and shades of feeling that English could only circle around. At last she asked, very low, very gently:

“Do you want me so very much?”

Before he could answer she turned quickly, and for the first time emotion broke her composure—not in tears, but in urgency, like a hand snatching a kettle from boiling over.

“Try not to,” she exclaimed earnestly. “For this one year try not to. You have much to occupy your thoughts. Try to forget me altogether.”

There was regret in her tone—enough to soften the blow, enough to make him believe, as he longed to believe, that she pushed him away not because she did not care, but because she feared to injure him; feared, as she had once injured another man. Before he could reply Mrs. Adair entered, and the moment closed like a door gently pulled to.

That evening Durrance dined at his club. The dinner was well served, as club dinners are, but he ate with little attention—meat that might have been excellent, sauces that might have been crafted with care, all turning dull because his senses were held elsewhere. Afterwards he sat under the big tree in the courtyard and smoked, in the same place where a year before he had sat night after night in his vain hunger for news of Harry Feversham.

The night was clear, the strip of sky overhead cloudless and starred. The air had the languor of June. The club’s windows threw squares of light that made the leaves look newly washed, spring-green. Beyond the gates, the roadway sounded with rolling carriages, a continuous thunderous hum like the sea—London’s own tide, never slackening, never pure, forever grinding and returning.

On that night, too, a man came into the courtyard who knew Durrance. But he did not hesitate. He walked straight up and sat down on the seat beside him.

Durrance dropped the paper he had been glancing at and held out his hand. “How do you do?”

It was Captain Mather.

“I was wondering whether I should meet you,” Mather said. “When I read the evening paper. I knew it was about the time one might expect to find you in London. You have seen, I suppose?”

“What?” Durrance asked.

“Then you haven’t.” Mather picked up the newspaper Durrance had let fall and turned the sheets, searching with the certainty of a man hunting a particular line on a map. “You remember that last reconnaissance we made from Suakin?”

“Very well.”

“We halted by the Sinkat fort at midday. There was an Arab hiding in the trees at the back of the glacis.”

“Yes.”

“Have you forgotten the yarn he told you?”

“About Gordon’s letters and the wall of a house in Berber? No, I have not forgotten.”

“Then here’s something which will interest you.” Mather folded the paper and handed it to him, pointing to a short paragraph.

It was no more than a summary—no flourish, no story, only a line of print; and Durrance read it between puffs of his cigar. It announced that certain letters the Mahdi had sent to Gordon, summoning him to surrender Khartum and inviting him to become a convert to the Mahdist religion, together with copies of Gordon’s curt replies, had been recovered from a wall in Berber and brought safely to Captain Willoughby at Suakin.

“The fellow must have gone back to Berber after all,” Durrance said. “A risky business. Abou Fatma—that was the man’s name.”

The paragraph did not mention Abou Fatma, nor any man at all except Captain Willoughby. Yet Durrance tasted in the news a faint grit of the desert: sun-baked mud, the acrid bite of fear, the parchment-dry secrecy of a thing hidden in a wall. Still, he could not yet see how it might reach into his own life.

“They were hardly worth risking a life for,” Mather said.

“Perhaps not,” Durrance answered, doubtfully. “But one is glad they have been recovered. Perhaps the copies are in Gordon’s own hand. They are, at all events, of an historic interest.”

“In a way, no doubt,” Mather said. “But even so, their recovery throws no light upon the history of the siege. It can make no real difference to any one—not even to the historian.”

“That is true,” Durrance agreed.

And it was nothing of the sort. In the very place where he had once sat listening for news of Feversham, news had come to him at last—only he did not know it. He was still in the dark; he could not feel, yet, that this small paragraph touched his life at the springs, and would one day set it moving in a new direction.

He dismissed it from his mind and sat thinking instead of Ethne—of her steady voice when she spoke Harry’s name; of the way she had asked him to try, for one year, to forget her. That request, to Durrance’s hopeful heart, did not sound like farewell. It sounded like a truce; like a postponement that might turn, at the end of its term, into permission. If she truly did not care, why ask him to struggle? Why not let him burn out? He convinced himself that the very labour she demanded implied that she expected he might fail—and that if he failed, she might answer him differently.

A few days passed and he called again at Mrs. Adair’s house, only to find Mrs. Adair alone.

“Ethne has left London,” she said. “She went back to Donegal—rather suddenly.” And though Mrs. Adair spoke with the vagueness of one who had not been fully taken into confidence, Durrance believed he understood the reason well enough.

Ethne was putting into practice the policy she had urged upon him. He was to forget; she would help him by her absence, by removing her face and voice from the daily sound of his world.

In that, he was right. But she had forgotten one thing: she had not asked him to stop writing.

So, in the autumn, the letters began again to come from the Soudan—sheets that carried, across heat and sand and the harsh jangling of military life, a different atmosphere. She was frankly glad to receive them; yet they troubled her. For all their careful reticence, a phrase would leap out now and then—perhaps only the repetition of some trivial sentence she had once spoken and long forgotten—and she could not help seeing that, despite her plea, she lived perpetually in his thoughts.

There was a strain of hopefulness in them too, as though he moved through a world painted with new colours and suddenly grown musical; as though even the hard noises of his days—the shouted orders, the clink of equipment, the rasp of the desert wind—had arranged themselves into something like harmony because her name was somewhere behind them like an unheard accompaniment.

Ethne had never freed herself from the haunting fear that one man’s life had been spoiled because of her. She had never wavered from her determination that this should not happen to a second. Yet with Durrance’s letters before her she could not evade a new, perplexing question, one that would not translate cleanly into any tidy rule of prudence.

By what means was that possibility to be avoided?

There were two ways. Which of them would truly fulfil her resolve?

She was no longer as sure as she had been a year before that his career was all in all, to be guarded like a fragile dish from any jolt of feeling. The question returned again and again. She took it with her when she went out on the hillside with his letters in her hand. The wind off the Atlantic combed the grass with a long whisper; birds sounded and vanished; the old land kept its own counsel in its own tongue. She pondered and puzzled, and did not come an inch nearer to an answer.

Even her violin failed her in this strait. The instrument could still make music, but not the one she needed—the tune that would name, in any language, what she owed to herself and what she owed to another, and how much of the truth could ever be spoken without turning it into something smaller than it was.

May had come up the Nile the way a good cook brings a pot to the simmer—slow at first, then all at once the air was full of a steady heat and a clean, pale light. Outside the mess-room at Wadi Halfa three officers had drawn their chairs onto a knoll of coarse grass above the water. The moon, full and merciless as a polished platter held too near the fire, washed the sky until even the planets looked scoured of colour. Below them the river moved with the lazy thickness of oil, shining like a mirror newly burnished; across it the desert lay spread—white as sifted flour, jeweled here and there with stones that caught the light like salt crystals.

They smoked without speaking, letting the night speak for itself: bull-frogs throbbing from an island midstream, the faint hiss of the river against the bank, the occasional far bark of a camel. Under all of it, deeper than any human talk, lay that familiar eastern hush—vast, patient, and listening—broken only by the small, living noises that kept a man sane.

Colonel Dawson struck a match. The flare lit his face—drawn, worried, as though he had been living too long on bad rations and worse news.

“I hope no harm has come to him,” he said, flicking the match away. “I wish I could say I believed it.”

Lieutenant Calder, young enough to have unlined cheeks despite the grey that the Soudan painted into men’s hair, tamped tobacco into his pipe with a thumb that moved carefully, like someone shaping dough.

“He left Halfa eight weeks ago,” he said.

“Eight weeks to-day,” Dawson answered.

Major Walters of the Army Service Corps—long-necked, spare, with the cheerfulness of a man who could find a joke in a sandstorm—let the cigar smoke drift from his lips.

“It’s early yet to decide Durrance has got scuppered,” he said. “Give him a fire, a couple of sheiks, and some coffee thick as mud, and he’ll sit all night swapping stories, happy as a man at his own hearth. Here there are offices and papers and a desk—everything he hates and can’t stomach. You’ll see. He’ll turn up. He just won’t hurry about it.”

“He is three weeks overdue,” Dawson said, tugging at his moustache as if he could pull certainty out of it. “And he’s methodical—after a fashion.”

Walters gestured across the water to the empty, glittering waste.

“If he’d gone west, I’d agree. But he went east—mountain country towards Berenice and the Red Sea. Those tribes have been quiet even when Osman Digna was at Suakin.”

Dawson’s worry did not soften. “He is three weeks overdue,” he repeated, as though the words themselves might summon a man out of the dunes.

Calder knocked the ash from his pipe, refilled, and leaned forward, elbows on knees. His voice came slowly, weighed out like spice.

“I’ve wondered whether a trap was laid for him. I never spoke of what I knew, because until lately I didn’t see any link. But now… I begin to wonder. You remember the night before he started?”

Dawson shifted his chair nearer. “Yes.”

Walters nodded. “Durrance dined with us. Went away early to pack, didn’t he?”

“His packing was done,” Calder said. “He went early—but not to his quarters. He walked down the river-bank to Tewfikieh.”

Tewfikieh: a mile north, a frontier town with Greek stores and dirty cafés and native cook-shops where oil and onions and strong coffee fought for mastery of the air; where dialects collided—Delta fellah against Dinka negro—until the very street seemed stitched from many tongues. And yet for all the shouting, European ears always noticed what was missing: the ring of footsteps. People walked on sand, bare feet whispering; and when a lull came, the crowd drifted like ghosts, and behind their noise you could hear the desert’s old, brooding silence, as if the earth itself refused to translate what men were saying.

“He went at ten,” Calder continued. “I went to his quarters at eleven. He hadn’t returned. He was to ride east at four, and there was a detail I wanted to settle. So I waited. He came in about a quarter of an hour later—restless, quick-spoken, like a man with a hot coal under his tongue. He told me I must be quick because he expected a visitor. He barely listened. One moment he seemed angry, the next he laughed—as though, in spite of himself, he was pleased. Then he hurried me out, and I heard him tell his servant to go to bed: he would admit the visitor himself.”

Dawson’s eyes narrowed. “Who was the visitor?”

“I don’t know. But when his servant went in at four to wake him, Durrance was still sitting on the verandah, as though waiting. The visitor had not come.”

“And he left no message?” Dawson asked.

“No,” Calder said. “I was up before he started. He looked worried, puzzled—like a man trying to name a taste he knows he has had before, but cannot place. I thought he meant to tell me. Even after he was mounted and the camel rose, he turned and looked down at me. Then he decided against it. He struck the camel and rode past the post-office into the desert with his head sunk on his breast.” Calder’s gaze fixed on the river as if it might show him the answer. “Who was this person he met in Tewfikieh? Who comes so secretly to Halfa? Whatever it was, it stirred him. And then—nothing. Was it a lure? Like the colonel, I am afraid.”

Silence fell, filled only by frogs and the thin crackle of tobacco. Walters rose at last, stretching.

“I still don’t think Durrance has got scuppered,” he said, stubbornly cheerful.

“I know what I’ll do,” Dawson decided. “Send a strong search party in the morning.”

So in the morning, while the Nile flashed hard light between the verandah posts and the breakfast smells rose—toast dry as gunpowder, bacon, coffee too long boiled—Dawson began describing the men and supplies he meant to send. Walters, however, had turned Calder’s story over through the night the way a cook turns a question over a slow flame.

“Did you ever find out who Durrance talked to at Tewfikieh?” Walters asked.

“I tried,” Calder said. He sat with his back to the Nile, facing the glass doors. “And there’s a point that puzzles me. I couldn’t learn he spoke to more than one person—and that one couldn’t likely be the visitor he expected. He stopped by a café where strolling musicians were playing for their lodging. One of them—a Greek, they said—came out with his hat. Durrance threw a sovereign in. The man thanked him, and they spoke a little.”

As Calder reached that point, his head lifted sharply. Something in his face changed—recognition first, then confusion, as though a familiar melody had been played in the wrong key. His hands gripped the table-edge; his feet drew back under his chair as if he might spring up. But he didn’t. He glanced around; Dawson was pouring cocoa, Walters looking down at his plate. No one had noticed.

Calder leaned back, eyes staring past Walters’s shoulder into the mess-room. “But I never heard that he spoke to anyone else. He seemed merely to stroll through the village and back.”

“That doesn’t help,” Walters said.

“And that’s all?” Dawson asked.

“No,” Calder replied, slowly. “I know, for instance, that the man we are talking about is staring me straight in the face.”

Every head turned.

In the doorway stood Durrance—dust still powdered into the seams of his clothes, his face burned to red brick by sun and wind. He listened with a strained, peculiar intentness, as though he were trying to taste words rather than hear them.

“Durrance!” Dawson cried, springing up. “When did you get back?”

“When did you come in?” Walters added.

Durrance stepped down into the verandah. It was then that Calder—who did not rise, did not greet him—felt his confusion settle into a quick, troubled comprehension. Something was wrong; not a mere illness, not fatigue. A wrongness in the way Durrance carried his head, the way his eyes rested without fastening.

“We expected you three weeks ago,” Dawson said, dragging a chair out.

“The delay couldn’t be helped,” Durrance replied, and sat.

“Does my story account for it?” Calder asked.

“Not a bit,” Durrance said with a laugh too quick to be easy. “It was the Greek musician I expected that night. I wanted to know what bad luck had thrown him up to scrape a zither for his bed in Tewfikieh. That’s all.” His voice softened, dragged slightly at the end, as if the last words hurt. “Yes. That was all.”

Dawson bustled, grateful for the ordinary. “You’re forgetting your breakfast. What will you have?”

Durrance looked around the table—looked, yet did not quite look—and then called the waiter.

“Moussa—get me something cold.”

Calder watched with a steadiness that missed nothing. Durrance did not reach for bread or dish the way a hungry traveller would. He kept his hands close, as if the table might shift under him.

“There’s tea, cocoa, coffee,” Calder said. “Help yourself.”

“Thanks,” Durrance replied. “I see. But have Moussa bring me a brandy-and-soda, I think.”

When the glass came, Durrance did not turn to it; his arm went out over his shoulder, palm waiting, until Moussa placed the tumbler safely into it. Calder’s mouth tightened in a faint nod: a difficulty cleverly avoided, yes—but a difficulty all the same.

Durrance ate, drank, and talked. He said he had travelled farther east than he meant; that the Ababdeh were quiet among their mountains—unwilling to swear themselves to Egypt, but paying no tribute to the Mahdi. The weather was good; ibex and antelope plentiful; he had reason to be content.

Calder listened and disbelieved every word. Not because Durrance lied poorly—he lied excellently—but because Calder had begun to hear what was missing: the easy ring of truth, the natural cadence. Durrance’s cheer was like store-bought sweetness poured over stale bread.

The officers drifted away to duty. Calder remained. Durrance lingered over his food as if delay were a wall he could hide behind. He asked endless questions about Halfa, answered his own, returned again to his journey. Finally he stopped with abrupt suspicion.

“You’re taking life easily this morning,” he said.

“I haven’t eight weeks’ arrears of letters to clear,” Calder returned lightly.

Durrance’s face clouded, his forehead contracted. “True. I’d forgotten my letters.”

He rose, climbed the steps, and went into the mess-room. Calder sprang up at once and watched. Durrance took the key of his office from the nail by the glass doors, crossed to the farther door, and went out, leaving it open. Calder saw him pass down the path between bushes in the tiny garden, unlatch the gate, and cross the sand to his office.

Only when he disappeared did Calder sit again, elbows on the table, face between his hands. He knew there were letters waiting in a woman’s hand. He knew, too, that Durrance had aged. Furrows had bitten into his mouth; his hair was dulled by something more than dust. The calamity was not in the desert—it was in him.

When at last Calder rose, he took his helmet and crossed the sand. He lifted the latch noiselessly, opened the door, and looked in.

Durrance sat at his desk with his head bowed on his arms. The letters lay unopened at his side, like a meal grown cold because the eater has lost all hunger.

Calder stepped inside and closed the door loudly.

Durrance turned his face toward the sound. “Well?” he said.

“I’ve a paper, Colonel, needing your signature,” Calder answered. “Authority for the alterations in C barracks. You remember?”

“Very well. I’ll look through it and return it signed at lunch. Give it me.”

His hand stretched out towards Calder, palm open, steady—too steady.

Calder removed his pipe from his mouth and, standing fully in Durrance’s view, set the pipe slowly, deliberately, into that outstretched palm.

The hot bowl burned. Durrance snatched his hand back. The pipe fell, snapped on the floor.

Neither spoke for a moment. Then Calder put an arm around Durrance’s shoulder and asked gently, in a voice softened almost to a woman’s:

“How did it happen?”

Durrance covered his face with his hands. The control he had held like a clenched jaw gave way. He did not answer, did not cry out, but his whole body shivered as if a cold wind had found him in the heat.

“How did it happen?” Calder whispered again.

Durrance asked instead, hoarsely, “How did you find out?”

“You stood in the mess-room doorway listening to discover whose voice came from where. When I raised my head and saw you—though your eyes rested on my face—there was no recognition. I suspected then. When you came down the steps I was almost certain. When you wouldn’t help yourself to food, when you put your arm out behind you so Moussa had to place the glass into your hand—I knew.”

“I was a fool to try to hide it,” Durrance said. “I knew all along I couldn’t for more than a few hours. But even those few hours… they seemed a gain.”

“How did it happen?”

“There was a high wind,” Durrance said, forcing the words out as if swallowing grit. “It took my helmet off. Eight in the morning. I wasn’t moving camp that day. I was outside my tent in my shirt-sleeves, no collar to cover the nape of my neck. I ran after the helmet—fool that I was. You’ve seen it: every time you stoop it skips away; every time you run it stops, as if waiting to be caught. Before you know it you’ve gone half a mile. I dropped, they told me, like a log—just when I had it in my hand. I don’t know how long ago. I was ill a time. Afterward it was hard to count, because you can’t tell day from night.”

Blind. The word did not need to be spoken; it filled the room like smoke.

He told the rest: how he made his followers carry him back toward Berber; how, out of a stubborn, human craving to keep his disaster private, he ordered them to silence. Calder listened to the end, then stood quickly.

“There’s a doctor,” he said. “Clever enough, and for a Syrian he knows a great deal. I’ll fetch him privately. We’ll hear what he says. It may be only temporary.”

The Syrian came, smelled of disinfectant and sweat, and spoke with the careful caution of a man handling a knife close to bone. He examined Durrance, pursed his lips, and shook his head.

“Go to Cairo at once,” he advised. “A specialist. I hesitate to pronounce—there is always hope—but this is grave.”

He asked questions. “Any injury to the head? Thrown from a horse? Wounded?”

“No,” Durrance said.

When the Syrian had gone, the room fell silent again. Calder had the sense that any consolation would be false, and that false comfort, if it betrayed his fear, might harm more than truth.

He turned to the pile of letters and sorted through them, fingertips brushing paper as if feeling for pulse.

“There are two letters here,” he said gently, “you might care to hear. They’re in a woman’s hand. Irish postmark. Shall I open them?”

“No,” Durrance exclaimed sharply, and his hand dropped onto Calder’s arm, urgent. “By no means.”

Calder did not put them down. He had his own reasons to want to know the girl behind those pages—Ethne Eustace, of whom Durrance had spoken rarely, and then with odd intensity, saying only her name and that he would be glad enough to change it if he could. Calder looked at Durrance—the man built for sun and long miles, who wore wild places as other men wore coats; the man to whom deserts were home, and hedged fields the strange country—and understood how immense this ruin was. If the girl failed him, what remained?

“You would like to hear them,” Calder insisted. “You’ve been away eight weeks.”

A harsh laugh cut him off.

“Do you know what I was thinking when I stopped you?” Durrance said. “That I’d read them after you’d gone.” His voice trembled, a hair’s breadth. “It takes time to get used to being blind when your eyes have served you all your life. You’ll have to help me answer them, Calder. So read them. Please.”

Calder tore open the envelopes. As he read, the cramped office filled with another world: Donegal’s small doings spoken plainly—who was ill, whose cow had calved, whose roof had leaked, who had made a joke sharp enough to sting and kind enough to mend. The words carried turf-smoke and damp stone, the rhythm of a village where old speech still held meanings that no official English could properly carry. It was as if, through her simple sentences, she fought back against the slow eroding of memory—holding the local names, the turns of phrase, the way of seeing, as carefully as a food historian preserves a recipe before it dies with its last speaker.

And beneath the everyday, a warmth for Durrance ran like an undercurrent: not proclaimed, but present in every choice of detail, every small confidence, as if his life, far away and harsh, mattered to her more than she would say.

When Calder finished, he looked up. Relief had softened his face. Durrance, too, seemed to breathe more freely.

“After all,” Durrance cried, seizing at brightness like a man grabbing a cup in the dark, “one has something to be thankful for. Think—suppose I’d been engaged to her! She would never have let me break it off once I’d gone blind. What an escape!”

“An escape?” Calder repeated, startled.

“You don’t understand.” Durrance’s voice hardened again, defensive, self-mocking. “I knew a man who went blind. A good fellow—before. Mind that. But a year after you wouldn’t have known him. He narrowed into the most selfish, exacting creature imaginable. I don’t wonder. I hardly see how he could help it. I don’t blame him. But it wouldn’t make life easier for a wife, would it? A helpless husband who can’t cross a road without her at his elbow is bad enough. But make him a selfish beast as well—full of questions, jealous of her power to go where she will, curious about every person she speaks to—what then?” His breath caught. “My God, I’m glad she refused me. For that I’m most grateful.”

“She refused you?” Calder asked, and his relief slipped away.

“Twice,” Durrance said. “What an escape. You see, Calder, I shall be more trouble even than that man. I’m not clever. I can’t sit in a chair and amuse myself with thinking—not having any intellect to buck about. I’ve lived out of doors and hard, and that’s the only life that suits me. In a year’s time you won’t be very anxious for much of my society,” and he laughed again—harsh, like a blade struck against stone.

“Oh, stop that,” Calder said. “I’ll read the rest of your letters.”

He did—though his mind kept circling back to the Donegal letters, to what was said and what could not be said, to how the truest things often hid in the untranslatable corners of a native tongue. Girls refused men for reasons that made no sense until years later; refused because of pride, fear, old obligations, words that meant one thing in one mouth and another in the next.

When at last the last envelope was finished, Durrance sat very still, hands resting on the desk as if learning its edges.

“I must answer the letters from Ireland,” he said quietly. “The rest can wait.”

Ethne stood at the open drawing-room window in Hill Street as though the pane were a listening board set to her cheek. London in June glittered below—green flare of the Park at the street’s end, the pale lacquer of carriage roofs, flower-boxes ruffled like garnishes in the opposite windows. Yet it was not colour she was waiting on. It was sound.

Mrs. Adair sat by the tea-table with the small domestic ceremony arranged to look effortless: cups warmed, spoons aligned, the thin slice of lemon floating like a coin in its saucer. The room held that faint, comforting fragrance of black tea just steeped—tannin and citrus—overlaid by the city’s summer breath coming in through the open sash: dust, horse, crushed leaves, a distant whiff of roasting chestnuts from some street brazier. Both women kept their faces turned, not toward one another, but toward the street’s acoustic weather, as if any particular jingle might be a fate.

At half-past five the sound they wanted rose at last—bells, quick and bright, the clipped trot of a cab-horse. Mrs. Adair’s head lifted as sharply as a kettle when it begins to sing.

“That’s a cab,” she said.

“Yes.” Ethne leaned forward, fingers on the sill. For an instant the jingle swelled, then slid past, fading as the wheels went on without pausing.

“But it’s not stopping here,” Ethne murmured, and she could not keep the small loosening in her breath from showing.

Mrs. Adair’s glance went to the clock and then to Ethne, measuring her. “Colonel Durrance is late.”

Ethne did not answer at once. She listened again, as if the street might correct itself and send back the right sound. Mrs. Adair watched her longer than friendship strictly allowed.

“I wonder why you didn’t go to the station and meet Colonel Durrance?” Mrs. Adair asked at last, slowly, as one turns a knife in a joint to find the seam.

Ethne replied without hesitation, too promptly to be entirely natural. “He might think I came because I looked upon him as helpless. I don’t wish him to think that. He has his servant with him.”

She looked out again. More than once she moved as if speech pressed at her, then swallowed it down like a too-hot mouthful. At last she turned from the window with decision that had the air of being practiced.

“You remember the telegram I showed you?”

“From Lieutenant Calder—saying Colonel Durrance had gone blind?”

“Yes. I want you to promise never to mention it. I don’t want him to know I ever received it.”

Mrs. Adair disliked puzzles as she disliked over-steeped tea—bitter and lingering. She had seen the telegram and had been given only the fact of an engagement, as though it had been long settled, as though nothing in it had been sudden, or merciful, or frightened. She had been permitted her mistaken ease, and now that ease was being barred.

“You will promise?” Ethne insisted.

“Certainly, my dear, if you like,” Mrs. Adair returned, with an ungracious lift of the shoulders. “But there’s a reason, I suppose. I don’t understand why you exact the promise.”

“Two lives must not be spoilt because of me.”

There it was again—that phrase, spoken not as a sentiment but as a rule of the house, like not wasting bread, like skimming fat carefully and saving it for another day. Mrs. Adair, who prided herself on plain speaking, thought she knew what lives Ethne meant. She thought of Ethne’s own youth, her motion, her years ahead; she thought of Durrance’s darkness. She did not think—could not be expected to think—of the third life threading through Ethne’s resolve like an unspoken dialect under a dominant tongue.

“Are you quite sure, Ethne,” Mrs. Adair said, “that the two lives will not be more surely spoilt by this way of yours—the way of marriage? Don’t you think you’ll come to feel Colonel Durrance, in spite of your will, something of a hindrance and a drag? Isn’t it possible he may come to feel that too? I wonder. I very much wonder.”

“No,” Ethne said, decisively. “I shall not feel it, and he must not.”

She let Mrs. Adair keep her misunderstanding. It was easier, perhaps kinder, to allow the older woman her simpler vocabulary for what was happening. Ethne went back to the window and to the street’s music, seeing ahead not pictures but snares: moments when a careless word might betray what she must not betray; pauses in conversation where silence might speak too loudly; the small involuntary turns of the head that disclose hope.

It was just a year since, in this very room, in such sunlight, she had asked Durrance to forget her. The year had been threaded with letters that proved forgetting was not in him. Even the last—three weeks ago from Wadi Halfa—scrawled in a child’s wavering hand because he could no longer place his own words on a line, was proof more certain than all the hopeful pages before it. In that letter he had told her of his blindness and had renounced her; and the renunciation itself was a kind of holding fast.

While she waited now, she understood with a steadiness that made her throat ache that it was she who must do the forgetting, or the imitation of it. If she could not erase, she must at least keep her face from translating it.

No—two lives should not be spoilt because of her.

Durrance by birth had inherited movement: the long roads, the far horizons, the camp-fire’s single red eye in a sea of black silence, sleep under stars with the desert wind cooling the skin, the clean work of government and command. Blindness had taken those from him. Everything that made his days a pride and a pleasure had been struck away. One thing, and only one, remained—herself, and only so long as he could believe she wanted him.

She had set her mind on that belief as one sets a lid tightly on a pot to keep heat from escaping. She must not let it lift.

The cab for which she waited came at last, so quietly into the stream of sound that she missed it. Only when Durrance’s servant rang did the bell at the street-door speak up into the room.

“He has come!” Ethne said, starting as though the servant had touched her shoulder.

She told herself there was no reason to fear. Durrance had never been inquisitive; he had always taken friends as they were offered, without holding them to a bright light. And now—now, surely, it would be easier than ever to hide what must be hidden. Yet despite her reasoning she found herself bracing, not welcoming.

Mrs. Adair slipped from the room, leaving Ethne alone.

Ethne stayed by the window, choosing to meet the change in him without flinching. She expected shock—the obvious helplessness, the groping, the broken confidence. But when Durrance entered, the changes she had rehearsed were notable chiefly by their absence.

His face was worn, and grey had come into his hair, but there was no air of a man stranded. He walked as if the room were mapped in his bones. Memory served him like sight: he moved between chair and table with a soldier’s certainty. Only once or twice did he put out a hand where he expected the back of a chair, touching it with the lightness of habit rather than need.

Ethne drew back into the window recess, suddenly at a loss for ordinary words.

He smiled—at once, without searching—and came straight toward her.

“Ethne,” he said.

“It isn’t true, then,” she cried, startled into the wrong sentence by the ease of his movement. “You have recovered.”

“It is quite true,” he answered, “and I have not recovered. But you moved at the window, and so I knew you were there.”

“How did you know? I made no noise.”

“No. But the window’s open. The noise in the street became suddenly louder. I knew someone in front of it had moved aside. I guessed it was you.”

It was not the greeting of lovers newly pledged, yet it spared them the clumsy pity that neither wanted. They shook hands—no more—and in the simple contact Ethne felt the steadiness of him, the controlled pressure, the old discipline that had not dissolved.

“I will give you some tea,” she said. “Then we can talk.”

“Yes,” Durrance replied gravely. “We must have a talk, mustn’t we?”

But the gravity did not last. He spoke with good humour of his journey back—of stations, porters, the odd indignities of being guided through familiar places. He even laughed at his first days of blindness and the mistakes he had made, as though they were a story told about another man. Ethne found herself breathing more freely. The tea’s fragrance thickened as it stood, rising like reassurance.

Then, as if a current had shifted under the surface, something in his posture brought her apprehension back. He sat a little forward, as a man does when he listens for a thread of sound.

“I wrote to you from Wadi Halfa,” he said. “I don’t know whether you could read the letter.”

“Quite well,” Ethne answered.

“I got a friend of mine to hold the paper and tell me when I was writing on it or merely on the blotting-pad,” he continued, with a short laugh. “Calder—of the Sappers—but you don’t know him.”

He spoke the name quickly, as if it were tossed casually onto the table. Yet Ethne felt it like a dropped knife. Lieutenant Calder. The telegram. Her stomach tightened. In the stillness of Durrance’s face there seemed to be an alertness, a waiting—an ear turned to catch the smallest betrayed knowledge.

Was this a trap? Did he suspect? Had he guessed her letter was pity answering pity? Ethne looked into his face and found no answer there, only that strange concentrated stillness. A year ago she would have known by the flicker of his eyes; now his blindness made him unreadable, as though the familiar language between them had died and she must speak in careful translation.

“I could read the letter without difficulty,” she said gently. “It was the letter you would have written. But I had written to you before, and of course your bad news could make no difference. I take back no word of what I wrote.”

Durrance leaned forward, hands on his knees. Ethne waited, unsure whether she had satisfied him or sharpened him. She felt how different this was now: the blind man did not look at her, but he listened with an accuracy that made looking seem a blunt instrument.

“Yes,” he said at length. “I know you. You would take nothing back. But there is my point of view.”

“Yes?” Ethne replied, striving for unconcern. “Will you tell me it?”

He nodded and began as a man who has arranged his thoughts in order, as one might set ingredients out before beginning a difficult dish: measured, spare, lucid.

“I know what blindness means to all men—a growing, narrowing egotism unless one is perpetually on one’s guard. And will one be perpetually on one’s guard? Blindness means that to all men,” he repeated, as though stating a fact of nature.

“But it must mean more to me, who am deprived of every occupation. If I were a writer, I could still dictate. If I were a business man, I could conduct my business. But I am a soldier—and not a clever soldier. Jealousy, a continual and irritable curiosity—there is no Paul Pry like your blind man—a querulous claim upon your attention—these are my special dangers.”

Ethne gave a soft laugh, as if to push the list away from them both.

“Well, perhaps one may hold them off,” he conceded. “But they are to be considered. I have considered them. I am not speaking to you without thought. I have pondered and puzzled over the whole matter night after night since I got your letter—wondering what I should do. You know how gladly, with what gratitude, I would have answered you, ‘Yes, let the marriage go on,’ if I dared. If I dared!”

He paused, then went on with an odd calm that made each word heavier.

“I think—don’t you?—that a great trouble rather clears one’s wits. I used to lie awake at Cairo and think; and the unimportant trivial considerations gradually dropped away; and a few straight and simple truths stood out rather vividly. One felt that one had to cling to them with all one’s might, because nothing else was left.”

“Yes,” Ethne said in a low voice. “That I do understand.”

She did. She had known that winnowing of thought, the stripping down to essentials until only a few unyielding facts remain—facts you cannot soften with prettier words. For the first time, looking at him—or rather, being near him and hearing him—she understood they had entered the same country of mind, though by different roads.

Two lives shall not be spoilt because of me.

“Well?” she asked.

“Well—here’s one of the straight and simple truths. Marriage between a man crippled like myself, whose life is done, and a woman like you, active and young, whose life is in its flower, would be quite wrong unless each brought to it much more than friendship. It would be quite wrong if it implied a sacrifice for you.”

“It implies no sacrifice,” Ethne answered firmly.

He nodded, and she felt—almost with physical certainty—that he was listening to her tone more than her words, weighing it as one weighs a coin by its ring. His concentration made her suddenly aware that what she had imagined as easier might be harder: blindness had sharpened him. The city’s soundscape, which most people endured as a blur, had become for him a script.

“Then do you bring more than friendship?” he asked abruptly. “You will be very honest, I know. Tell me.”

Ethne’s heart knocked once, hard. She could not evade; she must answer at once, clearly, honestly. Yet honesty itself had more than one dialect, and some truths did not translate without harm.

“There is nothing,” she said, steadying her voice, “nothing in the world which I wish for so earnestly as that you and I should marry.”

It was true as she meant it. She knew nothing of plans being shaped elsewhere, nothing of men in grill-rooms speaking of honour and the desert, nothing of letters pulled from mud and ruin on the Nile bank. So far as she knew—and so far as she had resolved—Harry Feversham had gone entirely out of her life. The wish was honest. But it was not the exact answer to his question, and she could only hope again that her tone would stand in for what she could not say.

Durrance seemed content. He did not press.

“Thank you, Ethne,” he said, and took her hand and shook it. His face smiled, as if he had heard what he needed.

No more questions came. Ethne told herself his suspicion, if it had stirred, was quieted—for today.

Mrs. Adair returned then with the discretion of one who times an entrance as carefully as she times the steeping of tea. She greeted Durrance as though his blindness were an irrelevance, as if she had seen him only last week, and her voice had the practised ease of a hostess keeping the room’s harmony in tune.

“I suppose Ethne has told you of our plan,” she said, taking her tea from Ethne’s hand.

“No, not yet,” Ethne replied.

“What plan?” Durrance asked.

“It is all arranged,” Mrs. Adair said. “You will want to go home to Guessens in Devonshire. I am your neighbour—a couple of fields separate us, that’s all. So Ethne will stay with me during the interval before you are married.”

“That’s very kind of you, Mrs. Adair,” Durrance exclaimed. “Because, of course, there will be an interval.”

“A short one, no doubt,” Mrs. Adair said.

“Well—it’s this way,” Durrance replied. “If there’s a chance that I may recover my sight, it would be better that I should seize it at once. Time means a good deal in these cases.”

“Then there is a chance?” Ethne cried, and could not keep the sudden lift in her voice.

“I am going to see a specialist here tomorrow,” he answered. “And, of course, there’s the oculist at Wiesbaden. But it may not be necessary to go so far. I expect I shall be able to stay at Guessens and come up to London when it is necessary.” He turned slightly toward Mrs. Adair. “Thank you very much. It is a good plan.”

He added more slowly, as though tasting the words for accuracy, “From my point of view there could be no better.”

Later Ethne stood again at the open window and watched—or listened as much as watched—Durrance’s cab take him away with his servant toward St. James’s Street, toward the rooms that had belonged to his old life. The city below had changed only in its rhythm: now the carriages were returning from the Park, wheels and hoofs sewing a steady seam of sound along the road.

Ethne remained where she was, in much the same attitude as before he came, but with a different weight in her thoughts. Her fear had not vanished; it had merely taken a clearer shape.

She believed she had quieted whatever suspicion he carried—today, at least. She did not know what had been said between Durrance and Calder in Cairo. She did not know that Durrance believed there was no cure, that the talk of specialists might be only a pretext, a convenient language for other motives. Yet uneasiness held her as firmly as her vow.

Durrance had grown more acute. Not only had his senses sharpened—as any blind man’s might, tuned to shifts of air and the smallest changes in sound—but trouble and thought had sharpened his mind too, making it more penetrating. He could no longer read faces, yet he seemed to read intention in the tiniest tremor of a voice, in the way street-noise swelled when a body moved aside from an open window.

She felt she was entering upon an encounter of wits, and feared she would be worsted.

“Two lives shall not be spoilt because of me,” she repeated, but now it was less a resolve than a prayer—because one truth, at least, had become plain: Durrance saw ever so much more clearly now that he was blind.

July wore itself out into August, and the days at Mrs. Adair’s house by the Salcombe creek went on with that deceptive quiet that belongs to places where water keeps time more faithfully than clocks. The estuary had its own kitchen-music: the sip of tide against mud, the dry whisk of reeds, the occasional click of a halyard on a mast down-creek, a sound like a spoon tapping porcelain. Even indoors, with the windows thrown wide, you could tell whether the breeze came off the sea or over the fields by what it carried—salt that sharpened the nostrils, or warm hay and crushed leaf that softened the mind.

Ethne stood at an open window in the sunlight, her hands resting on the sill as if it were a board on which she might steady herself. Behind her Mrs. Adair sat with a book on her lap, her posture composed, her stillness so complete that it seemed another sort of listening.

“I am afraid,” Ethne said.

Mrs. Adair’s mouth curved, the smallest movement, as though the line on the page had pleased her. “Of what? That some accident happened to Colonel Durrance yesterday in London?”

“No. Not of that.” Ethne spoke slowly, as if tasting each word to be sure it did not betray her. “For he is at this moment crossing the lawn towards us.”

Mrs. Adair did not lift her head. “I thought so,” she murmured, so low that it was almost a sound meant only for herself.

Ethne watched the figure moving over the grass, and the fear that had no proper name rose again. “Do you think he has any hope whatever that he will recover his sight?”

The question seemed to startle Mrs. Adair more than any mention of danger ever could. She considered it with the air of one weighing a new ingredient. “Would he travel up to town so often to see his oculist if he had none? Of course he hopes.”

“I am afraid,” Ethne said again, and turned sharply, the words no longer general but pointed like a pin. “Haven’t you noticed how quick he has grown—and is growing? Quick to interpret your silences, to infer what you do not say from what you do, to fill out your sentences, to make your movements the commentary of your words? Laura, haven’t you noticed? At times I think the very corners of my mind are revealed to him. He reads me like a child’s lesson book.”

Mrs. Adair’s voice was smooth. “Yes. You are at a disadvantage. You no longer have your face to screen your thoughts.”

“And his eyes no longer tell me anything at all,” Ethne added, and in the cool simplicity of the sentence was the whole reversal of their power. Where once Durrance had been bound to what her visible honesty offered him, now he was free—free to listen, to weigh, to catch the smallest shift in breath as though it were a spoken confession. And she, who had never doubted what he meant, often now stood in darkness before him.

Mrs. Adair’s interest sharpened. “There is something, then, to be concealed from him?” she asked, with that quietness that made a question feel like the turning of a key.

“Yes.”

“Something rather important?”

“Something which at all costs I must conceal,” Ethne exclaimed; and even as she said it, dread ran through her that he had already found it out.

She stepped out on to the stone-flagged terrace. The lawn fell away to the hedge; beyond it the grass fields undulated, and beyond them, among trees, the smoke from Colonel Durrance’s chimneys rose in a thin, domestic thread. To the left the creek lay framed by beeches and oaks, a gap cut in the fringe so that sunlight on water flashed like a knife’s edge, and a small sailing-boat down the creek worked slowly against the light wind.

Ethne hesitated, as if mustering her courage the way a cook gathers her ingredients before a difficult dish—choosing, measuring, discarding what might spoil the whole. Then she descended the broad shallow steps to the lawn and walked out to meet Durrance.

If Ethne moved as though toward an ordeal, Durrance came steadily, without a pause. His stick switched at the grass blades—not searching, not groping, but making a soft, habitual rhythm, as though his feet remembered the path and his hand merely kept company with them. There was something almost intolerable in the ease with which he approached: the calm of a man who had learned to replace sight with certainty.

Behind the curtain, Mrs. Adair let the book slip from her hands. She ran to the window and hid herself in the drapery’s fold, watching with a smile that did not warm her eyes. In them a fierce brightness kindled, and her face drew tight with a hunger that had nothing to do with food.

“Something which at all costs she must conceal,” she whispered, exulting. She had contempt, too, for Ethne—the girl of open air and open roads, now afraid of a blind man’s claim, shrinking from a marriage that would bind her freedoms by compassion and obligation. Mrs. Adair was fiercely glad of Durrance’s blindness. If her opportunity came—and each day seemed to promise it more—his dependence would be a possession no rival could contest. She watched Ethne and Durrance meet, turn, and walk side by side toward the creek. Ethne’s hands moved once, as if pleading, and Mrs. Adair’s whole being leaned toward the imagined sound.

Ethne was pleading.

“You saw your oculist yesterday?” she asked quickly when they met. “What did he say?”

Durrance shrugged. “That one must wait. Only time can show whether a cure is possible or not.”

Ethne bent forward, scrutinising his face as if she distrusted what it could no longer show her. “But must you and I wait?” she asked.

“Surely,” he replied. “It would be wiser on all counts.”

Then, with a suddenness that made her heart jump, he asked, “It was Mrs. Adair, I imagine, who proposed this plan—that I should come home to Guessens and that you should stay with her here across the fields?”

Ethne did not see why he asked, but she answered at once. “I was in great distress when I heard of your accident. So distressed that at the first I could not think what to do. I came to London and told Laura, since she is my friend, and this was her plan. Of course I welcomed it with all my heart.” The pleading note rang again; she was asking him to accept her sincerity as he used to accept the look in her eyes.

He turned his face toward her and smiled gently. “I know that very well, Ethne.”

Relief loosened her breath.

“It was kind of Mrs. Adair,” he resumed, “but rather hard on you, who would like to be back in your own country. I remember very well a sentence which Harry Feversham—”

He spoke the name carelessly, as if it were merely a name, and paused a fraction after it, listening. Ethne felt the pause like a hand hovering near a bruise. She would not move; she would not flinch. Not even a tightening of her fingers on her skirt.

“A sentence which Harry Feversham spoke a long while since,” Durrance continued, “in London, just before I left for Egypt. He was speaking of you, and he said: ‘She is of her country and more of her county. I do not think she could be happy in any place which was not within reach of Donegal.’ When I remember that, it seems selfish that I should claim to keep you here at so much cost to you.”

“I was not thinking of that,” Ethne exclaimed, “when I asked why we must wait. That makes me out most selfish. I was merely wondering why you preferred to wait—why you insist upon it. For, of course, although one hopes and prays with all one’s soul that you will get your sight back, the fact of a cure can make no difference.” She spoke with deliberate slowness, as though laying each word down carefully, like slices on a platter that must not topple. “It can make no difference.”

Durrance started, as if roused from a deep, inward listening. “I beg your pardon, Ethne. I was thinking at the moment of Harry Feversham. There is something which I want you to tell me. You said a long time ago at Glenalla that you might one day bring yourself to tell it me—and I should rather like to know now. You see, Harry Feversham was my friend. I want you to tell me what happened that night at Lennon House to break off your engagement—to send him away an outcast.”

Ethne’s silence was long enough for the creek’s small sounds to fill it: a distant gull, the low shush of water in reeds. At last she said gently, “I would rather not. It is all over and done with. I don’t want you to ask me ever.”

He did not press her. “Very well,” he said cheerily. “I won’t ask you. It might hurt you to answer, and I don’t want, of course, to cause you pain.”

“It’s not on that account that I wish to say nothing,” Ethne answered quickly, earnestly. She paused, choosing words as one chooses the right salt—not too much, not too little, for either would ruin. “It isn’t that I am afraid of any pain. But what took place, took place such a long while ago—I look upon Mr. Feversham as a man whom one has known well, and who is now dead.”

They were nearing the opening in the trees that showed the water bright beyond. Ethne lifted her eyes and saw that the little sailing-boat she had watched earlier had nosed into the shore. The sail had been lowered; the short mast stood up above the bank. A man had climbed out and stood uncertainly, staring toward the house as if the place refused to match his expectations.

“A stranger has landed from the creek,” she said. “He looks as if he had lost his way. I will go on and put him right.”

She ran forward, seizing on the interruption as on a sudden reprieve—like a witness in court when the judge rises for luncheon and the cross-examination stops mid-sentence. For each talk with Durrance left her feeling she had been questioned with a skill she could not fully trace, though she had suspected from the first that it was there.

The stranger advanced to meet her. He was of middle size, with a short snub nose, protruding brown eyes that stared vacantly, and a moustache of aggressive shape. He lifted his hat and showed a round forehead already going bald.

“I have sailed down from Kingsbridge,” he said, “but I have never been in this part of the world before. Can you tell me if this house is called The Pool?”

“Yes; you will find Mrs. Adair if you go up the steps on to the terrace,” Ethne replied.

“I came to see Miss Eustace.”

Ethne stared. “I am Miss Eustace.”

He looked at her in silence, his eyes taking their time. “So I thought.” He twirled one moustache, then the other. “I have had some trouble to find you, Miss Eustace. I went all the way to Glenalla—for nothing. Rather hard on a man whose leave is short!”

“I am very sorry,” Ethne said, forcing a smile, “but why have you been put to this trouble?”

Again he played with his moustache, as if winding himself up to speech. “You have forgotten my name, no doubt, by this time.”

“I do not think that I have ever heard it,” she answered.

“Oh, yes, you have, believe me. You heard it five years ago. I am Captain Willoughby.”

The name struck like a slap. Ethne drew sharply back; the colour dropped from her cheeks. Her lips set; her eyes hardened into a glare she could not soften even if she would.

Captain Willoughby was not discomposed. If anything, he spoke like a man indulgent of another’s rudeness. “I can quite understand that you do not welcome me, Miss Eustace, but none of us could foresee that you would be present when the three white feathers came into Feversham’s hands.”

Ethne swept the explanation away. “How do you know that I was present?” she demanded.

“Feversham told me.”

“You have seen him?” The cry leaped from her before she could hold it back, pure as a heartbeat made audible. It startled her as much as it surprised Willoughby. She had trained herself to omit Harry Feversham—out of thought, out of tenderness, out of the daily language of her life—yet the name, touched by the fact of his living speech, broke through every discipline.

“You have actually seen him?” she repeated, wonder shaking her voice. For a moment she envied this dull man his privilege. “You have spoken to him? And he to you? When?”

“A year ago, at Suakin. Else why should I be here?”

The answer came as a shock. Ethne could not yet find the shape of the reason, but she feared it in advance.

“Yes,” she said slowly, almost against her will. “After all—why are you here?”

Willoughby took a letter-case from his breast pocket and opened it with deliberate care. From one compartment he shook out into his palm a tiny white feather—soiled, limp with age and handling. He held it toward her.

“I have come to give you this.”

Ethne did not take it. She shrank as if it might stain her skin. “Why?” she asked, unsteady.

“Three white feathers, three separate accusations of cowardice, were sent to Feversham by three separate men. This is actually one of those feathers which were forwarded from his lodgings to Ramelton five years ago. I am one of the three men who sent them. I have come to tell you that I withdraw my accusation. I take my feather back.”

“And you bring it to me?”

“He asked me to.”

Ethne took the feather at last. It was almost weightless—nothing, a scrap from a bird—yet it filled her palm like a stone. The garden and trees seemed to tilt and turn; the sunlight on the creek went dizzy. She was aware of Willoughby speaking, but his voice had thinned and receded as if she stood underwater and he called to her from a far bank. A coldness spread through her despite the August warmth.

But Willoughby was one of the three. She would not show weakness before any of them. Pride steadied her as surely as a hand on a shoulder. With an effort she mastered herself, on the very brink of faintness.

“Come,” she said. “I will hear your story. Your news was rather a shock to me. Even now I do not quite understand.”

She led him away from the open space into a small plot of grass above the creek, enclosed on three sides by thick hedges. Tall elms and poplars rose behind; before them the water flashed and broke into ripples, and beyond it the opposite trees climbed toward sloping meadows. A gap in the hedge served as an entrance; a garden seat stood at the centre, sun-warmed wood, the faint smell of sap and old varnish rising when one sat.

“Now,” Ethne said, motioning him to sit beside her. “You will take your time, perhaps. You will forget nothing. Even his words, if you remember them! I shall thank you for his words.”

She held the feather clenched tight. Somehow, impossibly, Harry Feversham had redeemed his honour. Somehow she had been unjust. She was to learn how. The knowledge that she had been unjust should, later, call remorse; but not now. For the moment it was too great a happiness to allow any bitterness. She opened her hand and stared at the feather as though it were a relic and an indictment both.

Memories she had kept under lock rose with the speed of a tide that has turned: the spring in Dublin five years ago, the season before Ramelton received those feathers, the sound of voices speaking with the authority of a dominant tongue, naming cowardice as if the word itself were a sentence that could not be appealed. She had tried to make Harry Feversham “dead” in her mind by refusing him a place in her daily speech, by denying him the simple human grammar of presence—yet here was a proof that language could not do everything it claimed. There were experiences for which her carefully chosen words had been only a poor translation.

Willoughby began his story, and almost at once the Devonshire scene dissolved.
