This coast—Plymouth and Dartmouth and Brixham and the Start—was a country where red cliffs carried the memory of dead centuries, where even a quiet creek seemed haunted by the long-ago ring of shipwrights’ hammers and the brave departures of small barques on the Spanish Main. But Ethne had no mind for those echoes. The meadow-sweet air and salt tang were pushed aside by the scent of another world she had learned through Durrance’s confidences: dry dust, sun-scorched stone, the thin breath of desert night.

Before her inner sight—more vivid than the creek’s glitter—stood stunted mimosas, the yellow spread of sand like a sea without water, and sharp black rocks rising pyramidal and abrupt. It was part of the irony of her life that she could so much more completely imagine what one lover had endured because the other lover had given her the details.

And as Captain Willoughby spoke on, the quiet domestic creek with its gentle, healing sounds was only a shifting mist at the edge of her hearing.

“I will not interrupt you,” Ethne said, and Captain Willoughby—settling beside her on the bench where the hedge made a green alcove against the lawn—had scarcely begun before she broke her vow as naturally as one breaks bread.

“I am Deputy-Governor of Suakin,” he started.

“How did he look?” she cut in at once, as if the name had been a spice thrown on the coals and the smoke had stung her eyes.

Willoughby’s brows pinched together. He stared, hunting for a detail as a man hunts for a lost coin. “Really, I do not know. Much like other men, I suppose, who have been a year or two in the Soudan—overtrained a little, that sort of thing.”

Never mind, she told herself—and the disappointment tasted like cold tea. For five years she had lived on scraps: a rumour, a name half-mispronounced, the silence between letters. She wanted the smallest homely particulars—whether the sun had bitten his skin darker, whether his mouth still set itself in that old obstinate line, whether his hands, which once had been idle at a dance, had grown rough with work. But she glanced at Willoughby’s honest, obtuse face and knew she would get none of it.

“I beg your pardon,” she said evenly. “Go on.”

“I was sitting in the verandah on the first floor of the palace about ten o’clock at night,” Willoughby resumed, “looking out over the harbour and the distillation works, and wondering whether it was worth while to go to bed at all. You are fortunate enough not to know Suakin in May, Miss Eustace. No white woman can live in that town. It has a sodden, intolerable heat peculiar to itself. The air is heavy with brine; you can’t sleep for its oppression. Well—then a servant told me that a man, who refused to give his name, wished particularly to see me. The man was Feversham. There was only a lamp burning in the verandah, and the night was dark, so that I did not recognise him until he was close to me.”

Ethne’s fingers tightened around the white feather in her lap. It felt different in daylight than it must have under that lamp at Suakin: here, soft and almost frivolous; there, a small pale thing that might catch the lamplight like a knife-edge. She waited, listening not only to Willoughby’s words but to the garden’s own sound—wind through leaves, a far clink from the house, a thrush’s note dropped cleanly into the hush. The quiet made the story clearer, as though silence were a broth in which meaning steeped.

“I asked him what he wanted,” Willoughby went on, “and why he had not sent in his name. ‘You would not have seen me if I had,’ he replied, and he drew a packet of letters out of his pocket. Those letters had been written a long while ago by General Gordon in Khartum. They had been carried down the Nile as far as Berber. But the day after they reached Berber, that town surrendered to the Mahdists. Abou Fatma, the messenger, hid them in the wall of the house of an Arab called Yusef, who sold rock-salt in the market-place. Abou was thrown into prison on suspicion, and escaped to Suakin. The letters remained hidden in that wall until Feversham recovered them. I looked over them and saw they were of no value, and I asked him bluntly why he—who had not dared to accompany his regiment—had risked death and torture to get them back.”

Ethne did not miss the harshness. She heard it as one hears a dish judged by a palate that has never learnt the taste of hunger. Yet she sat still, because what mattered was not Willoughby’s tone but the chain of acts—step after step, as methodical as kneading, as stubborn as tending a slow fire.

On that hot verandah with the Red Sea breathing salt into the night, Harry Feversham had told his story “quietly and without exaggeration.” Willoughby, who could not paint a man’s face, could at least remember the skeleton of deeds.

Feversham had fallen in with Abou Fatma at Suakin. Together they planned the recovery of the letters. They travelled as far as Obak; then, since Abou dared not go farther, Feversham drove his grey donkey on alone to Berber.

“He did not even conceal an access of panic,” Willoughby said, “when he first saw the low brown walls and the towering date palms beyond. He told me how it loosened his joints—set him running and leaping across the empty desert like a marrowless thing of fear.”

Ethne could supply what Willoughby did not know he was leaving out: how a man might crouch on hot sand with his coat drawn over his head, and in that suffocating darkness summon—by sheer will—some remembered face, some domestic softness, like drawing water from a deep well with a rope that cuts the hands. She could imagine him doing it, because she had done the same, only with different deserts.

“He went down into Berber at the setting of the sun,” Willoughby said.

Ethne breathed out, and relief loosened her mouth into a smile before she could stop it. “He went down into Berber,” she repeated under her breath, as if repeating it kept it true.

“And found the old town destroyed by order of the Emir,” Willoughby continued, “and a new one building on its southern confines. All the landmarks by which he was to know the house where the letters were hidden were gone. The roofs torn off, houses dismantled, front walls carried away—narrow alleys of crumbling fives-courts, crossing this way and that and gaping to the stars. Here and there a broken tower—the remnant of a rich man’s house. But of any sign that could tell a man where the hut of Yusef, once a seller of rock-salt in the market-place, had stood—no hope in those acres of mud. The foxes had already made their burrows there.”

Her smile died; then she looked down at the feather again. It lay dusty, yellowed with desert grit, a ridiculous little thing to carry such weight. And yet because it existed—because it had travelled back—it promised there would be no word of failure. She laughed softly, a laugh of pure relief, as if tasting water after long thirst.

“Go on,” she said.

Willoughby told how Feversham despatched the negro with the donkey back to Abou Fatma at the Wells of Obak.

“He stayed for a fortnight in Berber,” Willoughby said. “A week during which he came every morning to the well and waited for the return of his negro from Obak, and a week during which the negro searched for Yusef. I doubt, Miss Eustace, if you can realise what that fortnight must have meant—the anxiety, the continued expectation that a voice would bid him halt and a hand fall upon his shoulder. And if the hand fell—death would be the least part of his penalty.”

Willoughby tried to sketch the town: low houses, broad streets of sand, pits dug for mud to build again, overhead a blistering, shadowless sky. A crowd all day, jostling and shouting—crowded deliberately so all might be watched and each man become his neighbour’s spy. Feversham buying food at booths and fearing talk; refusing any roof at night because he dared not trust his tongue; sleeping in the ruined old town among roofless courts. He could not even loiter to listen for a name, lest idle men draw him into speech. In daylight he could not prowl the ruins. From sunrise to sunset he must walk quickly up and down the public streets like a man with urgent business.

Ethne, listening, heard more than he said: the constant rasp of sandals on sand, the bark of voices, the far thump of drums, the thin metallic clatter of some new tool in the new town. Even now, in this English garden where leaves whispered properly and birds made music for no pay, the imagined noise of that other place seemed to press on her skull. She thought—bitterly—of what modern towns were becoming: engines and wheels and endless clamour, and how sound itself could be used like a lash. In Berber the noise had been policy, not accident.

Willoughby paused, struggling with his memory. “In front of us was the quiet harbour and the Red Sea; above us the African stars. Feversham spoke in the quietest manner possible, but with a peculiar deliberation—and with his eyes fixed upon my face, as though forcing me to feel with him. Even when he lighted his cigar he did not avert his eyes. By that time I had given him a cigar and offered him a chair. I had, I assure you. It was the first time in four years he had sat with one of his equals—indeed with any of his countrymen—on a footing of equality. He told me so.”

Ethne felt a pang at that: the chair, the cigar, the small ceremonial of fellowship—as intimate, in its way, as being offered bread. How much of a man’s life could be measured by such small permissions.

“Well,” Willoughby went on at last, “after he had skulked for a fortnight, the negro discovered Yusef—no longer selling salt, but tending a small plantation of dhurra on the river’s edge. From Yusef, Feversham obtained particulars enough to guide him to the house where the letters were concealed in the inner wall. But Yusef was no longer to be trusted. Possibly Feversham’s accent betrayed him. More likely Yusef took him for a spy, and thought it wise to confess to Mohammed-el-Kheir, the Emir, his own share in the concealment. That is conjecture. The important fact is this: on the same night Feversham went alone to old Berber.”

“Alone,” Ethne repeated, and the word came out sharper than she intended, like a knife struck on bone.

“Yes. He found the house fronting a narrow alley, sixth of the row. The front wall was destroyed, but the side walls and the back still stood. Three feet from the floor and two feet from the right-hand corner the letters were hidden in the inner wall. Feversham dug into the mud bricks with his knife—made a hole where he could slip his hand. The wall was thick; he dug deep, stopping now and again to feel for the packet. At last his fingers clasped it and drew it out; and as he hid it in a fold of his jibbeh, the light of a lantern shone upon him from behind.”

Ethne started as if the lantern had flared in the hedge-gap before her. She saw it all—the roofless alleys, the dead silence beneath the stars, the distant glow and noise of the new town. Harry with the packet in his hand, the taste of dust in his mouth, the scent of old mud warmed by day’s sun turning sharp and sour in the night air. And then that sudden spear of light.

“Well?” she forced out.

“He was standing with his face to the wall,” Willoughby said. “The light came from the alley behind him. He did not turn, but out of the corner of his eye he could see a fold of a white robe hanging motionless. He secured the packet with a care and composure which astonished him even then. The shock strung him to a concentration he had never known. His fingers trembled, he said—trembled with excitement, not fear; his mind worked rapidly but coolly. He came to a definite conclusion of what he must do. Every faculty was extraordinarily clear and extraordinarily still.

“He had his knife in his hand, faced about suddenly, and ran. Two men were waiting. He ran at the man with the lantern. He was aware of the point of a spear—ducked and beat it aside with his left arm—leaped forward and struck with his right. The Arab fell at his feet; the lantern was extinguished. Feversham sprang across the white-robed body and ran eastward toward the open desert. But not in panic—he had never been so collected. He was followed by the second soldier. He had foreseen it. If he was to escape, it was necessary. He turned a corner, crouched behind a wall, and as the Arab came running by he leaped out upon his shoulders. And again, as he leaped, he struck.”

Willoughby stopped and turned toward Ethne with a puzzled earnestness, as though he had found a spice in a dish he could not name.

“The strangest feature of those few fierce minutes,” he said, “was that Feversham felt no fear. From the first moment when the lantern shone, to the last when he ran through the ruins toward the desert and the Wells of Obak—he felt no fear. I don’t understand that. Do you?”

Ethne looked down at the feather until its barbs blurred. Willoughby, practical and stubborn, could not imagine the kind of terror that lives for years in a man’s blood—not the sharp terror of a spear-point in an alley, but the slow fear of disgrace, the dread of what one might do, of what one might be, of how one’s own name might taste in another man’s mouth. That fear was like a language learned in childhood and never forgotten; it shaped the world, cut its edges, made certain sounds unbearable.

“Yes,” she said, and her voice came out low. “I understand.”

She saw him again in the little room off the hall at Lennon House, speaking of his mother, of his father’s cold gulf, of the way fear had haunted his nights and disfigured the day. She had been too sure of her own words then, too proud of the clean, dominant meanings of them—coward, honour—as though those words translated perfectly from one soul to another. They did not. They never had.

“It was the possibility of cowardice he shrank from,” she said, “not the possibility of hurt.” Her throat tightened. “If only one had been a little older—less narrow. I should have listened. I should have understood. At all events I should not, I think, have been cruel.”

The fourth feather—her own—burned against her palm as though it had a hidden ember.

Willoughby, however, would not bend even here. “Yes, but these fine distinctions are too elusive for practical purposes,” he argued. “You can’t run the world on fine distinctions. I cannot bring myself to believe that we three men were at all to blame—and if we were not, you of all people can have no reason for self-reproach.”

The complacency in him, after what she had just heard, roused anger like a sudden bitter taste. She looked him over, from his clean boots to his stubborn jaw, and saw stamped on him an obstinate stupidity. How dare he sit in judgment on the meanest of his fellows, let alone Harry Feversham? And in the same instant she remembered that she herself had endorsed that judgment. Shame tingled through her blood. She kept her mouth shut, watching him from the corners of her eyes, waiting almost eagerly for him to say anything that would justify a savage retort.

But he said nothing. Ethne broke the silence herself.

“Which of you three first thought of sending the feathers?” she asked.

“Not you?”

“No. I think it was Trench.”

“Trench,” Ethne repeated, striking her clenched hand—feather and all—into the palm of the other. “I will remember that name.”

“But I share his responsibility,” Willoughby said sturdily. “I do not shrink from it. I regret we caused you pain and annoyance, but I do not acknowledge any mistake. I take my feather back now, and I annul my accusation. But that is your doing.”

“Mine?” She turned her head. “What do you mean?”

“A man may live in the Soudan and yet not be wholly ignorant of women,” he said, and smiled as if he were paying her a compliment. “And of their great quality of forgiveness. You gave the feathers back to Feversham in order that he might redeem his honour. That is evident.”

Ethne sprang to her feet before he had finished. She stood very still a little in front of him, her face turned away. In his ignorance he had struck true, not by thought but by chance: he had pointed out a way she might have taken and had not. Remorse, sharp as vinegar, told her it was the way she ought to have taken.

But she would not accept the comfort of that misconception. She had failed him then; she would not steal credit now.

“Repeat what you said,” she asked without turning. “And ever so slowly, please.”

“You gave the feathers back into Feversham’s hand—” Willoughby began.

“He told you that himself?”

“Yes—” Willoughby answered, surprised, and continued, “in order that he might by subsequent bravery compel the men who sent them to take them back, and so redeem his honour.”

“He did not tell you that?”

“No. I guessed it.” Willoughby leaned forward, pleased with his own penetration. “His disgrace was—on the face of it—impossible to retrieve. The opportunity might never have occurred. As it happened, he waited three years in the bazaar at Suakin before it did. No, Miss Eustace, it needed a woman’s faith to conceive that plan—a woman’s encouragement to keep the man who undertook it to his work.”

Ethne laughed and turned back, and for the first time since he arrived Willoughby saw something in her face that made him forget himself. Her pride—strangely—did not harden her; it made her tender, lit her eyes, warmed her mouth, as though the knowledge of another’s courage had fed her like bread.

“Yes,” he cried, carried away, “you were the woman to plan this redemption.”

She laughed again, more happily than he could understand. “Did he tell you of a fourth white feather?”

“No.”

“I shall tell you the truth.” She sat down again, very straight. “The plan was of his devising from first to last. Nor did I encourage him, for until to-day I never heard a word of it. Since the night of that dance in Donegal I have had no message from Mr. Feversham, and no news of him.”

Willoughby’s face altered a little; he had not expected that.

“I told him to take up those three feathers because they were his,” she continued, and each sentence came as if she were placing a dish on the table and naming it plainly, without garnish, because garnish would be a lie. “And I wished to show him that I agreed with the accusations of which they were the symbols. That seems cruel? But I did more. I snapped a fourth white feather from my fan and gave him that to carry away too.”

Her voice thinned on the edge of old pain, but she held it steady.

“I wanted to make an end for ever and ever—not only of my acquaintanceship with him, but of every kindly thought he might keep of me, of every kindly thought I might keep of him. I wanted to be sure myself, and I wanted him to be sure, that we should always be strangers now and—and afterwards,” and the last words fell into a whisper, as if she spoke them in a dialect too private for the air.

Willoughby did not understand. Perhaps only Harry Feversham, and Lieutenant Sutch, would have known what “afterwards” meant in her mouth.

“I was sad and sorry enough when I had done it,” she said. “Indeed, I think I have always been sorry since. I think I have never at any minute during these five years quite forgotten that fourth feather and the quiet dignity with which he took it. But to-day I am glad.”

Her voice, though low, was rich with pride.

“Oh, very glad. For this was his thought—his deed. They are all his, as I would have them be. I had no share, and of that I am proud. He needed no woman’s faith, no woman’s encouragement.”

“Yet he sent this back to you,” Willoughby said, pointing, perplexed, to the feather in her hand.

“Yes,” she answered simply. “He knew that I should be glad to know.”

And suddenly she drew the feather to her breast, pressing it there as though it were not a token but a living thing whose warmth might be returned. For a while she sat so, eyes shining, the garden’s soft sounds gathering around her like a shawl.

Then Willoughby rose abruptly and pointed toward the gap in the hedge by which they had entered the enclosure.

“By Jove! Jack Durrance,” he exclaimed.

Durrance was standing in the gap, which was the only way in or out.

Ethne had forgotten that Colonel Durrance existed at all.

The moment Captain Willoughby pressed the little feather into her palm—once white, now yellowed and soiled with travel and weather—everything else slipped away from her like steam from a lifted lid. Harry Feversham filled the space in her mind with an almost bodily insistence: the angle of his head when he listened, the impatient kindness in his voice, the exact look of his eyes when pride fought fear. Willoughby’s plain account had gaps, and into each gap her memory poured its own broth—richer, hotter, spiced with things she had pretended for five years not to taste.

The sun lay on the garden as on a table laid for a feast: bright nasturtiums spilling colour, roses heavy as jam, the air sugared with late-summer bloom. Yet Ethne was nowhere in that calm. She was back among the raw years she had endured upright, greeting the world with a smile as one serves guests when the pantry is bare—until she lifted her head and saw Durrance standing at the hedge-gap, his stick in his hand, his face turned in that direction which so cruelly imitated sight.

“Hush,” she breathed.

The word came out like the first warning of a kettle just before it boils over. Her face blanched; for a moment she shut her eyes tight, as though pain were a taste she could refuse by closing her mouth. But there was no leisure for weakness. Those minutes with Willoughby had been a holiday—an illicit sweetness. Now she must take up again the daily discipline of her life, the careful management of appearances, the hard economy of expression. If she could not forget—she knew, in that instant, that she never would—then she must do the next best thing: show nothing.

Durrance must go on believing that she brought more than friendship into the marriage she had promised him. He must not learn that Harry Feversham’s disgrace had been met and answered. He must not be given the knowledge that would compel him—because he was Durrance—to do what was “right” at his own expense.

Willoughby shifted on the bench. Ethne caught his sleeve.

“Keep still,” she whispered. “Do you know him?”

“Of course. Three years together at Suakin,” Willoughby murmured, his eyes on Durrance. “I heard he’d gone blind. I’m glad it’s not true.”

“Speak lower.” Her fingers tightened. “It is true. He is blind.”

Willoughby stared. Durrance’s walk was too sure, the easy swing of his body too confident.

“One would never have thought it,” Willoughby breathed. “Consolations seem so—what can I say to him?”

“Say nothing,” Ethne answered, and the firmness of it had the edge of a knife. “Not a word. Not a movement.”

Durrance stood just inside the enclosure, the clipped hedge-wall behind him and the creek glinting beyond. He did not peer about like a blind man. He simply lifted his head, as though the garden’s hush were a map.

“Ethne,” he said—rather than called; and the quiet, unquestioning tone made it worse, made the illusion of sight almost complete.

“It’s impossible,” Willoughby muttered. “He’s looking straight at us.”

“He sees nothing.” Ethne did not take her gaze from him. “But he listens.”

Again he said, louder, with doubt now threading his voice, “Ethne.”

“He isn’t sure,” Ethne whispered. “Do you hear? He mustn’t know you’re here.”

“Why?”

Because you are one of Harry Feversham’s ghosts, she thought, and I have built my life on persuading him there are none. Aloud she said only, “He must not.”

Willoughby did not question further. Something in her stillness commanded him. She sat hunched a little, as if she were trying to make herself smaller than her own beating heart. She held her breath, and the fear in her eyes infected him—an excitement without cause except her apprehension. A dry leaf scraping faintly against a hedge sounded to him as loud as a spoon dropped on stone.

“He’s coming towards us,” he whispered.

“Not a word,” she said, barely shaping the syllables. “Not a movement.”

Durrance advanced into the enclosure. Ethne and Willoughby watched him with the fixed stare of people who are ashamed of hiding but more afraid of being found. He walked past the bench and stopped—stopped facing them, the blind man’s eyes turned exactly where their faces were.

Surely, Willoughby thought, surely he sees.

Durrance’s eyes rested on them with a steadiness that made Ethne, for one dizzy second, doubt everything she knew. He seemed about to speak. Then, still in that first quiet voice, he said, “Ethne,” once more.

No answer came. His shoulders lifted in a small shrug, as though he were ridiculing his own uncertainty. He turned towards the creek, his back to them.

But Ethne had lived long enough beside him now to read what sight no longer gave her in his face. The poise of his head, the careless swing of the stick—no, not careless: performed carelessness. He was listening as a musician listens for the faintest returning note in an echoing hall. Every sound in the enclosure mattered: the brush of fabric, the click of a shoe on stone, the soft exhale of breath. The garden’s acoustic was suddenly a trap—hedges hemming the air, creek water holding a low, continuous murmur like the simmer at the back of a stove.

Her grip on Willoughby’s arm tightened until his sleeve creased.

For a full minute they held the silence, pressing themselves into it.

Then Durrance turned sharply and took a quick step towards the bench.

Ethne did not move. Not a fold of her skirt whispered. Willoughby, pinned by her hand, did not shift.

“I wonder where in the world she can be,” Durrance said aloud to himself; and with that he walked back out through the hedge-gap and was gone.

Only when his footsteps had faded beyond the enclosure did Ethne release Willoughby.

“That was a close shave,” he said, his voice too loud in the aftermath. “Suppose he’d sat down on the top of us?”

“Why suppose, since he did not?” Ethne replied, and the calmness was something she forced upon herself like composure forced on a face before one goes out to guests.

She looked down at the feather in her hand. She held it carefully, as if it were a fragile herb dried long ago and saved in paper—something that could crumble if pressed too hard, yet still keep its scent if protected.

“You’ve told me everything?” she asked.

“So far as I remember.”

“And all you’ve told me happened in the spring?”

“The spring of last year.”

She rose. “Then answer me this. Why did you not bring it to me last summer?”

Willoughby, taken aback by the bluntness, rubbed his jaw. “My leave was short. I spent it in the hills north of Suakin—after ibex.”

“I see,” said Ethne, softly, and the words tasted bitter. Last summer she had been free. Last summer one word from Willoughby, one visit, would have altered the whole recipe of her life. The cry rose in her—Why didn’t you come?—but she did not let it reach her tongue. She kept her voice even, the way one keeps a sauce from breaking by refusing sudden heat.

“I hope you had good sport,” she said.

“It wasn’t bad.”

“You might have written.”

“Feversham didn’t suggest there was any hurry,” Willoughby returned. “A letter would have been a long business—hard to explain. He asked me to see you when I had an opportunity, and I had none before now. To tell the truth, I thought I might find he’d come back before me.”

“Oh, no,” Ethne answered quickly. “There could be no possibility of that. The other two feathers still remain to be redeemed before he will ask me to take back mine.”

Willoughby shook his head, the motion slow. “Feversham can never persuade Castleton and Trench to cancel their accusations as he persuaded me.”

“Why not?”

“Major Castleton was killed when the square was broken at Tamai.”

“Killed?” Ethne echoed.

And then, against every rule of gracious feeling, she laughed—a short laugh, clean and satisfied, like the snap of a dry twig underfoot.

Willoughby stared at her, disbelieving.

But her face held no shame. She was a Celt; death, to her, did not always carry the weight other people demanded of it. And she could hate. She had agreed with Castleton and Trench once—she had given her feather to show that agreement—but agreement had never been affection. The men who had pushed Harry to ruin, and her with him, were not granted gentleness in her heart.

“And Colonel Trench too?” she asked, the eagerness bare now.

“No,” Willoughby said. “You’re disappointed? He’s worse off than that. He was captured on a reconnaissance. He’s a prisoner in Omdurman.”

“Ah,” Ethne said.

Willoughby’s mouth tightened. “You can’t have any idea what captivity there implies. If you had, however much you disliked him, you’d feel some pity.”

“Not I,” Ethne said stubbornly.

“I will tell you something of what it does imply—”

“No,” she cut in sharply. “I don’t wish to hear of Colonel Trench.” She steadied her breath. “Besides, you must go. But first—tell me one thing. What became of Mr. Feversham after he gave you that feather?”

“I told him he’d done all that could be reasonably expected,” Willoughby answered. “He took my advice. He went aboard the first steamer that touched at Suakin on its way to Suez. He left the Soudan.”

“I must find out where he is,” Ethne said, and the words came like a vow. “He must come back. Did he need money?”

“No. He still drew his allowance from his father. He told me he had more than enough.”

“I am glad of that,” Ethne said, and she meant it with a fierce, practical tenderness—relief that hunger and debt were not among the wounds he carried.

She bade Willoughby wait in the enclosure and went out alone to make sure the way was clear.

The garden beyond was empty. Durrance had vanished from sight. The great stone terrace and the house with its striped sunblinds looked drugged by midday heat, a place where even footsteps would feel like impertinence. It was nearing one o’clock; the birds were quiet in the trees, as if their own songs had been spent early and now they rested their throats. The silence struck Ethne as almost unnatural, like a kitchen at noon when the fire has been damped and every pot is covered.

Only the bees kept working, humming drowsily over the flowerbeds, and from the far meadow beyond the creek a boy’s voice called now and then—thin, bright, and distant.

She returned to Willoughby.

“You can go now,” she said. “I cannot pretend friendship for you, Captain Willoughby, but it was kind of you to find me out and tell me your story. You are going back at once to Kingsbridge? I hope so. For I do not wish Colonel Durrance to know of your visit—or anything you have told me.”

“Durrance was Feversham’s friend,” Willoughby objected. “His great friend.”

“He is quite unaware that any feathers were sent to Mr. Feversham,” Ethne replied. “So there is no need he should be informed that one has been taken back. He does not know why my engagement to Mr. Feversham was broken off. I do not wish him to know. Your story would enlighten him—and he must not be enlightened.”

“Why?” Willoughby asked. Obstinate by nature, he wanted the reason before he consented to silence.

Ethne gave it at once, simply, without ornament.

“I am engaged to Colonel Durrance,” she said.

Fear lay underneath the words, controlled but present: fear that Durrance already suspected she offered him no more than friendship. If he learned that the fault which had broken her engagement had been bravely atoned, he would insist—because he was Durrance, because honour was the language he still spoke fluently in the dark—upon setting her free. He would strip himself of the one remaining comfort. And Ethne, bound by her promise, stubbornly refused to let him do it. She would not answer Harry’s redemption by flinging away her own honour as though it were a cheap ingredient.

Willoughby pursed his lips and whistled under his breath. “Engaged to Jack Durrance!” he exclaimed. “Then I seem to have wasted my time in bringing you that feather.”

He pointed towards her hand.

Ethne drew it back sharply, as though he might snatch it away. The feather lay across her palm like a pale, weightless thing and yet it anchored her as firmly as any chain.

“I am most grateful for it,” she said.

“It’s a bit of a muddle, isn’t it?” Willoughby remarked. “It seems a little rough on Feversham perhaps. It’s a little rough on Jack Durrance too, when you come to think of it.”

Then he looked at her, really looked: at the care with which she held the feather, at the glow that had lit her while she listened, at the hungry precision of her questions, as if she were tasting the truth in each answer.

“I shouldn’t wonder,” he added more quietly, “if it was rather rough on you too, Miss Eustace.”

Ethne did not answer. She walked with him out of the enclosure towards the creek where his boat was moored, hurrying him down the bank, determined he should slip away unobserved.

But she had forgotten Mrs. Adair.

All morning Mrs. Adair had watched Ethne’s movements with the relish of a woman who considers herself a custodian of propriety and enjoys the power of a window. From behind the drawing-room curtains she had seen Ethne meet Durrance at the terrace steps; she had watched them walk towards the estuary; she had noticed the boat run aground in the opening between the trees; she had seen a man disembark and Ethne go forward to meet him. She was not a woman to leave her post at such a moment. She stayed behind the curtain with the intentness of someone who draws down a blind to see better.

Ethne and the stranger disappeared among the trees, leaving Durrance waiting alone.

At once Mrs. Adair thought of the little enclosure by the water’s edge. The conversation lasted long enough to trouble her. Since they did not promptly reappear, a thought leapt up—sharp and eager.

Could the stranger be Harry Feversham?

Ethne had no friends in this part of the country. Mrs. Adair wanted confirmation, and—more than that—she wanted the particular confirmation that would condemn Ethne Eustace. Her excitement rose when she saw Durrance, tired of waiting, follow in Ethne’s direction. She leaned closer to the glass, the house behind her quiet as a paused orchestra, the outside world filled with subtle sounds she did not heed: bees, creek-water, distant calling.

What happened next fascinated her even more. Durrance returned alone and came straight into the house, up the terrace, into the drawing-room.

“Have you seen Ethne?” he asked.

“Is she not in the little garden by the water?” Mrs. Adair said.

“No. I went into it and called to her. It was empty.”

“Indeed?” Mrs. Adair replied, carefully smoothing the word as though it were a tablecloth. “Then I don’t know where she is. Are you going?”

“Yes—home.”

She made no effort to detain him then. But he did not immediately go. He stood by the window, idly swinging the blind-tassel, though Ethne would have said there was nothing idle in him now—not even that small, repetitive sound.

“I did not know until to-day that it was your plan that I should come home and Ethne stay with you until I found out whether a cure was likely or possible,” he said. “It was very kind of you, Mrs. Adair, and I am grateful.”

“It was a natural plan to propose as soon as I heard of your ill-luck.”

“And when was that?” he asked unconcernedly.

Mrs. Adair did not mistake his carelessness; she felt the hook beneath the bait. “The day after Calder’s telegram reached her from Wadi Halfa, I suppose.”

“Oh, so you knew of that telegram,” she said quickly. “I thought you did not.” Ethne had begged her not to mention it on the very day Durrance came back to England.

“Of course I knew of it,” Durrance returned; and without waiting for more he went out on to the terrace.

Mrs. Adair, left alone with her thoughts, dismissed for the moment the matter of the telegram. Another picture absorbed her: Durrance in the little garden calling Ethne aloud, while within a dozen yards she had sat silent with another man. The idea had a flavour she enjoyed—scandal salted and ready.

And then the garden gave her proof.

She saw Ethne and her companion come out on to the open lawn. Was it Feversham? She leaned forward, hungry to know. They went down the bank towards the boat. Mrs. Adair stepped from the window and ran.

So it happened that as Willoughby rose from loosening the painter, he looked up and met Mrs. Adair’s eyes—bright with curiosity and, now, disappointment.

Mrs. Adair called to Ethne and came down the bank to them.

“I noticed you cross the lawn from the drawing-room window,” she said.

“Yes?” Ethne answered, and let the single syllable stand like a closed door.

Mrs. Adair did not move away. An awkward pause opened between them. At last Ethne yielded the smallest thing that courtesy required.

“I was talking to Captain Willoughby,” she said, and turned slightly. “You do not know Mrs. Adair, I think?”

“No,” Willoughby replied, lifting his hat. “But I know Mrs. Adair very well by name. I know friends of yours, Mrs. Adair—Durrance, for instance; and of course I knew—”

Ethne’s eyes moved to his, quiet and commanding. The rest of his sentence died in his throat. He bent with sudden energy to push the boat’s nose from the sand, making haste as though haste could repair a near-disaster.

“Of course what?” Mrs. Adair asked, smiling.

“Of course I knew of you, Mrs. Adair,” Willoughby said, shifting the words like a cook substituting one spice for another at the last second.

Mrs. Adair was quite clear that this was not what he had meant to say. He had almost added another name.

“Captain Willoughby,” she repeated softly to herself, storing it away.

Then she said, “You belong to Colonel Durrance’s regiment, perhaps?”

“No. I belong to the North Surrey.”

“Ah,” said Mrs. Adair pleasantly. “Mr. Feversham’s old regiment.”

Willoughby, guileless, had stepped neatly into her little trap, and the ease of it made her want more. Whatever he knew, she thought, would be easy to extract with patience.

But Ethne had disconcerting ways. She looked straight into Mrs. Adair’s eyes and said calmly, with a frankness that offered no handle for prying:

“Captain Willoughby and I have been talking of Mr. Feversham.”

At the same time she held out her hand to the captain. “Good-bye,” she said.

Mrs. Adair interrupted quickly, as though she would not allow the scene to end on Ethne’s terms. “Colonel Durrance has gone home, but he dines with us to-night. I came out to tell you that—but I am glad that I came, for it gives me the opportunity to ask your friend to lunch with us if he will.”

Willoughby, who had already swung one leg into the boat, withdrew it with eager alacrity. He had travelled all night from London, had made the scantiest breakfast at Kingsbridge—something swallowed rather than eaten, no doubt—and the notion of lunch struck him with the force of a remembered appetite.

“It’s awfully good of you, Mrs. Adair,” he began.

“It is very kind indeed,” Ethne continued smoothly, “but Captain Willoughby has reminded me that his leave is very short, and we have no right to detain him. Good-bye.”

Willoughby gazed at her with a rueful appeal. For a second the man in him—hungry, tired, tempted by hospitality—rebelled. But Ethne’s eyes rested on his with a quiet insistence that left him no way to disobey without betraying her.

He bowed, got into his boat, and pushed off from the shore.

“It’s a little bit rough on me too, perhaps, Miss Eustace,” he called back, half complaint and half admiration.

Ethne laughed, and turned back up the bank with Mrs. Adair.

As they walked towards the terrace, she opened her hand once or twice, as though to reassure herself the feather was still there. The small white plume lay in her palm like a shred of winter caught in summer. At the sight of it she laughed again—clear and rather low—laughing not because it was funny, but because the truth had at last taken a shape she could hold.

She gave Mrs. Adair no explanation of Captain Willoughby’s errand. Had she been in Mrs. Adair’s place, she would not have expected one.

It was her business, and only hers.

Mrs. Adair asked for nothing and so, by her own crooked creed, learned nothing. Behind her pale, well-bred composure lived a mind that preferred the scrape of a keyhole to the honest swing of a door. A fact won by trapping someone into offering it felt to her like a delicacy reduced in a careful sauce: richer because it had been coaxed, not served up plainly. All through luncheon, though the small white feather in Ethne’s possession prickled her curiosity like a burr caught in lace, though Captain Willoughby’s tidings about Harry Feversham set her thoughts tapping and turning, though the old night at Ramelton—years ago now—hovered at the edge of her guesses like a name half-forgotten in a dying dialect, she showed no sign. She poured out talk on indifferent things with practiced brightness.

It did no good.

“I don’t believe you hear a single word I am saying!” she broke out at last.

Ethne laughed, and admitted it as frankly as if she had been caught seasoning a dish absent-mindedly while someone read aloud a menu.

As soon as luncheon was done she escaped to her room and claimed the afternoon for solitude. From her window the estate lay mild and orderly, yet her thoughts ran far from it, running and returning like a tune that will not let itself be translated into speech. She repeated, slowly, the story Willoughby had brought her in the morning, and it went through her like music played with the right pressure and the right pause—thrilling not because it argued, but because it sounded true.

The small regret—that he had not come home and told it a year ago, when she had been free—was there, but it lay light, like a crumpled rose-leaf under a mattress that could not ruin sleep. The story itself overwhelmed it. Harry Feversham, who had struck the blow at Lennon House, had also, at peril to himself, restored the pride that blow had broken. It was a strange restitution: the hand that had shamed her had, without asking her faith or permission, laboured to give her back her own regard. Gratitude warmed her with a quickening like sunlight on skin after long cloud. Youth, which she had thought spent, came back on that August afternoon in the way a lost taste returns suddenly to the tongue.

She unlocked a drawer in her dressing-case and took out the one portrait—of all his gifts—the only one she had kept. She was clear with herself. The man in the picture was dead to her in the only way that mattered; there was no thought of disloyalty towards Durrance. Yet the dead may still be friends. She looked at the likeness with contentment not sharpened by longing, but softened by respect. He had needed no encouragement from her to keep him on the level of a hard inspiration. When she put the portrait away, she laid the little white feather beside it, as if sealing together two tokens whose meanings could not be rendered into any plain, dominant vocabulary, and locked the drawer.

She returned to the window.

A light breeze made the shadows of the high trees dance in broken patterns over the lawn. The sun mellowed and reddened; the creek beyond held the sky’s colour as a pan holds a slow-browned glaze. Yet, for all the richness of the scene, her heart tugged homewards. She was of her county—Harry Feversham had found that out long ago—and great sorrow and great joy drew her alike to Donegal, where endurance came easier and gladness felt complete. It was August: the first heather would be out on the hillsides, and she could almost smell it, a dry sweetness over peat and granite. She wished the news had found her there, among the brown mountains and the voices of streams that spoke an older language than any drawing-room ever managed. Here, in this strange land, she could not help feeling that even happiness had a different accent.

But Donegal was not wholly absent. Dermod’s old collie had become her shadow; his warm, patient presence was a kind of hearth. To him she spoke her confidence, and if her voice broke, he would never carry the tale beyond her knees. With him at her feet she filled in, from Willoughby’s omissions and from what Feversham had never told, the missing parts as one reconstructs a vanished recipe from scent and texture. Those three years of concealment in Suakin, for instance: the small crowded streets, the troops marching out and coming back dust-stained, bleeding, crowned with victory. She saw Feversham slipping away at their approach, his whole body tuned to danger, afraid an old friend—Durrance, perhaps, or Willoughby, or Trench—might catch a familiar movement and pierce a disguise. She saw the first panic when Berber’s dark brown walls rose ahead; the night in the ruined acres; the stumbling search for the well among Obak’s shifting sandhills. Each picture came with its own taste of fear—metal on the tongue, sand in the throat—and with each she asked, not as a reproach but as a startled accounting, Where was I then? What was I doing?

She sat in that golden drift of thought until the light changed on the creek, until rooks wheeled noisily from the treetops to settle themselves for the night, their calls stitching the evening together. The sound drew her back as surely as a bell recalls a cook from the far end of the market.

At dinner she brought a buoyancy that startled her companions. Mrs. Adair, peering sideways as ever, had to admit that seldom had Ethne’s eyes shone so starrily, or colour sat so freshly on her cheeks. She grew more certain that Willoughby’s news had been of the stirring sort; she grew more tortured by her failure to name it. Yet the meal passed with an ease they had not known since Durrance’s return to Guessens. He, too, threw off some restraint. His spirits rose to meet Ethne’s; he answered laugh with laugh, and that habitual tension—the look of a man listening with his whole life, making his ears stand in for lost eyes—fell away from his face for once, as if the house’s soundscape had softened enough to let him rest inside it.

“You will play on your violin to-night, I think,” he said when they rose.

“Yes,” she answered, “I will—with all my heart.”

He laughed and held the door.

The violin had stayed locked in its case these last two months. Durrance had come to treat that case like a gauge. When the world went well with Ethne, the lid opened and the instrument was allowed to speak; when it went ill, it stayed shut, lest it say too much, lay open old wounds, and expose them to eyes that had no right. Ethne herself knew it for an indiscreet friend—like some cherished dish that reveals, by its very aroma, what has been happening in the kitchen.

But to-night it would be brought out.

Mrs. Adair lingered until Ethne was out of earshot.

“You have noticed the change in her to-night?” she asked.

“Yes. Have I not?” Durrance replied.

“One has waited for it, hoped for it, despaired of it.”

“Are you so glad of the change?”

Durrance tipped his head back.

“Do you wonder that I am glad? Kind, friendly, unselfish—these things she has always been. But there is more than friendliness evident to-night, and for the first time it’s evident.”

A look of pity crossed Mrs. Adair’s face. She went out without another word.

Durrance took all of that change to himself.

Mrs. Adair, restless as a kettle just before boiling, drew up the blinds in the drawing-room, opened the window, and admitted the moonlight. Then, seeing Ethne unlocking the violin-case, she stepped out onto the terrace; she could not sit patiently in the room and watch a secret unfold without her.

So when Durrance entered the drawing-room, he found Ethne alone. She sat in the window, already tightening the strings, turning each peg with the careful, exact ear of someone who understands that a world can be made unbearable by the wrong pitch. Outside, the night’s sounds lay clear: the faint lap from the creek, a distant insect’s steady rasp, leaves shifting with the smallest wind. No harsh machinery intruded; no bought entertainment shouted over the quiet. The house held an older peace, the sort modern noise tries to sell back to people in bottles.

Durrance took a chair behind her in the shadows.

“What shall I play to you?” she asked.

“The Musoline Overture,” he said. “You played it on the first evening when I came to Ramelton. I remember how you played it then. Play it again to-night. I want to compare.”

“I have played it since.”

“Never to me.”

They were alone. The windows stood open on moonlight. Ethne rose suddenly, crossed to the lamp, and put it out. The room deepened; the moon became their only light. She returned to her seat. Durrance remained behind her, leaning forward, hands on his knees, listening—not with the relaxed pleasure he had shown at dinner, but with an intentness he had hidden, like a man tasting for a secret ingredient.

He was making, as he believed, a final test on which his life and hers should turn. The violin would tell him what words could not. Would it speak only friendship—or the thing beyond friendship?

Ethne began the overture, and as soon as the bow drew its first clear line of sound, she forgot he was there.

The garden lay still, warm with summer; scent rose from the borders, thick and sweet as if crushed leaves had been rubbed between fingers. The creek held the moonlight like a solid floor of silver. Trees stood dreaming up at the stars. The music floated out over the lawn into the clean, attentive night, and with it a fancy took her—swift, irrational, irresistible—that the sound might travel down the creek, over Salcombe Bar, and across the bright seas until it struck, small but piercingly clear like fairy music, on the ears of a man sleeping far away under southern stars, the cool desert wind on his face.

If he could only hear, she thought. If he could only wake and know it was a message of friendship.

And with that in her mind she played with a skill she had never used before, not for display but for meaning. She made the violin a voice of sympathy, as if tenderness could be tuned and carried.

The fancy grew as the overture unfolded. The music became a bridge swung in mid-air across the world, and for a few minutes she and Harry Feversham might meet upon it and clasp hands. They would part at once, each returning to the path allotted—yet those minutes would strengthen both to continue.

The chords rang out into silence, and it seemed to her they declared the pride that had returned to her that day. Her fancy hardened into belief. It was no longer If he should hear; it was He must hear. And then, swept past caution, a strange hope sprang up and took her whole attention:

If he could answer.

She lingered on the last bars, holding them as one holds back the final taste of a dish, waiting for the moment after when the mouth understands what it has been given. When the music died, she sat with the violin across her knees and stared out across the moonlit garden, listening for an impossible reply.

An answer came.

Not from the creek, not across the lawn, not from any distance that could be crossed by wind and water.

It came from the dark behind her, spoken in Durrance’s voice.

“Ethne, where do you think I heard that overture last played?”

She started, as if shaken awake.

“Why—you told me. At Ramelton, when you first came to Lennon House.”

“I have heard it since,” he said. “Though it was not played by you. It was not really played at all. But a melody of it—and not even that really, but a suggestion of a melody—I heard stumbled out on a zither, with many false notes, by a Greek in a bare little whitewashed café, lit by one glaring lamp, at Wadi Halfa.”

“This overture?” she whispered. “How strange!”

“Not so strange after all. For the Greek was Harry Feversham.”

So the answer had come.

Ethne sat very still in the moonlight. Anyone bending close might have seen her eyelids closed, as if she were trying to keep the moment intact against the bluntness of sight. A long silence followed. She did not ask why Durrance, having kept such knowledge for so long, chose to speak of it now. She did not ask what had driven Harry Feversham to scrape at a zither in a mean café at Wadi Halfa. The details did not yet want naming; the experience felt untranslatable, like some private dialect of the heart that would only be damaged by forcing it into common words.

It seemed to her simply that he had spoken to her as she had played to him. The music, after all, had been a bridge. It did not even feel strange that he had used Durrance’s voice.

“When was this?” she asked at last.

“In February of this year. I will tell you about it.”

“Yes,” she said. “Please, tell me.”

And Durrance, out of the shadows, began to speak.

Ethne did not turn her head. She kept the violin laid along her knees as though it were a child she had lulled to sleep, her fingers resting on the varnished belly where the wood still held the warmth of her hands. Beyond the window the garden lay washed in moonlight: roses shut tight as clenched fists, borders dark with leaf-shadow, and, between two elms, a pale ribbon of water or path—silver and quiet as a blade.

She held herself so deliberately still because stillness helped her commit a small, necessary deceit. If she did not look at Durrance—if she stared instead into the moonlit gap—then it was easier to pretend that Harry Feversham himself spoke to her, and not Durrance speaking for him. She wanted the voice to go slowly, syllable by syllable, like broth coaxed to clarity over a low flame, so she could gather every word and hoard it.

“It was on the night before I started eastward into the desert—for the last time,” Durrance said.

The weight he gave to last time—its longing, its regret—did not touch her. She had no room left for it.

“Yes,” she answered. “That was in February. The middle of the month, wasn’t it? Do you remember the day? I should like the exact day.”

“The fifteenth.”

“The fifteenth,” she repeated, tasting the date as if it were a spice whose name she once knew in a dead dialect. “I was at Glenalla all February. What was I doing on the fifteenth? It does not matter.”

Yet it did matter, in the strangest way. All morning, while Willoughby told his story, she had been haunted by surprise—surprise that she had not somehow known, in her blood or bones, at the very hour things happened. That astonishment returned now, sharpened. It was odd that she had been made to wait for this August night, for this garden where even the flowers kept their mouths shut, before she learned of a meeting on February 15 and heard a message that ought, by all rights, to have reached her like a familiar tune carried on wind.

Remorse slid in under the door of her heart.

It was my own fault, she thought. If I had kept faith, I should have known. I am well punished.

Still she assumed the message would be good. It would complete what she had already been told; it would round the day to perfection, like a circle closed without a seam.

“Well?” she urged softly. “Go on.”

“I had been busy all that day in my office finishing up my work,” Durrance said. “I turned the key in the door at ten o’clock, glad I shouldn’t open it for six weeks, and I strolled northward out of Wadi Halfa along the Nile bank into the little town of Tewfikieh. As I entered the main street I saw a small crowd—Arabs, negroes, a Greek or two, and some Egyptian soldiers—standing outside a café, lit by a hard glare from within. And as I came nearer I heard the sound of a violin and a zither—both most vilely played—jingling out a waltz.”

Ethne’s fingers tightened on the neck of her own violin. Her garden was full of night-sounds she had always loved: the small click of insects, the hush of leaves, the distant wash of water in the dark. Even Durrance’s voice, measured and low, belonged to that gentler ecology. But what he described—an unshaded paraffin lamp, a room made loud and flat by whitewashed walls, a crowd that jeered—came to her as a kind of acoustic pollution, the ugliness of noise where there should have been resonance.

“I stood at the back of the crowd,” he went on, “and looked over their shoulders into the room. Four bare whitewashed walls. A bar in a corner. A bench or two. A single paraffin lamp swinging and glaring from the ceiling. And a troupe of itinerant musicians playing to that crowd for a night’s lodging and the price of a meal.”

Ethne saw it as he spoke: the lamp’s crude circle of light, the shadows it made too black, the faces turned inwards like a ring around a pot. She imagined the air—hot paraffin, sour sweat, dust carried in from the street and ground into grit. The waltz, not flowing but stumbling, like dough beaten without care.

“There were four of them,” said Durrance, “and so far as I could see they were Greeks. Two were man and wife—both old, slatternly, almost in rags: he thin and sallow, grey hair and black moustache; she fat and coarse of face, heavy-bodied. Of the other two, one seemed their daughter—a girl of seventeen. Not really good-looking, but dressed with scrupulous care, which in those sordid surroundings lent her good looks. A clean ribbon to bind back her hair, an untorn white frock, shoes neat. I was rather touched by it—the father and mother in rags so she might be trim. The fourth was a young man, seated in the window with his back to me, bending over his zither. I could see he wore a beard.”

Ethne’s mind caught, fast as a hook. A young man, beard, back turned, a zither in the window. She thought of hands she knew—hands that had once been so useless at anything delicate, hands that had treated music as if it were a foreign tongue spoken too quickly, hands that had only ever listened with any attention when she herself had played and he could pretend it was meant for him alone.

“What was he doing with that troupe?” she thought. Harry had money. Willoughby had said so. He had no need to barter a meal for a tune.

When Durrance spoke of the old man’s violin, Ethne almost winced.

“When I came up,” he said, “the old man was playing the violin, though playing is not the word. The noise was like a pencil squeaking on slate—it set one’s teeth on edge; the violin itself seemed to squeal with pain. And while he fiddled and the young man hammered at the zither, the old woman and the girl slowly revolved in a waltz.”

Ethne could hear it: the bow scraping too close to the bridge, the strings crying, the zither notes dropping like pebbles into a tin basin. In her own quiet room, that imagined racket was an intrusion—like a factory whistle blown into a garden.

“It may sound comic to hear about,” Durrance said, “but if you could have seen—It fairly plucked at one’s heart. The crowd outside—negroes, mind you—laughing, passing low jests at their expense. And inside the four white people: the old woman clumsy and heavy-footed, shining with heat, panting; the girl young and lissom; the two men with discordant torturing music. And just above you the great planets and stars of an African sky, and just about you the silent dignity of the moonlit desert. Imagine it! The very ineptness hurt one.”

He paused, and Ethne let the picture form in her: the desert’s vast hush pressing against a room that could not hear itself properly; the lamp’s glare and the jeering crowd; the taste of dust at the back of the throat.

But her sympathy did not go to the troupe. It ran ahead, impatient, to the figure with the beard in the window.

“You spoke to him?” she asked suddenly.

“To whom? Oh—to Harry?” Durrance returned.

“Yes. How did you find out?”

“The waltz ended. The old woman sank exhausted on the bench; the young man lifted his head from the zither; the old man scraped a new chord; and the girl stood forward to sing. Her voice had youth and freshness, but no other music. Yet the old man smiled, the mother beat time with her heavy foot, nodding with pride at their daughter. And again in the throng outside the ill-conditioned talk—the untranslatable jests—went round. Horrible, don’t you think?”

“Yes,” Ethne said, but her voice wandered. Untranslatable. The word pricked her. In those jeers there would be meanings that could not be carried across tongues—cruelty seasoned with local knowledge, contempt preserved in idiom. Sound as a weapon; language as a net.

She sat gazing into the moon’s broad panel on the floor by the window, as if it were itself a doorway into that café in Tewfikieh. If she stared hard enough, perhaps she would see it—tiny, far off, yet blazing.

“Well?” she whispered. “After the song?”

“The young man with his back towards me began to fumble out a solo,” Durrance continued. “He struck so many false notes that at first there was no tune. The laughter grew. I was just turning away, sick at heart, when by chance he played a succession of notes correctly. They arrested me. I listened again, and a sort of haunting melody began to emerge—a weak thin thing with no soul in it, a ghost of a melody, and yet familiar. I stood there in the sandy street between hovels and stunted trees, and I was carried away out of the East to Ramelton, to a summer night in Donegal, when you sat by the open window as you sit now and played the Musoline Overture—which you played again tonight.”

Ethne’s breath caught, as if she had bitten into something too sharp.

“It was a melody from the overture?”

“Yes. And it was Harry Feversham who played it.”

For an instant she could not keep the exultation from her voice. “You are sure?”

“I did not guess it at once,” said Durrance. “I was not very quick in those days.”

“But you are now,” she said.

“Quicker, at all events. I should have guessed it now. Then I was only curious—how an itinerant Greek had picked it up. I determined to reward him for his diligence. I thought you would like me to.”

“Yes,” Ethne breathed, the word escaping like steam from a covered pot.

“When he came out from the café,” Durrance said, “and with his hat in his hand passed through the jeering crowd, I threw a sovereign into the hat. He turned to me with a start. In spite of his beard I knew him. Besides—before he could check himself—he cried out, ‘Jack!’”

“You can have made no mistake then,” Ethne said, wonder thickening her voice.

“No. The man who strummed the zither was—” Durrance stopped, and she felt how near he was to speaking Harry’s name aloud into the room. “—Feversham.”

“But he knew no music,” she said quickly, half laughing at the remembered certainty of it. “He had no ear. You couldn’t invent a discord harsh enough to attract his attention. He could never have remembered any melody from the Musoline Overture.”

“Yet it was Harry Feversham,” Durrance answered. “Somehow he had remembered. I can understand it. He would have so little he cared to remember, and that little he would strive with all his might to bring back clearly. Imagine him working the scrap of music out in his brain—humming, whistling it uncounted times, making perpetual errors—until one day he got it safe and fixed. And then picking it out laboriously on the strings. I can. Indeed, I can.”

So the answer came to her: that he had not forgotten. Not her—she would not name that. But the sound she had once made at an open window, the tune that belonged to her hands and his watching, had lodged in him like a seed. He had tended it in foreign soil until it sprouted thinly, pale and stubborn, in a café under a paraffin lamp.

She sat silent, moved in spite of every vow she had made herself. It seemed fitting—almost mythic in its plainness—that the message should travel not by letter but by melody, and that it should arrive broken, altered by distance and bad instruments, yet still itself. A tune could carry what language could not; no dominant lexicon could quite colonise it. It did not translate, it endured.

She pictured him in that bare room, the zither on his knee, his beard hiding his face, his fingers searching for her overture while the crowd laughed. Had he dreamed, as she had dreamed tonight, that even thin and feeble an echo might reach across the world?

She knew now, with an ache that made her throat taste of iron, that she would never truly forget him. At best she would pretend. And because she had once been unjustly harsh, she grew more resolved that Durrance must not suffer for her fault. Whatever grief she carried, she would carry it quietly.

“I told you last year, Ethne, in Hill Street,” Durrance resumed, “that I never wished to see Feversham again. I was wrong. The reluctance was all on his side, not mine. The moment he realised he had called my name he tried to edge backward into the crowd. He began to gabble Greek. But I caught his arm and wouldn’t let him go. He had done you some great wrong—that I know; that I knew. But I couldn’t remember it then. I only remembered that years before Harry Feversham had been my friend—my one great friend. We rowed in the same college boat at Oxford, he at stroke, I at seven—those stripes on his jersey made my eyes dizzy in the last hundred yards past the barges. We bathed at Sandford Lasher on summer afternoons. We supped on Kennington Island. We cut lectures and paddled up the Cher to Islip. And there he was at Wadi Halfa, herding with that troupe—an outcast, sunk to such ill-fortune that he must come to that squalid town and play vilely for a night’s lodging and the price of a meal.”

“No,” Ethne broke in, sharp as a snapped string. “It was not for that reason that he went to Wadi Halfa.”

“Why then?”

“I cannot think. But he was not in need of money. His father had continued his allowance, and he had accepted it.”

“You are sure?”

“Quite sure. I heard it only today,” she said—and did not even notice the slip. Her mind was too intent on the story’s missing piece.

Durrance, lost in his own recollection, let it pass.

“So you never knew what brought him to Halfa?” she pressed. “Did you not ask him? Why didn’t you? Why?”

The disappointment tasted bitter, like something burned at the bottom of a pan. Here was the last news of Harry Feversham and it came incomplete, like a letter torn in half. The missing part might never be recovered.

“I was a fool,” Durrance said. Regret thickened his voice until it almost matched hers. “I shall not easily forgive myself. He was my friend, you see. I had him by the arm—and I let him go. I was a fool.” He knocked his forehead with his fist.

“He tried Arabic,” Durrance continued. “He pleaded that he and his companions were poor peaceable people. If I had given too much money, I should take it back. All the while he dragged away. But I held him fast. I said, ‘Harry Feversham, that won’t do.’ And then he gave in and spoke English—whispering. ‘Let me go, Jack, let me go.’ The crowd was about us. He had some reason for secrecy—shame, perhaps, at his downfall. I said, ‘Come up to my quarters in Halfa as soon as you are free,’ and I let him go.”

Ethne leaned forward unconsciously, as if she could pull more out of the air by force.

“All that night I waited for him on the verandah,” Durrance said, “but he did not come. In the morning I had to start across the desert. I almost spoke of him to a friend who came to see me start—to Calder, in fact—you know of him—the man who sent you the telegram.”

“Yes, I remember,” Ethne answered—another slip, falling from her without sound.

“At the very last moment,” Durrance went on, “when my camel had risen, I stooped to speak to Calder, to tell him to see to Feversham. But I did not. I knew nothing about his allowance then. I only thought he had fallen low. It didn’t seem fair that another should know. So I rode on and kept silence.”

She nodded. She could not help approving the instinct, even while it stung her.

“So you never saw him again?”

“I was away nine weeks. I came back blind,” he said simply.

The simplicity pierced her. In the hush of her room, with the garden’s soft sounds beyond, the word blind fell heavy as a stone into water.

“I went at once to Cairo,” Durrance continued, “and Calder came with me. There I told him of Feversham—how I had seen him at Tewfikieh. I asked Calder, when he got back to Halfa, to make inquiries, to find and help him if he could; and to let me know the result. I received a letter from Calder a week ago, and I am troubled by it—very much troubled.”

“What did he say?” Ethne asked, and this time fear slid coldly beneath her ribs.

She turned away from the moonlight into the room’s shadow toward Durrance, bending forward as though sight could be forced from darkness. But the dark hid his face; only his voice came, and it seemed to her suddenly an unbearable human noise in a night that ought to have been quiet.

“That the two women and the old Greek had gone back northward on a steamer to Assouan.”

“Feversham remained at Halfa then?” she asked quickly. “That is so—isn’t it?”

“No,” Durrance replied. “Feversham did not remain. He slipped past Halfa the day after I started east. He went out in the morning—to the south.”

“Into the desert?”

“Yes. The desert to the south—the enemy’s country. He went just as I saw him, carrying his zither. He was seen. There can be no doubt.”

For a little while Ethne could not make a sound. The garden noises seemed to retreat, as if the world held its breath.

Then she forced herself to speak. “You have that letter with you?”

“Yes.”

“I should like to read it.”

She rose and crossed to Durrance. He took the letter from his pocket and gave it to her. She carried it to the window where the moonlight lay thick and clear on the sill, and stood there with a hand pressed to her heart as though to keep it from breaking its bounds.

She read it once, and then again. It was explicit: the Greek who owned the café admitted that Joseppi—the name under which he knew Feversham—had wandered south with a water-skin and a store of dates, though why he either did not know or would not tell.

Dates, she thought wildly, dates and water—provisions measured like rations in a recipe too small for the hunger it must face.

She had a question, but it took time to shape it without faltering.

“What will happen to him?”

“At the best, capture; at the worst, death,” Durrance said. “Starvation, thirst, or the Dervishes. But there is just a hope it might be only capture and imprisonment. He was white. If caught, they might think him a spy—sure he knew our plans and strength. They would most likely send him to Omdurman. I have written to Calder. Spies go out and in from Halfa; we often hear things that happen in Omdurman. If Feversham is taken there, sooner or later I shall know. But he must have gone mad. It is the only explanation.”

Ethne had another explanation. It rose in her like a remembered word from a language she had once spoken at home—something no outsider could translate cleanly, because it belonged to the private country of loyalty and shame.

Off her guard, she spoke it aloud.

“Colonel Trench,” she said, “is a prisoner at Omdurman.”

“Oh, yes,” Durrance answered. “Feversham will not be quite alone then. There is comfort in that, and perhaps something may be done. When I hear from Calder I will tell you. Perhaps something may be done.”

He had misconstrued her, and she knew he must remain in the dark. She would not betray Harry’s motive with any careless syllable. She would still pretend—pretend to have forgotten, pretend that this news did not tear her. She must not cry out. She must not swoon. She must stand very still, like a cook who has burnt her hand and cannot drop the pan because the meal must still be served.

She stood at the window, the letter clenched, as Durrance began to speak again.

“Perhaps you will wonder,” he said, “why I have told you tonight what I have up till now kept to myself. I did not dare to tell you before. I want to explain why.”

Ethne did not hear the exultation in his voice; she did not even weigh what his explanation might be. The mere fact of speech—any speech—had become unendurable. The night outside called to her with its cool quiet: the dark of the trees, the honest rustle of leaves, the soft, living sounds that did not demand translation and did not wound with meaning.

She was close by the window. One step, silent, and she would be across the sill and free.

Durrance continued, engrossed in his own words, and she did not listen. She gathered her skirts with careful fingers so they would not rustle, and stepped out through the window into the garden.

This was the third slip she made upon that eventful night.

Ethne had meant to slip from the house as a cook steals out to taste a sauce in secret—quietly, without witness, without the clatter of notice. But Mrs. Adair sat on the terrace, in the lee of the warm stone wall where the day’s heat still breathed, and from that shadowed angle she commanded the open drawing-room window as neatly as an archivist commands a shelf.

She saw Ethne cross the flags with the lightness of a girl who has forgotten her own dignity, and then—too quickly—take the steps into the garden. Not the unhurried walk of someone seeking air, but the sudden flight of someone escaping a sound that has turned sharp.

The room behind the window had changed a moment before: Ethne had turned down the lamp, and the bright rectangle of domesticity—chairs, music-stand, the familiar instruments of polite evening—had fallen into darkness, leaving only moonlight. To Mrs. Adair that swift snuffing was like a lid clapped over a pot to keep in fragrance: secrecy, closeness, the private heat of words spoken low. She had not run from the sting. She stayed, listening.

Music floated out—its sweetness and yearning rolling over the terrace and into the hedges the way the smell of baked bread drifts into a lane and makes you suddenly hungry for a home you do not possess. When the notes ceased, there followed that other intimacy: the murmured talk that comes after a shared song, as if the air itself has been seasoned and any word will take the flavor.

Mrs. Adair’s jealousy flared—clean and scorching, like spirits set alight in a pan.

Then Ethne ran.

Mrs. Adair could not believe in a quarrel. Ethne was not made for loud scenes; she did not slam doors. And yet something more singular happened: Colonel Durrance continued speaking in the darkened room, his voice steady, confident—addressing an audience that was no longer there. Mrs. Adair rose and moved to the open window on the soft soles of her shoes, careful as anyone crossing a kitchen at night so as not to wake the sleepers. She drew near enough that the sill between her and the room was no wider than a knife’s back.

Durrance sat in shadow. She could not see him clearly at first, but she could hear him laugh—light-hearted, the laugh of a man recounting a difficulty already mastered, like one describing how he once spoiled a dish and now knows exactly where his hand went wrong.

“I began to find out,” he was saying, “even on that first afternoon at Hill Street two months ago, that there was only friendship on your side. My blindness helped me. With your face and your eyes in view I should have believed without question just what you wished me to believe. But you had no longer those defences. I on my side had grown quicker. I began in a word to see. For the first time in my life I began to see.”

Mrs. Adair did not move. It was dreadful, this confession poured into darkness, as if he were decanting his heart into an empty glass.

“I should have definitely broken off our engagement, I suppose, at once,” he went on. “For I still believed, and as firmly as ever, that there must be more than friendship on both sides. But I had grown selfish. I warned you, Ethne—selfishness was the blind man’s particular fault. I waited and deferred the time of marriage. I made excuses. I led you to believe that there was a chance of recovery when I knew there was none. For I hoped, as a man will, that with time your friendship might grow into more than friendship. So long as there was a chance of that, I—Ethne, I could not let you go.”

His voice softened on her name, as if he were tasting it, searching for an after-note.

“So I listened for some new softness in your voice, some new buoyancy in your laughter, some new deep thrill of the heart in the music which you played—longing for it—how much! Well, to-night I have burnt my boats. I have admitted to you that I knew friendship limited your thoughts of me. I have owned to you that there is no hope my sight will be restored. I have even dared to-night to tell you what I have kept secret for so long—my meeting with Harry Feversham and the peril he has run. And why? Because for the first time I have heard to-night just those signs for which I waited. The new softness, the new pride, in your voice, the buoyancy in your laughter—they have been audible to me all this evening. The restraint and the tension were gone from your manner. And when you played, it was as though some one with just your skill and knowledge played, but some one who let her heart speak resonantly through the music as until to-night you have never done. Ethne, Ethne!”

But Ethne was not there to receive his ardour. She was already in the small enclosed garden by the bank—the place she had used that morning like a larder for secrets, where one could shut a gate and make the world manageable. Her collie had come to her, padding without fuss, its paws muffling the ground—one creature in the house who understood that comfort is sometimes only the absence of noise. She had reached what she needed most: solitude and the thin, honest soundscape of the night.

A few more sentences from Durrance and her prudence would have cracked. The careful wall she had built these months—brick by brick of attention, mortar of practiced tenderness—so that he would never look over into the country of her true thoughts, would have been knocked flat. The bitter part was that he had already begun to see over it; he was peering now with that quickened inner sight. Ethne did not know it. She fled to stop him from knowing it.

The creek lay silvered, as if the moon had poured skimmed milk across its surface. Trees stood black and tall, their leaves barely stirring. The tide lapped the bank with the soft persistence of a spoon against a bowl; it was no louder than a river’s continuous whisper. Ethne sat on the bench, trying to draw the quiet into herself the way one draws warmth from a hearth, trying to learn from the growing things around her their patience, their obstinate perseverance through seasons.

But the day had stretched her beyond endurance.

This morning, in this very garden, good news had come and she had regained Harry Feversham—so she thought of it, as if he were a lost heirloom returned to the household: not held in hand, but recovered in possibility. This evening bad news had come and she had lost him again, perhaps for the whole remainder of mortal life. Harry meant to pay for his fault to the uttermost scruple, and Ethne—who had once demanded that scruple as the only language of honour—cried out against his thoroughness now that it turned its blade upon him.

Surely he might have been content. Surely, in redeeming himself to one of the three, he had redeemed himself to all. But he had gone south to join Colonel Trench in Omdurman.

Ethne knew nothing of Omdurman’s squalor and bare glare, nothing of what a prison-house meant there; yet Willoughby’s hints had seasoned her imagination with dread until every thought tasted of iron. He had offered to tell her more, and she had refused—like someone who cannot bear to hear how an animal is slaughtered, yet cannot stop picturing it.
