What cruelties might not be practiced? Even now, perhaps, at this very hour—on a summer night that ought to have smelled of honeysuckle and cut grass—someone might be—

She checked herself. She could not go that way.

The tide’s small sound kept up its steady work, and in it she heard another water: a Donegal river that had sung in her ears five years ago while she watched a whole night drain into dawn. She hungered for her own country with a fierceness that was bodily—the imagined peat-reek, the damp sweetness of heather, the homely speech that fit the mouth the way a familiar spoon fits the hand. Donegal had always seemed to her a place where one’s very words were cleansed, where the old names held the right weight. Here, among people who spoke smoothly and listened for advantage, she had learned concealments and maneuvers like recipes she despised but must keep making.

And now, longing towards those brown hills and streams, she felt a sudden loathing for the shifts she practiced—and still must practice. Weariness fell on her like a heavy cloak. Yet her resolve did not change. Two lives were not to be spoiled because she lived in the world. Tomorrow she would gather her strength and begin again. Durrance must never know that another man stood before him in her heart.

Inside the drawing-room Durrance’s confession ran to its close, his voice returning to plain statement.

“So you see,” he said, “I could not speak of Harry Feversham until to-night. For I was afraid that what I had to tell you would hurt you very much. I was afraid that you still remembered him, in spite of those five years. I knew, of course, that you were my friend. But I doubted whether in your heart you were not more than that to him. To-night, however, I could tell you without fear.”

Now he waited. Mrs. Adair heard him shift in the gloom.

“Ethne!” he called, surprised at the silence.

No answer.

He rose and walked toward the chair where Ethne had been sitting. Mrs. Adair could see him now as he moved into the moon’s wash. His hands went out and found the chair-back. He bent over it, as though he imagined Ethne leaning forward, hands on knees, stricken by his news.

“Ethne,” he said again, and the second time his voice carried trouble and doubt, the dread that he might find her weeping without sound.

He leaned further, his fingers exploring—and suddenly a violin string twanged loud in the room. Ethne had left the instrument on the chair, and his hand had brushed it. The sound cut the air with a nakedness that made Mrs. Adair’s skin prickle. In that one note was exposure: a thing handled without seeing.

Durrance straightened and stood perfectly still. Then he passed his hand over his forehead once or twice, as if to smooth away the shock, and without calling again he moved to the open window.

Mrs. Adair did not retreat. She held her breath. The sill’s narrow width separated them like a line between two territories.

Moonlight struck Durrance’s face full, and she saw comprehension dawn slowly, as if a picture were coming up in a tray of developing fluid. He sensed her presence.

“Ethne,” he said a third time—appealing now—and stretched out a hand timidly. His fingers touched the fabric of Mrs. Adair’s dress.

“It is not Ethne,” he said, starting back.

“No, it is not Ethne,” Mrs. Adair answered quickly.

He withdrew a step. For a little while he said nothing.

“Where has she gone?” he asked at last.

“Into the garden. She ran across the terrace and down the steps very quickly and silently. I saw her from my chair. Then I heard you speaking alone.”

“Can you see her now?”

“No. She went across the lawn towards the trees and their great shadows. There is only the moonlight in the garden now.”

Durrance stepped over the sill and stood beside Mrs. Adair on the terrace. The final slip Ethne had made betrayed her to the man who had grown quick. There could be only one reason for such sudden, secret flight. He had spoken of Feversham wandering south from Wadi Halfa into savage country; he had spoken his fears without reserve, thinking her indifferent—almost blaming her in his heart for what seemed callousness. But that callousness had been a mask, and she had run because she could not keep it in place.

His first suspicions had been right. Feversham still stood between Ethne and himself, holding them apart.

“She ran as though she was in great trouble and hardly knew what she was doing,” Mrs. Adair added. “Did you cause that trouble?”

“Yes.”

“I thought so, from what I heard you say.”

She wanted to hurt him, and despite his composed face she felt she had done it—a thin compensation for weeks of mortification. There were extenuations, if not defenses, for Mrs. Adair. She too had been overtaxed. Her calm, pale face hid passions that ran hot and sudden, and tonight they had shaken her to the edge. There had been something grotesque—almost horrible—in Durrance’s confession poured out to an empty chair. She had stood outside with a bitter hunger that he should speak like that to her and the bitter certainty that he never would. The irony stung like smoke in the eyes. And at the bottom of it all lay that memory of the lamp extinguished, the voices in the dark—an intimacy she could not buy.

So she struck where she could.

“There’s one thing I don’t understand,” Durrance said. “The change we both noticed in Ethne to-night. I mistook the cause of it, that’s evident. I was a fool. But there must have been a cause. The gift of laughter had been restored to her. Her gravity, her air of calculation, had vanished. She became just what she was five years ago.”

“Exactly,” Mrs. Adair said. “Just what she was before Mr. Feversham disappeared from Ramelton. You are so quick, Colonel Durrance. Ethne had good news of Mr. Feversham this morning.”

Durrance turned sharply toward her. At last—movement, a crack in the polished surface. Mrs. Adair took a small pleasure in it, as one might take pleasure in hearing a silent room finally creak.

“Are you quite sure?” he asked.

“As sure as that you gave her the worst of news to-night.”

He did not need the answer. Another small thing Ethne had said earlier came back to him now—unremarked at the time, now suddenly weighted: her mention that Feversham still drew an allowance from his father. “I heard it only to-day,” she had said.

“Yes,” Durrance murmured. “Ethne heard news of Feversham to-day.” His voice slowed, as if he were turning a hard seed between his teeth. “Did she make a mistake five years ago? There was some wrong thing Harry Feversham was supposed to have done. But was there really more misunderstanding than wrong? Did she misjudge him? Has she to-day learnt that she misjudged him?”

“I will tell you what I know,” Mrs. Adair said. “It is not very much. But I think it is fair that you should know it.”

“Wait a moment, please, Mrs. Adair,” Durrance said sharply.

He had been speaking as much to himself as to her, and suddenly the idea of taking Ethne’s secret from another mouth struck him as treacherous. He walked away to the balustrade and leaned, face toward the garden, listening—to what? Not to voices, but to the night’s fine textures: the tide’s small repetition, the distant leaf-rustle, the absence of wheels and machinery that made every soft sound significant. In such quiet a man’s thoughts spoke loud. He knew why Ethne concealed the truth: she wanted him never to suspect that she loved Harry Feversham still. Yet he could not abandon his conviction that marriage between a crippled man and a vigorous woman could not be right unless both brought more than friendship.

He turned back.

“I am no casuist,” he said. “But here disloyalty seems the truest loyalty of all. Tell me what you know, Mrs. Adair. Something might be done perhaps for Feversham. From Assouan or Suakin something might be done. This news—this good news came, I suppose, this afternoon when I was at home.”

“No,” Mrs. Adair said. “This morning when you were here. It was brought by a Captain Willoughby, who was once an officer in Mr. Feversham’s regiment.”

“He is now Deputy-Governor of Suakin,” Durrance said. “I know the man. For three years we were together in that town. Well?”

“He sailed down from Kingsbridge. You and Ethne were walking across the lawn when he landed from the creek. Ethne left you and went forward to meet him. I saw them meet, because I happened to be looking out of this window at the moment.”

“Yes,” Durrance said. “Ethne went forward. There was a stranger whom she did not know. I remember.”

“They spoke for a few moments, and then Ethne led him towards the trees at once, without looking back—as though she had forgotten,” Mrs. Adair said, and could not resist the sting.

“As though she had forgotten me, you mean,” Durrance said quietly. “No doubt she had.”

She went on. “They went together into the little enclosed garden on the bank.”

Durrance started at that, the smallest jerk betraying him.

“Yes,” Mrs. Adair continued, watching him closely. “You followed them.”

“They were there then,” he said slowly, “on that seat, in the enclosure, all the while.”

She waited, curious, for him to explain how he had missed them—how blindness had been cheated by the place’s shape and hedge and gate. But he gave her nothing.

“Well?” he asked.

“They stayed there a long while. You had gone home across the fields before they came outside into the open. I was in the garden, and indeed happened to be actually upon the bank.”

“So you saw Captain Willoughby. Perhaps you spoke to him?”

“Yes. Ethne introduced him, but she would not let him stay. She hurried him into his boat and back to Kingsbridge at once.”

“Then how do you know Captain Willoughby brought good news of Harry Feversham?”

“Ethne told me they had been talking of him,” Mrs. Adair replied. “Her manner and her laugh showed me no less clearly that the news was good.”

“Yes,” Durrance said, and nodded. Willoughby’s tidings had fermented into that buoyancy and pride he had heard in Ethne’s voice tonight. He had taken the warmth for himself, as a man mistakes the smell of bread for welcome when in truth the oven was lit for another. His very penetration had led him astray.

Mrs. Adair searched his face in the moonlight for anger, for resentment of Ethne’s secrecy, for any bruise she could press. She found none.

“And that is all?” he asked.

“Not quite. Captain Willoughby brought a token from Mr. Feversham. Ethne carried it back to the house in her hand. Her eyes were upon it all the way, her lips smiled at it. I do not think there is anything half so precious to her in all the world.”

“A token?”

“A little white feather,” Mrs. Adair said, “all soiled and speckled with dust. Can you read the riddle of that feather?”

“Not yet,” Durrance answered.

He paced once or twice along the terrace and back, lost in thought, the way a man walks off a shock as if movement could grind it smaller. Then he went into the house and returned with his cap from the hall. Coming back to Mrs. Adair, he paused.

“It was kind of you to tell me this,” he said. “I want you to add to your kindness. When I was in the drawing-room alone and you came to the window—how much did you hear? What were the first words?”

Mrs. Adair’s reply relieved him. Ethne had heard nothing of that confession, nothing of his admission that he had used the pretence of hope to delay their marriage.

“Yes,” he said. “She moved to the window to read a letter by the moonlight. She must have escaped the moment she had read it. Consequently she did not hear that I had no longer any hope of recovering my sight, and that I merely used the pretence of a hope in order to delay our marriage. I am glad of that—very glad.”

He held out his hand and shook hers. “Good-night.”

Then, as if his mind had already stepped away from the terrace into the harsher world of deserts and garrisons, he added absently, “You see—if I hear that Harry Feversham is in Omdurman, something might perhaps be done—from Suakin or Assouan, something might be done. Which way did Ethne go?”

“Over to the water.”

“She had her dog with her, I hope.”

“The dog followed her,” Mrs. Adair said.

“I am glad,” Durrance replied.

He knew, with that quickened hearing of his, what comfort the animal’s silent companionship would be—the soft pad of paws, the warm press at the knee, the steady breathing that steadies one’s own. Perhaps, in the depth of his own distress, he envied the dog.

Mrs. Adair watched him cross the garden to the stile in the hedge. He walked straight and steady along the path like a man who sees. Nothing in his gait or bearing confessed that the one thing he had clung to—his belief in Ethne’s heart—had been taken from him that evening as cleanly as a plate removed from a table. The night’s small natural sounds continued behind him, uncommercial, unadorned, as if the world itself refused to turn tragedy into spectacle.

When Durrance came across the dark fields to Guessens, his body-servant was waiting in the passage with that patient, trained stillness which belongs to a house that has long learned its master’s moods.

“You can put the lights out and go to bed,” Durrance said.

He did not need lamps to find his way to the room they called his study, though the name had always been a jest between the walls. It was less a place of books than of barrels and locks, oil-flasks and leather cases, the faint, clean tang of gun-grease living in the boards as a spice lives in a mortar. In the dark he sat awhile, upright and listening—listening not with the leisure of comfort, but with the taut attention of one who must make a map out of sound: the settling of the old house, the far breath of the creek, the thin ticking of a clock as if it were measuring out not time but limitation.

Then he began to move, gently, as though courtesy were due even to furniture. His hands reached for the scattered cups and goblets he had once won, and the metal met his fingers with different temperatures and weights, each one a remembered note. It was odd—how a polished rim could be recognized by a nick no larger than a grain of salt, how a stem could tell its own story by a twist no eye need witness. He drew a kind of comfort from their shapes, and yet the comfort came with a sting: the trophies were still there, but the shining of them was now only a rumor told to him by other people.

He lifted a thin-stemmed goblet and held it as though it were a warm glass just rinsed and set down in a kitchen after dinner. Colchester came back at once—clouds low and grey, ploughed fields dull between hedges, the sound of hooves striking wet ground with that steady, cooking-pot rhythm of a chase. He set it down in its old place and reached for the pewter on the writing-table, a blunt, homely weight, which had once been a convenient holder for pens when pens had been of use. Oxford returned with it: voices in quadrangles, laughter, the scraped chair-legs of rooms where young men spoke too loudly because they believed time was endless.

On the mantelpiece his fingers found the silver-mounted hoof of a favorite horse. It was cold and smooth and obstinately alive in his palm, as if the animal were still stamping somewhere behind the plaster. All of it together—cup, pewter, hoof—made the room a gigantic diary, and he, blind, read it by touch the way a food historian reads a vanished cuisine from a scorched pan dug out of soil: not merely what was eaten, but who ate it, and with what hunger.

At last his hands came to the racks.

He took down the guns and rifles one by one. They had their own speech, as Ethne’s violin had once spoken—wood and metal that carried a private history in their grain. He sat with a Remington across his knee, and the room filled with a remembered heat: a long day in the hills west of Berenice, sun hammering stone until the air itself tasted of iron, the distant cough of a lion moving through open country. He could feel again, in the old grooves of his hands, the patience of the stalk, the slow tightening of intention, the moment just before sunset when the sight settled and the shot went true.

Another weapon called up the Khor Baraka and his first ibex, the dry, peppered smell of rock, the thin wind that carries sound like a string carries vibration. Another murmured of antelope northward of Suakin. Then the little Greener gun, used on midwinter nights in a boat upon this very creek of Salcombe: salt breath, black water with a skin of starlight, the muffled wing-beat and the sudden heavy drop of the first mallard he had ever brought down. He slid his left hand along the barrel’s underside, a familiar, almost domestic caress, and let the butt settle into the hollow of his shoulder. The gesture was perfect; the world it belonged to was not.

For the weapons began to talk too loudly, as Ethne’s violin had once talked too sharply in those first raw days after Harry Feversham went away and left her alone with notes that pricked like nettles. Each lock he handled, each place his thumb knew by instinct, told him the same truth: he could no longer see the sights. The loss stood up before him, definite as a door barred from the inside.

He put the guns away.

And then, with a suddenness that shook him, he was seized by a desire—not to accept, not to endure, but to defy. To disregard his blindness. To pretend it was no hindrance, and to pretend so fiercely that pretense might harden into fact. The craving rose like thirst and carried him, winged, out of the dim, star-sown country of his narrowed nights straight into the East.

The East came to him first by scent and sound. He smelled hot stone and damp rope, dust and sweat and spices bruised underfoot—cumin, coriander, pepper—odors that clung to cloth the way songs cling to memory. Noise gathered itself into images: domes of mosques under a steel-blue sky, a rabble in streets, vendors calling in tongues whose cadences meant more than their words, because the voice is always the first homeland.

He was no longer in Guessens. He was in Port Said, marshalled in the long procession of steamers down the canal. He heard the Arabs coaling the ship—their chant monotonous as work and as necessary as bread—and in the volume of it he could see them in his mind as plainly as if his eyes were whole: an endless chain of naked figures going up and down planks between barges and deck, lurid in the red glare of braziers. The sound was an ecology of labor: the scrape of coal, the slap of feet, the human voice holding men together against fatigue as salt holds meat from spoiling.

Out of the canal he travelled, past the red headlands of Sinai, into the chill of the Gulf of Suez. The Great Bear swung low above the quarterdeck rail; later the Southern Cross began to blaze in the south. He touched at Tor and at Yambo. He saw Yeddah lift its tall white houses from the sea, admired the dark, brine-withered woodwork of carved casements. In the dusk of its roofed bazaars he walked as the homesick walk when they come back after long years: every sound a familiar utensil placed back in its rightful drawer, every scent a remembered dish brought steaming to the table. From Yeddah he crossed between narrowing coral-reefs into the land-locked harbor of Suakin.

And westward from Suakin stretched the desert—his inheritance and his exile both—full of meanings no map could translate. He heard again the quiet padding of camels’ feet in sand, a soft percussion that soothed even as it desolated. He saw, inwardly, the great rock-cones rising sheer from the plain like islands from a rippleless sea, towards which you march all day and get no nearer. He felt the gorgeous, momentary blaze of sunset in the west; the rustle of wind through the short twilight when the west becomes pure pale green and the east the darkest blue; the planets swooping down out of nothing to the earth with an inevitability that made a man feel small and yet strangely kept.

So he tramped to and fro in his room, forgetful of the dark around him, parched with desire as with fever—until, unexpectedly, the dream broke on the homely sound of England: blackbirds and swallows bustling and piping in the garden. Their thin, bright notes, threaded through dawn air, were as domestic as a kettle beginning to sing. He stood still and knew that outside his windows the world was white with morning.

He woke from the East into his own house, and the knowledge came with a heavy quiet: there were to be no more journeys for him. Affliction had caged him and soldered a chain about his leg. He felt his way by the balustrade up the stairs and went to bed. As the sun rose, he fell asleep.

* * * * *

But at Dongola, on the great curve of the Nile southward of Wadi Halfa, the sun had been blazing for hours and the town was awake.

Under the few palm trees before the house of the Emir Wad El Nejoumi, sport was prepared—holiday cruelty, as ordinary there as the baking of bread might be elsewhere. A white prisoner, captured a week before near the wells of El Agia on the great Arbain road by a party of Arabs, had been brought in during the night and now waited for the Emir’s pleasure. The news flew through the streets like a spark through dry grass. Men, women, children poured towards the palms, drawn by the promise of spectacle as surely as flies come to sweetness.

In front of the trees an open space stretched to the gateway of Nejoumi’s house. Behind them sand sloped down, flat and bare, to the river, which lay there shining and indifferent, carrying away all voices as it carried away all blood.

Harry Feversham stood under the palms, guarded by four Ansar. His clothes had been stripped; he wore only a torn, ragged jibbeh and a twist of cotton on his head to shield him from the sun. His bare shoulders and arms were scorched and blistered as meat blisters when held too close to fire. His ankles were fettered; his wrists bound with a rope of palm fibre. An iron collar was locked about his neck with a chain attached, and one soldier held the chain like a leash.

Feversham stood and smiled at the crowd that mocked him, and seemed well pleased—like a lunatic. It was the part he had chosen. If he could sustain it—if he could baffle them into uncertainty whether he was truly daft or an agent carrying promises of help and arms to the disaffected tribes of Kordofan—then there was a chance they would fear to dispose of him themselves and would send him on to Omdurman. It was hard work. Madness, to be believed, must be performed with a steady hand.

Inside the house the Emir and his counsellors debated his fate. On the river-bank, within Feversham’s view, a high gallows stood black and stark against the yellow sand.

He was very glad of the chain about his neck and the fetters on his legs. They helped him betray no panic by assuring him of its futility. The waiting—while the sun climbed and no one came from the gateway—was the worst he had endured. In Berber, through the fortnight, a hope of escape had sustained him; when the lantern shone on him from behind in ruined acres, what had to be done was done so quickly there was no time for fear. Here there was time, and too much of it. Time to anticipate, to see beforehand.

His heart sank until he was faint, as in those distant days when he had heard hounds scuffling and whining in covert and had sat shaking on his horse. He glanced furtively at the gallows and foresaw vultures perched upon his shoulders, fluttering around his eyes. Yet the man had grown during his years of probation. The fear of physical suffering was not uppermost; nor even the dread that he would walk unmanfully to the scaffold. A greater fear gnawed at him: if he died now, here at Dongola, Ethne would never take back the fourth feather, and his fierce hope of the afterwards would never be fulfilled.

Again he was glad of collar and fetters. He summoned his wits and, alone amid enemies, laughed and scraped and grimaced at his tormentors as though their hatred were a clumsy joke told in a foreign accent.

An old hag danced before him, gesticulating, singing a monotonous song. Her gestures were pantomime and menace—promises of mutilation. The words, in a plainness that needed no translation, described the death agonies awaiting him and the tortures of hell thereafter. Feversham understood; something in him shuddered as a body shudders at the smell of blood. But he only imitated her, nodded and mowed as though she were singing of Paradise.

Others pressed close, lifted their war-trumpets, and placed the mouths against his ears. They blew with all their might. The noise was not music but violence—sound used as a club, an acoustic assault that made the air itself bruise. A child danced with delight.

“Do you hear, Kaffir?” it cried. “Do you hear our ombeyehs? Blow louder! Blow louder!”

Feversham clapped his hands, crying out that the music was good, as though the blasting were a wedding tune.

At last a tall warrior came forward with a long, heavy spear. A cry rose, and a space was cleared as for a game. The warrior poised the spear and swung it backward and forward to supple his arm, like a bowler preparing to deliver at cricket. Feversham glanced wildly about; seeing no escape, he suddenly flung out his breast to meet the blow.

But the spear never reached him. As the warrior lunged, one of the guards jerked Feversham’s neck-chain violently from behind, and the prisoner was flung half throttled on his back. Three times—each time to a roar of delight—the pastime was repeated.

Then a soldier appeared in the gateway of Nejoumi’s house.

“Bring him in!” he cried.

Dragged under the arch, across a courtyard, into a dark room, Feversham could see nothing at first. Then his eyes adapted to the gloom. He distinguished a tall bearded man sitting upon an angareb, and two others squatting beside him on the ground. The man on the angareb was the Emir.

“You are a spy of the Government from Wadi Halfa,” Nejoumi said.

“No, I am a musician,” Feversham returned, and laughed happily, like a man who has made a jest and is pleased with his own wit.

Nejoumi made a sign. An instrument with many broken strings—a zither—was handed to the captive. Feversham sat on the ground, bent over it. His fingers moved slowly, fumbling, as though the strings were threads he must find by feel. He breathed hard. Then, coaxed from what was nearly wreckage, a wavering melody emerged.

It was the tune Durrance had listened to in the street of Tewfikieh on the eve of his last journey into the desert; the tune Ethne Eustace had played only the night before in the quiet drawing-room at Southpool. It was the only melody Feversham knew. In that dim room at Dongola, it sounded thin, like a remembered language spoken by someone with an accent—yet it was still itself, stubbornly itself, untranslatable into any other.

When he had done, Nejoumi began again.

“You are a spy.”

“I have told you the truth,” Feversham answered, stubborn.

Nejoumi changed his tone. He called for food, and they set before Feversham the raw liver of a camel, covered with salt and red pepper. The smell of blood rose warm and metallic; the pepper stung the nostrils; the salt lay upon it like frost. Seldom has a man had less inclination to eat, but Feversham ate nonetheless—even that. He knew reluctance would be construed as fear, and fear might condemn him. He chewed with deliberation, forcing the body to obey the role, as one might force down a harsh ration because pride will not permit refusal.

While he ate, Nejoumi questioned him in the silkiest voice about the fortifications of Cairo, the strength of the garrison at Assouan, rumours of dissension between the Khedive and the Sirdar.

To each question Feversham returned the same answer, as if it were a refrain learned by heart.

“How should a Greek know of these matters?”

Nejoumi rose from the angareb and gave an order, rough now.

The soldiers seized Feversham and dragged him back into the sunlight. Water was poured upon the palm-rope that bound his wrists so that the thongs swelled and bit into the flesh, tightening as dough tightens when it is worked and left to set. Pain sprang bright and immediate.

“Speak, Kaffir. You carry promises to Kordofan.”

Feversham said nothing. He clung doggedly to the plan he had pondered so long and carefully. He could not improve upon it now by any alteration suggested by fear at a moment when his mind could not afford to wobble.

A rope was flung about his neck. He was pushed and driven beneath the gallows.

“Speak, Kaffir,” said Nejoumi. “So shall you escape death.”

Feversham smiled and grimaced and shook his head loosely from side to side, as though all were a jest between friends. It astonished him that he could do it—that he did not fall to his knees and beg. More astonishing still, he felt no temptation to. His heart beat very fast, but with a kind of excitement, like a man about to step into cold water.

He did not even think of Ethne then; the great dread that his hope might die with him did not trouble him. He had an allotted part to play, and he played it. That was all.

Nejoumi looked at him sourly. He turned to the men ready to draw away the angareb on which Feversham stood.

“To-morrow,” he said, “the Kaffir shall go to Omdurman.”

Only then did Feversham feel, clearly and fiercely, how the palm-fibre rope tortured his wrists.

Mrs. Adair sat with her needlework in her lap and a restlessness in her mind that no stitch could tame. She had spoken—too much, perhaps—to Durrance; she had laid bare the mistake he had made, and now she waited for the price of it. If he chose to be plain with Ethne, if he admitted he had learned the truth—that Ethne’s tenderness for him was a dutiful counterfeit, and her real love ran, like a hidden spring, toward Harry Feversham—then no care in the telling would keep Ethne’s quick, honest gaze from turning upon Mrs. Adair and demanding, in that clear voice of hers, why.

At first, no storm broke. Day followed day in Devonshire with a surface calm that seemed almost indecent. Ethne and Durrance met, spoke, even laughed, as though the strange landing of Willoughby at the garden and that evening of the Musoline Overture—Ethne’s bow biting and singing on the violin while the house listened—had never happened. Mrs. Adair drew a breath, grateful; then, as fear ebbed, a sharper discomfort took its place. Had her interference done nothing? Was the tragedy to continue by mere politeness?

But Durrance had decided quickly, and with the hard gentleness of a man who has learned to spare others because he cannot spare himself. Ethne had wished him not to know. Somewhere in her distress she had clung to one consoling thought—that she had brought happiness, or something like it, to the friend who sat beside her in darkness and spoke to her with so much steady cheer. Durrance understood that, and for the present he let her keep it. There were things he needed first: to know whether Ethne and Feversham were separated by a misunderstanding that might yet be mended, or by a break no courage could bridge; and—more urgently—to learn what had become of Feversham.

So he feigned ignorance. He even abandoned that habit of attention which had once made Ethne feel as though she were forever under a quiet scrutiny. Now he forced an ease upon himself; he made light of his blindness; he turned his misfortune into a topic for smiling courage, and his voice was so naturally composed that Ethne, listening, believed herself safe.

“You see,” he said one morning as they sat where the terrace looked down upon the creek, “one can get used to blindness and take it as the natural thing. But one does not get used to you, Ethne. Each time one meets you, one discovers something new and fresh to delight one. Besides, there is always the possibility of a cure.”

The reward of that careful gaiety was plain. Ethne’s face softened; she took it as proof that he had laid aside suspicion. She began to lean upon his cheerfulness as against her own misery, as a person in a rough sea may lean upon a plank, not because it saves, but because it keeps the mouth above water.

For her misery had returned in full. If she had once, for a single day, seized again the lightness that was hers before the three white feathers fell upon Ramelton like cold ash, she had now slipped back into those long nights of wakefulness when the head burns and the dawn comes grey and indifferent. Yet the grief had changed. Pride, at least, was no longer crushed; and Durrance’s hint—spoken as though it were a mere possibility—had planted a thin green shoot of hope. Harry might be rescued. Harry might still come back. Because she could not pretend any longer that he was simply a friend who had died. He was alive; and she dreaded what that life must be, and yet thirsted to know it, as one thirsts for water that may be brackish but is water still.

Now and then impatience broke through her careful restraint.

“I suppose that escape is possible from Omdurman,” she said one day, smoothing her voice into indifference.

“Possible? Yes, I think so,” Durrance replied, readily. “Of course it is difficult and would in any case take time. Attempts have been made, for instance, to get Trench out, and others, but the attempts have not succeeded. The difficulty is the go-between.”

Ethne’s eyes turned sharply upon him.

“The go-between?” she asked; then, as understanding leapt into place with a painful swiftness, she added, “I think I begin to understand,” and stopped too abruptly.

“You mean the Arab who can come and go between Omdurman and the Egyptian frontier?” she asked, after a beat.

“Yes. Usually some Dervish pedlar or merchant trading with the tribes of the Soudan. He slips into Wadi Halfa or Assouan or Suakin and undertakes the work. His risk is great; he would have short shrift in Omdurman if his business were detected. So it isn’t surprising he shirks the danger at the last moment. As often as not, too, he is a rogue. You make arrangements with him in Egypt and hand him the money. In six months or a year he comes back alone, with excuses. It was summer, and the season unfavourable for an escape. Or the prisoners were more strictly guarded. Or he himself was suspected. And he needs more money. Perhaps the tale is true, and you give more. Then again he comes back alone.”

Ethne nodded slowly, as if to say: yes—exactly. Something in her mind, which had been a tangle of fear and conjecture, straightened. She had been sure—without understanding how—that Harry Feversham meant to help Colonel Trench; and she could not see how his own capture could serve that aim. Now she could. He would be his own go-between. He would move between captivity and freedom by his own daring, carrying messages, laying plans, bribing where bribery was possible. The thought gave her hope a sudden strength, because Harry’s habits—his cold self-discipline, his precision—would make him plan with care. He would not be content to save another man and leave his own token of shame hanging in her mind. If he could bring Trench out, he would not stay behind.

They sat on the terrace while the sunset reddened the creek like spilled wine. The garden below lay in English quiet—trim grass, moist earth, the faint clean smell of water and late flowers. A thrush called, then stopped, and the pause that followed had the soft authority of peace.

“Life would not be easy, I suppose, in the prison of Omdurman,” Ethne said, again as though she did not care.

“Easy!” Durrance exclaimed. “No. A hovel crowded with Arabs, without light or air, the roof perhaps two feet above your head, locked up from sundown to morning. Very likely men have to stand all night, so closely packed are they. Imagine it even here in England, on an evening like this. Think what it would be on an August night in the Soudan—especially if you had memories of a place like this to make the torture worse.”

Ethne looked over the cool garden as though it reproached her by its mere existence. At this very moment Harry Feversham might be fighting for breath in darkness, throat dry, skin fevered, with visions of Ramelton’s slopes and, in his inner ear, the liquid music of the Lennon River.

“One would pray for death,” Ethne said slowly, “unless—” She had almost said, unless one went there deliberately with a fixed thing to do, unless duty were a rope under the hands; but she cut the sentence.

Durrance finished it for her.

“Unless there was a chance of escape,” he said. “And there is a chance—if Feversham is in Omdurman.”

He feared he had painted too fierce a picture and tried to soften it.

“Of course what I’ve described is hearsay. We don’t know. Prisoners may not have such bad times as we think.”

He let the subject fall, and Ethne did not raise it again. Sometimes she wondered what explanation he had found for her abrupt disappearance from the drawing-room on that night when he had spoken of meeting Harry at Wadi Halfa. But he never mentioned it, and she had sense enough to follow his silence. The change in him—the absence of that cautious attention which had once distressed her—lulled her fear. He had, it seemed, discovered some simple, natural story for himself.

And sometimes she asked herself why he had told her of Wadi Halfa at all, and of Harry’s departure to the south. She made an explanation for it that was strange but comforting: that the news had been meant for her, and for her alone; that Durrance had only been the instrument of a will above him. His real reason she had never stayed to hear.

So through September the pretence was maintained. Each morning, if Durrance was in Devonshire, he crossed the fields to The Pool. Mrs. Adair watched them as they talked and laughed without apparent embarrassment or estrangement, and her anger grew, not the blazing anger of a quarrelsome spirit, but the tight, righteous anger of a woman who feels herself trapped into complicity. The month strained all three. And when Durrance went to London to see his oculist, it was a relief so palpable it almost seemed shameful.

Those visits became more frequent and longer. Even Ethne was grateful. In his absence she could lay down the mask for a few days; she could be tired; she could sit alone and gather strength as one gathers fuel against a hard winter. There were hours when despair came upon her with its blunt question.

Shall I be able to keep up the pretence when we are married—when we are always together?

She pushed the thought away as if it were an open flame too near dry cloth. She dared not look forward; even now, she felt, her strength might fail.

After his third visit to London, Durrance said to her quietly, “Do you remember that I once mentioned a famous oculist at Wiesbaden? It seems advisable that I should go to him.”

“You are recommended to go?” Ethne asked.

“Yes—and to go alone.”

Ethne looked up with a quick, shrewd glance, as though she were weighing not only his words but the spaces between them.

“You think that I should be dull at Wiesbaden,” she said.

“There is no fear of that. I can rout out some relative to go with you.”

“No; it is on my own account,” he answered. “I may have to go into a home. It is better to be quite quiet and to see no one for a time.”

“You are sure?” Ethne’s voice tightened. “It would hurt me if I thought you proposed this because you believed I would be happier at Glenalla.”

“No,” he replied, and in that he spoke truth. Separation was necessary—for both. He suffered, no less than she, under the tyranny of perpetual simulation. Only because he knew how much she clung to the resolve not to spoil two lives because of her did he keep himself from crying out that he knew everything.

“I am returning to London next week,” he added, “and when I come back I shall be able to tell you whether I am to go to Wiesbaden or not.”

He had reason afterward to be glad he had spoken of his plan before any news arrived. When Calder’s telegram came from Wadi Halfa, Ethne could not connect the thought of his leaving with the receipt of news about Feversham.

The telegram came one afternoon. In the evening Durrance carried it across to The Pool and, with a new delicacy born from suffering and long thought, contrived that Ethne should read it alone. He moved away as soon as he gave it to her, turning toward the drawing-room where Mrs. Adair sat with a book. He had even folded the slip so that by the time Ethne unfolded it she was by herself on the terrace, with the open air about her.

There were only four words:

Feversham imprisoned at Omdurman.

The September evening lay hushed upon fields and creek. A light mist rose and crept over the garden bank and across the lawn. Far away, in the fierce country where the Niles join, the prison doors would already be shut for the night.

Ethne remembered Durrance’s description and her imagination enlarged it until it seemed she could smell the sour closeness of breath and sweat, feel heat pressing like a hand upon the face.

“He is to pay for his fault ten times over, then,” she cried, revolting against the disproportion. “And the fault was his father’s—and mine too—more than his own. For neither of us understood.”

She thought of the fourth feather she had given in her bitterness, and shame rose like a physical sickness. She leaned on the stone balustrade and closed her eyes. Was Harry alive now? Would he outlive this night? The coolness of the stone under her palms felt like accusation: smooth, safe, English—while he choked in a den under an African roof.

“Something can now be done,” said Durrance, and she heard his steps before his voice. He had come from the window of the drawing-room, speaking as he approached so as not to startle her.

“He was and is my friend; I cannot leave him there. I shall write to-night to Calder. Money will not be spared. He is my friend, Ethne—you will see. From Suakin or from Assouan something will be done.”

He placed the offered help to the credit of friendship alone, as though Ethne had no deeper concern. It was a mercy, and also an iron cruelty, and she could not bear either for long.

She turned suddenly, almost cutting across his words.

“Major Castleton is dead?” she asked.

“Castleton?” Durrance repeated, startled. “There was a Castleton in Feversham’s regiment. Is that the man?”

“Yes. He is dead?”

“He was killed at Tamai.”

“You are sure—quite sure?”

“He was within the square of the Second Brigade, on the edge of the great gulley, when Osman Digna’s men sprang out of the earth and broke through. I was in that square too. I saw Castleton killed.”

“I am glad,” said Ethne.

She said it plainly, without flourish, as if she had spoken of a gate being shut. The first feather had been brought back by Willoughby; it was possible, she thought, that Trench might bring back the second. Harry had succeeded once under peril; perhaps he might succeed again, though the peril now was greater and the difficulties heavier. Her faith, stubborn and unreasoning as roots in stone, held to that. But she was relieved—coldly relieved—to know the sender of the third feather could never be approached. Moreover she hated him; and that, to her mind, ended the matter.

Durrance stared. He was not of the braggart kind, not the loud talker who loves war as an occasion for big words. There was in him the quiet severity of a man who does his work without noise, and whose compassion is deep because it is disciplined. This hard little barbarism from Ethne he could not understand.

“You disliked Major Castleton so much?” he asked.

“I never knew him.”

“Yet you are glad that he is dead?”

“I am quite glad,” Ethne replied, stubbornly.

It was another slip. And Durrance did not let it pass into nothing. He carried it away and turned it over afterward in his gun-room at Guessens, fitting it into the explanation that had been slowly shaping itself under the sharpened wits which blindness had forced upon him.

Captain Willoughby’s visit and the token he brought had given him a clue. A white feather could mean only one thing—cowardice. Yet Durrance could not remember ever seeing cowardice in Harry Feversham; the charge still startled him into incredulity. But something had happened on the night of the ball at Lennon House, and from that hour Feversham had been an outcast.

Suppose a white feather had been sent to Lennon House and opened in Ethne’s presence—perhaps more than one. Ethne, Mrs. Adair had told him, had come back from her long talk with Willoughby holding that feather as though nothing in the world were so precious. It followed that in one particular the accusation had been answered; the cowardice, in one fashion, atoned. Ethne’s return to youth—her sudden lightness—pointed that way.

But Feversham had not returned. He still moved in by-ways like a man who cannot yet come into the open. Then Willoughby was not the only accuser; there had been others—two others. One of them Durrance had already identified. When he had suggested Omdurman, Ethne had answered at once, “Colonel Trench is in Omdurman,” as though she needed no explanation of Harry’s disappearance from Wadi Halfa into the southern Soudan. It had been deliberate: he had gone to be captured, to be taken to Omdurman. And her quick understanding of the go-between had helped Durrance further. There was an obligation on Feversham to come to Trench’s help. Perhaps he had ventured out into the desert to become his own go-between, to rescue Trench and so bring back the second feather.

And now, to-night, Major Castleton joined himself in Durrance’s mind to Trench and Willoughby. Ethne’s satisfaction at the death of a man she had never met could mean only one thing: there would have been the same obligation upon Feversham towards Castleton if he had lived. It seemed likely a third feather had come—and that Castleton had sent it.

So the story grew clearer, joint by joint, like a weapon assembled in the dark by touch alone. There remained one man who could tell him the truth and had refused—who would no doubt refuse still. Yet that man’s help Durrance meant to enlist. And to enlist it, he must come not begging for explanation, but speaking as though he already knew.

“Yes,” he said aloud to the emptiness of the gun-room, “I think that after my next visit to London I can pay a visit to Lieutenant Sutch.”

Captain Willoughby had the sort of voice that could fill a room the way steam fills a scullery: everywhere at once, dampening talk, softening resistance, leaving only the slow drip of endurance. At his club he was famous—famous in the specific, weary way a bad smell becomes famous in a kitchen. He told stories about people nobody knew, threaded with maxims nobody wanted, and he delivered each stale morsel as if it had just come sizzling from a pan.

Men who had learned the sound of him—his approaching tread, the clearing of his throat, the first confident, meaningless “I remember a fellow—”—would lift their papers and flee. There was a small choreography to it, as practiced as a dining-room drill: chairs scraping, murmured excuses, a sudden need to see a man about a horse. Willoughby always chose the thickest knot of listeners, like a wasp choosing the sweetest fruit, and within half an hour the most popular corner would be empty but for him.

On an early October afternoon he sat in possession of his usual kingdom of deserted leather chairs and stale cigar-smoke. The club’s air had that familiar mixture—polished wood, old wool, extinguished matches, and tobacco that had seeped into the curtains over decades like a sauce reduced too far and clung to the bottom of the pan. Outside, London’s modern racket—iron wheels, shouted news, the ceaseless grind of traffic—pressed at the windows. Inside, the room’s quiet had a gentler soundscape: pages turned, a glass set down, the soft consonants of men trying not to be overheard.

A waiter crossed to him and offered a visiting card.

Willoughby took it with greedy relief. Company was a kind of sustenance to him; he could not taste his own talk unless someone sat opposite to swallow it. But the name on the card drained his eagerness as if someone had sluiced cold water through a warm cup.

“Colonel Durrance!” he said, scratching his head.

Durrance had never, in all the years of acquaintance, sought him out for friendliness. If Durrance came now, he came for a reason; and Willoughby’s mind, slow as it was, could still sniff danger the way a cook sniffs milk on the turn.

“Does Colonel Durrance know I am in the club?” he asked.

“Yes, sir.”

“Very well. Show him in.”

He told himself, with a stubborn little pride, that he had no intention of meddling further in Miss Ethne Eustace’s affairs. Feversham and Durrance could fight their own campaign. Willoughby had already gone as far as he meant—too far, perhaps. He did not doubt his own diplomacy; he only disliked spending it in this particular currency.

Durrance entered with the quiet assurance of a man who has learned to measure rooms by sound rather than sight. He moved as if he were listening to the air’s contours—the hum of the place, the soft footfalls, the angle of a voice—and Willoughby, watching him, felt that uncomfortable sensation of being mapped without being seen.

Willoughby rose and guided him to the empty corner.

“Will you smoke?” he began, then stopped, remembering, and flushed. “I beg your pardon.”

“Oh, I’ll smoke,” Durrance answered easily. “It’s not quite true that a man can’t enjoy his tobacco without seeing the smoke of it. If I let my cigar go out I should know at once. But you will see—I shall not let it out.”

He took his time striking a match, shielding the flame, bringing it to the cigar as carefully as if he were searing the surface of meat to seal in juices. The first draw was measured; the ember caught; a faint crackle like dry leaves sounded at the tip. He leaned back, face angled upward, as if he could read the ceiling’s patterns by memory.

“I am lucky to find you, Willoughby,” he said. “I am only in town for to-day. I come up from Devonshire now and then to see my oculist, and I was anxious to meet you if I could. On my last visit Mather told me you were away in the country. You remember Mather? He was with us in Suakin.”

Willoughby’s relief was immediate. Mather was safe ground: the talk of campaigns and men and posted orders, none of it pointing like a bayonet at Kingsbridge.

“Of course I remember him,” he said, and meant it. He clung to the name as to a familiar flavour.

“We are both curious,” Durrance continued, “and you can clear up the point we are curious about. Did you ever come across an Arab called Abou Fatma?”

Willoughby repeated the name slowly, as though rolling a seed on his tongue to decide whether it would crack.

“Abou Fatma… one of the Hadendoas?”

“No. A man of the Kabbabish tribe.”

“Abou Fatma,” Willoughby said again. “No, I never came across him.” He stopped there—too abruptly.

The silence that followed had its own sound: the faint hiss of a lamp, the scrape of a chair far away, the soft pressure of London outside. Durrance, trained by necessity to hear what men did not say, noted that unnatural stopping-place as clearly as if it were written on paper.

“That makes me still more curious,” Durrance went on. “Mather and I were together on the last reconnaissance in ’84, and we found Abou Fatma hiding in the bushes by the Sinkat fort. He told us about the Gordon letters which he had hidden in Berber. Ah—you remember his name now.”

“I was merely getting my pipe out of my pocket,” Willoughby said.

“But you do remember the name now that I mention the letters?”

“Yes. I remember it,” Willoughby admitted, and felt, too late, that the admission was a door left ajar.

“They were brought to you in Suakin fifteen months or so back,” Durrance said. “Mather showed me the paragraph in the Evening Standard. I am curious whether Abou Fatma returned to Berber and recovered them. But since you have never come across him, it follows he was not the man.”

A small nausea of regret moved in Willoughby. He had been too quick to deny—too eager to be clean of the name. He tried to patch the hole.

“No. It was not Abou Fatma,” he said, awkwardly, hearing in his own voice that particular strain men use when they are lifting a pot they know is too heavy and praying not to drop it.

Durrance did not pounce. He let the matter sit, as if he were letting flavours settle.

“I wondered,” he said slowly. “I thought Abou Fatma would hardly return to Berber. For whoever undertook the job undertook it at the risk of his life—and since Gordon was dead, for no very obvious reason.”

“Quite so,” Willoughby said quickly, relieved at the retreat.

“For no obvious reason, I think I said,” Durrance remarked, mild as milk, but with a thin edge that could curdle it.

Willoughby glanced at him with suspicion. He wondered how much Durrance knew. But Durrance lay back, smoking, the cigar’s ember brightening and dimming with each measured breath. He seemed to have lost all interest in the Gordon letters now that he had touched them—like a man who, having tested the crust of a loaf, is satisfied there is warmth within and turns to other matters.

Yet Durrance’s mind, unseen, was busy. The past years had taught him to weigh pauses, to listen for caution in a word, to hear the tension in a throat. Ethne Eustace’s voice had once had that same careful accent, that same guardedness, and it stung him now to hear it echoed. He had laid small traps—the phrase about “no obvious reason” among them—and he had felt Willoughby’s tiny starts as surely as a fisherman feels a tremor on the line.

He spoke again, and his voice changed, becoming more deliberate, more intimate.

“I want to tell you why I was anxious to meet you. It was because of Harry Feversham.”

Willoughby jumped in his chair as if a coal had dropped on his knee. Durrance did not acknowledge the movement.

“Something happened to Feversham,” he said. “More than five years ago now. He did something, I suppose—or left something undone. The secret has been closely kept. He dropped out, and his place knew him no more.” He turned his head slightly, listening to Willoughby’s breathing. “Now you are going back to the Soudan?”

“Yes,” Willoughby answered. “In a week’s time.”

“Well, Harry Feversham is in the Soudan,” Durrance said, leaning forward, the chair leather sighing under him.

“You know that?” Willoughby blurted.

“I do. I came across him this spring at Wadi Halfa,” Durrance replied, and he told of the meeting outside the café at Tewfikieh—of the ruin and squalor, the man who had once stood straight and clean among friends now fallen low. He spoke with the flat honesty of someone who has no use for pity as ornament. “It’s strange, isn’t it? A man you knew well going under like that in a second—disappearing before your eyes, dropping plumb out of sight as though down an oubliette in an old French castle.”

Outside, as Durrance spoke, a cart rumbled by, iron-shod wheels biting the stones. London’s noise, constant as a factory, had an indifference that made private grief feel smaller, squeezed thin between clanging days.

“I want you to look out for him, Willoughby,” Durrance said. “Do what you can to set him on his legs again. Let me know if you chance on him. Feversham was a friend of mine—one of my few real friends.”

“All right,” Willoughby said, and his cheerfulness came too easily, like a ladling of sauce meant to hide a burned taste. “I will look out for him. I remember he was a great friend of yours.”

He reached for the matches. The phosphorus scraped; the match flared. He put it to his pipe, sucking at the stem. The briar was well-seasoned but badly in need of cleaning; it bubbled wetly as it drew. The smell of scorched tobacco rose—sharp, sour, like a spice used without care.

“Yes,” Durrance said. “A great friend. You and I dined with him in his flat above St. James’s Park just before we left England.”

At that casual sentence the pipe stopped bubbling, abruptly, as if the breath behind it had been held. There was a moment of stillness. Then Willoughby swore—an ugly, violent oath—and the next instant he stamped on the carpet.

Durrance’s imagination, quickened by sound, painted the scene with cruel precision: Willoughby staring at the blind man’s face, searching it for knowledge; the match burning down unnoticed to the fingers; the curse, the dropped flame, the stamp to kill it. The smallest domestic accident had betrayed a whole hidden panic.

Durrance had not thought of that dinner in years. Now it unfolded in his mind like a cloth pulled from a covered dish, releasing a smell long trapped.

“There were you and I and Feversham present,” he went on, as if he were merely recounting an old menu. “Feversham asked us there to tell us of his engagement to Miss Eustace. He had just come back from Dublin. That was almost the last we saw of him.” He took a pull at the cigar, then added, lightly, “By the way, there was a third man present.”

“Was there?” Willoughby asked too quickly. “It’s so long ago.”

“Yes—Trench.”

“To be sure,” Willoughby said. “Trench was present. It will be a long time, I am afraid, before we dine at the same table with poor old Trench again.”

His carelessness sounded manufactured, like tinned food arranged on a platter. He leaned forward, struck another match, and tried to light his pipe again with elaborate calm.

Durrance laid his cigar along the table edge. The club’s table had the faint tackiness of long-spilled spirits wiped too late.

“And we shall never dine with Castleton again,” Durrance said slowly.

“Castleton wasn’t there,” Willoughby exclaimed, quick enough to show that the dinner was not distant at all in his mind, not softened by time’s cooking.

“No. But he was expected,” Durrance said.

“No—not even expected,” Willoughby corrected. “He was dining elsewhere. He sent the telegram, you remember.”

“Ah, yes,” said Durrance. “A telegram came.”

That small correction, offered so eagerly, landed in Durrance’s mind with the weight of a stone dropped into a well. The water at the bottom was dark, but the splash told him where it was.

Castleton. Trench. Willoughby. The three names circled around Feversham’s disgrace like birds around carrion. Durrance tried to recover the evening in detail: Feversham’s bright room high above the park, the cool darkness outside, the tattoo from Wellington Barracks tapping the air like a heartbeat. He remembered himself leaning by the window, listening, while at the table Feversham read a telegram—read it again—and then, without a word, crumpled it and threw it into the fire.

Now, today, Willoughby had named the sender.

“Castleton was dining with a big man from the War Office that night,” Durrance said, and heard a small movement beside him—Willoughby shifting, heated by the searchlight suddenly turned in his direction.

Durrance, however, did not press harder. He talked on for a little while about the prospects of the Soudan—of routes and garrisons and the old, dry taste of desert wind—until the moment felt safe enough to end. Then he rose.

“Well,” he said, “I can rely on you, Willoughby, to help Feversham if ever you find him. Draw on me for money.”

“I will do my best,” Willoughby answered, and the words came with a swift eagerness, as though generosity might blot out suspicion. “You are going? I could have won a bet off you this afternoon.”

“How?” Durrance asked.

“You said you did not let your cigars go out. This one’s stone cold.”

Durrance’s hand went to the cigar on the table. He had not noticed; or rather, he had noticed only other flames.

“I forgot about it,” he said quietly. “I was thinking of Feversham. Good-bye.”

He took a cab from the club door and drove away.

Willoughby, left behind, exhaled like a man who has finally set down a tray of glasses without dropping one. He congratulated himself on his diplomacy. Poor old Durrance—blind, sentimental, easy to mislead. It would have been strange indeed if Willoughby had not been able to hoodwink him. He returned to the smoking-room and ordered a whiskey and potass, letting the sharp medicinal tang sting his nose and throat as if it could cleanse the air of anxiety.

But Durrance had not been hoodwinked.

As the cab rolled over London’s stones, the city’s noise rose and fell around him—hoofbeats, wheels, distant shouts—an industrial tide that drowned subtle things. Yet within that roar, Durrance’s mind held to the small, decisive sounds of the afternoon: the pipe’s bubbling cut off, the oath, the stamp, the too-quick protest about Castleton. Those were the true words, spoken by accident.

He saw the sequence now complete—not the secret thoughts, not the inexplicable moment of panic, but the chain of facts, linked cleanly one to the next. At the dinner no one had mentioned the sender of the telegram. Feversham had read it in silence, and in silence had fed it to the flames. To-day Willoughby had named Castleton. Castleton had been dining with a high official of the War Office. And Durrance knew, with the cold certainty of a man aligning evidence, that Feversham had not resigned when that telegram came.

The message could have carried only one kind of news: that Feversham’s regiment was ordered on active service.

Almost the next day Feversham had told Durrance in the Row that he had resigned his commission.

The story, at last, stood clear: the act of cowardice, the three white feathers at Ramelton, the disappearance. And beyond it, equally clear now, another truth—harder, simpler, and, to Durrance, of real importance: Harry Feversham was atoning for that one act with a full and overflowing measure of atonement.

Why he had been seized with panic at that particular moment—why, just after his engagement to Ethne Eustace, when every manly quality ought to have been strongest—remained a question that would never be answered for Durrance. It belonged to that region of life he had learned, painfully, to set aside: the private, untranslatable core where motives live beyond any dominant lexicon’s reach, beyond any neat vocabulary of honour or shame. The world might name the act. It could not name the sensation inside it.

“I shall astonish old Sutch,” Durrance thought, and the thought carried a brief, rough chuckle.

That evening he took the night mail down into Devonshire, back toward quieter country sound—hedgerows in wind, wheels softened by earth, the old rural hush that let a man hear himself think. Before midday he reached his home.

Durrance chose the moment of leaving with the same care a cook gives to lifting a soufflé from the oven—no lingering at the door, no chance for the thing to collapse. In the drawing-room at The Pool he said good-bye to Ethne with a brevity that felt almost like a restraint of the senses. Outside, the carriage waited at the steps of Guessens, luggage strapped tight upon the roof; the servant stood ready, as still as a pantryman before a formal dinner.

Ethne came with him onto the terrace. Above the flight of steps Mrs. Adair was stationed, a figure set like a decanter on a sideboard, all polish and purpose. Durrance held his hand out to her; she did not take it. Instead she turned to Ethne.

“I want to speak to Colonel Durrance before he goes.”

“Very well,” Ethne answered. She looked at Durrance then, and for a moment her voice softened as if she were pressing something warm into his palm. “Then we will say good-bye here. You will write from Wiesbaden? Soon, please!”

“The moment I arrive,” he said.

He went down the steps with Mrs. Adair, leaving Ethne on the terrace—alone where, for months, she had so often been made to stand alone in one way or another. The last scene of pretence had been played; the long season of watching and guarded speech was over. Durrance’s relief showed itself in the set of his shoulders and the briskness of his stride as he crossed the lawn. Mrs. Adair, by contrast, let the air hold her back, as though each step were through thick water.

“So you are going,” she said. “In two days’ time you will be at Wiesbaden and Ethne at Glenalla. We shall all be scattered. It will be lonely here.”

She had won what she had laboured for: distance. No longer would she be tormented by the sound of their voices coming together in a room while she stood outside, counting the pauses. And yet the victory tasted flat—like tea gone cold because it has been left too long for someone who never comes.

“The house will seem very empty after you are all gone,” she added; and she turned with him down into the garden.

“We shall come back, no doubt,” Durrance said, as if routine could be relied upon to mend what feeling had torn.

Mrs. Adair looked about her as they walked. The garden had been stripped to its bones. Flowers were gone; sunlight too seemed to have been taken away and put in storage. A blanket of cloud pressed down on the sky, and underfoot the grass had the dull, sodden hue of lettuce left too long in water. The stream ran grey between trees, and the wind sent russet and yellow leaves skittering along the lawns with a dry, whispering scrape—an anxious, incessant little sound that filled the spaces where summer’s bees had been. Even Durrance, sightless as he was, seemed to feel the season through the air’s thin edge and the way the wind worried at the hedges.

“How long shall you stay at Wiesbaden?” she asked.

“I can hardly tell. But as long as it’s advisable.”

“That tells me nothing at all,” she said quickly. “I suppose it was meant not to tell me anything.”

He did not answer. His silence had the clean finality of a lid shut down. It roused her like a sudden clang in a quiet room—one of those noises that make you aware, all at once, of how carefully you have been listening.

“You distrust me?” she demanded, and anger rode through her voice.

He answered gently, almost wearily, as if he had weighed the matter and found no relish in it. “Have I no reason to distrust you? Why did you tell me of Captain Willoughby’s coming? Why did you interfere?”

“I thought you ought to know.”

“But Ethne wished the secret kept. I am glad to know—very glad. But, after all, you told me, and you were Ethne’s friend.”

“Yours, too, I hope,” Mrs. Adair returned, and then, in a sudden rush, “How could I go on keeping silence? Don’t you understand?”

“No.”

He might have understood if he had ever spent much thought on her; but he had not, and she knew it with the same sharp certainty one knows the absence of salt in a broth. That simple word—no—was not merely refusal; it was proof that she had been speaking into a void.

“I spoke brutally, didn’t I?” she said. “I told you the truth as brutally as I could. Doesn’t that help you to understand?”

Again he said, “No,” and the quietness of it struck her like a slap. Something in her broke loose then. She seemed to hear herself from a distance, as though her own voice were an instrument playing too loud in a small room, yet she could not damp it down.

“I told you the truth brutally on purpose. I was so stung because you would not see what was so visible had you only the mind to see. I wanted to hurt you. I am a bad, bad woman, I suppose. There were you and she in the room talking together in the darkness; there was I alone upon the terrace. It was the same again to-day. You and Ethne in the room, I alone upon the terrace. I wonder whether it will always be so. But you will not say—you will not say.”

She struck her hands together, a small crack of sound in the wind, like the snap of a twig. Durrance walked on without answering. The garden path drew them toward the stile at the far end—toward the boundary where cultivated ground gave up to fields. He quickened his pace. Mrs. Adair had to hurry to keep beside him, her skirts catching the breeze with a faint, fretful rustle.

His speed was an answer, but not the one she wanted. Her words kept spilling out, heedless of prudence, as if a pot had boiled over.

“I do not think I would have minded so much,” she went on, “if Ethne had really cared for you; but she never cared more than as a friend cares—just a mere friend. And what’s friendship worth?”

“Something, surely,” Durrance said.

“It does not prevent Ethne from shrinking from her friend,” Mrs. Adair cried. “She shrinks from you. Shall I tell you why? Because you are blind. She is afraid. While I—I will tell you the truth—I am glad. When the news first came from Wadi Halfa that you were blind, I was glad; when I saw you in Hill Street, I was glad; ever since, I have been glad—quite glad. Because I saw that she shrank. From the beginning she shrank, thinking how her life would be hampered and fettered.”

Her voice sank to a whisper, but the whisper was fiercer than a shout, the way a knife is more dreadful than a club. And then she began, over and over, as though insisting could turn desire into fact:

“I am not afraid. I am not afraid. I am not afraid.”

To Durrance, who had known pain in many forms and had learned to take it without flinching, this outburst had a particular horror—because it came from the woman who called herself Ethne’s friend, and because it arrived so wholly unexpected. The wind worried at the trees; leaves rasped and tumbled; somewhere a gate creaked on its hinge. The garden’s small autumn noises seemed suddenly loud, as if the place itself were listening.

“Ethne wrote to you at Wadi Halfa out of pity,” she pressed on. “That was all. She wrote out of pity; and, having written, she was afraid of what she had done; and being afraid, she had not courage to tell you she was afraid. You would not have blamed her if she had frankly admitted it; you would have remained her friend. But she had not the courage.”

Durrance knew another explanation for Ethne’s hesitations and timidities, and he knew it was the true one. But tomorrow he would be gone from Salcombe estuary, and Ethne would be on her way toward Donegal. To argue now against these slanders felt as useless as shouting at the wind to change. Besides, the stile was close—freedom only a few steps away. He said only, quietly, “You are not just to Ethne.”

At that, something snapped back into place inside Mrs. Adair. The wildness went out of her as suddenly as a pan dies when the flame is turned down. She saw—clearly, bitterly—the futility of what she had said. True or false, it could achieve nothing. He was always with Ethne, never with her. She became conscious of her own abasement and began at once to scramble for excuses, as if she might hide the ugly taste with a little sugar.

“I am a bad woman, I suppose. But after all, I have not had the happiest of lives. Perhaps there is something to be said for me.”

It sounded feeble even to her. But they had reached the stile. Durrance stopped and turned toward her. The sternness in his face softened, the way a hard line in a voice can soften when a person hears, beneath the words, the human need. In the old days, when he could see, he had always associated Mrs. Adair with dignity and reticence. It seemed scarcely possible that the woman before him—breathing too quickly, voice unsteady—was the same.

“Will you tell me?” he asked, gently.

“I was married almost straight from school,” she began. “I was the merest girl. I knew nothing, and I was married to a man of whom I knew nothing. It was my mother’s doing, and no doubt she thought she was acting for the very best. She was securing for me a position of a kind, and comfort and release from any danger of poverty. I accepted what she said blindly, ignorantly. I could hardly have refused, indeed, for my mother was an imperious woman, and I was accustomed to obedience. I did as she told me and married dutifully the man whom she chose. The case is common enough, no doubt, but its frequency does not make it easier of endurance.”

“But Mr. Adair?” Durrance said. “After all, I knew him. He was older, no doubt, than you, but he was kind. I think, too, he cared for you.”

“Yes. He was kindness itself, and he cared for me. Both things are true. The knowledge that he did care for me was the one link, if you understand.” She paused as though listening to her own life for its rhythm, then went on. “At the beginning I was contented, I suppose. I had a house in town and another here. But it was dull,” and she stretched her arms outward, as if she could not bear the smallness of what she had been given. “Oh, how dull it was!

“Do you know the little back streets in a manufacturing town? Rows of small houses, side by side, with nothing to relieve them of their ugly regularity—each with the self-same windows, the self-same door, the self-same doorstep. Overhead a drift of smoke, and every little green thing, down to the plants in the window, dirty and black. The sort of street whence any crazy religious charlatan who can promise a little colour to their grey lives can get as many votaries as he wants. Well, when I thought over my life, one of those little streets always came into my mind.”

As she spoke, the air around them was full of its own small particulars: the steady shush of wind in bare branches, the stream’s low, tired murmur, the far-off clatter of a farm cart on a road beyond the fields. It was a country soundscape, honest and unforced—nothing like the harsh, metallic insistence of the city that drives thought into corners. Here, each noise seemed to leave room for feeling, rather than crowd it out.

“There are women—heaps of them, no doubt—to whom the management of a big house, the season in London, the ordinary round of visits, are sufficient,” she continued. “I, worse luck, was not one of them. Dull! You, with your hundred thousand things to do, cannot conceive how oppressively dull my life was. And that was not all!”

She hesitated, but she had gone too far to retreat with any dignity left intact.

“I married, as I say, knowing nothing of the important things. I believed at the first that mine was just the allotted life of all women. But I began soon to have my doubts. I got to know that there was something more to be won out of existence than mere dullness—at least, that there was something more for others, though not for me. One could not help learning that. One passed a man and a woman riding together, and one chanced to look into the woman’s face as one passed; or one saw, perhaps, the woman alone and talked with her for a little while, and from the happiness of her looks and voice one knew with absolute certainty that there was ever so much more. Only the chance of that ever so much more my mother had denied to me.”

Durrance’s face had lost its hardness entirely. Mrs. Adair was no longer violent; she was simple, speaking as one speaks when the performance of life drops away and only the plain story remains.

“And then you came,” she said. “I met you, and met you again. You went away upon your duties and you returned; and I learnt now, not that there was ever so much more, but just what that ever so much more was. But it was still, of course, denied to me. However, in spite of that I felt happier. I thought that I should be quite content to have you for a friend, to watch your progress, and to feel pride in it. But you see—Ethne came, too, and you turned to her. At once—oh, at once! If you had only been a little less quick to turn to her! In a very short while I was sad and sorry that you had ever come into my life.”

“I knew nothing of this,” Durrance said. “I never suspected. I am sorry.”

“I took care you should not suspect,” Mrs. Adair replied. “But I tried to keep you; with all my wits I tried. No match-maker in the world ever worked so hard to bring two people together as I did to bring together Ethne and Mr. Feversham—and I succeeded.”

The words struck him with the shock of a door flung open on a room you had forgotten existed. Durrance leaned back against the stile, and a laugh rose in him—not from mirth, but from the stunned recognition of how lives are seasoned by another hand. Here, then, was the origin of the whole sad business. From what small beginnings it had grown. A nudge here, an arranged meeting there—like altering a recipe by a pinch—and the result carried forward for years: disgrace and ruin, unhappiness, pretence piled upon pretence until it became habit. He thought himself backward into those days when he walked on his own path, heedless, not imagining that other people were at work around him, turning choices as easily as one turns a page.

“I succeeded,” Mrs. Adair repeated. “You told me I had succeeded one morning in the Row. How glad I was! You did not notice it, I am sure. The next moment you took all my gladness from me by telling me you were starting for the Soudan. You were away three years. They were not happy years for me. You came back. My husband was dead, but Ethne was free. Ethne refused you, but you went blind and she claimed you. You can see what ups and downs have fallen to me. But these months here have been the worst.”

“I am very sorry,” said Durrance.

It was plain to him now that there was, indeed, something to be said for her. The world had not dealt gently with Mrs. Adair; it had given her comfort without colour, position without language for what she needed, and then, at the end, had tempted her with the sound of a happiness she could not enter. He could understand her jealousy because he carried his own version of it—his own ache at being close to what he could not hold. He could not excuse what she had done, but he could be gentle with the suffering beneath it.

“I am very sorry, Mrs. Adair,” he repeated, because he had no more fitting words. Then he held out his hand.

“Good-bye,” she said, and this time her voice did not strike; it simply fell, tired.

He climbed over the stile and crossed the fields to his house. Behind him Mrs. Adair remained, standing by the boundary between garden and open land, listening to his footsteps diminish—first distinct on the nearer ground, then softened by distance, until the wind and the stream took back the air for themselves. She had shot her bolt and hit no one but herself and the man she cared for. That much she understood with painful clarity.

And she looked forward, too, seeing the future in the way one sees tomorrow’s weather in the present sky. If Durrance did not keep Ethne to her promise—did not marry her, did not go with her to her country—then he would come back to Guessens. The thought made her folly sharper. If she had kept silence, she might have had a true, constant friend near at hand, a steady presence like a hearth that warms a house through winter. It would have been something—more than something. But since she had spoken, they could never meet without embarrassment; and however carefully they might practise cordiality, there would always remain between them the memory of what she had said and what he had listened to, on the afternoon when he left for Wiesbaden.

Calder’s furlough began with the Nile and a taste in his mouth like metal—sun-baked, inevitable. The steamer slid southward of Wadi Halfa toward Assouan, and the river’s broad, patient body took all the glare the sky could pour into it, swallowing light and giving it back in hard flashes. Along the banks the land showed a thin seam of green—palms, a few plots clawed out of sand—then immediately the merciless ochre rose again in blunt pyramids of rock. Even the wind, when it came, arrived dry and scouring, worrying at canvas and rope as if it resented softness.

He leaned over the upper rail, listening. The steamer’s engines beat a steady, manufactured pulse, but beneath that there were older sounds: the water slapping the hull with a thick, intimate smack; the creak of timbers; a distant cry from the shore that rose and fell in a pattern he could not parse.
