All of it made a kind of music—a soundscape that ought to have steadied a man—if only it were not constantly pierced by the harsher noises of neglect: a cough left unattended, a shouted joke thrown like a stone, laughter too loud for the narrow strip of living earth that clung to the river’s edge.

Lashed alongside rode a barge crowded with native passengers. On its lower deck stood an angareb—matting stretched tight across a four-legged frame—bearing a human figure laid out straight as a board and shrouded in a black veil. Two Arabs who had carried it aboard at Korosko sat in the stern, chattering and laughing as if the bundle on the bedstead were no more than spare baggage.

It might have been a corpse. It might have been a woman. The veil lay so close that it mapped the nose and the hollows of the eyes and mouth; it did not tell him whether a moustache shadowed the lip, whether a beard roughened the chin. The body did not shift. Not a toe flexed beneath the cloth. The veil did not lift with breath.

The sun, slanting toward afternoon, crept across the deck inch by inch like a slow blade. The people nearest the angareb edged away into whatever shadow the upper deck granted, but no one moved the bedstead itself. The two in the stern did not so much as glance at their charge.

Calder watched the light climb the shrouded figure from the feet upward, turning the black cloth into a punishing heat-trap. When at last it burned full on the face beneath the veil and still the body did not stir, something in him tightened—the soldier’s familiarity with suffering, and the civilized man’s intolerance of pointless cruelty.

He shouted down at the men in the stern.

“Move the angareb into the shade,” he called. “At once!”

They rose with a slowness that was almost defiance, yet his voice carried authority. They dragged the bedstead back under the upper deck’s shelter.

“Is it a man or a woman?” Calder asked.

“A man,” one answered. “We take him to the hospital at Assouan. He will not live. He fell from a palm-tree three weeks ago.”

“You give him nothing? No water?”

“He is too ill.”

Too ill. The phrase had the neatness of a proverb: an answer that closed the mouth of argument, a fatalism passed from tongue to tongue until it hardened into law. Calder had heard it before, in different dialects, under different suns. It always meant the same thing: what happens will happen; therefore do nothing. He thought of what would have cured a bruise in a week—washing, clean cloth, a little cooling drink with salt, a compress, a hand that touched a wound as if life mattered. Instead, this man had been left lying as he lay now, face to flies, flesh to heat, thirst unreckoned. A bruise would have become a sore; a sore would have rotted. Then, when it was too late for simple remedies, the Mudir at Korosko—stirred perhaps by a scrap of conscience, perhaps by the fear of paperwork—had shipped him downstream like freight.

The story was common, but the stillness of the veiled man would not leave Calder’s mind. It held him with a kind of dreadful fascination. When evening came and a strong wind sprang up against the current, raising chop and white-edged waves, Calder felt a grim comfort: at least the air would cool that hidden face; at least the flies would be driven off.

At dinner, the saloon smelled of boiled meat and onions softened into their own sweetness, of damp tablecloth, of coffee that had been kept too long on the heat. The talk was the usual travel-talk—Cairo, temples, permits, the petty tyranny of officials. Calder ate without appetite, listening less to the clink of cutlery than to the different tempo of the river outside: the wind worrying at the deck fittings, the water’s slap sharpening as the steamer took the waves.

His neighbour spoke with a German accent. He was young, spectacled, tidy in his movements, as if he handled words the way an apothecary handled powders—careful not to waste.

On impulse Calder asked, “You are not a doctor, by any chance?”

“Not a doctor,” the man replied, smiling good-naturedly. “A student of medicine at Bonn. I came from Cairo to see the Second Cataract, but they would not let me farther than Wadi Halfa.”

“Then I shall trespass on your holiday,” Calder said at once, “and claim your assistance.”

“For yourself? With pleasure—though I should not have guessed you were ill.”

“Nor am I. It is an Arab below.”

“The man on the bedstead?” The student’s eyebrows rose behind the eyeglasses.

“Yes. I warn you—three weeks hurt, and no care. This is not a country where wounds are kindly treated. The sight will not be pretty.”

The student shrugged, accepting experience as a kind of currency. “All experience is good,” he said; and they left the table.

On deck the wind had freshened. The steamer and barge pitched; water broke aboard in sudden sheets. The spray, caught by lamplight, looked like driven snow. It struck the planks with a sound like a whip’s lash—sharp, repetitive, insistent. Calder thought how noise like that could be torture when a man was too weak to turn his head.

They leaned over the rail toward the barge’s lower deck. Night had come down thick and dark. One lamp swung from the upper deck and threw a small wavering circle of light—uncertain gold surrounded by blackness. Beyond it there was nothing to see but, at the bows, the white flare of spray.

“He was below there,” the German student said, peering.

“He has been moved,” the student added.

“No doubt,” Calder answered. “There is no one in the bows.”

He stared a while longer into the darkness, listening for any human sign—a groan, a cough, the thin thread of breath. The only answer was the wind and the slap of water.

“I believe the angareb is there,” he said at last. “I believe it is.”

They went down to the lower deck and to the side, and from there Calder could make it certain. The angareb stood exactly where he had ordered it moved that morning—yet now it was at the very front, in the wash of water, where each plunge of the barge sent a cold flood across the planks. The shrouded figure lay upon it as immobile as before, as if even discomfort had become too much effort to register. Spray broke continually upon the veiled face.

“I thought it would be so,” Calder said, and his voice came out hard.

He fetched a lantern. Its flame made a small warm world in his hand—oil and glass and a steady glow that smelled faintly of soot. With the German student he climbed across the bulwarks onto the barge.

He summoned the two Arabs.

“Move the angareb from the bows,” he ordered.

They did as they were told, dragging the bedstead back. The matting scraped against wet boards.

“Now take the covering off,” Calder said. “My friend—he is a doctor—must see the wound.”

They hesitated. One drew himself up with an insolent tilt of the chin. “There are doctors at Assouan,” he said. “We take him there.”

Calder lifted the lantern and, without waiting, reached down and drew the veil away himself.

The face beneath was not dead. It was alive in the most stubborn, reduced way: skin drawn tight, lips cracked, eyes sunk but watchful. A smell rose—sweat turned sour, old blood, the sweet-sick rot of infection that clung to cloth and flesh. It was the smell of a body that had been denied the ordinary rituals by which humans keep each other human: washing, changing, tending.

“Now, if you please,” Calder said to the student.

The German knelt and examined the thigh. Calder held the lantern high, the light brightening the horror of it. The wound crawled—an obscene movement where life’s smallest scavengers prospered. The student’s mouth tightened. He worked quickly, with the grim composure of someone who has studied illness in books and now meets its true texture.

When he had seen enough, he was glad to cover it again.

“I can do nothing,” he said quietly. “In a hospital, perhaps—with baths and dressings—relief at least. More? I do not know. Here…I could not even begin.”

He glanced up at Calder. “Do these men understand English?”

“No,” Calder answered.

“Then I will tell you something,” the student said, lowering his voice. “He did not get this by falling from a palm tree. That is a lie. This was done by a spear-blade—or some weapon like it.”

“Are you sure?”

“Yes.”

Calder bent down sharply toward the wounded man’s face. Until now the stillness had seemed like absence; now Calder saw it was restraint. The man had been listening. His eyes had followed the words as if the words were something tangible moving through air.

“You understand English?” Calder asked.

The Arab could not answer. His lips attempted movement and failed. Yet in his eyes a flicker of comprehension lit and held.

Calder’s pulse quickened. He had been waiting for news—any scrap, any thread that could lead back to the thing Durrance asked for in every letter: Harry Feversham. Calder dropped to his knee beside the man’s head and brought the lantern closer, so the light steadied on the eyes.

“Where do you come from?” he asked slowly.

The lips strained again. No whisper came. But the eyes pleaded to be understood, as if he were trapped behind a wall of glass.

Calder began to name places, each one pronounced with care, like tasting unfamiliar syllables and trying not to distort them. The names felt clumsy in his mouth, foreign sounds that carried whole lives and histories he did not own.

“From Dongola?”

No response.

“From Metemneh? From Berber? From Omdurman?”

At “Omdurman” the man’s eyelids fell in assent, closing as if the word itself hurt.

Calder leaned nearer. “You were wounded there?”

The eyes denied it.

“At Berber?” Calder tried.

The eyes answered yes.

Calder sat back on his heel, mind moving quickly through what he knew and what he feared. The river’s noise went on around them—spray lashing, timbers creaking—an indifferent percussion to a human attempt at speech.

“You were in prison at Omdurman and escaped?” Calder asked.

No.

“Yet you were wounded,” Calder said, and the contradiction sharpened.

He lowered his voice, bringing it close to the man’s ear as if secrecy could bridge the gap that weakness had opened.

“You were helping some one to escape?” he asked.

Yes—an unmistakable assent in the eyes.

“Who?” Calder whispered. “El Kaimakam Trench?”

No.

He tried other names—white captives, officers and officials whose fates were spoken of in mess-rooms and letters home, names that carried rank and empire. Each time the eyes said no.

“It was Effendi Feversham, then?” Calder said at last.

The eyes answered yes as plainly as speech.

A chill ran through Calder that had nothing to do with the river spray. Effendi—Harry Feversham, called by the title the Soudanese used, a name altered by other mouths until it became both recognition and disguise. Calder felt, for an instant, the strange tyranny of language: how a man’s identity could be carried across deserts in a word that was not quite his own, how meanings shifted as they passed from tongue to tongue, and how, in that shift, truths could hide.

“I too am pledged to help Effendi Feversham,” Calder said, urgency thick in his throat. He waited, willing the man to answer with something more—a name, a place, a sign, any detail that could be sent to Durrance, any direction by which help might be turned into action.

But the wounded Arab could not speak. Not even his own name could he give. His companions would not supply it. Whatever they knew or guessed, they kept themselves clear of danger with the stubbornness of men who have learned that curiosity is punished. They clung to their palm-tree story as if it were a charm against responsibility.

“Kinsmen at Korosko,” one repeated dully when Calder questioned him, “asked us to take charge of him. He is a stranger. We go to Assouan.”

That was all Calder could wrest from them.

So he stood beside the angareb, lantern in hand, looking down at a man whose eyes held locked information as a sealed jar holds spice—fragrance trapped, useless to anyone who cannot break the lid. The Arab’s eagerness to speak was there, vivid and helpless, and the extremity of his weakness laid a finger on his mouth more effectively than any vow.

All Calder could do was to see him delivered, alive if possible, to the hospital at Assouan.

At Assouan the air smelled different—less of open sand, more of human settlement: dung and cooking smoke, the sourness of crowded rooms, water warmed in basins. The hospital had its own odours—carbolic and sweat, damp cloth, the faint sweetness of decay fought back by soap and disinfectant. Those scents, at least, belonged to effort rather than abandonment.

Calder spoke to the doctors. They examined the man and exchanged looks that were professional and guarded.

“Will he recover?” Calder asked.

They shook their heads, not committing themselves, as if certainty would be a cruelty.

“There is a chance,” one admitted. “A very slight chance. And if he lives, it will be slow.”

Calder left him there, in clean sheets and under human eyes, and continued his journey toward Cairo and then Europe. The river widened behind him, carrying away the barge, the wind, the lash of spray, and that silent face under the lantern-light.

An opportunity had slipped through his fingers—not for lack of will, but because the one man who could have spoken was trapped in the untranslatable prison of his own broken body. The news Durrance hungered for was present and yet inaccessible, as if written in a script Calder could see but not read.

For the Arab on the angareb—wounded, voiceless, and carried downstream like an unwanted burden—was Abou Fatma of the Kabbabish tribe; and his presence between Korosko and Assouan meant that Harry Feversham’s careful plan to rescue Colonel Trench had failed.

When Calder, balked of news and heavy with the disappointment of it, stepped into his carriage at Assouan, Lieutenant Sutch was far away on a Hampshire height-road as white as sifted flour. The afternoon lay clean and bright on the common: gorse flared like banked embers; heather spread a dull purple as though the earth wore mourning in its own homely fashion. A pony’s hooves made a measured, domestic music on the chalk—tap, scuff, tap—steady as a kitchen clock; but Sutch could find no comfort in that rhythm. Like many men who live much alone, he had taken to speaking to himself, as though sound itself might hold thought together.

“From the beginning I foresaw trouble,” he said, and again, because repeating was a kind of oath, “from the beginning.”

The ridge dipped abruptly. Below, a small railway station sat snug as a loaf under its red tiles. The rails shone in the sun with a cold, metallic insistence; telegraph poles marched away, a rigid line of black strokes against the sky, and the whole straightness of it—iron, wire, and regulation—seemed to press on Sutch’s nerves. He pulled up, staring down, frowning.

“I promised Harry to say nothing,” he muttered; and as if the words could be kneaded into something firmer than scruple, he worked them again: “Faithfully—in the Criterion grill-room.”

A whistle cut the air far off: shrill, thin, and modern, a noise like a blade. It jarred the afternoon’s softer acoustics—the wind’s hush through pine needles, the pony’s patient breathing. Sutch saw the white smoke of a train unspool in the distance like a ribbon torn from a parcel.

“I wonder what brings him,” he said, doubtful; and then, with the effort of a man forcing himself down a hard road, “No use shirking.”

He flicked the pony, drove down between dark pines. Their resinous scent—sharp as a cut branch—filled the hollow. The train drew in; steam sighed and settled. Only two passengers stepped down: Colonel Durrance and his servant. They came straight out to the road as though they had been expected there by fate itself.

Sutch hailed him. Durrance turned his blind face towards the voice, the lifted foot feeling for the step.

“You received my telegram in time?” Durrance asked.

“Luckily it found me at home.”

“I have brought a bag. May I trespass upon you for a night’s lodging?”

“By all means,” Sutch said; but the words, meant to be open-handed, came out with a reluctance Durrance could hear. Durrance had anticipated that; he had sent the telegram at the last moment for that very reason. The message had carried no explanation, but it was enough to make Sutch’s mind bristle. Something grave must have happened—something untoward between Ethne Eustace and Durrance. And with the suspicion came the old dread: Durrance would renew those questions about Harry Feversham which Sutch was sworn not to answer.

Then Durrance’s raised foot groped again, and blindness—no longer an abstract misfortune but a fact of flesh and bone—was thrust upon Sutch’s notice. He reached out, caught Durrance’s arm, and helped him up.

After all, he thought with a mean little wash of relief, it should not be hard to keep a blind man from the truth. Ethne herself, somewhere in Devonshire, had once found the same cruel comfort.

But pity rose in him too, unasked.

“I was very sorry, Durrance, to hear of your bad luck,” he said, as the trap climbed again out of the pine hollow. “I know something of being stopped and put aside just when the road seems to level—though my leg’s nothing beside your eyes. I won’t talk compensations. That’s the gabble of people who have their health and call it philosophy. We know better. For a man who’s young and active, and doing well in a career that needs activity—there are no compensations if it’s cut short through no fault of his.”

“Through no fault of his,” Durrance repeated, slowly. “I agree. It is only the man whose career is cut short through his own fault who gets compensations.”

Sutch glanced at him sharply, as if a blind man could not feel a look. The sentence was too apt. Was it chance—or knowledge?

“Compensations of what kind?” Sutch asked, uneasily.

“The chance of knowing himself, for one,” Durrance said. “The chief thing. He is brought up short, perhaps disgraced.” The word fell like a dropped utensil in a silent room; Sutch started. “Yes—disgraced. But the shock is his opportunity. Up to then his life has been sham and illusion. The man he believed himself—he never was. Now he knows. And once he knows, he can set himself to retrieve. There are compensations for such a man. You and I know a case in point.”

Sutch felt the trap’s wood tremble faintly under the pony’s pull. So it was Harry. Durrance had some knowledge—how gained, Sutch could not guess—and, worse, the shape of it seemed to do Harry an injustice. Sutch’s regard for Feversham had hardened with years into something fierce and maternal; he could not bear to hear the memory bruised.

“The case isn’t quite in point,” he argued. “You mean Harry Feversham.”

“Who believed himself a coward—and was not,” Durrance replied. “He commits the fault that stops his career, he finds his mistake, he sets himself to retrieve his disgrace. It’s in point enough.”

“I see,” Sutch said slowly. “There’s another view—a wrong one, as I know—but for a moment I thought you held it: that Harry fancied himself brave and found out he was a coward. How did you find out? No one knew the whole truth except myself.”

“I am engaged to Miss Eustace,” Durrance said.

“She didn’t know everything. She knew of the disgrace—but not the determination to retrieve it.”

“She knows now,” Durrance returned; and his voice sharpened, as though he heard a change of breath. “You are glad of that—very glad.”

Sutch, startled, wondered how he had betrayed anything at all. Yet he did not deny it.

“It’s true,” he said stoutly. “I am glad. From your point of view it might be better if she didn’t know. But I can’t help it. I am glad.”

Durrance laughed—briefly, not unkindly. “I like you the better for being glad.”

“But how does Miss Eustace know?” Sutch pressed. “Who told her? I didn’t—and there’s no one else could.”

“You are wrong. There is Captain Willoughby. He came to Devonshire six weeks ago. He brought with him a white feather, which he gave Miss Eustace, to prove he withdrew his charge.”

Sutch drew up in the road as if the pony had hit a sudden bank of wind. He sat quite still, and for a moment Durrance might have thought him stunned. The truth was simpler and more astonishing: joy—real joy, the kind that makes an old man feel his blood move like it did when he was twenty—rose in him and held him speechless.

Five years ago, on Dover pier, he had watched a mail packet steam away into rain and darkness, and had prayed—prayed like a man bargaining with God—that he might live to see Harry’s name cleared. Since the night outside the War Office in Pall Mall, when Feversham had touched his arm and spoken out his despair, Sutch had carried a weight that was not wholly Feversham’s disgrace but his own failure. Harry was Muriel Feversham’s boy. Muriel was dead. It had seemed to Sutch that her eyes watched him from beyond, not accusing loudly, but reproaching gently: Since I was taken away where I could only see and not help, surely you might have helped—just for my sake—your work being ended. And the long account of his quiet, inactive years had risen nightly to condemn him.

Now the guilt lifted, not by Sutch’s word, but by Harry’s act. The air itself seemed to brighten. The world, which had been grey with worry, took colour again as though sunlight had been poured over it like wine.

“I knew it,” he burst out. “I knew he wouldn’t fail. Oh, I’m glad you came to-day, Colonel Durrance. It was partly my fault, you see, that Harry ever incurred that charge. I could have spoken—there was a chance at Broad Place, one of those Crimean nights—a word might have helped—and I held my tongue. I’ve never ceased blaming myself. You have the particulars? Willoughby was in peril—and Harry came to his aid?”

“No,” Durrance said. “Not that exactly.”

“Tell me. Tell me.”

Durrance told him of the Gordon letters, and how Feversham had recovered them. To Sutch it was too short, too bare, like a story of a banquet related by a man who had eaten nothing but remembered the plates.

“Oh, but I’m glad you came,” he cried again, and meant it.

“You understand, at all events,” Durrance said, “that I have not come to repeat to you the questions I asked in the courtyard of my club. On the contrary, I can give you information.”

Sutch drove on, up into the clearer air. Durrance’s certainty about Sutch’s fears made him uneasy—yet the new gladness would not let small perplexities lodge.

“So Miss Eustace told you, then,” Sutch said, “and showed you the feather?”

“No, indeed,” Durrance answered. “She said not a word. She never showed me. She even forbade Willoughby to hint of it. She sent him away from Devonshire before I knew he had come.” Then, quick as a knife finding its mark: “You are disappointed at that.”

Sutch stiffened. It was true. He was jealous on Harry’s behalf; he wanted Harry first in her thoughts. Harry had taken up his dreadful work for her sake. Sutch wanted her to remember as fiercely as he did. He had hoped she would go to Durrance at once, break the engagement, set Harry’s image back in its rightful place. It would be hard on Durrance—but love is capable of a perfect cruelty.

“Then how did you learn it?” he asked.

“Some one else told me that Willoughby had come and brought a white feather, and that Ethne had taken it. Never mind who. That gave me a clue. I lay in wait for Willoughby in London. He is not clever; he tried to obey Ethne’s command of silence, but I managed to extract what I wanted. The rest I put together. Ethne now and then was off her guard.” He laughed. “You are surprised that I was clever enough to find out truth with my own wits?”

Sutch jumped in his seat; again that uncanny accuracy. “I have said nothing,” he returned testily, “to suggest I was surprised.”

“Quite true,” Durrance said, “and yet you are surprised. You could not know that it is only since I have been blind that I have begun to see. Shall I give you an instance? This is the first time I have come into this neighbourhood or got out at your station. Yet I can tell you you drove me up a hill between forests of pines, and now across open country of heather.”

Sutch turned quickly. “The hill—yes. But the pines?”

“The air was close. Trees. I guessed pines.”

“And the open country?”

“The wind blows clear across it. There is a dry stiff rustle besides. I have never heard that sound except when wind goes over heather.”

For a moment Sutch listened—not merely to Durrance’s words, but to the sound-world those words described: resin-thick hush, then the heather’s crackling whisper. It made him think—strangely—how a man’s health of mind might depend on the honest voices of a place, and how the shriek of engines and the hard straight lines of telegraph poles were an invasion, a new language laid over an old one.

Durrance turned back to Feversham. He spoke of Harry’s disappearance and its cause. He made no mention—Sutch noted it—of the fourth white feather Ethne herself had snapped from her fan. But he knew the history of the three feathers that had come by post to Ramelton down to the last detail.

“I was acquainted with the men who sent them,” he said. “Trench, Castleton, Willoughby. I met them daily in Suakin—ordinary officers. One rather shrewd, one commonplace, one distinctly stupid. I watched them go quietly about their routine. It seems strange now. There should have been a mark on them, setting them apart as messengers of fate. But there was nothing. They were prosaic, like everybody. And yet—by a thoughtless act, without a spoken word—they dealt out misery, estrangement, years of suffering, and went about their business as though they’d only ordered dinner. Then much later a consequence of what they did—perhaps forgotten—rises up and strikes you down.”

“Yes,” Sutch said, and meant it. He wondered again what object had brought Durrance into Hampshire if not those questions; but Durrance did not yet say.

They came at last to Sutch’s house, standing alone by the roadside and looking over a wide sweep of downs. Within its walls life had the careful quiet of a man long solitary: doors that shut without clamour, clocks that ticked, a fire laid ready though unlit, and windows that admitted the countryside’s steady breathing. Sutch took Durrance over the stable, described his horses; he explained the garden with the pride of one who has learned to husband small things—where the beds lay, how the paths turned, what flowers came earliest, and which scents were strongest at dusk. Durrance listened with real interest. In that small domestic inventory Sutch heard the long practice of surviving—how to keep the days from turning into an unbroken grey.

“You don’t live within sight of the sea,” Durrance said at last, as they stood at the door.

“No,” Sutch answered quickly. “I dare not.”

Durrance nodded, understanding without sight. “You care for it too much. You would have the full knowledge of your loss presented to you each moment.”

They went in. They dined together and sat over their wine. The room held the soft, civil sounds of a private house: the clink of glass, the servant’s discreet tread, the sigh of air at the windows. Outside, the day’s louder noises had thinned; the countryside spoke more plainly. Still Durrance did not name the purpose of his visit, and at last Sutch, with an awkwardness born of thinking of Ethne and of Harry together, forced his thought into speech.

“Harry Feversham must come back to England,” he said. “He has done enough to redeem his honour.”

Durrance answered at once. “I was waiting for you to say it. I wanted you to realise, without any suggestion of mine, that he must return. That is why I came.”

Sutch’s relief was so great he blurted out another sentence with clumsy haste.

“His return will not really trouble you or your wife—since Miss Eustace has forgotten him.”

Durrance shook his head. “She has not forgotten him.”

“But she kept silence—after Willoughby brought the feather back. You told me so. She said nothing to you. She forbade Willoughby to tell you.”

“She is true,” Durrance said. “Loyal. She has pledged herself to me, and nothing—no promise of happiness, no thought of Harry—would induce her to break her pledge. I know her. But I know, too, she only plighted herself to me out of pity, because I was blind. And I know she has not forgotten Harry.”

Sutch leaned back and smiled; he could have laughed outright. He asked for no proofs. Pride flooded him—pride in Harry, his favourite, his charge, still held in that girl’s heart after disgrace and absence. In the same instant he endowed Ethne with every virtue imaginable, because her constancy magnified Harry’s worth. In his mind, Durrance would have to step aside—of course—but then Durrance should never have come between Harry and Ethne in the first place. Love made him merciless.

“You are very glad of that,” Durrance said quietly. “Very glad Ethne has not forgotten him. It is a little hard on me, perhaps—who have not much left. It would have been less hard if, two years ago, you had told me the truth when I asked it of you that summer evening in the courtyard of the club.”

Compunction caught Sutch by the throat. The gentleness of Durrance’s voice, and a tiredness in it, made Sutch ashamed of the sharp, bright joy he had just been nursing.

“It was impossible,” he exclaimed. “I promised Harry—solemnly—that I would tell no one until he came back himself. I was sorely tempted. But I had given my word. Even if he never came back—even if I knew for certain he was dead—I was only to tell his father, and even his father not all that could be told on his behalf.”

He pushed back his chair and went to the window. “It is hot in here,” he said, and raised the sash without waiting. Cooler air moved in, carrying the faint night-smell of earth and leaf. For a little he stood there, silent, balancing pity against loyalty.

Durrance did not know of Ethne’s fourth feather. He had not heard Harry’s words in the Criterion. There were certain sentences, spoken then, that it seemed only fair Durrance should hear now. Silence had already done harm. Sutch turned from the window, mastered his love, and went back to the table.

“There is something it is fair you should know,” he said. “When Harry went away to redeem his honour, he had no hope—indeed no wish—that Miss Eustace should wait for him. She was the spur to urge him, but she did not even know that. He did not wish her to know. He had no claim upon her. There was not in his mind a hope that she might one day be his friend—in this life, at all events. When he went away from Ramelton he parted from her, as he thought, for all his mortal life. It is fair you should know.” Sutch’s voice tightened, but he went on. “You tell me Miss Eustace is not the woman to withdraw from a pledged word. Well—what I said to you that evening at the club I say again: there will be no disloyalty to friendship if you marry her.”

It cost him; he was glad when it was done. Still he waited, uncertain what answer would come.

“It is kind of you to tell me,” Durrance said, and smiled with genuine friendliness. “I can guess what it cost you. But you have done Harry Feversham no harm by speaking. For Ethne has not forgotten him; and I have my point of view. Marriage between a man blind like myself and any woman—let alone Ethne—could not be fair or right unless on both sides there was more than friendship.” He paused, and his next words were as practical as orders given on a march. “Harry must return to England. He must return to Ethne, too. You must go to Egypt and do what you can to bring him back.”

Sutch’s suspense broke cleanly. Conscience was satisfied; Harry was not betrayed.

“I will start to-morrow,” he said. “Harry is still in the Soudan?”

“Of course.”

“Why of course?” Sutch demanded. “Willoughby withdrew his accusation; Castleton is dead—killed at Tamai; and Trench—I know, for I have followed their careers—Trench is a prisoner in Omdurman.”

“So is Harry Feversham.”

For a moment Sutch did not understand. The statement was too abrupt, too large; it did not fit the bright relief he had been holding. Then comprehension came, and with it a refusal—no, it cannot be—and then the knowledge that refusal was folly. He sat down opposite to Durrance as though the chair had been pulled from under him and placed again.

The room fell silent for a long time. Through the open sash came the countryside’s night-voices: a wind’s low passage over grass, some small creature’s stir, the far-off, softened mutter of the world. Compared with those living, ancient sounds, the railway’s shriek and the telegraph’s rigid line seemed like a foreign tongue trying to overwrite the old dialect of the land.

“What am I to do?” Sutch asked at last.

“I have thought it out,” Durrance replied. “You must go to Suakin. I will give you a letter to Willoughby—he is Deputy-Governor—and another to a Greek merchant there whom I know, and on whom you can draw for as much money as you require.”

“That’s good of you, Durrance—upon my word,” Sutch broke in; and forgetting, as men forget in strong feeling, that Durrance could not see, he held out his hand across the table. “I wouldn’t take a penny if I could help it—but I am a poor man. Upon my soul, it’s good of you.”

Lieutenant Sutch, though he had turned in late, was on his feet with the first thin light, and he filled the house as wind fills a chimney. Doors clicked, drawers slammed, boots were dragged from one room to another, and a dozen times the same battered valise was packed, unpacked, and packed again, as if the very act of folding a collar and laying it atop a shirt might compel the day to hurry and the long years behind him to loosen their grip.

For thirty years he had lived on what he called the world’s half-pay list, with nothing demanded of him except patience; and now, like yeast suddenly wakened in warm water, he rose—restless, expanding, foaming over into every corner. The servants, who had grown used to a quieter household than any farm at harvest, scurried along the passages, their footsteps and whispers chased by Sutch’s sharper summonses. The house had not held so much commotion in all his decades there. Even the clocks seemed offended, their ticks drowned by the flap of linen and the clatter of buckles.

At breakfast, the table was steady enough—white cloth, the faint scent of tea leaves opening in the pot, toast browned to the edge of bitterness—but the air around Sutch trembled as if with kettledrums. He ate in snatches, as though food were merely an inconvenience between himself and the platform lights of a station.

“I shall be ready this afternoon,” he said briskly to Durrance. “Night mail to the Continent. We might go up to London together; for London lies on your road to Wiesbaden.”

“No,” Durrance answered. “There is one more visit I must pay in England. I did not think of it until I was in bed last night. You put it into my head.”

Sutch set down his knife and fork with a small, decisive tap, like the strike of a spoon against a saucepan to test its temper. “Oh,” he observed. “And whom do you propose to visit?”

“General Feversham.”

Surprise took Sutch full in the face. “Why in the world do you wish to see him?”

Durrance’s hand, moving with the careful economy he had learned, found the rim of his cup. The china was warm; the tea smelled of Assam and faint smoke, and beyond the window a rook called once, as if to mark the point.

“I want to tell him how Harry has redeemed his honour—how he is still redeeming it. You said last night you were bound by a promise not to tell him anything of his son’s intention, or even of his son’s success, until the son returned himself. But I am bound by no promise.” His voice did not rise; it did not need to. “Such a promise bears hardly on the General. Nothing could pain him so much as the proof that his son was a coward. Harry might have robbed and murdered; the old man would have preferred both those crimes. I shall cross into Surrey this morning and tell him that Harry never was a coward.”

Sutch shook his head, stubborn as a man salting meat against a winter he cannot prevent. “He will not understand. He will be grateful, of course. He will be glad that Harry has atoned his disgrace, but he will never understand why he incurred it. And after all, he will only be glad because the family honour is restored.”

“I don’t agree,” Durrance said. “I think the old man is rather fond of his son—though he would never admit it. I rather like General Feversham.”

Sutch had seen little of the General during these last years, and what he had seen left an old sourness on his tongue. He could not forgive the man for his share in the ruin of Harry Feversham. Had there been sympathy enough at Broad Place—had there been any understanding of a boy’s hidden terrors—the white feathers would never have flown into Ramelton like carrion birds. In Sutch’s mind’s eye the General sat stern on his terrace, certain he was blameless, half pleased to play the martyr before the dark, uniformed Fevershams staring down from the hall walls. Sutch felt he could not endure a patient conversation with so fixed a conscience, nor did he believe argument could shift it an inch.

“You are very thoughtful for others,” he said instead.

“It’s not to my credit,” Durrance replied. “I practise thoughtfulness out of self-preservation. Selfishness is the natural, encroaching fault of the blind. I know that; so I am careful to guard against it.”

So Durrance travelled that morning—branch lines that stitched Hampshire to Surrey, carriages that smelt of coal-dust and damp wool, stations where the announcements bounced and died beneath iron roofs. The soundscape changed with every stop: hedge-birds giving way to the hard, metallic echo of towns, then back again to the softer hush of fields. In each place, noise pressed upon him as a thing with weight; he measured space by it, as a cook measures a broth by the way its simmer speaks.

By afternoon he came to Broad Place, carried into its long-established quiet where even the gravel seemed to crunch underfoot with deliberate restraint. The pines held the wind; their needles made a thin, dry susurration like flour sifted slowly from a sieve.

General Feversham was within a few months of eighty. His back was still stiff, his bearing still the old drill-ground straightness, but he had shrunk, and his face, once carved and commanding, seemed now to have grown smaller upon his skull, as if time had tightened the skin. Durrance had walked with him on the terrace two years before; blind though he was, he heard and felt the difference—heavier steps, a breath that came more grudgingly, and a childish note in the voice, not of softness but of fretful impatience.

“You have joined the veterans before your time, Durrance,” the General said. “I read of it in a newspaper. I would have written had I known where to write.”

If he suspected why Durrance had come, he hid it well. He rang the bell. Tea was brought into the great hall where the portraits hung—dark canvases of dead men in uniform, their painted medals catching what little light lay in the room. The tray arrived with the faint aroma of bergamot and warm milk, and the small domestic sounds—the spoon against the pot, the cup set into its saucer—fell oddly among those rigid, watchful faces.

The General asked after officers in the Soudan, discussed the iniquities of the War Office, and declared, with a rising irritability, that the country was going to the deuce.

“Everything through ill-luck or bad management is going to the devil, sir,” he exclaimed. “Even you, Durrance—you are not the same man who walked with me on my terrace two years ago.”

The words were blunt, and the solitude he kept had not improved his tact. Durrance might have answered him in kind, but he did not.

“I come upon the same business,” he said instead.

Feversham sat up as though a string had been drawn tight within him. “And I give you the same answer. I have nothing to say about Harry Feversham. I will not discuss him.”

He spoke the name without tremor or tenderness, as though it belonged to some stranger in the county paper. Durrance, listening, wondered whether affection had dried up entirely in that stern heart.

“It would not please you, then, to know where Harry Feversham has been,” Durrance asked, “and how he has lived during the last five years?”

There was a pause—not long, but real; a pause in which the hall seemed to hold its breath, in which even the kettledrum beat of modern life could not intrude upon the old house’s thick silence. Then:

“Not in the least, Colonel Durrance.”

“Nor on what business he has been engaged?”

“I am not interested in the smallest degree. I do not wish him to starve, and my solicitor tells me that he draws his allowance. I am content with that knowledge.”

Durrance set his cup down carefully. “I will risk your anger, General. There are times when it is wise to disobey one’s superior officer. This is one of the times. Of course you can turn me out of the house. Otherwise I shall relate to you the history of your son and my friend since he disappeared from England.”

General Feversham laughed once, short and dry. “Of course I can’t turn you out,” he said, and added severely, “But I warn you that you are taking an improper advantage of your position as my guest.”

“Yes. There is no doubt of that,” Durrance returned, as calmly as a man adjusting the flame under a pan.

And then he told him—plainly, without ornament and without mercy. He spoke of Berber and the Gordon letters recovered from danger; of Wadi Halfa, and the meeting there; of Omdurman and the imprisonment. He brought the tale down to this very day, ending with Lieutenant Sutch’s departure for Suakin. His voice paced the events with the steadiness of a knife’s stroke through a loaf: each slice clean, each fact laid down where it could not be denied.

Through it all the General did not interrupt. He did not shift in his chair. He did not even make the small sounds men make when they doubt. He listened, and his silence was as rigid as the painted men above them.

When Durrance finished, Feversham sat for some time without speaking. He raised his hand to his forehead and shaded his eyes as though the other man’s blindness might be reversed and sight restored by that old habitual gesture. Pride would not let him show those portraits any open joy, any weakness so common as relief.

“What I don’t understand,” he said at last, slowly, “is why Harry ever resigned his commission. I could not understand it before; I understand it even less now, since you have told me of his great bravery. It is one of the queer inexplicable things. They happen, and there’s all that can be said.” He drew breath, and something in the breath was nearer to gratitude than he had ever allowed himself. “But I am very glad you compelled me to listen to you, Durrance.”

“I did it with a definite object. It is for you to say, of course; but for my part I do not see why Harry should not come home and enter in again to all that he lost.”

“He cannot regain everything,” Feversham said. “It is not right that he should. He committed the sin and must pay. He cannot regain his career, for one thing.”

“No,” Durrance agreed. “But he can find another. He is not yet so old but that he can find another. And that is all he will have lost.”

Now Feversham took his hand away and moved in his chair. He looked quickly at Durrance; his mouth opened as though to ask a question, then closed again. The question hovered in the air like an unspoken dialect word—full of private meaning, with no safe translation into the harsh public language he lived by.

“Well,” he said briskly, as though the matter were trivial, “if Sutch can manage Harry’s escape from Omdurman, I see no reason why he should not come home.”

Durrance rose. “Thank you, General. If you can have me driven to the station, I can catch a train to town. There’s one at six.”

“But you will stay the night, surely,” Feversham cried, and the sudden eagerness startled even him.

“It is impossible. I start for Wiesbaden early to-morrow.”

The General rang. Orders were given; somewhere deeper in the house wheels were readied, leather creaked, a groom’s voice answered. In those practical sounds—soothing, orderly—there was a kind of domestic comfort, like hearing bread crackle as it cools after the oven.

“I should have been very glad if you could have stayed,” Feversham said, turning back. “I see very few people nowadays. To tell the truth, I have no great desire to see many. One grows old and a creature of customs.”

“But you have your Crimean nights,” Durrance said, attempting cheerfulness.

Feversham shook his head. “There have been none since Harry went away. I had no heart for them.”

For a second the mask lifted. His stern features softened, and behind them Durrance saw—by tone, by pause, by the way the words fell—the depth of the man’s grief. He had suffered alone through five years of old age, and none had been allowed to hear it. He had held a stubborn front to the world; made pride of the fact that no one could point and say, There is a man struck down. Yet in that one sentence he admitted the emptiness of his evenings, the way courage and cowardice alike, spoken of by guests as if they were stories for a fireside, would have cut him.

Outside, the carriage wheels rattled on the gravel, a brisk, reassuring rhythm against the great stillness of the estate.

“Good-bye,” Durrance said, and held out his hand.

Feversham took it; then, as though remembering a practical matter at the last moment, he asked, “By the way—to organise this escape from Omdurman will cost a great deal of money. Sutch is a poor man. Who is paying?”

“I am.”

Feversham’s grasp tightened, firm and sudden. “It is my right, of course,” he said.

“Certainly. I will let you know what it costs.”

“Thank you.”

He accompanied Durrance to the door. On the steps he lingered, uneasy, as if the pines had turned to listeners and the gravel itself might carry tales. The delicate question—so difficult to phrase in the General’s hard vocabulary—pressed at him again.

“Didn’t I hear, Durrance,” he began, with an air of careless inquiry, “that you were engaged to Miss Eustace?”

Durrance paused with one foot on the carriage step. “I think I said that Harry would regain all that he had lost—except his career,” he replied.

He got in. The carriage rolled away between the pines, the sound shrinking down the drive until it was swallowed by the estate’s hush. Durrance’s work was done. There was nothing more now but to wait at Wiesbaden and pray that Sutch might succeed. He had devised the plan; it remained for those with eyes to execute it.

General Feversham stood on the steps looking after the carriage until it disappeared. Then he turned and walked slowly back into the hall, where the portraits watched from their shadows. The air held the faint ghost of tea and old polish, of stone that had absorbed generations of footsteps.

“There is no reason why he should not come back,” he said, lifting his gaze to the painted Fevershams in their uniforms. The dead men would not be disgraced. “No reason in the world,” he repeated, and the words, spoken in that resonant chamber, seemed less like a declaration than a charm.

“And, please God, he will come back soon.”

As night came, the dangers of an escape from the Dervish city—remote among sands that swallowed tracks and men alike—grew in his mind until they crowded the room. He admitted to himself, in the privacy he had always guarded, that he felt tired and old. Many times he repeated his prayer, “Please God, Harry will come back soon,” as he sat erect upon the bench that had once been his wife’s favourite seat and listened to the countryside.

The house was quiet, but the world outside was not mute: a distant owl, the soft push of wind through pines, the small, honest noises of a living land—sounds older than any war-office grievance and truer than any polished talk. Across the moonlit country, toward the Sussex Downs, he gazed as if sight could pull a son home along the dark, and the name he had refused to speak with tenderness moved at last, unspoken, in the spaces between each measured breath.

These were the days before the Khalifa’s masons lifted that great mud wall about the House of Stone. As yet only a thorn zareeba ringed the place—an enclosure of spiteful branches that scratched the skin and caught at rags, as though even the hedge had learned the prison’s trade. The House itself squatted on the eastern lip of Omdurman, and Omdurman was a capital built as if the world had forgotten how to bless a thing with shade.

No garden softened it. No green blade dared show its tongue. The ground was a hard, pepper-coloured plain, stoned and baked, and on it sprawled a long, narrow crawl of hovels—mud and wattle and corrugated misery—alive with vermin, tainted with fever, and forever exhaling a sour, human steam. All day the air rang with the place’s particular music: chains clinking in patient cadence, flies humming like a perpetual wire-string, vendors bawling from hoarse throats, camels coughing, and in the pauses the river’s far hiss, like a memory trying to speak in a language no one cared to learn.

Between the prison and the Nile there were no houses then, only that half-mile of broken slope where stones lay like scattered teeth. While daylight lasted, captives were allowed—so long as their irons would let them—to stumble down to the bank, draw water for their use, and perform their ablutions. For natives and negroes the river was not merely water but possibility. Dhows moored thick along the shore; the river traffic made its harbour there, and the wide foreshore became a market-place where the day’s loud barter was seasoned with secret bargains. A man could rub shoulders with a friend, concert an escape in the cover of ordinary speech, and slip, as unnoticed as a grain of millet in a sack, into the densest knot of buyers and sellers. A blacksmith might be found whose love of iron outweighed his fear of Idris’s whip; and in Omdurman, the clink of fetters was so common a rhythm that it drew no more attention than a pestle striking grain in a courtyard.

But for a European escape was a different recipe altogether—more costly, longer in preparation, and liable to be spoiled at the last moment by one brutal hand. There were not so many white prisoners that they could be lost in the crowd. Each was a marked man, a rare spice in the Khalifa’s storehouse, counted and watched. There were relays of camels to be stationed through the desert, money to be gathered and hidden, and, above all, devoted natives—men who would risk their lives for a foreigner, or think they would, and then, at the pinch of danger, shrink like meat touched too near the fire. The camels might be procured and placed, and then their drivers would drift away. The plan might be kneaded and shaped for months, and then the gaoler’s suspicion would ruin it with one burst of violence—one flogging on the mere rumour that a man had money.

Colonel Trench, methodical by nature and made more so by captivity, began to lose all hope by slow degrees, as a man loses the taste of his own language when no one speaks it back to him. He knew his friends were working. Sometimes the boy who brought his food would mutter, eyes lowered, that he must be ready. Sometimes, when he was paraded in the Khalifa’s triumph—shown like a captured banner as an emblem of Turkish destiny—a man would jostle his camel and, without looking up, breathe a quick encouragement into the air. But nothing came of any of it. The sun rose daily beyond the bend of the river behind Khartum’s tall palms and burned its way across the sky, and the months dragged after one another like chained men.

On an evening toward the end of August—one of those nights when the heat did not fall so much as settle deeper into the bones—Trench sat in a corner of the enclosure watching the sun drop westward with an agony of anticipation. Day was torture enough, but it was bearable because it had edges: water at noon, the river’s breath, the faintest shift in wind. Night in the House of Stone had no edges. It was a weight, absolute and blind, renewed every evening with the punctual cruelty of a ritual.

Twilight came, and with it Idris es Saier, the great negro of the Gawaamah tribe, and the fellow-gaolers who moved with him like a pack.

“Into the House of Stone!” Idris cried.

Praying and cursing—voices rising and cracking—men stumbled forward, their chains dragging sparks from stone in the fading light. At the narrow entrance they jammed and fought, and behind them the pitiless whips fell perpetually on the hindmost backs. The House of Stone was already occupied by some thirty captives who had been too sick to be driven out by day: lumps of humanity on a swamped mud floor, or propped against the wall in the last extremities of weakness and disease. At night two hundred more were thrust in and penned there until morning.

The room was perhaps thirty feet square; in the middle a solid pillar, four feet across, supported the roof like a thumb pressing down on a wound. There was no window. A few small apertures near the roof made a pretence of air, as though the building offered a mock courtesy: a ration of breath measured like grain. The prisoners were packed in, screaming and striking, and when the door was closed the last strip of twilight vanished and utter darkness replaced it. A man could not even distinguish the outlines of the heads that wedged him in.

Trench fought like the rest.

There was a corner near the door he coveted with a fierceness that surprised him, until he understood it was not comfort he was fighting for but the mere possibility of lasting until dawn. In that corner he might have a little shelter from blows, stamping feet, the bruises of neighbours’ shackles; he might have a support against which to brace his back through ten interminable hours of suffocation.

If I were to fall.

The fear lived in him every night, working like a drug that produced madness. A man who went down amid that yelling, heaving throng did not rise; he was stamped out of shape. Trench had seen the bodies dragged from the prison each morning. He was a small man. So he fought for that corner as if it were a lifeboat in a black sea, kicking with his fetters, thrusting with elbows, diving under a larger man’s arm, burrowing between two others, tearing at clothes, using nails and fists, even striking at heads with the chain that dangled from the iron ring about his neck.

He reached the corner at last, streaming with heat, gasping. The rest of the night he would spend in holding it.

“If I were to fall!” he choked, and in that clamour—where nothing less than a shout was audible—he called to the darkness beside him. “Is it you, Ibrahim?”

“Yes, Effendi,” a like shout answered.

Relief came in a single syllable. Ibrahim, a tall Arab of the Hadendoas, stood near him each night, and between them a friendship had grown—not born of sentiment but of necessity, like bread leavened by hunger. There were no prison rations in Omdurman; each captive lived on his own money or the charity of friends outside. To Trench, at long intervals, money came secretly by a native from Assouan or Suakin; then there were stretches when no help came and he lived on the charity of Greeks who had sworn conversion to the Mahdist faith, or starved with such patience as he could. There were times, too, when Ibrahim had no friend to send him his meal. Each had known the other’s empty-handed days. They shared what could be shared, and at night stood side by side against the wall.

“Yes, Effendi, I am here,” Ibrahim repeated, and groping in the black, he steadied Trench against the wall with a hand that was firm as a post.

In an extreme corner a fight raged with more than common violence. So closely packed were the prisoners that with each advance and retreat, the whole crowd swayed in rhythm from side to side, end to end—like a monstrous body breathing and convulsing. Men fought to keep their feet, fought even with their teeth. Over the din of harsh breathing and the clank of chains rose, now and then, a wild sobbing cry for mercy, or an inhuman shriek stifled as soon as uttered—the sound that meant a man had gone down beneath stamping feet. Missiles flew too: scraps of filth, clods of foul earth scraped from the floor. Since none could tell from what quarter they were flung, heads battered against heads in the effort to avoid them. All in the blackest darkness.

For two hours Trench stood in that prison ringing with noise, rank with heat. There were eight hours to follow before the door would open and he could stumble into the clean air and fall asleep in the zareeba. He stood on tiptoe to lift his head above the press, but even so he could barely breathe. The air was moist and sour, as if it had been breathed and sweated and suffered through too many bodies and was now spoiled beyond salvation. His throat was parched; his tongue felt swollen and stringy like a dried fig left too long in dust. It seemed to him the imagination of God could devise no worse hell than the House of Stone on an August night in Omdurman. It could add fire, he thought, but only fire.

“If I were to fall!” he cried again—and as he spoke his hell was made perfect, for the door opened.

Idris es Saier appeared in the opening.

“Make room,” he cried. “Make room!” And he threw fire among the prisoners to drive them from the door. Lighted tufts of dried grass blazed in the darkness and fell upon shoulders and heads. The captives were so crowded they could not avoid them; some could not even lift a hand to pluck the fire away. Whips enforced Idris’s command; lashes fell upon all within reach, and a little space was cleared. Into that space a man was flung, heavily ironed, slight of figure, bent with suffering. The door slammed shut again.

Trench, close to the entrance, had caught the briefest glimpse in the strip of twilight: a man small like himself, and weakened, the kind who would not keep his footing when the crowd swayed.

“He will fall,” Trench said through clenched teeth. “He will fall to-night. God—if I were to—” The crowd heaved; curses rose sharper and shriller.

The new prisoner was the cause. He clung to the door with his face against the panels, seeking the thin trickle of actual air through the chinks. Those behind plucked him away, jostled him, pressed him backward to take his place. He was driven like a wedge between bodies until at last he was flung against Trench.

In the daytime, outside, common misery sometimes drew men together; a faithful might help an infidel, a Turk might share water with a Greek. But in the House of Stone at night kindness died quickly. The one creed was to keep upright. The one sacrament was a mouthful of morning air. Trench, practical and unimaginative, was like the rest; the need to live even to dawn drowned everything.

“Back!” he shouted violently. “Back, or I strike!” And as he wrestled to lift his arm above his head, he heard the man incoherently babbling in English.

Something in Trench’s chest lurched as if a hand had tightened around it. English—his own tongue—here, in this black choking kennel. It was not merely speech; it was a door into another world, a taste of a remembered food. It civilised him in an instant, like a woman’s touch in a savage place.

“Don’t fall,” Trench cried. He caught the man by the arm. “Ibrahim—help! God, if he were to fall!”

The crowd swayed again; cries and curses stabbed at the ears. Trench and Ibrahim supported the stranger, fought and wrestled until those about them yielded, spitting in fear and anger.

“Shaitan! They are mad!”

They cleared a space in the corner. Setting the Englishman down on the ground, they stood in front of him to keep the stamping feet from trampling him. Behind them, in lulls of the noise, Trench heard the babble continue.

“He will die before morning,” Trench shouted to Ibrahim. “He is in a fever!”

“Sit beside him,” said Ibrahim. “I can keep them back.”

Trench squatted in the corner. Ibrahim planted his legs wide and became a barrier of flesh and will, guarding the little hollow of space as a man guards a cooking-fire against a crowd of thieves: not by argument, but by stance, by readiness, by the threat of sudden violence.

Bending his head, Trench could hear the stranger’s words more clearly now. They came disjointed, pleading, shining strangely clean in that cesspit of sound, like a songbird heard through smoke.

“I saw the riding lights of the yachts—and the reflections shortening and lengthening as the water rippled—there was a band, too, as we passed the pier-head. What was it playing? Not the overture—and I don’t think that I remember any other tune…” He laughed with a crazy chuckle. “I was always pretty bad at appreciating music, wasn’t I? except when you played.” Then, as if the rhythm of water drew him, he came back to the sea. “There was the line of hills upon the right as the boat steamed out of the bay—you remember there were woods on the hillside—perhaps you have forgotten. Then came Bray, a little fairyland of lights close down by the water at the point of the ridge… you remember Bray, we lunched there once or twice, just you and I, before everything was settled… it seemed strange to be steaming out of Dublin Bay and leaving you a long way off to the north among the hills… strange and somehow not quite right… for that was the word you used when the morning came behind the blinds—it is not right that one should suffer so much pain… the engines didn’t stop, though, they just kept throbbing and revolving and clanking as though nothing had happened whatever… one felt a little angry about that… the fairyland was already only a sort of golden blot behind… and then nothing but sea and the salt wind… and the things to be done.”

The man lifted himself on an elbow and fumbled at his breast as though searching for something hidden there. “Yes, the things to be done,” he repeated, and sank into whisperings with his head fallen forward.

Trench slipped an arm around him and raised him a little, but there was nothing more he could do. Even crouched close to the ground the heat was almost beyond endurance; it had a thickness, like breath trapped under a wet cloth. In front the din went on—shouts, chains, the scrape of skin on mud. In one corner men sang in a mad frenzy; in another a few tried to dance in their fetters, a hideous parody of merriment. Ibrahim held his line like a wall. And beside Trench lay a man who, one night, had sailed out of Dublin Bay past the riding lanterns of yachts, had watched Bray’s fairyland of lights dwindle to a golden blot.

That thought—sea and salt wind, the cool dim night, a bell telling the hour—worked upon Trench until, practical creature as he was, yearning rose in him like tears. In this place, where the only smells were human sourness and scorched grass and the rankness of old mud, the very idea of clean air felt like the fragrance of baked bread in a quiet kitchen—something so ordinary it became holy when withheld. But the stranger began to speak again.

“It is funny that those three faces were always the same… the man in the tent with the lancet in his hand, and the man in the back room off Piccadilly… and mine. Funny and not quite right. No, I don’t think that was quite right either. They get quite big, too, just when you are going to sleep in the dark—quite big, and they come very close to you and won’t go away… they rather frighten one…” He clutched Trench with a nervous grip like a boy in sudden terror.

“It’s all right, old man,” Trench said, in the tone a man uses to soothe a child. “It’s all right.”

The fear passed as quickly as it had come. The man’s mind lurched into another scene, another rehearsal, as though he were tasting over and over a phrase he could not bear to mispronounce.

“Will you take it back?” he asked with great hesitation and timidity. “Really? The others have, all except the man who died at Tamai. And you will too!” He spoke like a man who can hardly trust good fortune. Then his voice shifted, making light of misery as though by belittling it he could swallow it. “Oh, it hasn’t been the best of times, of course. But then one didn’t expect the best of times. And at the worst, one had always the afterwards to look forward to… supposing one didn’t run… I’m not sure that when the whole thing’s balanced, it won’t come out that you have really had the worst time. I know you… it would hurt you through and through, pride and heart and everything, and for a long time just as much as it hurt that morning when the daylight came through the blinds. And you couldn’t do anything! And you hadn’t the afterwards to help you—you weren’t looking forward to it all the time as I was… it was all over and done with for you…” He lapsed again into mutterings.

Trench’s first delight in hearing English had cooled into curiosity. He had once described himself, on a free day in London, as “an inquisitive, methodical person,” and captivity had not burned that out of him. Here, amid the roaring dark, a whole life seemed to be unspooling in fevered scraps, and not a happy life—something tragic at its heart. The word afterwards came and went like a refrain, a motif in the man’s speech, and Trench began to wonder if it had been the man’s real religion.

The heat grew more stifling; the darkness pressed closer. Yet the cries and shouts were dying down. Their volume lessened; their shrillness dulled. Stupor and fatigue and exhaustion took their toll. Trench bent his head to listen and now heard more clearly.

“I saw your light that morning… you put it out suddenly… did you hear my step on the gravel?… I thought you did, it hurt rather.” Then he broke into an emphatic protest. “No, no, I had no idea that you would wait. I had no wish that you should. Afterwards, perhaps, I thought, but nothing more, upon my word. Sutch was quite wrong… Of course there was always the chance that one might come to grief oneself—get killed, you know, or fall ill and die—before one asked you to take your feather back; and then there wouldn’t even have been a chance of the afterwards. But that is the risk one had to take.”

The allusion was not direct enough for Trench’s comprehension. He heard “feather,” but could not yet connect it with any action of his own. Still, wonder stirred in him at the thought of how many men walked the world with calm, commonplace faces, while beneath, hidden and unguessed, lay quaint beliefs and poetic fancies—never to be revealed until illness unstitched the mind and let its private dialect spill out.

“No, one of the reasons why I never said anything that night to you about what I intended was, I think, that I did not wish you to wait or have any suspicion of what I was going to attempt.” His voice softened into a tone of interest, almost discovery. “Do you know, it has only occurred to me since I came to the Soudan, but I believe that Durrance cared.”

The name struck Trench like a blow. This man knew Durrance. He was not merely English; he belonged to the same narrow circle of men whose histories crossed and re-crossed like tracks in a field. Trench’s curiosity about the life in fragments became a sharper curiosity: who was he? He strained to see, though in that black hovel sight was nothing. But perhaps he would hear enough to be sure. If the stranger knew Durrance, perhaps he knew Trench too. Trench listened for some familiar cadence in the voice—found none. He waited for words, and the words came.

“Durrance stood at the window, after I had told them about you, Ethne—”

Ethne. The delirium had its listener, then—a woman’s name flung like a flare in the dark. Trench could recall no Ethne, and yet the voice went on, laying down details as a cook lays spices, one by one, until a flavour becomes unmistakable.

“All the time when I was proposing to send in my papers, after the telegram had come, he stood at the window of my rooms with his back to me, looking out across the park. I fancied he blamed me. But I think now he was making up his mind to lose you… I wonder.”

Trench uttered so startled an exclamation that Ibrahim turned his head sharply.

“Is he dead?” Ibrahim demanded.

“No, he lives—he lives,” Trench answered.

It was impossible, Trench argued—and yet memory rose stubbornly. He remembered Durrance standing by a window with his back to a room. He remembered a telegram that took a long time to read, and the odd suspense it spread among all except Durrance. He remembered a man speaking of his betrothal and of sending in his papers. Was the woman’s name Ethne? A woman of Donegal—yes. And the stranger had spoken of sailing out of Dublin Bay. He had spoken of a feather.

“Good God,” Trench whispered. “Was the name Ethne? Was it? Was it?”

For a while there was no answer, only talk in the fever of a mud-walled city and an intolerable sun burning upon an endless round of desert; a man lying all day with a linen robe drawn over his head, drawing one face across three thousand miles until, at sunset, it was near, and then taking courage and going down into a gate. And then four words stabbed through everything.

“Three little white feathers.”

Trench leaned back against the wall. It was he who had devised that message. In London it had seemed a neat, almost playful cruelty, like a phrase coined and sent out to do its work. Here, spoken in the House of Stone, it was no longer neat—it was a blade.

“This afternoon we were under the elms down by the Lennon River—do you remember, Harry?—just you and I. And then came three little white feathers; and the world’s at an end.”

Now Trench had no doubt. These were quoted words—words spoken by Ethne, no doubt, on the night the feathers came. “Harry,” she had said. “Do you remember, Harry?” Trench was certain. The name in the fevered mouth was the last key.

“Feversham!” Trench cried. “Feversham!”

He shook the man he held, as though he could shake sense into him through heat and delirium. “Under the elms by the Lennon River—” Visions came unbidden: green shade touched with gold; sunlight flickering between leaves; water running cool and clear. In this desert of stone and breath, those images dragged at him like a mirage.

Feversham had been under the elms of the Lennon River on that afternoon before the feathers came—and now he lay in the House of Stone at Omdurman.

But why?

Trench asked himself the question—and he was not spared the answer.

For three days Harry Feversham’s mind ran like water spilled on hot stone—hissing, vanishing, returning in sudden pools of sense and then scattering again. Through those days, and through three nights when the House of Stone coughed out its hoarse cries and clanking chains, Trench kept him alive with the stubborn patience of a man basting a joint through a long, mean fire: fetching Nile water in an earthen bowl, breaking his own scant ration, dampening Feversham’s cracked lips, and, when the gate shut and the roof of horror pressed down, standing with Ibrahim to take blows meant for the new-comer.

On the fourth morning Feversham woke cleanly, as if the fever had at last boiled down and left only a bitter crust behind. He opened his eyes and saw Trench’s face bending over him.

At first it seemed part of the sickness—one of those nightmare masks that swell in a child’s dark until they fill the world. Feversham lifted a weak arm and thrust at it, half in fear, half in anger. But his hand met warm flesh; the face did not dissolve. He blinked, and the hard blue sky steadied into place above him; the brown, trampled soil, peppered with droppings and dried scraps, took its true shape; the prisoners dragging chains or lying as still as empty sacks regained their terrible meaning.

He turned sharply, caught at Trench’s sleeve as if the next blink might snatch him away, and then—astonishingly—smiled.

“I am in the prison at Omdurman,” he said. “Actually in the prison. Umm Hagar—the House of Stone. It seems too good to be true.”

He leaned back against the wall with an exhale of relief so deep it sounded like gratitude.

To Trench, the satisfaction rang at first like sarcasm. It was a tone a man might use who prided himself on being past pain and pleasure, who wore indifference as another wears a well-cut coat. Yet Feversham was no such man; his delirium had proved it. The relief was genuine: Dongola’s danger behind him, Trench found, Omdurman reached. Better this enclosure than a gibbet far down the Nile with vultures balancing on his shoulders and the one purpose of his life left undone.

“You have been here a long while,” Feversham said, and his eyes searched Trench’s face the way a cook reads a bruise on fruit.

“Three years.”

Feversham looked round the thorn zareeba, the wedge of shade, the bodies in rags, the dust that never settled.

“Three years of it,” he murmured. “I was afraid I might not find you alive.”

Trench nodded without pride. “The nights are the worst. The nights in there.” He jerked his head towards the squat building of stone. “It’s a wonder any man lives through a week, and yet I have lived through a thousand nights.” Even to him, saying it made endurance seem like something unreal. “A thousand nights of the House of Stone.”

“But we may go down to the Nile by daytime,” Feversham said, and tried to lift himself with sudden alarm, as though liberty might be a rumour that could evaporate. His eyes went to the hedge of thorns. “Surely we are allowed so much. An Arab at Wadi Halfa told me.”

“And it’s true.” Trench pointed to the earthen bowl by his side, damp at the rim. “I filled that this morning.”

“I must go,” Feversham insisted, and forced himself upright. “I must go this morning.”

His voice rose, sharp with the kind of eagerness that makes men careless. Trench’s hand clamped his arm.

“Hush. There are many prisoners here,” he whispered, “and among them many tale-bearers.”

Feversham sank back, partly from weakness, partly from the warning.

“But they cannot understand what we say.”

“They can see that we talk together—earnestly. Idris would know within the hour, and the Khalifa before sunset. Then there would be heavier fetters, and the courbatch.” Trench’s eyes went briefly to the gaolers’ whips, rhinoceros-hide, coiled like snakes. “Lie still. You are weak. I am tired. We will sleep. Later we go together.”

Trench lay down beside him and was asleep almost at once, as if his body had been waiting for permission to drop. Feversham watched him. In the loosened lines of the sleeping face he read the three years plainly: the cheeks sharpened, the mouth set as though it had long chewed grit, the skin drawn tight as dried hide. Noon was creeping near when Trench woke.

“There is no one to bring you food?” he asked.

“A boy should come,” Feversham answered. “He should bring news as well.”

They waited. When the gate opened and the friends and wives of prisoners crowded in, the enclosure turned at once into a feeding-pen without grass. The gaolers took their share first, fingers quick as rats. What remained was mostly aseeda—the town’s staple: dhurra pounded to a heavy paste, shaped into damp, yielding lumps, bland as mud and as necessary as breath. Its smell—sour grain and human hands—rose at once; even hunger has an odour, and here it was everywhere.

Men thin as stripped reeds hopped and lurched towards the entrance as fast as chains allowed. Groups fought groups—four against three, strong against weak—with a blind, animal purpose. A man stumbled, went down hard, and, once fallen, lay flat in a dull surrender, knowing the day’s portion had passed him by. Others grabbed at the messengers and tore the food away even as the courbatch cracked on their backs. There were thirty gaolers, each with his whip, but this was the single moment when the whip seemed neither feared nor, for sheer starvation, felt.

Among the food-bearers, a boy tried to hide behind taller bodies and looked about with the uncertainty of someone carrying contraband. He was spotted. A fist knocked him down; his bundle was snatched; his hands were suddenly empty. But the boy had lungs, and his screams dragged Idris-es-Saier himself through the press.

“For whom do you come?” Idris demanded, shoving prisoners aside with the impatience of a man separating dogs from a carcass.

“For Joseppi, the Greek,” the boy cried.

Idris’s eyes flicked to the corner where Feversham lay, and he pointed. The boy crawled and then rose, pushing forward with palms held out, empty, as if to show he had been robbed. He came close enough to Feversham to seem merely continuing his explanation—lips moving, voice low—then, with a small, practised motion, loosened the gazelle-skin pouch hidden beneath his jibbeh and let it slip to the ground beside Feversham’s hip.

The pouch was warm. Inside, folded tight as a secret, was a chicken.

In that place, where dhurra paste kept men alive like a grudged ration of air, the smell of meat was like a memory made physical. Trench and Feversham ate in quick, careful bites, tearing flesh from bone with the neatness of men who know how much noise hunger makes. The meat tasted of dust and smoke and the animal’s own sweetness; it tasted, above all, of fat—of life stored up—and it struck their mouths with a richness so sudden it was almost painful, like music heard after long silence.

That chicken served them for breakfast and dinner and supper.

An hour later permission came for them to pass out of the zareeba and go down to the Nile. They walked slowly, halting often. Feversham’s legs shook under him; Trench’s strength, worn into stubborn cord, dragged him onward.

During one halt Trench said, “We can talk here.”

Below them, at the water’s edge, prisoners unloaded dhows; others waded knee-deep, paddling in mud the colour of old broth. The shore was crowded with men shouting and yelling—sound without meaning, a constant tearing at the ears. The gaolers were within sight, but the noise and distance kept words from carrying.

“Yes,” Feversham said. “We can talk here.”

Trench looked at him. “Why have you come?”

“I was captured in the desert, on the Arbain road,” Feversham answered slowly, choosing each word as though it might be weighed.

Trench’s mouth twitched. “Yes—masquerading as a lunatic musician who wandered out of Wadi Halfa with a zither. I know. But you were captured by your own deliberate wish. You came to join me in Omdurman.”

“How do you know?”

“You told me.” Trench’s voice stayed low, but there was an odd strain in it, as if he could not decide whether to be grateful or offended. “During the last three days you have told me much.”

Feversham’s head turned sharply. Alarm ran across his face. “Very much?”

“Very much.” Trench paused. “You came because five years ago I sent you a white feather.”

Feversham’s throat tightened. “And was that all I told you?”

“No.” Trench dragged the word out like a chain. He sat up, clasped his head between his hands, and stared at the river so that he did not have to see Feversham’s eyes. “You spoke of a girl—the same girl you spoke of in London, when Willoughby and Durrance and I dined with you. I know her name now—her Christian name. She was with you when the feathers came. I had not thought of that. She gave you a fourth feather.” His voice sank. “I am sorry.”

Silence lay between them, thick and hot as the still air before a storm. At last Feversham answered, slowly, as if tasting the truth before speaking it.
