“For my part, I am not sorry. I mean—I am not sorry she was present. I think, on the whole, I am rather glad. She gave me the fourth feather, yes, and I am glad of that too. Without her presence, without that fourth feather snapped from her fan, I might have given up there and then. Who knows? I doubt if I could have stood up to the three years in Suakin.”

Trench turned his head a fraction, listening like a man hearing a tune he once despised and now cannot forget.

“I used to see you and Durrance and Willoughby,” Feversham went on, “and men who had once been my friends. You were all going about the work I was used to. You cannot think how the routine of a regiment—routine I cursed when it belonged to me—came to seem like bread in a famine. I could have slipped on a boat to Suez. I could have run away easily enough.” His mouth tightened. “But the chance I waited for never came—for three years.”

“You saw us?” Trench asked, and the question held both wonder and hurt. “And you gave no sign?”

“How would you have taken it if I had?” Feversham asked quietly.

Trench was silent.

“No,” Feversham continued. “I saw you, but I was careful you should not see me. And I doubt I could have endured it without remembering that night at Ramelton—without the feel of the fourth feather to keep the memory sharp. Without it I should never have gone down from Obak into Berber. I should never have joined you in Omdurman.”

“She would be glad to hear you say that,” Trench said, and the words came out before he could soften them. “I have no doubt she is sorry about her fourth feather—sorry as I am about the other three.”

“There is no reason she should be sorry,” Feversham replied. “Nor you either. I do not blame you—or her.”

They both looked towards the river. The air was shrill with cries. The shore swarmed with Arabs and negroes in robes of blue, yellow, and dirty brown; dhows bumped and scraped; loads thudded; men cursed and laughed; a constant racket rose and fell like waves. Across the water, beyond the fork, the palms of Khartum stood black against the cloudless sky. The sun was moving down behind them, and with it crept the knowledge that in a few hours the House of Stone would take them back.

Yet their minds, at the same time, were far from mud and shouting. They were under elms by the Lennon River; they were in a hall where a door stood open to the cool night; they heard the soft echo of a waltz; they saw three white feathers on the floor between a man and a girl. One of them remembered it. The other imagined it. To both, it was equally vivid—and in that vividness lay a kind of hunger too, not for meat but for home, for the old language of familiar places.

Feversham smiled at last. “Perhaps she has now seen Willoughby. Perhaps she has taken his feather.”

Trench held out his hand. “I will take mine back now.”

Feversham shook his head. “No—not yet.”

Trench’s face brightened, abruptly, like a lamp sheltered from wind. A hope that had struggled in him through three days and nights—fought down, for fear it should be false—sprang up alive.

“Not yet—then you have a plan.”

Feversham’s own face tightened with a new fear. “I said nothing of it,” he pleaded. “Tell me that. When I was delirious in the prison there—did I say nothing? I told you of the four feathers, I told you of Ethne, but of the plan—did I breathe a word?”

“Not a single word,” Trench replied. “So I doubted, and did not dare to believe.”

Feversham’s breath left him with relief so sharp it was almost a laugh. His hand trembled on Trench’s arm.

“You see, if I spoke of that in the House of Stone,” he said, “I might have spoken of it in Dongola. For in Dongola as well I was delirious. But I didn’t, you say—not here, at all events. So perhaps not there either.” He swallowed. “I was afraid I should. There was a woman in Dongola who spoke some English—very little, but enough. She had been in the kauneesa of Khartum when Gordon ruled there. She was sent to question me. I had unhappy times in Dongola.”

“I know,” Trench murmured. “You told me things which made me shiver.” He caught Feversham’s arm, shoved back the loose sleeve. The wrists were scarred, the skin ridged and shiny where the bonds and the lash had done their work.

Feversham pulled the sleeve down. “I felt myself getting light-headed there,” he went on. “I made up my mind I must let no hint slip of your escape. So when I felt my mind going, I tried to think of something else—with all my might.” He gave a small, strained laugh. “That was why you heard me talk of Ethne.”

But Trench had barely listened to the last. He was staring out at the water, as if already he could see it turn into a road.

“So it is true,” he said in a quiet, wondering voice. “There will be a morning when we shall not drag ourselves out of the House of Stone. There will be nights when we shall sleep in beds—actually in beds.” He hesitated, shy as a man confessing a tenderness. “There will be—something more.”

He stood up abruptly. “We have sat here too long. Let us go forward.”

They moved nearer to the river and sat again, closer to the crowd so their low talk might vanish into the general roar.

“You have more than hope,” Trench pressed. “You have a plan?”

“More than a plan,” Feversham said. “The preparations are made. There are camels waiting in the desert—ten miles west of Omdurman.”

“Now?” Trench’s whisper cracked with disbelief. “Now?”

“Yes—now. There are rifles and ammunition buried near them. Provisions. Water. We travel by Metemneh, where fresh camels wait; from Metemneh to Berber. There we cross the Nile; camels are waiting five miles from Berber. From Berber we ride in over the Kokreb pass to Suakin.”

“When?” Trench demanded, and the single word held three years’ thirst in it.

“When I have strength enough to sit a horse for ten miles,” Feversham answered, “and a camel for a week.”

“How soon?”

“Not long,” Feversham said. “Not long, Trench. I promise you.” He rose with care, and as he did he murmured, “As you get up, glance round.”

Trench did, casually, as if following a movement in the crowd.

“You will see a man in a blue linen dress,” Feversham said, “loitering between us and the gaol. As we came past, he made me a sign. I did not return it. I shall return it on the day we escape.”

“He will wait?”

“For a month. We must manage, on one night during that month, to escape from the House of Stone. We can signal him to bring help. A passage might be made through the wall in one night; the stones are loosely built.”

They walked to the water’s edge. Among the shouting, shoulder to shoulder with labouring men, they spoke again of escape with faces composed into the expression of idle amusement, as though their words were no more dangerous than a jest.

“There is a better way than breaking through the wall,” Trench said, and he let out a laugh to cover the sentence. He pointed, as though entertained, at a prisoner with a load on his back who had fallen face-first into the river. Fetters and weight pinned him; he half rose, then soused down again, splashing mud and water, struggling like a man drowning in shallow soup.

“There is a better way,” Trench repeated. “You have money?”

“Ai, ai!” Feversham shouted, roaring with laughter at the man’s second collapse, playing his part in the noisy theatre. “I have some concealed on me. Idris took what I did not conceal.”

“Good,” Trench said. “Idris will come to you to-day or to-morrow. He will talk of Allah’s goodness in bringing you out of the wicked world to the holy city of Omdurman. He will tell you at length about the peril of your soul and the only means of averting it. Then he will finish with a few significant sentences about his starving family. If you come to the aid of his starving family—and bid him keep fifteen dollars out of what he took—you may get permission to sleep in the zareeba outside the prison. Be content with that for a night or two. Then he will come again. Again you will assist his starving family, and this time you will ask permission for me to sleep in the open too.” Trench’s laugh rose again, but his eyes had gone hard with calculation. “Come—there’s Idris shepherding us home.”

It happened as Trench had foretold, as if Idris were following a recipe learned by heart. That afternoon Idris gave Feversham a lecture of uncommon length. Feversham learned that now God loved him. He heard, for the first time, how Hicks Pasha’s army had been destroyed—by holy angels, so the story ran, without a single shot fired, without a single spear thrown by the Mahdi’s soldiers. The spears, it was said, had flown from their hands by angelic guidance and pierced the unbelievers. He heard of a most convenient spirit, Nebbi Khiddr, who was the Khalifa’s eyes and ears and reported all that went on in the gaol. Idris made sure Feversham understood that if Nebbi Khiddr reported against him, heavier shackles would be riveted on, and other unpleasant things would follow.

At last came the familiar turn to the starving children. Feversham listened with the meekness expected of him and then begged Idris to take fifteen dollars.

That night Feversham slept in the open zareeba outside the prison walls. Two nights later Trench lay down beside him. Overhead the sky was clear and the stars burned with a fierce steadiness, as if the desert had scrubbed the darkness clean. After the stone-roofed stench of Umm Hagar, the night air felt almost like food: cool, sharp, filling the lungs with something that tasted of distance.

“Only three more days,” Feversham whispered.

He heard Trench draw in a long breath beside him, slow and hungry, like a man smelling bread from an oven far down a street.

For a while they lay without speaking. From within the House of Stone came muffled shouts and yells—sound trapped by walls, turned dull and savage. Here, outside, the world’s noises changed character. The Nile’s presence could be felt even when it could not be seen; the night wind moved in the thorn hedge with a faint hiss like dry grain poured into a sack. In a place where the day was one continuous clamour of commerce and cruelty, those small natural sounds were mercy, and they soothed something in the mind that chains could not reach.

Trench spoke at last.

“Are you awake?”

“Yes.”

“Well—” He hesitated. “Each man has his weak spot of sentiment, I suppose. I have mine. I am not a marrying man, so it’s not sentiment of that kind. Perhaps you will laugh.” His voice tightened. “It isn’t merely that I loathe this squalid, shadeless, vile town, or the horrors of its prison. It isn’t merely that I hate the emptiness of the desert. It isn’t merely that I am sick of the palms of Khartum, or these chains, or the whips. It’s something more.”

He drew breath again, and the confession came out as simply as a truth in an old dialect, unpolished but exact.

“I want to die at home. I have been desperately afraid so often that I should die here. I want to die at home—not merely in my own country, but in my own village—and be buried under the trees I know, in sight of the church and the houses I know, and the trout stream where I fished when I was a boy.” He gave a small, self-mocking sound. “You’ll laugh, no doubt.”

Feversham did not laugh. The words struck him with a strange familiarity—like a phrase once heard, half forgotten, and now returning with its whole weight. He knew why: it was the kind of longing Ethne Eustace might have spoken, though she had never said it to him in those exact syllables. It was not a longing you could translate into the loud, thin talk of Omdurman without losing what made it human.

“No,” Feversham answered quietly. “I am not laughing. I understand.”

There was warmth in his tone that surprised Trench. From that night a real friendship began between them—something made not of shared jokes or old club manners, but of shared deprivation and the unguarded naming of what mattered.

They lay side by side, speaking in low voices. Inside the prison the muffled uproar went on, and, by contrast, the open air seemed almost comfortable. They could breathe; they could see; no low roof pressed on their faces. Before morning the cold would sharpen and wake them to shiver in their rags, and they would huddle together in their corner like men guarding a last ember. But for now they lay on their backs, hands clasped behind their heads, and watched the great stars and planets burn in the blue dome.

“It will be strange to find them dim and small again,” Trench said.

“There will be compensations,” Feversham answered with a brief laugh, and they began to make plans—of what they would do after the desert, after the Mediterranean, after Europe, back in their own country of softer skies. The simplest phrases conjured whole worlds: hedgerows, wet grass, the smell of smoke from a kitchen chimney, the particular hush that falls on a village at dusk when the loudest sound is water over stones.

They were no longer prisoners in that barbarous town that lay like a murky stain upon the wide spaces of sand. In their minds they were at home, following old pursuits. They stood outside clumps of trees with guns in their hands while the sharp cry “Mark! Mark!” rang out—clean, precise sound, not the senseless yelling of the riverbank. Trench heard again the unmistakable rattle of the reel of his fishing-rod as he wound in line upon the bank of his trout stream. They talked of theatres in London and the last plays they had seen and the last books they had read—six years ago, as if six years were only a pause between pages.

“There goes the Great Bear,” Trench said suddenly. “It is late.”

The tail of the constellation dipped behind the thorn hedge.

They turned over on their sides.

“Three more days,” Trench whispered.

“Only three more days,” Feversham replied.

And in a minute they were neither in England nor the Soudan. The stars marched on towards morning unnoticed above their heads, and the two men, at last, slipped into the pleasant countries of sleep.

“Three more days.”

They had said it like a prayer, like the last crumb saved in a palm-leaf packet, and sleep took them with the words still warm on their tongues. But when the dawn’s first thin light slid into the zareeba, Trench woke with his skin burning and his mouth dry as old flour. By noon his mind was running ahead of itself, stumbling over shadows, and Feversham—who had leaned on Trench’s strength for so long—found his own hands suddenly enlisted in the homely, unheroic labor of keeping a man alive.

It was not chance that struck Trench down at the very moment the world had begun to open. The House of Stone had seasoned him for three years the way heat seasons a pot—hardening, cracking, leaving a taste you cannot scrub out. Yet it was joy, the sharp, unlooked-for surge of it, that finished what hunger and vermin had begun. The prospect of escape was the last stress on a body already worn as thin as parchment.

“In a few days he will be well,” Feversham insisted, as though saying it could make it true. “It is nothing.”

Ibrahim shook his head, and the beads of his worry clicked softly as he moved—one of the few clean sounds in that place. “It is Umm Sabbah,” he said. The name went through the enclosure like a fly’s buzz: the typhus that rose from foul stone and stale breath, and took men on the seventh day.

Feversham would not believe it. He repeated, “It is nothing,” with a stubbornness that tasted of panic. But another thought, sharp as vinegar, would not leave him: Will the men with the camels wait?

Each day he went down to the Nile and saw Abou Fatma posted as always in his blue robe, the folds hanging as neat as a cook’s apron despite dust and heat. Each day Abou Fatma made his sign. Each day Feversham gave none, and walked back as though he had not seen him. The river’s small, constant noises—the lap of water against mud, the creak of a rope on wood, the low talk of men—felt like a world with its own laws, indifferent to fever.

With Ibrahim’s help he nursed Trench. The boy who brought food came daily, and Feversham sent him out to buy tamarinds, dates, bitter roots—anything Ibrahim said would cool the blood. Ibrahim worked like a careful kitchen man, crushing and soaking and straining, mixing soot-black water with tart pulp until the draughts were thin and sour and lifesaving. They carried Trench from shade to shade as the sun moved across the zareeba, as though shifting a pot away from a too-hot fire.

By giving a little more help to Idris’s starving family, Feversham got the forty-pound chains removed from Trench. It was bargaining of the plainest kind, the sort that happens over grain and salt: a little mercy purchased, not granted. Trench was fed vegetable marrow soaked in salt water; butter was packed into his mouth to keep it from cracking. His body was anointed—oil rubbed in as one rubs fat into a dry skin—and wrapped close in camel-cloths until he lay swaddled like stored food against spoilage.

The fever took its course. On the seventh day Ibrahim said, in a voice as flat as a chopping block, “This is the last. To-night he will die.”

“No,” Feversham answered at once. “That is impossible.” In his mind words rose that did not belong to this place at all—beneath the trees he knew—like a tune remembered in a foreign street. “Not here. No.”

He was not thinking now of Abou Fatma waiting by the river, nor of camels, nor of the third feather and the fourth and all the reckonings beyond Omdurman. The fear that Trench would die in that enclosure had pushed everything else aside. He remembered instead the first night, the awkward words he had spoken to Trench in the corner under the open sky, when the two of them had been strangers forced into brotherhood by chains.

“No,” he said again. “He must not die here.”

Through that day and the night that followed, he watched the long hard battle between life and death. Sometimes the three years of the House of Stone seemed to be winning; sometimes Trench’s wiry frame and stubborn constitution rose up, like yeast in dough, refusing to lie flat. That night, at least, they held. The seventh day passed. The struggle was prolonged.

Even Ibrahim began to hope. On the thirteenth day Trench slept and did not ramble; and when he woke his eyes were clear. He found himself alone, wrapped so tightly in camel-cloths he could not stir. The day’s fiercest heat had gone, and the shadow of the House of Stone lay black upon the sand like a lid.

He lay wondering, lazily, how long he had been ill. While he wondered, noises swelled outside: the gaolers shouting, prisoners crying, a commotion running toward the river like a sudden wind through reeds. The gate opened; prisoners flocked in, jostling and panting. Feversham was among them. He came straight to Trench’s corner and dropped down beside him with the heaviness of a man who has carried sacks all day.

“Thank God!” he said. “I would not have left you, but I was compelled. We have been unloading boats all day.”

“How long have I lain ill?” Trench asked.

“Thirteen days.”

Trench’s gaze drifted for a moment as though watching smoke. “It will be a month before I can travel. You must go, Feversham. You must leave me here and go while you still can. Perhaps when you come to Assouan you can do something for me. I could not move at present. You will go to-morrow?”

“No,” Feversham said. “I should not go without you in any case.” He hesitated, then let the truth fall. “As it is, it is too late.”

“Too late?” The words took time to settle. Trench wished only to lie quiet in the cool of sunset, as though stillness could be kept like water in a jar. Then the meaning came, slow and heavy. “Too late? Then the man in the blue gown has gone?”

“Yes. He spoke to me yesterday by the river. The camel men would wait no longer. They were afraid of detection, and meant to return whether we went with them or not.”

“You should have gone with them,” Trench said, without bitterness. In that moment he did not care whether he lived and died in prison, so long as he was allowed to lie still. “So our one chance is lost.”

“No, deferred,” Feversham answered. “The man who watched by the river brought me paper, a pen, and some wood-soot mixed with water. He dropped them by my side as I lay on the ground. I hid them beneath my jibbeh, and last night—there was a moon last night—I wrote to a Greek merchant who keeps a café at Wadi Halfa. I gave him the letter this afternoon, and he has gone. He will deliver it and receive money. In six months, in a year at the latest, he will be back in Omdurman.”

“Very likely,” Trench muttered. “He will ask for another letter, so that he may receive more money, and again he will say that in six months or a year he will be back in Omdurman. I know these people.”

“You do not know Abou Fatma,” Feversham said, and for once his voice had the ring of certainty. “He was Gordon’s servant before Khartum fell; he has been mine since. He came with me to Obak and waited there while I went down to Berber. He risked his life in coming to Omdurman at all. Within six months he will be back.”

Trench let it pass. His eyes wandered around the enclosure and fixed at last on a pile of newly turned earth in one corner. The smell of it—damp beneath the crust—reached even where they lay.

“What are they digging?” he asked.

“A well,” Feversham replied.

“A well? So close to the Nile!” Trench’s voice fretted. “Why? What’s the object?”

“I don’t know,” Feversham said. But fear sat in his stomach like bad grease. He suspected well enough and would not say it while Trench was weak.

Within a few days the suspicion stood up in daylight. It was announced openly that a high wall was to be built about the House of Stone. Too many prisoners had escaped in their fetters along the Nile bank. Henceforward they were to be kept from year’s beginning to year’s end within the wall.

The prisoners built it themselves of mud-bricks dried in the sun—earth mixed, pressed, baked by heat, like loaves made without kindness. Feversham worked. As soon as Trench could stand, he worked too. Day after day they lifted, set, mortised. The wall rose a foot each day. With their own hands they closed themselves in.

Twelve feet high it stood at last—smooth, strong, no projection for a toe, no seam that could be worried open in a night. Trench and Feversham looked at it until their eyes ached. Even the palm trees of Khartum were hidden now. Their world had become a square of bright blue by day, a square of dark blue by night, jewelled with points of silver and flashing gold.

Trench covered his face. “I daren’t look at it,” he said, his voice breaking. “We have been building our own coffin, Feversham—that’s the truth of it.”

Then he flung up his arms and cried aloud, as if his words might carry over mud and sand and river and reach England itself. “Will they never come up the Nile, the gunboats and the soldiers? Have they forgotten us in England? Good God—have they forgotten us?”

“Hush,” Feversham said. “We shall find a way of escape. We must wait six months. Well, we have both waited years. Six months—what are they?”

He spoke stoutly for Trench, but his own heart sank all the same. The months that followed did not deserve a chronicle; they deserved only endurance. In that pestilent enclosure only the vermin thrived. No news filtered in. Thoughts became their food, and a lizard on the wall an occasion for excitement. Scorpions stung them at night. Gaolers flogged them at times by day. They lived at the whim of Idris-es-Saier, and at the whim of Nebbi Khiddr, who always seemed to report against them to the Khalifa at the very moment Idris most needed money for his family.

Religious men came to convert them, and the long theological disputations became, strangely, events—because they broke the day with human voices that were not only commands and curses. Sometimes they were freed from heavier shackles and allowed to sleep in the open; sometimes, without reason, those privileges were withdrawn, and they struggled for air within the House of Stone, as though the place itself were a sealed jar.

Six months ended. The seventh began; a fortnight passed. The boy who brought Feversham food never came with the word they lived on.

“He will never come,” Trench said at last, despair made plain.

“Surely he will—if he is alive,” Feversham answered. Then the doubt he had fenced away crept in. “But is he alive?”

The seventh month passed. One morning early in the eighth, two of the Khalifa’s bodyguard came to the prison and spoke with Idris. Idris advanced to the two prisoners with an oily show of pleasure.

“Verily God is good to you, you men from the bad world,” he said. “You are to look upon the countenance of the Khalifa. How happy you should be!”

Trench and Feversham got to their feet with no happiness in them. The same clear questions rose in both minds: What does he want with us? Is this the end?

They were led out into the street and up toward the Khalifa’s house.

“Does it mean death?” Feversham asked quietly.

Trench shrugged and gave a sour laugh. “It is on the cards that Nebbi Khiddr has suggested something of the kind.”

They were taken into the great parade-ground before the mosque, then into the Khalifa’s house. On the threshold sat another white man in attendance, silent and watchful. Inside, the Khalifa sat on an angareb. Beside him stood a grey-bearded Greek.

The Khalifa told them they were to be employed in the manufacture of gunpowder, with which the armies of the Turks would shortly be overwhelmed.

Feversham was about to protest that he knew nothing of the process, but Trench spoke first, in fluent Arabic, with the boldness of a man who has decided to gamble on words.

“There is nothing connected with gunpowder which I do not know about,” he declared.

So both were ordered to work at the powder factory under the supervision of the Greek. That Greek they would remember with gratitude to their dying day. Out of sheer pity—knowing them to be herded in the House of Stone—he had suggested their employment. The same pity taught him to cover what they did not know, to guide their hands without betraying their ignorance.

“I know nothing whatever about the making of gunpowder except that crystals are used,” Trench confessed later, when they were alone.

“But we shall leave the prison each day,” Feversham said. “That is something, though we return each night. Who knows when a chance of escape may come?”

The powder factory lay in the northern part of the town, on the Nile’s bank just beyond the limits of the great mud wall, behind the slave market. Each morning they were let out from the prison door. They tramped along the river-bank on the outside of the town wall and came into the factory past the storehouses of the Khalifa’s bodyguard. Each evening they returned by the same road to the House of Stone. No guard went with them. Flight seemed impossible.

Each journey they scanned the dusk for the blue robe. Months passed. May came with summer heat that pressed on the ear as well as the skin, making every drumbeat and shout inside the town wall feel like an insult to quiet.

“Something has happened to Abou Fatma,” Feversham said. “He has been caught at Berber perhaps. In some way delayed.”

“He will not come,” Trench replied.

Feversham could no longer pretend. He did not know that Abou Fatma had been recognised at Berber by an old gaoler and had escaped with a sword-thrust in the thigh—a cheap price for his life, dear enough to slow him for months in the hospital at Assouan. Feversham heard nothing of that. But near the end of May, news came—thin as a strip of paper, yet heavy as bread in a starving hand—that someone else was working for their escape.

One evening, as they passed in the dusk between the storehouses and the town wall, a man in the shadow of a narrow alley whispered to them to stop. Trench knelt and examined his foot as though a stone had cut it. While he knelt, the man walked past and dropped a slip of paper at their feet.

A Suakin merchant—one who kept a booth in the grain market of Omdurman.

Trench picked up the paper, hid it in his hand, and limped on with Feversham beside him. Once they had left the houses behind and had only the wall on their right and the Nile on their left, Trench sat again. There was a crowd at the water’s edge; men moved up and down, their voices overlapping like water over pebbles. Trench took his foot into his lap and examined the sole, but in the hollow of his hand he unfolded the paper and read aloud.

His voice trembled so he could barely sound the words. Feversham could barely hear; the blood sang in his ears, louder than the river.

“A man will bring to you a box of matches. When he comes trust him.—Sutch.”

“Who is Sutch?” Trench asked.

“A great friend of mine,” Feversham said. “He is in Egypt, then. Does he say where?”

“No. But since Mohammed Ali, the grain merchant, dropped the paper, we may be sure he is at Suakin. A man with a box of matches—think, we may meet him to-night!”

They did not. A month passed. Then, one evening on the river-bank, an Arab pushed past them and murmured, “I am the man with the matches. To-morrow by the storehouse at this hour,” and as he went he dropped a box of coloured matches to the ground.

Feversham stooped at once.

“Don’t touch them,” Trench said sharply, and pressed the box into the earth with his foot before walking on.

“Sutch,” Feversham breathed. “So he comes to our help. How did he know I was here?”

Trench shook as he walked. He did not speak of the hope that had lit in him; he dared not. He tried even to pretend to himself that no message had come at all. He feared what hope could do to the mind—how it could pull and stretch until something snapped.

Both slept brokenly that night. Each time Feversham woke, a dim certainty hung in him that something great had shifted. Lying on his back beneath the stars, he had a sudden fancy he had fallen asleep in the garden of Broad Place on the Surrey hills, that he had only to raise his head to see dark pines on either side, and only to look back to see the gables against the sky. The fancy did not comfort him so much as it hurt—like a remembered word in a language you may never speak again.

Toward dawn he slept, and within an hour Trench shook him awake violently. Trench’s face was grey with fear.

“Suppose they keep us in the prison to-day,” he whispered, plucking at Feversham. “It has just occurred to me. Suppose they did that!”

“Why should they?” Feversham answered. But the fear caught him too. They sat and listened for Idris’s footsteps as though listening could change what they would hear.

Idris crossed the yard and unbolted the prison door without looking at them.

The captives fought and screamed and jammed together in the entrance, dragged back, thrust forward. One man ran out foaming at the mouth and dashed his head against the wall.

“He is mad,” Trench said, and because he was unmanned that morning he began to speak rapidly, near incoherence. “That’s what I have feared, Feversham—that I should go mad. To die, even here, one could put up with that. But to go mad!” He shivered. “If this man with the matches proves false—God, I shall be near to it. A man one day, a raving, foaming idiot the next—a thing to be put away out of sight, out of hearing. That’s horrible.”

He dropped his head between his hands, and did not look up until Idris bade them go about their work.

What work they did that day neither could later tell. The hours moved with extraordinary slowness, as if time itself were chained. Evening came at last.

“Among the storehouses,” Trench said, and they dived into the first alley they passed. Turning a corner, they saw the man who had brought the matches.

“I am Abdul Kader,” he began at once. “I have come to arrange for your escape. But at present flight is impossible.”

Trench swayed as though struck.

“Impossible?” Feversham asked.

“Yes. I brought three camels to Omdurman. Two have died. The Effendi at Suakin gave me money, but not enough. I could not arrange for relays. But if you will give me a letter to the Effendi telling him to give me two hundred pounds, I will have everything ready and come again within three months.”

Trench turned his back so Feversham would not see his face. Something in him sagged and emptied, like a bag split open. The truth stood appallingly clear: Abdul Kader would not risk his life. He would be a shuttle running back and forth between Omdurman and Suakin as long as letters could be written and money paid—but the shuttle would weave nothing.

“I have nothing with which to write,” Feversham said.

Abdul Kader produced paper and pen at once. “Be quick,” he urged. “Write quickly, lest we be discovered.”

Feversham wrote as Abdul suggested, but the futility of it was as plain to him as to Trench. When the letter was done he handed it over without ceremony, took Trench by the arm, and walked away.

They came out of the alley to the long mud wall. Sunset lay on the Nile in shifting colours—olive, then pink, then a green like glass. Above them stars were beginning to show; in the east dusk had already settled. Behind them, in the town, drums began to beat—monotonous, barbaric, the kind of sound that fills space and leaves no room for thought. Feversham, who had once found solace in quiet voices and remembered landscapes, felt the pounding as an assault on the mind’s last thin shelter.

Both men walked with chins sunk on their breasts, eyes on the ground. Their fetters seemed to have grown ten times heavier. They did not speak. They were so struck down by despair that an Arab fell into step beside them and neither noticed.

After a few moments the Arab spoke.

“The camels are ready in the desert, ten miles to the west.”

He spoke low; they were too absorbed to hear. He repeated the words. Feversham looked up.

Slowly, as though waking from a drugged sleep, meaning broke through. Slowly he recognised the voice, the shape of the man.

“Abou Fatma,” he said.

“Hoosh,” Abou Fatma whispered. “The camels are ready.”

“Now?” Feversham asked.

“Now.”

Trench leaned against the wall, eyes closed, with the face of a sick man. He looked as if he might swoon. Feversham held him by the arm.

“Is it true?” Trench asked faintly.

Before Feversham could answer, Abou Fatma went on in a swift undertone, like a cook giving instructions when the fire is at its worst and there is no time to repeat.

“Walk forwards very slowly. Before you reach the end of the wall it will be dusk. Draw your cloaks over your heads. Wrap these rags about your chains so they do not rattle. Then turn and come back. Go close to the water beyond the storehouses. I will be there with a man to remove your chains. Keep your faces covered and do not stop. He will think you slaves.”

Holding his hands behind his back, he passed rags to them while they stood close, as if they were merely bartering scraps. Then he turned and hurried away.

Very slowly Trench and Feversham walked forward toward the prison. Dusk crept across the river, mounted the long slope of sand, and enveloped them. They sat down and wrapped the rags around the iron, binding them tight until the metal’s clink was muffled, as though they were wrapping a knife so it would not betray them by shining. The sunset’s colours faded from the west. Darkness gathered quickly.

They turned and walked back along the road. The drums were more numerous now, the sound thick and crowding. Above the wall rose a glare of light. By the time they reached the water’s edge opposite the storehouses, it was fully dark.

Abou Fatma waited with a blacksmith. No words were wasted. The iron was knocked off, the last ring of it swallowed by river-noise and drumbeat.

“Come,” Abou Fatma said. “There will be no moon to-night.”

“How long before they discover you are gone?” Feversham asked.

“Who knows?” Abou Fatma answered. “Perhaps already Idris has missed you. Perhaps he will not till morning. There are many prisoners.”

They ran up the slope of sand, between the quarters of the tribes, across the narrow width of the city, through the cemetery where the ground lay uneven and the air seemed to hold its breath. Beyond the cemetery stood a disused house. A man rose in the doorway as they approached and went in.

“Wait here,” Abou Fatma whispered, and he too went into the house.

In a moment both men came back, each leading a camel and making it kneel. The animals smelled of warm hide and dry grass, a clean, living scent after stone and sweat and sickness.

“Mount,” Abou Fatma said. “Bring its head round and hold it as you mount.”

“I know the trick,” Trench said hoarsely, and swung himself up. Feversham climbed behind him. The two Arabs mounted the second camel.

“Ten miles to the west,” Abou Fatma said, and struck the camel on the flanks.

Behind them the glare of lights dwindled. The tapping of the drums diminished, until at last the night’s own sounds—hoof and sand, breath and leather—began to take back the world.

The north wind came down like a knife laid flat to the skin—keen, clean, and cold enough to make the camels lift their knees and lengthen their stride as though the air itself were a whip.

“Quicker,” Trench said through his teeth.

Feversham did not waste breath on argument. “Even if Idris has missed us, it will take time to gather men—time to fetch camels. And it is dark.”

Yet he kept turning his head, as if his eyes could pull the light of Omdurman away from the horizon by sheer insistence. The town was only a low, angry glare now, a bruise of fire pressed against the night. The drums had fallen silent; that was a mercy. But the silence here was worse for the nerves. In a land where a man could travel fast and make no sound, there would be no warning thunder of hooves, no clatter of pursuit—only the soft, padded certainty of something drawing near.

Feversham strained his hearing until his ears hurt. Trench did not look back at all. He rode with his face set forward, jaw clenched so hard the muscles stood out. If Feversham feared the darkness behind them, Trench feared the thought of turning and seeing it.

When Feversham spoke again, it was because he had to give his mind something to hold. “I no longer see the fires of Omdurman.”

“The golden blot, eh? Very low down,” Trench answered, in a voice that sounded as if it had travelled a long way to reach his mouth.

Feversham did not ask why the phrase mattered. It had come to Trench like an old taste suddenly remembered—something once swallowed in a different life, returning now with the odd rightness of repetition.

They rode on without speech until two shapes rose straight out of the darkness at the camels’ feet, so near that the animals shied and huffed. Abou Fatma’s whisper cut across them.

“Instanna!”

The camels were forced to their knees. Men melted away and returned with four fresh beasts, snorting softly, their breath steaming. Saddles were stripped from the tired camels and shifted with quick hands, straps tightened, girths pulled until the leather creaked.

“The new camels are here?” Abou Fatma demanded, and the question was not really a question so much as a prayer to the night to keep its promises.

Feversham, fingers working at a buckle, felt the way hunger and fear both sharpened the body. “They are good camels?”

“Of the Anafi breed,” Abou Fatma said. “Quick. Quick.” He kept turning his head to the east, listening as though the darkness had a voice.

Trench’s impatience broke out in a harsher hunger than the need for speed. “The arms? You have them? Where are they?” He leaned down, feeling at the ground like a man searching for a dropped coin that is the price of his life.

“In a moment.”

That moment was too long for him. He could not wait as he had waited for water, for daylight, for the end of a beating. This was different. His hands wanted the familiar assurance of metal and mechanism.

“There is ammunition?” he asked, too quickly.

“Yes—yes. Ammunition, rifles, revolvers.”

Abou Fatma led them a little way off, into a patch where long desert grass whispered against their legs. The sound—dry, thin, persistent—was strangely domestic, like reeds brushing a village wall in a wind, but there was no village here, only the habit of life clinging in scraps to the sand. Abou Fatma dropped to his knees and dug with his hands.

“Here.”

Trench flung himself down beside him and scraped at the sand with both fists. A whimpering, animal sound came from his throat, the kind of eager, helpless noise a hound makes at cover. Feversham watched, and something in him recoiled—not at cowardice, but at how quickly suffering could strip away the last veneer and leave a man all appetite and instinct. Trench’s hands worked like paws, urgent, frantic.

Then his fingers struck the lock and trigger of a rifle, and he stopped as though a switch had been thrown. He rose at once, quiet, human again, holding the weapon with a reverence that was almost tenderness.

The other arms came up from their sandy grave. Ammunition was divided. Metal clicked softly; cartridges rasped; the night seemed full of small, precise sounds, each one loud with consequence.

“Now,” Trench said, and the laugh that broke out of him was raw joy, a man tasting freedom on his tongue. “Now I don’t mind. Let them follow from Omdurman. One thing is certain: I shall never go back there—no, not even if they overtake us.” He ran his hand along the rifle as though it were alive, as though it were a companion that could answer him.

Two of the Arabs mounted the old camels and turned back towards Omdurman, moving off at a slow, indifferent pace meant to mislead. Abou Fatma and the remaining man stayed with the fugitives. They mounted the fresh camels and set their faces north-east.

It had taken no more than a quarter of an hour—this exchange of beasts and buried iron—yet it felt like a whole season had turned.

All that night they forced a path through halfa grass and among thorny mimosa. The grass hissed against camel legs; branches scratched; now and again a seedpod snapped underfoot like brittle bone. They made slow time in that tangle, and the slowness was torture because it was earned by the land, not by choice.

At sunrise the country opened into flat bare ground broken by little hillocks, the sand pale and hard as flour sifted thin. Abou Fatma looked them over with a practical eye.

“Are the Effendi tired? Will they stop and eat? There is food upon the saddle of each camel.”

“No,” Feversham said. “We can eat as we go.”

The meal was what it could be: dates with their sticky, honeyed darkness; bread already dry at the edges, crumbling into the mouth; and a draught from the zamsheyeh that tasted of warm leather and last night’s smoke, yet felt like life sliding back into the veins. They ate without ceremony, chewing while the camels trotted, swallowing with eyes fixed on the horizon.

Freed of the long grass, the Anafi camels lengthened into a quick, swinging pace, their pads making almost no noise on the hard ground. Silence became an enemy again—soundless speed that could belong to pursuer as well as fugitive.

At sunset they stopped for an hour, and in that hour Feversham felt every bruise and stiffness announce itself, as if the body had been too busy to complain before. Then they rode again into the night, and the next day, and the next night—up onto high rocky ground where the stones tore at the camels’ feet, down into valleys where the air held heat like a pot held too long over the fire, and across plains of honey-coloured sand that looked, in the slanting light, almost edible—like fine meal spread out ready for kneading.

Yet to each man the pace always felt as slow as a funeral.

A mountain would lift itself on the rim of the horizon at sunrise and stand there all day, unwavering, and still at evening it would seem no nearer. Sometimes a few men working a scant patch of sorghum—stalks thin and brave in the reluctant soil—would send terror into their throats; they would swing wide in a detour, hearts thudding, listening for any shout that might carry. Sometimes Abou Fatma’s eyes would catch a caravan far off, a moving line of black dots; then they would force their camels to kneel and lie behind a rock, rifles loaded across their forearms, waiting while the distant world passed by.

Ten miles from Abu Klea another relay waited, and they changed again, keeping a day’s march westward of the Nile. From there they entered the Ababdeh desert, and at last Abou Fatma pointed to a broad grey tract lying across their way like a scar.

“The road from Berber to Merowi,” he said. “North of it we turn east to the river. We cross that road to-night. If God wills, to-morrow evening we shall have crossed the Nile.”

“If God wills,” Trench murmured. The nearer safety came, the more his fear sharpened, as if the mind could not bear the idea that deliverance might be real and thus could be stolen.

They were in country used by travellers now. The emptiness had been replaced by routes and habits, by the invisible net of other men’s movement. Daylight was no longer their friend. They dismounted and lay hidden behind a belt of shrubs on high ground, watching the road. People moved on it like insects crossing a line drawn in dust—specks that might at any moment turn into riders with guns.

When darkness fell they crossed quickly and pushed on towards the river. At dawn Abou Fatma halted them again. The land here was bleak and open, so flat that even a small rise seemed a fortress. Feversham and Trench stared to their right, hungry for the sight of water, but the Nile—so near, the spine of the country—kept itself hidden beyond the folds of earth.

“We must build a circle of stones,” Abou Fatma said. “You must lie close to the ground within it. I go forward to the river and see that the boat is ready, that our friends are prepared. I come back after dark.”

They gathered stones and built a low ring, no more than a foot high, but in that open world it was the only shelter they could make. Inside it Feversham and Trench lay down with a water-skin and rifles beside them. Abou Fatma looked at their supplies like a careful steward laying out a household’s last provisions.

“You have dates, too?”

“Yes.”

“Then do not stir. I take your camels. I bring you fresh ones in the evening.”

He rode away with the other Arab towards the unseen river, leaving Feversham and Trench pressed to the sand like men buried alive.

They dug the sand down within the ring, making a shallow hollow to lower their bodies. They watched the horizon through the gaps between stones, blinking against the brightness. The day that followed was perhaps the longest either had lived. To Trench it felt longer than a night in the House of Stone; to Feversham longer even than those days six years ago, when he had sat above St. James’s Park and waited for darkness before he dared go out, the civilised city around him as threatening as any desert.

Here there was no shade. The sun roasted their backs until the skin felt tight and raw, and the stones around them grew hot enough to sting when brushed. There was no room to move; any movement would be visible. Their mouths grew dry; the dates, so sweet in the morning, became cloying, thickening the tongue. They spoke hardly at all—speech felt like waste, and the desert itself seemed to listen.

At last sunset came, the light draining away as if poured out. Friendly darkness gathered, and a cool wind moved across the sand with a whispering that was almost like water.

“Listen,” Trench said.

They strained their ears and heard it—the soft padding of camels very near, the faint click of stones underfoot. A low whistle threaded through the night.

Feversham rose from the ring. “We are here,” he said quietly.

“God be thanked,” Abou Fatma answered. His voice carried fatigue and relief together, like a man who has carried a heavy pot a long way and at last sets it down. “I have good news and bad. The boat is ready; our friends wait. Camels are prepared for you on the caravan track by the river-bank to Abu Hamed. But your escape is known. The roads and ferries are watched. Before sunrise we must have struck inland from the eastern bank.”

They crossed the river about one o’clock. The Nile was black and thick, flowing with a slow muscular sound, as though it spoke a language older than any man on the banks. The boat’s wood creaked; water lapped; every tiny noise seemed loud. On the far side they sank the boat, letting it fill and disappear, a small sacrifice to concealment.

Fresh camels waited, shifting and snorting softly, the smell of their warm bodies strong in the night. They rode inland, but the ground was cluttered with boulders, and the beasts could seldom do more than walk. The enforced slowness was agony.

All through the next day they hid again within a stone ring while the camels were led to higher ground to graze. Night brought movement and, with it, a loosening of the heart. They made better progress. In two days they reached the groves of Abu Hamed and rested twelve hours there, under trees that smelled faintly of green sap and dust. Then another relay, another tightening of straps, another push onward.

From Abu Hamed their route lay across the great Nubian Desert.

In later days, a traveller would take this stretch in a train and sleep in a berth while coal-black rocks and yellow sand slid past outside. Morning might show him a tent, a pile of coal, a water tank, and a number painted on a white signboard—station names replaced by numerals, the land translated into the brisk, dominant lexicon of industry. Telegraph poles would march from rim of sky to rim of sky, their wires humming faintly—a new music imposed upon the desert, a commercial song that did not belong to wind or bird. Twelve hours would enclose the journey unless the engine failed.

But for Feversham and Trench there was no iron road, no numbered halt. They kept eastward of where the railway would one day run, following the line of wells among the hills—old knowledge passed mouth to mouth, the kind of knowledge that dies when tongues fall silent and names are forgotten. A well was not a “station”; it was a story, a taste of water with a particular mineral tang, a certain echo when you dropped a pebble and listened for the depth.

On the second night of that stage, Trench shook Feversham awake, fingers urgent on his shoulders.

“Look,” he whispered, and pointed south.

Feversham sat up and followed the line of his hand. The sky was scattered with stars, hard and cold. But the familiar four slanting points were gone.

“To-night there’s no Southern Cross,” Trench said, and his voice broke as though something inside him had been held taut for years and had at last snapped—not into pieces, but into release. “For six years—every night of six years—until this night, I have seen it. How often have I lain awake watching it, wondering whether the night would ever come when I should not see those four slanting stars. This is the first moment when I have really dared to think we shall escape.”

They watched the southern sky as though it were a pot on the fire and they were waiting for the first sure sign of a boil—proof that the thing they hoped for was truly happening. They murmured prayers, not grandly, but with the plain gratitude of men who have lived too long on the edge of being hunted.

When they slept, it was with the strange habit of waking again and again, half certain that when they opened their eyes the Southern Cross would have returned low on the horizon like a brand burned into the night, and half confident now that it would not.

After seven days they came to Shof-el-Ain, a tiny well set in a barren valley between featureless ridges. The place looked like nothing—stone, sand, a small dark mouth of water—but it held the weight of salvation.

By the well they made camp. They were in the country of the Amrab Arabs, and at last, Abou Fatma said, they had come to the end of peril.

“We are safe,” he cried. “God is good. North to Assouan, west to Wadi Halfa—we are safe!”

He spread a cloth on the ground and heaped dhurra before the kneeling camels. The grain smelled faintly sweet and dusty; the camels nosed it with greedy snorts. In his gratitude Abou Fatma even patted one animal’s neck. It turned its long head, lips curling, and snarled like a bad-tempered dog.

Trench laughed hoarsely. “Even now,” he said, “a camel keeps its manners.”

Then he held out his hand to Feversham. “Thank you,” he said simply.

“No need,” Feversham answered. He did not take it. “I served myself from first to last.”

“You have learned the churlishness of a camel,” Trench said, half jesting, half hurt. “It will carry you where you want to go—carry you till it drops dead—and yet if you show gratitude it resents and bites. Hang it, Feversham—there’s my hand.”

Feversham’s fingers went instead to the breast of his jibbeh. He untied a knot and drew out three white feathers—two small heron feathers and one larger ostrich feather, broken from a fan. Even in the dim light they looked unnaturally clean, as though they belonged to a different world—a drawing-room world of polished floors and spoken judgements.

“Will you take yours back?” he asked.

“Yes.”

“You know what to do with it.”

“Yes. There shall be no delay.”

Feversham wrapped the remaining feathers carefully into a corner of his ragged garment and tied them safe again, as one might tie up a precious spice in cloth to keep its scent from wasting into the air.

“We shake hands, then,” he said at last.

Their hands met. Feversham’s grip was firm, but his face was closed. “To-morrow morning we part company.”

“Part company—you and I—after the year in Omdurman, the weeks of flight?” Trench stared as though he had misheard. “Why?”

“There’s no more to be done. Castleton’s dead. You keep the feather he sent—but he is dead. You can do nothing with it. You must come home.”

“Yes,” Feversham said, “but after you. Certainly not with you. You go on to Assouan and Cairo. At each place you will find friends to welcome you. I shall not go with you.”

Trench fell silent. He understood. Feversham’s story—Feversham’s shame—would be easier for others to hear if Feversham himself were not beside him as the living proof of it. Words change in the mouth that speaks them; the same tale can be mercy or cruelty depending on whose tongue shapes it. And there were things, too, that had never been said aloud at all, agreed into silence for the regiment’s sake.

“I ought to tell you,” Trench said at last, “no one knows why you resigned your commission, or of the feathers we sent. We never spoke of it. We agreed never to speak—for the honour of the regiment. I can’t tell you how glad I am we kept to it.”

“Perhaps you will see Durrance,” Feversham said. “If you do, give him a message. Tell him that the next time he asks me to come and see him—whether in England or Wadi Halfa—I will accept the invitation.”

“Which way will you go?”

“To Wadi Halfa.” Feversham pointed west over his shoulder. “I shall take Abou Fatma with me and travel slowly and quietly down the Nile. The other Arab will guide you into Assouan.”

They slept that night beside the well as men sleep who have forgotten what true safety feels like and are learning it again. The water’s presence changed even the sounds—the soft gulp as it was drawn, the faint drip, the way a voice seemed less exposed near it. It was as if the well gave the air a gentler acoustics.

In the morning they parted. Trench mounted first. As his camel rose, he bent down to Feversham, who handed up the nose rein.

“Ramelton—that was the name?” Trench asked. “I shall not forget.”

“Yes. Ramelton,” Feversham said. “There’s a ferry across Lough Swilly to Rathmullen. You must drive the twelve miles to Ramelton. But you may not find her there.”

“If not there, I shall find her somewhere else,” Trench said, and his voice had the stubborn heat of a man who has lived too long without tenderness and will not lose the chance of it now. “Make no mistake, Feversham. I shall find her.”

He rode forward, alone with his guide. More than once he turned and saw Feversham still standing by the well—a solitary figure in a bare valley. More than once he was drawn to stop and go back, but he did not. He lifted his hand in farewell. Feversham did not answer the salute.

Feversham had no eyes for him. Six years of harsh probation had ended that morning, and yet what he felt most was not joy but a hollowing out, as if the one sustaining purpose that had kept his heart moving had been scraped away, leaving an emptiness that the world’s ordinary promises could not easily fill.

Ethne? No doubt she was long since married.

Bitterness came on him suddenly, sharp as vinegar on a raw mouth. One futile, unnecessary mistake made six years ago—one act of folly—and the taste of it still lingered, untranslatable into any language but the private dialect of regret. He saw again a room in London overlooking the quiet trees and lawns of St. James’s Park; heard the knock at the door; felt the telegram in his hand. The memory did not come as a thought so much as a sensory return—the weight of the paper, the dry sound of it unfolding, the way the world changed temperature in an instant.

He forced himself back into the present with the knowledge that some work remained.

There was his father, perhaps even now reading The Times after breakfast on the terrace at Broad Place among the Surrey pines. There was the fourth feather to return to Ramelton. There was a telegram to send to Lieutenant Sutch at Suakin.

He mounted his camel and turned west with Abou Fatma, riding slowly towards Wadi Halfa. Yet the sense of loss did not leave him, nor the anger at the old mistake that had brought his downfall. Across the shimmer of the desert air he could see, with an unnerving clarity, the wooded slopes of Ramelton as though they were painted on the horizon—a landscape of home that seemed at once impossibly near and separated from him by the whole long silence of what he had been.

And in the depth of his depression, Harry Feversham doubted—for the first time that day—the faith he had held in the “afterwards.”

On an August morning of that same year Harry Feversham rode across the bridge over the Lennon and into Ramelton.

The Soudan had baked him to a darker shade than the folk of Donegal remembered, and the long probation of his years had thinned his cheeks and hollowed his eyes. He went up the narrow street unrecognised, the iron-shod hooves ringing a hard, clean note on the stones—one sound among many: the far lace of the river under the bridge, a cartwheel grumbling somewhere behind a wall, the soft, tireless hiss of wind in the hedges. The town lay quiet as a kitchen before the first pan is set to the fire.

At the top of the hill he turned into the broad highway that runs, in one straight line, down through valleys and up over the shoulders of hills towards Letterkenny. He rode quickly, and yet it was as though he travelled in company. Not men beside him—shadows. The years between had been falling away from his mind all through his road across Egypt and the Continent, flaking off like old soot from a pot scoured too often. They were no more than visions now. He was not occupied with dreams of what might have been had he acted otherwise; the might-have-beens had grown thin and ghostly.

What had been here in this small Irish town was not thin at all. It had weight and taste. Here he had been most happy; here he had known the uttermost of misery. Here his presence had brought pleasure; here too he had done his worst harm.

Once he checked his horse opposite the church set high above the road on his right, and wondered whether Ethne was still at Ramelton—whether old Dermod Eustace lived, and what manner of welcome might await him. But the thought broke against the plain fact of where he was: sitting astride a sweating horse in an empty road on a clear August morning.

Sound came to him as it always does to a man who has been too long in the hush and thunder of alien deserts. Larks stitched their bright song into the pale blue overhead; a landrail rasped from the meadow on the left; and from the valley below the crow of a cock rose sharp and domestic, as if someone had just opened a back door and thrown down grain. Those familiar notes steadied him more than any argument. He looked about him and rode briskly down the incline and up the ascent beyond.

Again the road filled with his ghosts—phantoms of people with whom upon this very stretch he had walked and ridden and laughed; ghosts of old thoughts, and remembered words, and the particular tones in which they had been spoken. He came to a thick grove of trees, a broken fence, and a gateway with no gate. Without attending to these small signs of abandonment, he turned in and rode along a drive grown weedy and neglected, where nettles leaned over the ruts and the branches had begun to meet above like hands clasped too late.

At the end of it he came out upon an open space before a ruined house.

The sight of the tumbling walls and the rooms open to the sky struck him as a slap of cold water. He swung down, tied his horse to the branch of a tree, and ran into the house calling aloud.

No voice answered.

He ran from deserted room to deserted room. The air inside had that dead smell which comes when a place is no longer lived in: damp plaster, char, dust, and the faint green reek of plants that have begun to take possession. He descended into the garden. No one came to meet him; and from the uncut grass, the tangled disorder of flower-beds gone to seed, he understood that no one would come.

He mounted again and rode back at a sharp trot, as though speed could outstrip what he had just seen.

In Ramelton he stopped at the inn, gave his horse to the ostler, and ordered lunch. The room smelt of bread and tea and the sharpness of pickles; a good smell, homely and honest, though he scarcely tasted what he was given. The landlady waited on him with a napkin over her arm and curiosity ready as a ladle.

“So Lennon House has been burned down?” he asked. “When was that?”

“Five years ago,” she replied. “Just five years ago this summer.”

And, without needing further invitation, she poured out her story as if it were stew long kept warm: the night of the conflagration and the cause of it; the ruin of the Eustaces; the inebriety of Bastable; the death of Dermod Eustace at Glenalla. There were names and judgements and the relish with which small towns season any calamity.

“But we hope to see the house rebuilt,” she concluded, drawing breath. “It’s likely to be, we hear, when Miss Eustace is married.” Her voice carried that particular note which suggests there is more salt in the pot if only one will ask.

He did not.

“And where does Miss Eustace live now?” he said.

“At Glenalla.” She lifted her chin as though pointing with it. “Halfway on the road to Rathmullen there’s a track leads up to your left. It’s a poor mountain village is Glenalla, and no place for Miss Eustace, at all, at all. Perhaps you will be wanting to see her?”

“Yes. I shall be glad if you will order my horse to be brought round to the door,” he answered, rising to end the interview.

But she was not to be put off so neatly. She lingered in the doorway and said, as if sharing a morsel too good to keep to herself: “Well, that’s curious—that’s most curious. For only a fortnight ago a gentleman burnt just as black as yourself stayed a night here on the same errand. He asked for Miss Eustace’s address and drove up to Glenalla. Perhaps you have business with her?”

“Yes, I have business with Miss Eustace,” he replied, with a patience he had learned in harsher places. “Will you be good enough to give orders about my horse?”

While he waited he turned the leaves of the hotel book, and there, under a date towards the end of July, he saw the name of Colonel Trench. The sight of it was like the sudden taste of a spice one had not expected—bitter at first, then bringing back a whole dish of memory with it.

“You will come back, sir, to-night?” the landlady asked as he mounted.

“No,” he said. “I do not think I shall come again to Ramelton.”

And he rode down the hill and crossed the Lennon bridge once more, the river’s running talk under him taking no notice of his going, as rivers do.

Four miles on he came to the track opposite a little bay of the Lough. He turned in and rode past a few white cottages, their doors shut against the late afternoon, their chimneys quiet. The road climbed into the purple hollow of the hills. It was about five o’clock when he came to the long, straggling village of Glenalla, built without plan, as though each cottage had set itself down where it pleased and left the next to find room.

A few cottages stood together; then a gap of fields; then a small plantation of larches and a house standing by itself. Beyond the house was another gap, through which he could see straight down to the water of the Lough shining in the afternoon sun, and the white gulls poised and swooping above it. Their cries came thinly up, and with them the faint lap of water on stones—a clean, soothing sound after so much in the world that was made to clang and shriek for profit. There were no machines here, no iron engines tearing at the air; only wind, birds, and the minute, persistent noises of living things.

After passing that gap he came to a small grey church, bare to the winds upon its tiny plateau. A pathway of white shell-dust led from the door to the little wooden gate. As he drew level with it a collie barked at him from behind the gate.

Feversham looked at the dog. It was very grey about the muzzle. He noticed the markings and stopped his horse altogether. He glanced towards the church and saw the door standing open.

At once he dismounted, fastened his horse to the fence, and entered the churchyard. The collie thrust its muzzle into the back of his knee, sniffed once or twice doubtfully, and then, as if some remembered scent had unlocked a whole buried language in it, broke into an exuberant welcome.

The dog had a better memory than the landlady at the inn. It barked and wagged its tail, crouched and sprang up at the stranger’s shoulders, whirled round in front of him, burst into sharp, excited screams of pleasure, ran to the church door and barked furiously there, then ran back and jumped again upon its friend.

Feversham caught it as it stood up with its forepaws upon his chest, patted it, and laughed—once, briefly, like a man who has almost forgotten the sound of his own ease. Then he stopped laughing and stood stock-still, his eyes fixed upon the open door.

In the doorway Ethne Eustace stood.

He put the dog down and walked slowly up the shell-white path towards her. She waited on the threshold without moving, without speaking, watching him until he was close enough that he could see every alteration time had made, and every thing it had not.

Then she said simply, “Harry.”

After that there was silence; nor did he speak. At the utterance of his name the company of ghosts in which he had travelled all day vanished. Words in the mouth of someone who knows you are never merely words; they are a dialect that contains what cannot be translated. Six years had passed since his feet had crushed the gravel on the dawn of a June morning beneath her window. Now the sound of gravel under his step, and the thin cries of gulls, and the dog’s panting joy, made a kind of choir about them that had nothing to do with any distant battlefield.

They looked at one another and saw, with the clear cruelty of first sight, the changes those six years had brought. Changes unnoticed by those who had lived daily with them sprang distinct to these two.

Feversham was thin; his face wasted. The strain of life in the House of Stone had left its marks about his sunken eyes and in the look of age beyond his years. But Ethne saw at once what mattered more than fatigue and tanning. He came back without anger towards her for that fourth feather snapped from her fan. He stood quietly, self-possessed—not arrogant, yet no longer diffident. That timidity of old, the continual expectation of other men’s contempt, had gone. He had tested himself long and hard and knew he had not failed.

She saw it all, and her face lightened.

“It is not all harm which has come of these years,” she said. “They were not wasted.”

He thought then of her years in this lonely village—of her youth set down among these scattered cottages and bare hills—and with a man’s thought assumed it for exile, not knowing that nowhere else would she have chosen to live. He looked into her face and saw time’s marks there too. She had not aged greatly; her big grey eyes shone as clearly as before, and the colour was still bright on her cheeks. But there was more of character. Suffering had done what it always does: it had altered the flavour of her, deepened it. She had eaten of the tree of knowledge.

“I am sorry,” he said. “I did you a great wrong six years ago, and I need not.”

She held out her hand.

“Will you give it me, please?”

For a moment he did not understand.

“That fourth feather,” she said.

He drew his letter-case from his coat and shook two feathers out into the palm of his hand. The larger one, the ostrich feather, he held out to her. But she said, “Both.”

There was no reason why he should keep Castleton’s feather any longer. He handed them both to her. She clasped them, and with a smile pressed them against her breast as though they were not mere plumes but tokens with warmth still in them.

“I have the four feathers now,” she said.

“Yes,” Feversham answered. “All four. What will you do with them?”

Her smile broke into a laugh, quick and bright as the sudden rattle of shells underfoot.

“Do with them!” she cried, with scorn for the idea. “I shall do nothing with them. I shall keep them. I am very proud to have them to keep.”

She kept them as she had once kept his portrait. Perhaps Durrance had been right when he said that women, more than men, gather up their experiences and live upon them, looking backwards, keeping the past like a book of recipes written in a hand no one else can read. Feversham, for his part, would have dropped the feathers then and there and crushed them into the shell-dust with his heel. They had done their work; they could no longer reproach, were no longer needed to encourage; dead things.

Ethne held them tight. To her they were not dead.

“Colonel Trench was here a fortnight ago,” she said. “He told me you were bringing it back to me.”

“But he did not know of the fourth feather,” Feversham replied. “I never told any man that I had it.”

“Yes. You told Colonel Trench on your first night in the House of Stone at Omdurman. He told me.” She added, with gravity and no smile, as if stating a truth that must be precisely spoken: “I no longer hate him.”

“I am glad of that,” Feversham said. “He is a great friend of mine.”

Ethne was silent for a moment. The collie sat down, its tail thumping now and again on the hard ground, listening with that canine attentiveness that seems to take meaning from tone as much as from words, as if it too belonged to the place’s old speech.

Then she said, “I wonder whether you have forgotten our drive from Ramelton to our house when I came to fetch you from the quay? We were alone in the dog-cart, and we spoke—”

“Of the friends whom one knows for friends the first moment,” Feversham interrupted, “and whom one seems to recognise even though one has never seen them before. Indeed I remember.”

“And whom one never loses whether absent or dead,” Ethne continued. “I said that one could always be sure of such friends, and you answered—”

“I answered that one could make mistakes,” he said, interrupting again.

“Yes, and I disagreed. I said that one might seem to make mistakes, and perhaps think so for a long while, but that in the end one would be proved not to have made them.” Her voice shook a little and pleaded as she went on. “I have often thought of those words. I remembered them very clearly when Captain Willoughby brought to me the first feather, and with a great deal of remorse. I remember them again very clearly to-day, although I have no room in my thoughts for remorse. I was right, you see, and I should have clung firmly to my faith. But I did not.”

She paused, opened her hand, and gazed for a while at the white feathers lying there—mute, unmistakable, a little bundle of history in a language only the two of them fully understood.

“I was young,” she said softly. “I knew very little. I was unaware how little. I judged hastily; but to-day I understand.”

Then she turned and went inside the church.

Feversham followed her.

Ethne chose the pew at the end, where the worn oak had been darkened by generations of palms—farmers’ hands, fisherwives’ hands, children’s hands sticky with summer fruit. She sat in the corner nearest the aisle. Feversham remained standing beside her, as if he did not trust his legs to fold without betraying him.

The little church at Glenalla held its hush the way a larder holds coolness: faithfully, without fuss. Sunlight came in high through the small upper windows and lay under the roof-beams in a thin honey-coloured haze. The open door admitted the countryside in soft portions—grass-sweet air, peat-smoke from some distant kitchen fire, and the untroubled, layered sound of life: bees worrying the hedges, swallows cutting the space with their quick cries, the far clink of a gate-chain, and beyond it all the sea’s steady breathing. No cart-wheels here, no iron shriek, no factory hammering—only the acoustic order of a place that still knew its own rhythm.

“I am glad you remembered our drive,” she said, looking straight ahead, not at him, as though she were speaking a lesson learned by heart. “And what we said. It is important to me that you should remember.”

Her voice had the steadiness of someone who had been seasoning these words for days, turning them over like grain in a wooden bowl until the husk fell away and only the necessary remained.

“Because—though I have got you back—I am going to send you away from me again. You will be one of the absent friends whom I shall not lose because you are absent.”

She did not falter. If the sentence cut her, she would not let it cut raggedly. She had given herself two weeks to make it clean.

“I know you will mind,” she continued, still facing forward, “just as I do. But there is no help for it. At all events you are at home again, with the right to be at home. That is a great comfort to me. And there are other reasons—greater reasons—from which we can both take comfort.”

A swallow darted through the open door and out again, so quick it seemed a thought. Ethne’s hands lay quiet in her lap.

“Colonel Trench told me enough of your captivity to convince me that we both see with the same eyes,” she said. “We both understand that this second parting—hard as it is—is still a very slight thing compared with the other, our first parting six years ago.”

The words went on evenly, but the church seemed to listen, and the summer murmur outside drew back, respectful.

“I felt very lonely after that,” she said, “as I shall not feel lonely now. There was a great barrier between us then, separating us forever. We should never have met again—here or afterwards. I am quite sure of that. But you have broken the barrier down by all your pain and bravery during these last years. I am no less sure of that.”

She paused, and the quiet pressed in like cool linen.

“So although we shall not see one another here, and as long as we live,” she said at last, “the afterwards is quite sure for us both. And we can wait for that. You can—you have waited with so much strength all these years since we parted. And I can too, for I get strength from your victory.”

The silence that followed was not empty. To Feversham her words fell like water on a mouthful of dry bread—simple, life-giving, and almost unbearable for the relief of it. They drew the sting from memories he had carried like grit under the skin: nights of waking in strange rooms with the smell of dust and sweat, the far call to prayer sliding over rooftops, the endless, sand-choked days. He had told Sutch once that in his last moment he would hear Ethne’s bitter words again and die to their echo. Standing now in this little church, he knew that prophecy to have been false. If any voice would follow him into the dark, it would be this one—steadfast, forgiving, and sorrowful in its courage.

Her proposal to separate did not surprise him. He had heard she was engaged. He did not argue. He only waited, for he felt there was more she meant to lay down between them like a final offering.

Ethne delayed. Not with tricks, but with pauses—those small stretches of time in which the heart, like dough, is allowed to rest before it must be shaped and baked. When he went out through the church door into the sunlight and summer sound, his shadow would leave the threshold; and after that she meant never to speak with him or look upon him again until life ended. She had the right—she told herself—to keep him a little longer, just as one keeps a last mouthful in the mouth to catch every note of taste before swallowing.

At length she said, “I am going to be married—immediately. I am to marry a friend of yours, Colonel Durrance.”

Feversham answered without pause, for this at least he could give her: no additional struggle.

“He has cared for you a long while. I was not aware of it until I went away. But thinking everything over—I thought it likely. In a very little time I became sure.”

“He is blind.”

“Blind!” The word broke from him before he could keep it contained. “He, of all men—blind!”

“Exactly,” Ethne said. “He—of all men.” Her eyes remained fixed on the pale stone ahead. “His blindness explains everything—why I marry him, why I send you away. It was after he went blind that I became engaged to him. It was before Captain Willoughby came to me with the first feather. It was between those two events.”

She breathed once, carefully.

“After you went away one thinks,” she said, “rather carefully. I used to lie awake and think, and I resolved that two men’s lives should not be spoilt because of me.”

“Mine was not,” Feversham said quickly. “Please believe that.”

“Partly it was,” she returned, as if she had weighed that sentence too and found it true. “You would not own it for my sake, but it was. I was determined that a second should not be. And so, when Colonel Durrance went blind—you know the man he was—you can understand what blindness meant to him: the loss of everything he cared for—”

“Except you.”

“Yes,” she said quietly. “Except me. So I became engaged to him.”

Outside, a cart creaked somewhere along the village street; the sound was old, wood on wood, and did not jar. Modern noise did not belong in this place. Here, even grief had room to speak without being drowned.

“But he has grown very quick,” Ethne went on. “You cannot guess how quick. And he sees so very clearly. A hint tells him the whole hidden truth.”

“At present,” she added, “he knows nothing of the four feathers.”

“Are you sure?” Feversham asked suddenly.

She turned her face toward him for the first time since she had sat down, startled into movement as though a hand had touched her shoulder.

“Yes. Why?”

“Lieutenant Sutch was at Suakin while I was at Omdurman,” he said. “He knew I was a prisoner there. He sent messages to me—he tried to organise my escape.”
