“Oh,” Ethne said, her surprise softening into comprehension. “Colonel Durrance certainly knew you were in Omdurman. He saw you in Wadi Halfa, and he heard you had gone south into the desert. He was distressed about it. He asked a friend to get news of you, and the friend got news that you were in Omdurman. He told me so himself—and yes, he told me he would try to arrange for your escape. No doubt he did it through Lieutenant Sutch.”

She gathered herself again.

“He has been at Wiesbaden with an oculist,” she said. “He only returned a week ago. Otherwise he would have told me about it. Very likely he was the reason Lieutenant Sutch was at Suakin.”

Then, with the precision of someone naming the one ingredient that would ruin a whole pot, she said, “But he knows nothing of the four feathers. He only knows that our engagement was abruptly broken off; he believes I have no longer any thought of you at all.”

Her voice thinned, and the next words came as if they were pulled from her with careful force.

“But if you come back—if you and I saw anything of each other—however calmly we met, however indifferently we spoke—he would guess. He is so quick he would be sure to guess.”

She paused and whispered, the blood rising in her face as if the truth itself were heat.

“And he would guess right.”

Feversham did not move closer. He would not add pressure to an hour already heavy with it. But he understood—understood with a clarity that felt like cold water poured into his chest.

“Yes,” he said. “I see. And he must not guess.”

“No,” she returned at once. “He must not. I am so glad you see that too, Harry. The straight and simple thing is the only thing for us to do. He must never guess, for—as you said—he has nothing left but me.”

“Is Durrance here?” Feversham asked.

“He is staying at the vicarage.”

“Very well.” Feversham’s voice was steady now, made so by the very finality of what they were doing. “It is only fair I should tell you—I had no thought you would wait. I had no wish that you should; I had no right to such a wish.”

He looked down at the old boards underfoot, their grain like faint ripples in a dark sea.

“When you gave me that fourth feather in the little room at Ramelton,” he said, “with the music coming faintly through the door, I understood your meaning. There was to be a complete—an irrevocable end. We were not to be the merest acquaintances. So I said nothing to you of the plan which came clear and definite into my mind at the very time when you gave me the feathers.”

He lifted his eyes briefly, not to plead, but to be exact.

“You see, I might never have succeeded. I might have died trying. I might even perhaps have shirked the attempt. It would have been time enough to speak if I came back. So I never formed any wish that you should wait.”

“That was what Colonel Trench told me,” Ethne said.

“I told him that too?”

“On your first night in the House of Stone.”

“Well—it’s just the truth.” His mouth tightened, then softened. “The most I hoped for—and I did hope for it every hour of every day—was that, if I did come home, you would take back your feather, and that we might—not renew our friendship here, but see something of one another afterwards.”

“Yes,” Ethne said. “Then there will be no parting.”

She spoke simply, without a sigh. But she looked at him then and smiled—a small, brave thing. It told him, more than any outcry could have done, what the cost would be to her.

And in that moment he understood, with a completeness he had never reached in all his solitary rehearsals of the past, what it must have cost her six years ago to be left standing with her pride and heart both struck down.

“What trouble you must have gone through!” he cried.

She looked him over as if to remind him he was not the only one who had been wounded and lived.

“Not I alone,” she said gently. “I passed no nights in the House of Stone.”

“But it was my fault.” His words came hurriedly now, as if he could pay some last debt by naming it. “Do you remember what you said when the morning came through the blinds? ‘It’s not right that one should suffer so much pain.’ It was not right.”

“I had forgotten the words,” she said, “oh, a long time since—until Colonel Trench reminded me. I should never have spoken them. When I did, I was not thinking they would live so in your thoughts. I am sorry that I spoke them.”

“Oh, they were just enough,” Feversham said, and even managed a short laugh, cracked at the edge. “I never blamed you for them. I used to think they would be the last words I should hear when I turned my face to the wall. But you have given me others to-day wherewith to replace them.”

“Thank you,” she said quietly.

There was nothing more to be decided. The solemn issue had been met, faced, and settled. Feversham wondered, in a distant part of himself, why she did not rise at once and end it. He could not bring himself to talk of his travels now, of dangers and escapes, as though he were recounting a campaign over supper. The occasion seemed too vital for that, too much like the closing of a door on a room where a fire still burned.

Yet Ethne kept him there—by stillness, by the fact of her presence. Calm as she looked, longing ached in her like hunger. Just for a little longer, she begged herself, measuring time not by clocks but by light.

The sun was withdrawing from the church walls. A bright strip of gold still lay on the grey stone, and she watched it narrow as if it were a wick being consumed.

“I am glad you escaped from Omdurman without the help of Lieutenant Sutch or Colonel Durrance,” she said at last. “I wanted so much that everything should be done by you alone—without anybody’s help or interference.”

After that came another stretch of silence, full of the summer’s faint, living noises outside. Once or twice she looked toward the wall. Each time the golden space was smaller. Each time she knew her minutes were thinning.

“You suffered horribly at Dongola,” she said in a low voice. “Colonel Trench told me.”

“What does it matter now?” Feversham answered. “That time seems rather far away to me.”

Ethne’s voice softened further, as if she were touching something fragile.

“Had you anything of mine with you?” she asked.

“I had your white feather.”

“But anything else? Any little thing I had given you—in the other days?”

“Nothing.”

“I had your photograph,” she said. “I kept it.”

Feversham bent down toward her, startled as if warmth had suddenly been put into his hands.

“You did!”

Ethne nodded.

“Yes. The moment I went upstairs that night I packed up your presents and addressed them to your rooms.”

“Yes—I got them in London.”

“But I put your photograph aside first of all to keep,” she said. “I burnt all your letters after I had addressed the parcel and taken it down to the hall to be sent away. I had just finished burning your letters when I heard your step upon the gravel in the early morning underneath my windows. But I had already put your photograph aside. I have it now. I shall keep it and the feathers together.”

After a moment she added, almost as if she spoke to herself, “I rather wish you had had something of mine with you all the time.”

“I had no right to anything,” he said.

The strip of gold on the stone had become no more than a thin blade; it seemed impossible that something so bright could be so narrow.

“What will you do now?” she asked.

“I shall go home first and see my father,” he said. “It will depend upon the way we meet.”

“You will let Colonel Durrance know,” she said. “I would like to hear about it.”

“Yes,” Feversham replied. “I will write to Durrance.”

The last gold went out. The church filled with the clear light of a summer evening—colourless, truthful, and without mercy.

“I shall not see you for a long while,” Ethne said, and for the first time her voice broke into a sob. “I shall not have a letter from you again.”

She leaned forward a little, bowing her head because tears had gathered and would spill. Then she rose—bravely, as though bravery were a habit she would not set aside even now.

Together they went out of the church side by side. As they walked she leaned toward him so that their sleeves touched. The contact was slight, but it struck through him like a taste remembered.

Feversham untied his horse and mounted. As his foot found the stirrup Ethne drew her dog close against her skirt, holding it as if it were something steady in a world turning.

“Good-bye,” she said.

She did not try to smile. She held out her hand.

He took it and bent down from the saddle close to her. She kept her eyes on his though the tears brimmed in them, bright as the last light had been on the stone.

“Good-bye,” he said.

He held her hand for a little while longer than he ought, then released it and rode down the hill.

After a hundred yards he stopped and looked back.

Ethne had stopped too. With that space between them and their faces turned toward one another, they remained. She made no sign—no wave, no final gesture—only stood and looked, as if to wave would be to admit a lesser ending than the truth.

Then she turned away and went up the village street toward her house alone and very slowly.

Feversham watched until she went in at the gate. She grew dim and blurred to him before she reached it. Yet he could see, even through the blurring, that she did not look back.

He turned his horse and rode down the hill. The wrong he had done so long ago could not be undone, not even by six years of labour, pain, and courage. It would go on living; and its consequence would be sorrow to the end of life for someone other than himself.

That she bore the sorrow without complaint—doing the straight, simple thing her loyal nature demanded—did not lessen his remorse. It sharpened it. It taught him, with a bitter clarity, that she least of all deserved unhappiness. The harm was irreparable. Others might have forgotten. Ethne would not.

For Ethne was of those who neither lightly feel nor lightly forget; who, if they love, cannot love with half a heart. And she would be alone now, he knew—alone in spite of her marriage—alone to the very end, and at the last moment of death.

The dusk came down softly on the hills, but Ethne felt no softness in it. She went into the farmhouse parlour as though she were stepping back into a life that had been paused and now must be resumed—chairs in their places, the clean hearth, the familiar scent of turf and woodsmoke waiting like an old habit. Though the evening was warm by any sane measure of summer, chill sat inside her bones, so she had the fire lit. The first flare caught the iron bars, and soon the coals glowed like pomegranate seeds split open, bright with a heat that promised comfort and offered none.

She sat close, hands folded as if to keep them from trembling, and stared into the heart of the fire. That particular stillness took her—an attitude that in her meant not calm but strain drawn so tight it could hum. The moment she had lived toward, counted down to in her mind as women count the minutes to bread rising, had come and been spent. The half-hour was over. Harry Feversham had gone.

Now the village was itself again: remote, small, set out of the world among the hills, with its own evening noises—distant cattle shifting in a byre, a cartwheel somewhere complaining once and then falling silent, the soft punctuation of a gate latch. All of it sounded too clear in the quiet, as though the air had been rinsed. And in that rinsed air, she was alone—more alone than she had been since Willoughby sailed away down the Salcombe estuary on that August morning.

For months—no, for longer than she cared to measure—she had lived with an inward appointment. From the time of Willoughby’s coming she had looked forward night and day to the one half-hour when Harry would be with her. She had thought she was practical, brisk, capable of managing a farm and a household with firm hands and plain speech; but the truth lay there, undeniable as a pot left to boil over: she had made a life around that half-hour. It had come, it had passed, and the world felt suddenly emptied, as if someone had carried away a table and left the room echoing.

Yet she had known this hour would follow. She had foreseen it the way you foresee the sharpness after a cordial—how sweetness can make the next taste bitter by contrast. She had always looked beyond it. Tomorrow she would be steadier; tomorrow she would compare today’s parting with the parting on the night of the ball at Lennon House, and the comparison would shame this pain into proportion. Today would be a small thing beside that.

The fire snapped, a resinous little crack that sounded in her ear too loud. She wondered—because the mind will busy itself when the heart is raw—what Harry would do now he had returned. She found herself building him a future in the careful way she planned the rebuilding of a wall: stone upon stone, sound work, a great distinction rising from patient effort. The thought warmed her for a moment, and then the warmth went out again.

Dermod’s old collie came padding to her knee with the quiet certainty of animals that read distress more truly than words. His muzzle, greyed with age, nudged under her hand. His breath smelled faintly of the day’s milk and damp grass, and there was comfort in the honest animal scent. Ethne rose, took his head between her hands, and kissed it, pressing her lips into the wiry fur as though she could borrow steadiness from him.

He was very old. He would die soon and leave her. The fact arrived without drama, like a cold draft. After that—years and years perhaps—before she herself lay down and knew that the great moment was near. The thought made her shiver, and she drew closer to the fire, as if warmth could be hoarded against the future.

A knock came at the door.

A servant entered to say Colonel Durrance was waiting.

“Yes,” Ethne said, and the word was clipped, controlled. She rose as he entered, went forward to meet him as though she had not been on the edge of breaking. She did not shirk the part she had set herself. Whatever secret chamber her grief kept, she stepped out of it the instant she was summoned.

She spoke with him as though no unusual thing had happened an hour before. She talked of their marriage, of the rebuilding of Lennon House—rooflines, rooms, the practical matters that could be spoken of without betraying the pulse underneath. Her voice did what she required of it; her face held its lines. Difficult, yes, but she had grown used to difficulty as to weather.

The maid brought in the tea. The little domestic ritual—cup on saucer, the faint rattle of china, the smell of strong leaves steeping—made a frame around the evening, as if a table could hold together what a heart could not. Durrance sat, listening as blind men listen, not merely to words but to the space between them.

Then he asked, with a quietness that was nearly harsh, “Will you play to me the Musoline Overture?”

“Not to-night,” Ethne said at once. “I am rather tired.”

The refusal had hardly left her mouth before she felt it like a retreat, and retreat was one of the small things she had promised herself not to permit. It was in the small daily happenings that she must be most careful. She forced a smile that might have deceived anyone less trained in attention than Durrance.

“Still—I think I can play the overture.”

She took down her violin. The varnished wood was cool under her fingers, the familiar weight settling against her collarbone. She drew the bow across the strings, and the first note rose into the room, thin at first and then full, as though the instrument breathed. Outside, the farm noises seemed to withdraw. Even the fire, for a time, kept its tongue.

She played it through from beginning to end. It was not mere accomplishment—her technique precise, her phrasing controlled—so much as something laid bare without permission. The overture, always, carried with it more than melody: it carried the memory of another place, another hour, another kind of light.

Durrance stood at the window with his back towards her while she played, still as a post. When the last note died, he turned and walked to her side.

“I was rather a brute,” he said quietly, “to ask you to play that overture to-night.”

“I wasn’t anxious to play,” she answered, and laid the violin aside as though it were hot.

“I know.” There was no apology in his tone now, only purpose. “But I was anxious to find out something, and I knew no other way of finding it out.”

Ethne turned a startled face up to him. The blood seemed to drain from her skin, leaving it too pale under the lamplight.

“What do you mean?” Her voice held suspense like a held breath.

“You are so seldom off your guard. Only—indeed—at rare times when you play. Once before, when you played that overture, you were off your guard. I thought that if I could get you to play it again to-night—the overture which was once strummed out in a dingy café at Wadi Halfa—to-night again I should find you off your guard.”

The name of that place, spoken here among the clean Devon hills, struck like a foreign coin dropped into a quiet room. It took her breath away. For a moment she could only look at him, wide-eyed, as though he had reached into a locked drawer.

He could not know. It was impossible. He did not know.

But Durrance went on, gently relentless.

“Well?” he asked, and there was even a faint smile in his voice. “Did you take back your feather? The fourth one?”

The words were incredible. They seemed to hang in the air like smoke that would not disperse. It took a long time before Ethne could be sure they had been spoken and not imagined by a mind rubbed raw by the day. Her senses were overstrained; she distrusted them. But he repeated his question, and disbelief fell away.

“Who told you of any fourth feather?” she asked, and the effort to make the question sound ordinary made it sound sharp instead.

“Trench,” he said. “I met him at Dover. But he only told me of the fourth feather.” The smile was still there, quiet, almost rueful. “I knew of the three before. Trench would never have told me of the fourth had I not known of the three. For I should not have met him as he landed from the steamer at Dover. I should not have asked him, ‘Where is Harry Feversham?’ And for me to know of the three was enough.”

“How do you know?” she cried, and despair slipped through her composure like water through a cracked cup. She moved closer, and he took gently hold of her arm—not possessive, but steadying, as though he feared she might fall.

“But since I know,” he protested, “what does it matter how I know? I have known a long while—ever since Captain Willoughby came to The Pool with the first feather. I waited to tell you that I knew until Harry Feversham came back, and he came to-day.”

Ethne sat down again, stunned. For so long she had guarded her secret with the vigilance of someone watching a small flame in wind; to realise that for a year it had been no secret came like a blow. Yet even in the shock, she understood she must gain time, gather her scattered faculties into command. So she spoke of something unimportant, a small domestic diversion, as one stirs a pot slowly to keep it from sticking.

“You were in the church, then?” she asked. “Or you heard us upon the steps? Or you met—him as he rode away?”

“Not one of the conjectures is right,” Durrance said, and she heard the satisfaction he took in that. He had his vanities like other men; since blindness had come upon him, one vanity had grown particularly keen. He prided himself on the quickness of his perception. It delighted him to make discoveries no one expected a blind man to make, and to announce them unexpectedly. It was an added pleasure to tell the steps by which he had arrived at his conclusion, to let his listener follow the trail by sound and inference.

“Not one of your conjectures is right, Ethne,” he repeated, almost inviting the question.

“Then how did you find out?” she asked, giving him what he wanted, buying herself a few seconds more.

“I knew from Trench that Harry Feversham would come some day, and soon. I passed the church this afternoon. Your collie dog barked at me—so I knew you were inside.” He paused, as if hearing again the bark in his memory. “But a saddled horse was tied up beside the gate. So some one else was with you, and not any one from the village. Then I got you to play, and that told me who it was who rode the horse.”

“Yes,” Ethne said vaguely. She had scarcely listened; the words were like rain striking a window while a room burns. “Yes, I see.”

Then her voice changed—became definite, controlled. She had gathered herself.

“You went away to Wiesbaden for a year,” she said. “You went away just after Captain Willoughby came. Was that the reason why you went away?”

“I went because neither you nor I could have kept up the game of pretences we were playing,” Durrance replied, and there was no bitterness, only plainness. “You were pretending that you had no thought for Harry Feversham—that you hardly cared whether he was alive or dead. I was pretending not to have found out that beyond everything in the world you cared for him. Some day or other we should have failed, each one in turn. I dared not fail, nor dared you.

“I could not let you”—and his voice softened here, as though he touched something fragile—“you who had said, ‘Two lives must not be spoilt because of me,’ live through a year thinking that two lives had been spoilt. You on your side dared not let me, who had said, ‘Marriage between a blind man and a woman is only possible when there is more than friendship on both sides,’ know that upon one side there was only friendship, and we were so near to failing. So I went away.”

“You did not fail,” Ethne said quietly. “It was only I who failed.”

The self-reproach came from deep. She had set herself one task worth doing: to guard him from knowledge that would set the crown upon his calamities. She had failed. He had set himself to guard her from understanding she had failed, and he had succeeded. It was not humiliation that stung—no petty wound of being outwitted. It was the knowledge that her failure, if it had hurt him, would rob him of his last chance at happiness. That was the thorn.

“But it was not your fault,” Durrance said. “Once or twice—as I said—you were off your guard, but the convincing facts were not revealed to me in that way. When you played the Musoline Overture before, on the night of the day when Willoughby brought you such good news, I took to myself that happiness of yours which inspired your playing. You must not blame yourself. On the contrary, you should be glad that I have found out.”

“Glad!” The word broke from her, incredulous.

“Yes—for my sake—glad.” He spoke with a careful gentleness, as if guiding her over rough ground. “Two lives should not be spoilt because of you. Had you had your way, had I not found out, not two but three lives would have been spoilt because of you—because of your loyalty.”

“Three?” The number seemed to accuse her.

“Yours. Yes—yes, yours, Feversham’s, and mine. It was hard enough to keep the pretence during the few weeks we were in Devonshire. Own to it, Ethne! When I went to London to see my oculist it was a relief; it gave you a pause, a rest wherein to drop pretence and be yourself. It could not have lasted long even in Devonshire. But what when we came to live under the same roof, and there were no visits to the oculist, when we saw each other every hour of every day? Sooner or later the truth must have come to me.

“It might have come gradually—a suspicion added to a suspicion and another to that until no doubt was left. Or it might have flashed out in one terrible moment. But it would have been made clear. And then, Ethne? What then? You aimed at a compensation; you wanted to make up to me for the loss of what I love—my career, the army, the special service in the strange quarters of the world. A fine compensation to sit in front of you knowing you had married a cripple out of pity, and that in so doing you had crippled yourself and foregone the happiness which is yours by right. Whereas now—”

“Whereas now?” she echoed, because the old habit of obedience to the flow of conversation still held, though her heart was stumbling.

“I remain your friend,” he said very gently, “which I would rather be than your unloved husband.”

Ethne did not answer. The decision had been taken out of her hands as cleanly as a knife takes a thread. Relief and shame, gratitude and grief, all pulled at her at once, and she could not speak without choosing one and betraying the others.

“You sent Harry away this afternoon,” Durrance said. “You said good-bye to him twice.”

At “twice” Ethne lifted her head sharply. But before she could ask what he meant, he explained, with that quiet precision which made his blindness seem almost a different kind of sight.

“Once in the church—again upon your violin.”

He took up the violin from the chair where she had laid it, handling it with care, as one might hold something living. The instrument’s polished body caught the lamplight; its strings gave a faint, sympathetic whisper.

“It has been a very good friend, your violin,” he said. “A good friend to me—to us all. You will understand that, Ethne, very soon. I stood at the window while you played it. I had never heard anything in my life half so sad as your farewell to Harry Feversham, and yet it was nobly sad. It was true music; it did not complain.”

He set the violin down again.

“I am going to send a messenger to Rathmullen. Harry cannot cross Lough Swilly to-night. The messenger will bring him back to-morrow.”

The day had brought Ethne many shocks, and this one, though it was mercy, struck her with a force that finally broke her restraint. As Durrance bent slightly towards her, he became aware that she was crying—silently, without the least sound, as if even tears must not disturb the house’s quiet. For once they had their way.

He took his cap and walked noiselessly to the door. The restraint in his movements was not caution but respect, as though the very air around her grief should not be jarred. As he opened the door, Ethne rose quickly.

“Don’t go for a moment,” she said, and crossed from the hearth to the centre of the room, as if moving could keep her from sinking. “The oculist at Wiesbaden?” she asked. “He gave you a hope?”

He stood still. In the pause she heard everything—the fire’s low roar, the tick of a cooling kettle, the far-off murmur of night creatures outside, sounds that made the silence more intimate. He seemed to weigh whether he should lie, as people lie out of kindness, or speak truth, as people speak out of dignity.

“No,” he said at length. “There is no hope. But I am not so helpless as at one time I was afraid that I should be. I can get about, can’t I? Perhaps one of these days I shall go on a journey—one of the long journeys amongst the strange people in the East.”

Then he went from the house upon his errand.

He had learned his lesson long ago, and the violin had taught it him. It had spoken again that afternoon, and though with a different voice had offered him the same message. The true music cannot complain.

In the first clear weeks of the next summer, when the roses at Broad Place had begun to smell warm in the noon and the clipped yews held last night’s blackbird-song like water in a bowl, two old men sat after breakfast upon the terrace with their newspapers spread between the cups and the silver.

It was a quiet meal—eggs just set, bacon crisped to the edge where fat turns sweet, toast that snapped under the knife and gave out that brown, comforting scent of wheat well baked. The servant had warmed the pot so the tea would not thin at the last pour. Even the country had its proper music: a rake drawn in some distant garden; a cartwheel complaining softly on the lane; larks stitching the sky with thin, persistent sound.

General Feversham, older and drier, turned over a sheet with the deliberate care of a man who had learned to husband every small comfort. His finger halted on a column.

“I see Osman Digna’s back at Suakin,” said he. “There’s likely to be some fighting.”

Lieutenant Sutch, who had always carried the world behind his eyes like a second map, made a small, dismissive noise and laid his paper down.

“Oh,” he said, “he will not do much harm.”

But the terrace, the linen, the steady clink of spoon against china—these slid away from him as if some hand had drawn a curtain. He saw instead the white city by the Red Sea trembling in heat, the brown plains round it, halfa grass tangled like rope, and far off the hills towards Khor Gwob—hard lines against a harder sky.

“A stuffy place Suakin, eh, Sutch?” Feversham said, as if tasting the word.

“Appallingly stuffy. I heard of an officer who went down on parade at six o’clock of the morning there—sunstruck in the temples right through a regulation helmet. Yes, a town of dank heat! But I was glad to be there—very glad,” he added, and there was feeling in it, as though a man might be glad even of a furnace if it gave him at last something that mattered.

“Yes,” said Feversham briskly; “ibex, eh?”

“No,” replied Sutch. “All the ibex had been shot off by the English garrison for miles round.”

“No? Something to do, then. That’s it?”

“Yes, that’s it, Feversham. Something to do.”

They bent again to their papers. The terrace held its morning peace, as though war were only print and not a taste of dust in the teeth. Yet even in that peace there were sounds that insisted on being heard: the bees worrying at lavender; the distant hammering of some farm repair; the clean, unbought song of a thrush that no one had paid to sing. Feversham listened without seeming to, with the instinct of a soldier grown old who finds his rest not in silence, but in honest, living noises—those that belong to land and weather, not to engines.

After a little, a footman came out with the post and set beside each man a small pile of letters. Feversham ran over his envelopes with a quick eye, selected one, and gave a grunt of satisfaction. He drew from a case a pair of spectacles and set them upon his nose.

“From Ramelton?” asked Sutch, dropping his newspaper upon the flags.

“From Ramelton,” Feversham answered. “I’ll light a cigar first.”

He laid the letter down on the garden table between them, took a cigar-case from his pocket, and, in spite of Sutch’s impatience, proceeded with the ceremony as though it were part of the day’s proper order. He cut the end neatly, as a careful cook trims a joint, and struck his match so that the flame took with a clean whisper and no sulphurous stench to offend the morning. The old man had grown an epicure in this respect; a letter from Ramelton was a luxury to be taken with every accessory of comfort he could command.

He settled back, stretched his legs, drew and tested the cigar until he was satisfied it drew well, and only then took up the letter and opened it.

“From him?” Sutch asked.

“No; from her.”

“Ah!”

Feversham read slowly. Sutch, leaning forward and pretending an interest in the roses beyond the balustrade, tried not to peep across the table. When Feversham finished, he turned back to the first page and began again, as though a second reading might translate something which stubbornly refused to be put into his own plain, military vocabulary.

“Any news?” Sutch asked at last, with a casual air.

“They are very pleased with the house now that it’s rebuilt.”

“Anything more?”

“Yes. Harry’s finished the sixth chapter of his history of the war.”

“Good!” Sutch said. “You’ll see, he’ll do that well. He has imagination, he knows the ground, he was present while the war went on. Moreover, he was in the bazaars, he saw the under side of it.”

“Yes.” Feversham’s mouth tightened at the corner, half approval, half that familiar stiffness. “But you and I won’t read it, Sutch.”

Sutch made a sound of protest, but Feversham held up a hand.

“No; I am wrong. You may, for you can give me a good many years.”

He went back to the letter; and again Sutch, unable to help himself, asked—

“Anything more?”

“Yes. They are coming here in a fortnight.”

“Good,” said Sutch. “I shall stay.”

He rose and took a turn along the terrace, as if movement could keep his thoughts from crowding too thickly. Under his steps the stone held yesterday’s warmth. The air tasted faintly of clipped box and the burnt-sugar scent of sun on grass. When he returned, he found Feversham sitting with the letter upon his knees and a frown of great perplexity on his face, as though some word in it had been written in a dialect his mind had never learned.

“You know, Sutch, I never understood,” Feversham said quietly. “Did you?”

“Yes,” Sutch replied. “I think I did.”

He did not try to explain. Some experiences, like a dish whose flavour depends upon a herb known only in one valley, cannot be rendered into the speech of another place without turning thin and false. And it was as well, he thought, that Feversham never would understand. For he could not understand without much self-reproach; and the general had always lived under a code in which the wrong words—coward, disgrace—were stamped so hard into a man’s life that even their erasure left a bruise.

“Do you ever see Durrance?” Feversham asked suddenly.

“Yes, I see a good deal of Durrance. He is abroad just now.”

Feversham turned towards him, the cigar’s smoke drifting between them in slow blue folds.

“He came to Broad Place when you went to Suakin, and talked to me for half an hour. He was Harry’s best man. Well, that too I never understood. Did you?”

“Yes,” said Sutch. “I understood that as well.”

“Oh!” Feversham exclaimed; and, as he had always done, he took the questions he could not understand and put them aside out of his thoughts like papers in a drawer.

He did not turn to his other letters. He sat smoking and looked out across the summer country, listening to the sounds rising distinctly from the fields: the far-off clatter of a reaper; the wind nudging through leaves; children’s voices—small, sharp notes that carried and vanished. There was health in the pattern of it, a soundscape made by living things and honest work. Sutch, who had finished his correspondence, watched him and let the silence run its proper length.

“I have been thinking,” Feversham said at last. “Have you noticed the date of the month, Sutch?”

Sutch looked up quickly, as if a bugle had sounded in his head.

“Yes,” said he. “This day next week will be the anniversary of our attack upon the Redan, and Harry’s birthday.”

“Exactly,” replied Feversham. “Why shouldn’t we start the Crimean nights again?”

Sutch sprang up as though youth had been returned to him for the space of a heartbeat.

“Splendid!” he cried. “Can we muster a tableful, do you think?”

“Let’s see,” said Feversham; and, ringing a handbell upon the table, he sent the servant for the Army List.

So, bending over that Army List, their heads close together like conspirators over a recipe, the two veterans may be left—counting names as though names alone could summon back a time, and a table laid in friendship could keep memory from thinning and dying away.

But of one other figure in this story a final word must be said.

That night, when the invitations had been sent out from Broad Place and no longer a light gleamed from any window of the house, a man leaned over the rail of a steamer anchored at Port Said and listened. Arab coolies tramped up and down the planks with their coal baskets between the barges and the ship’s side. Their song rose and fell with the labour—call and answer, breath and burden—so that the night itself seemed to move in rhythm. It was not music for sale, not a tune dressed up for travellers; it was sound made to hold bodies together through work, and it carried a comfort that the shriek of modern machinery never could.

Across the water came the clamour of the streets: voices, footsteps, a donkey’s bray, laughter breaking like crockery in a courtyard, and beneath it all the low, ceaseless hiss and lap of the harbour. He pictured the flare of braziers upon the quays—the fat smoke of charcoal, the metallic tang of hot iron, the quick sting of spices tossed into oil somewhere in a stall. He imagined lighted port-holes, and dark funnels ahead and behind in the procession of anchored ships, each one a floating town with its own breath and noise.

Attended by a servant, he had come back to the East again.

Early the next morning the steamer moved through the canal; and toward sunset it passed out into the chills of the Gulf of Suez. Kassassin, Tel-el-Kebir, Tamai, Tamanieb, the attack upon McNeill’s zareeba—Durrance lived again through the good years of his activity, the years of plenty. The names returned like the refrains of an old song, and yet each man hears such refrains in his own inner language, where no other ear can truly follow.

Within that country on the west the long preparations were going steadily forward which would one day roll up the Dervish Empire and crush it into dust. Upon the glacis of the ruined fort of Sinkat, Durrance had promised himself to take a hand in that great work; but the desert which he loved had smitten and cast him out.

But at all events the boat steamed southwards into the Red Sea.

Three nights more, and though he would not see it, the Southern Cross would lift slantwise into the sky.
